.

If
(2^{y}\geq\dots\geq 2^{x_{0}}=1)
is
(2^{x_{0}}=1)
then the loop exists, and the algorithm checks if
\varphi(\bm{0})
is a solution.

Figure 1:
Flowchart of the main loop of
[
CMS24
]
.

Step I (division by
2^{y}
).

Let
2^{x}
and
2^{y}
be the largest and second-largest terms in
\theta

(so,
{x\neq x_{0}}
).
The first step to eliminate
x
is to symbolically divide all linear occurrences of this variable, as well as all remainder variables
\bm{r}
, by
2^{y}
.
That is, the algorithm rewrites the linear occurrences of
x
as
q_{x}\cdot 2^{y}+r_{x}
, and
\bm{r}
as
\bm{q}\cdot 2^{y}+\bm{r}^{\prime}
, where the fresh variables
(q_{x},\bm{q})
and
(r_{x},\bm{r})
represent the
quotient
and
remainder
of the division by
2^{y}
, respectively.
The variables
r_{x}
and
\bm{r}^{\prime}
are remainder variables in the next iteration of the main loop;
and indeed the algorithm adds the constraints
r_{x}<2^{y}
and
\bm{r}^{\prime}<2^{y}
to the system. The variables
q_{x}
and
\bm{q}
are called
quotient variables
.
The procedure introduces a further expression
u=2^{x-y}
, with
u
fresh,
and through several manipulations decouples the quotient variables from all other variables except
u
(this is similar to a
monadic decomposition

[
Lib03
]
). The key equivalences enabling
this decoupling are given in the next lemma. In the context of the algorithm, the integer
t
in this lemma corresponds to a linear term featuring the variable
u
and the quotient variables
\bm{q}
, whereas the integer
s
corresponds to a linear-exponential term involving the remainder variables
r_{x}

and
\bm{r}^{\prime}
, linear occurrences of
y
, and all the variables in
\theta
that are distinct from
x
and
y
. The key point is that, in the right-hand side of the equivalences in the lemma,
t
and
s
are decoupled (that is, they never appear together within a single (in)equality).

Lemma 3
[
CMS24
]
.

Let
C,D\in\mathbb{Z}
, with
C\leq D
. For
y\in\mathbb{N}
,
t\in\mathbb{Z}
, and
{s\in[C\cdot 2^{y}..D\cdot 2^{y}]}
, the following equivalences hold:

1.

t\cdot 2^{y}+s=0\iff\bigvee_{r=C}^{D}\big(t+r=0\land s=r\cdot 2^{y}\big)
,

2.

t\cdot 2^{y}+s\leq 0\iff\bigvee_{r=C}^{D}\big(t+r\leq 0\land(r-1)\cdot 2^{y}<s\leq r\cdot 2^{y}\big)
,

3.

t\cdot 2^{y}+s<0\iff\bigvee_{r=C}^{D}\big(t+r+1\leq 0\land s=r\cdot 2^{y}\big)\lor\big(t+r\leq 0\land(r-1)\cdot 2^{y}<s<r\cdot 2^{y}\big)
.

To see
Lemma

3
in action,
consider the equality
2^{x}-2^{y}+y-z=0
.
Assuming
\theta=(2^{x}\geq 2^{y}\geq 2^{z})
and
u=2^{x-y}
, we can rewrite
this equality as
(u-1)\cdot 2^{y}+y-z=0
.
Moreover, we see that
\theta
implies that
y-z
belongs to
[0\cdot 2^{y}..1\cdot 2^{y}]
.
Then,
Lemma

3
.
1

tells us that the equality can
be rewritten as
\bigvee_{r=0}^{1}((u-1)+r=0\land(y-z)=r\cdot 2^{y})
.
Here, the equation
(u-1)+r=0
is in a sense the “quotient” of the division by
2^{y}
,
whereas
y-z=r\cdot 2^{y}
indicates properties of the “remainder” of the division (in this case, that
y-z
has remainder zero when divided by
2^{y}
).

The effects of Step I of the main loop are formalized in the next lemma,
where the output formula
\gamma_{\beta}
contains
the “quotients” of the divisions by
2^{y}
, and
\psi_{\beta}
contains constraints on the “remainders”.

Lemma 4
[
CMS24
]
.

There is a non-deterministic procedure with the following specification:

Input:

\theta(\bm{x})

:

ordering of exponentiated variables;

[Below, let
2^{x}
and
2^{y}
be the largest and second-largest terms in this ordering,
and

let
\bm{y}
be the vector obtained by removing
x
from
\bm{x}
.]

\varphi(\bm{x},\bm{r})

:

linear-exponential program with divisions, implying
\bm{r}<2^{x}
.

Variables
\bm{r}
do not occur in exponentials.

Output of each branch (
\beta
):

\gamma_{\beta}(q_{x},\bm{q},u)

:

linear program with divisions;

\psi_{\beta}(\bm{y},r_{x},\bm{r}^{\prime})

:

linear-exponential program with divisions,
implying
r_{x}<2^{y}\land\bm{r}^{\prime}<2^{y}
.

Variables
r_{x}
and
\bm{r}^{\prime}
do not occur in exponentials.

The variables
q_{x}
,
\bm{q}
,
u
,
\bm{y}
,
r_{x}
and
\bm{r}^{\prime}

are common to all outputs, across all non-deterministic branches.
The procedure ensures that the system

\left[\begin{matrix}x\\
\bm{r}\end{matrix}\right]=\left[\begin{matrix}q_{x}\\
\bm{q}\end{matrix}\right]\cdot 2^{y}+\left[\begin{matrix}r_{x}\\
\bm{r}^{\prime}\end{matrix}\right],

(3)

yields a one-to-one correspondence between the solutions of

\varphi\land\theta
and the solutions of the formula

{\bigvee_{\beta}\big(\gamma_{\beta}\land\psi_{\beta}\land(u=2^{x-y})\land(x=q_{x}\cdot 2^{y}+r_{x})\land\theta\big)}
.
This correspondence is the identity for the variables these two formulae share (that is, the variables in
\bm{x}
).

The one-to-one correspondence
described in
Lemma

4
implies that
no solution is lost in this step of the procedure.
We can therefore use Step I also in the context of optimization.

Step II (variable elimination: the problematic step).

The procedure now considers the
linear
program with divisions
\gamma(q_{x},\bm{q},u)
in output of Step I,
and applies a quantifier elimination procedure to remove all the quotient variables in
\bm{q}
.
(Not
q_{x}
, this quotient variable cannot be eliminated yet, because the equalities
u=2^{x-y}
and
x=q_{x}\cdot 2^{y}+r_{x}
shown in
Lemma

4
make the variable
u
depend exponentially on
q_{x}
.)
This elimination step mixes ingredients from the quantifier elimination procedure for Presburger arithmetic
[
Wei90
]

with Bareiss’ version of Gaussian elimination
[
Bar68
]
.
As in the case of the former of these two procedures,
this step introduces new divisibility constraints.
Here is the specification of Step II:

Lemma 5
[
CMS24
]
.

There is a non-deterministic procedure with the following specification:

Input:

\gamma(q_{x},\bm{q},u)

:

linear program with divisions.

Output of each branch (
\beta
):

\gamma_{\beta}^{\prime}(q_{x},u)

:

linear program with divisions.

The procedure ensures that the formulae
\exists\bm{q}\,\gamma

and
\bigvee_{\beta}\gamma_{\beta}^{\prime}
are equivalent.
Let
\bm{q}=(q_{1},\dots,q_{k})
.
For every branch
\beta
, there is a system of equalities

\displaystyle a_{1}\cdot q_{1}=\tau_{1}(u,q_{x})\,,\,\dots\,,\,a_{k}\cdot q_{k}=\tau_{k}(u,q_{x})\,,

(4)

where each
a_{i}\in\mathbb{Z}
is non-zero
and each
\tau_{i}
is a linear term, with the following property.
The formula
\gamma_{\beta}^{\prime}
is obtained from
\gamma

by performing the sequence of substitutions
[\frac{\tau_{1}}{a_{1}}\,/\,q_{1}],\dots,[\frac{\tau_{k}}{a_{k}}\,/\,q_{k}]
and conjoining the system of divisibilities

(a_{1}\mathrel{|}\tau_{1}(u,q_{x}))\land\dots\land(a_{k}\mathrel{|}\tau_{k}(u,q_{x}))
.

Concerning optimization, the guarantees achieved by this crucial step of the procedure are too weak.
Rather than establishing a one-to-one correspondence between
the solutions of the input and those of the outputs, it only achieves an equivalence with respect to the variables
q_{x}
and
u
.
Notably, if some variables from
\bm{q}
appear in the objective function, then optimal solutions may be lost.

Step III (elimination of
x
and
u
).

The third step of the main loop is somewhat similar to the first one.
We start with the formula
\gamma^{\prime}(q_{x},u)
obtained from Step II,
add the constraints
x=q_{x}\cdot 2^{y}+r_{x}
and
u=2^{x-y}
,
and decouple
q_{x}
from all other variables.
By using machinery developed by Semenov in
[
Sem84
]
,
this decoupling makes it possible to eliminate the variables
x
and
u
.

Here is the specification of Step III:

Lemma 6
[
CMS24
]
.

There is a non-deterministic procedure with the following specification:

Input:

\gamma^{\prime}(q_{x},u)

:

linear program with divisions.

Output of each branch (
\beta
):

\gamma_{\beta}^{\prime\prime}(q_{x})

:

linear program with divisions;

\psi_{\beta}^{\prime\prime}(y,r_{x})

:

linear-exponential program with divisions.

The procedure ensures that the equation

x=q_{x}\cdot 2^{y}+r_{x}

(5)

yields a one-to-one correspondence between the solutions
of
\gamma^{\prime}\land(u=2^{x-y})\land(x=q_{x}\cdot 2^{y}+r_{x})

and the solutions
of
\bigvee_{\beta}\big(\gamma_{\beta}^{\prime\prime}\land\psi_{\beta}^{\prime\prime}\big)
.
This correspondence is the identity for the variables these two
formulae share (that is,
y
,
q_{x}
and
r_{x}
).

As in the case of Step I, the one-to-one correspondence described in
Lemma

6
ensures that optimal solutions are preserved
during this step of the main loop.

Step IV (elimination of
q_{x}
).

After Step III completes, we are left with its output formulae
\gamma^{\prime\prime}(q_{x})
and
\psi^{\prime\prime}(y,r_{x})
, and
the formula
\psi(\bm{y},r_{x},\bm{r}^{\prime})
computed in Step I.
The main loop of now performs one last operation: it checks whether
\gamma^{\prime\prime}
(a univariate linear program with divisions) is satisfiable. If it is,
\gamma^{\prime\prime}
can be replaced with
\top
, effectively eliminating the variable
q_{x}
. (Otherwise, the non-deterministic branch of the program rejects.)
An alternative way of implementing Step IV is to apply the variable elimination procedure underlying
Lemma

4
, but again this may cause the algorithm to lose all optimal solutions. In this particular case, since
\gamma^{\prime\prime}
only features the variable
q_{x}
,
the formula constructed by the variable elimination procedure simply replaces
q_{x}
with an integer
c
; i.e., the system of equalities analogous to the one in
Equation

4
simplifies in this case to just
q_{x}=c
.

This concludes the current iteration of the main loop of the procedure.
If
y
is not the variable
x_{0}
,
the loop performs another iteration. In that iteration, the input to Step I becomes the ordering
\theta^{\prime}
obtained from
\theta
by removing the term
2^{x}
(
2^{y}
is now the largest term), together with the linear-exponential program with divisions
{\psi(y,r_{x})\land\psi^{\prime\prime}(\bm{y},r_{x},\bm{r}^{\prime})}
.

2.1
Where are the ILESLPs?

As emphasized in
Lemmas

4
,
5
and
6
, the procedure in
[
CMS24
]
is in a sense guided by
Equations

3
,
4
and
5
.
Upon closer inspection, we see that these equations are constructing an ILESLP. Let us reason bottom-up and suppose that we have constructed an ILESLP
\sigma
that is a solution to the formula
\psi(y,r_{x})\land\psi^{\prime\prime}(\bm{y},r_{x},\bm{r}^{\prime})\land\theta^{\prime}
described above. We construct an ILESLP that is a solution for
\varphi\land\theta
by appending further assignments to
\sigma
.
The first three assignments are

z_{1}\leftarrow 2^{y},\ \ z_{2}\leftarrow c\cdot z_{1},\ \ x\leftarrow z_{2}+r_{x},

where
z_{1}
and
z_{2}
are auxiliary fresh variables, and
c
is
the integer in the equation
q_{x}=c
.
We are essentially performing the assignment
x\leftarrow c\cdot 2^{y}+r_{x}
,
accordingly to
Equation

5
.
Observe that
\sigma
already assigns expressions to
y
and
r_{x}
.
Next, we add assignments to represent each variable in
\bm{r}
.
For each variable
v
belonging to
\bm{r}
we have

\displaystyle v

\displaystyle=q_{v}\cdot 2^{y}+v^{\prime}

\displaystyle\Lbag\text{\lx@cref{creftypecap\lx@tilde refnum}{eq:CMS:first-step}, where $v^{\prime}$ is some variable in $\bm{r}^{\prime}$}\Rbag

\displaystyle=\frac{\tau(u,q_{x})}{a}\cdot 2^{y}+v^{\prime}

\displaystyle\Lbag\text{\lx@cref{creftypecap\lx@tilde refnum}{eq:CMS:second-step}}\Rbag

\displaystyle=\frac{b\cdot 2^{x-y}+d}{a}\cdot 2^{y}+v^{\prime}

\displaystyle\Lbag\text{using $u=2^{x-y}$ and $q_{x}=c$}\Rbag

\displaystyle=\frac{b\cdot 2^{x}+d\cdot 2^{y}}{a}+v^{\prime},

for some integers
a,b,d
, with
a\neq 0
.
We can easily add assignments to
\sigma
to obtain
{v\leftarrow\frac{b\cdot 2^{x}+d\cdot 2^{y}}{a}+v^{\prime}}
. The resulting ILESLP is guaranteed to be a solution to
\varphi\land\theta
.

2.2
OptILEP
: from feasibility to optimization

Following the above description of the procedure from
[
CMS24
]
,
it should be now clear that a way to obtain a procedure for the optimization
problem of ILEP is to focus on the “variable elimination step” (Step II),
strengthening it into a procedure that is guaranteed to explore an optimal solution.
In the remainder of the paper, we refer to the resulting procedure as
OptILEP
.
We will present the pseudocode of this procedure in
Section

6
(see
Algorithm

6
). For now, the specific details of the procedure are unimportant;
the only features to keep in mind are the following:

•

The procedure begins by guessing an ordering
\theta
of the form
2^{x_{n}}\geq\dots\geq 2^{x_{1}}\geq 2^{x_{0}}=1
, where
x_{1},\dots,x_{n}
are the variables appearing input instance of ILEP.

•

It then iteratively eliminates the variables
x_{n},\dots,x_{1}
(in this order), updating both the linear-exponential program and the objective function.
Every iteration of this “main loop” appeals to Step I and Step III of
[
CMS24
]
, interposed with an optimum-preserving “variable elimination step”. This elimination step instantiates the template given by
Algorithm

1
(
ElimVars
),
introduced in
Section

1.4
.
The main loop concludes with a step analogous to Step IV,
modified along the same lines as Step II to ensure preservation
of optimal solutions.

3
Exploring optimal solutions through monotone decompositions

As explained in the overview given in
Section

1.4
, the template for the “variable elimination step” given by
Algorithm

1
(
ElimVars
) eliminates some variables
\bm{x}
from a system of constraints
\varphi
(in which the variables
\bm{x}
occur linearly) and an objective function
f
, by iteratively

1.

Guessing an equation
{a\cdot x=\tau}
from a finite set of

test points

\textit{TP}(\bm{x},f,\varphi)
, where
a
is a non-zero integer, and
x
is a variable from
\bm{x}
that still occurs in
f
or
\varphi
.

2.

Appealing to an
elimination discipline

Elim
, which updates
f
and
\varphi

by “replacing
x
with
\frac{\tau}{a}
”.
(Intuitively, this means that we will only be searching for solutions
lying inside the hyperplane described by the equation
{a\cdot x=\tau}
.)

In this section, we describe a method, based on
monotone decompositions
of the search space, for constructing sets of test points that are guaranteed to preserve at least one optimal solution. We develop the approach in a general setting, where the objective function is treated as a black box.

Some notation.

Given
d\in\mathbb{N}
and
i\in[1..d]
, we write
\bm{e}_{i}^{d}
for the
i
-th vector of the canonical basis of
\mathbb{R}^{d}
(i.e.,
\bm{e}_{i}^{d}
has a
1
at the
i
-th component and
0
elsewhere);
omitting the superscript
d
when clear from the context.
A set
S\subseteq\mathbb{N}^{d}
is said to be
(i,p)
-periodic
if
for any
\bm{v}\in S
and
\bm{v}+m\cdot\bm{e}_{i}\in S
with
m\geq p
,
we also have
\bm{v}+p\cdot\bm{e}_{i}\in S
.
We write
\Delta_{i}^{p}[f]
for the
i
-th
p
-spaced partial finite difference
of a function
f\colon\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}
.
It is defined as
\Delta_{i}^{p}[f](\bm{x})\coloneqq f(\bm{x}+p\cdot\bm{e}_{i})-f(\bm{x})
, for every
\bm{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
.

A function
f\colon\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}
is said to be
(i,p)
-monotone locally to a set
S
if there is a sign
{{\sim}\in\{<,=,>\}}

such that for every
\bm{v}\in S
with
\bm{v}+p\cdot\bm{e}_{i}\in S
, we have
\Delta_{i}^{p}[f](\bm{v})\sim 0
.
When considering logical formulae,
we will abuse this notation and, in the above definitions, replace the indices of the entries of vectors by variable names.
For instances, we will say that the set of solution of a formula
\varphi(\bm{x})
is
(x,p)
-periodic, with
x
being a variable from
\bm{x}
,
and we will write
\Delta_{x}^{p}[f]
for the
p
-spaced partial finite difference of
f
with respect to (the coordinate corresponding to)
x
.

Locating optimal solutions for monotone functions.

We start by adapting a folklore result from Presburger arithmetic to ILEP: with respect to a linearly occurring variable, the set of solutions of an integer linear-exponential program is periodic. (See
[
Smo91
, Theorem 4.10]
for a similar result.)

Lemma 7.
†
†
margin:

Proof in page
C.1

Let
\varphi
be a linear-exponential program with divisions, and let
x
be a variable occurring linearly in
\varphi
.
The set of solutions of
\varphi
is
(x,\textit{mod}(x,\varphi))
-periodic.

The periodic behavior described in the above lemma implies that,
for monotone functions, optimal solutions are located near
the boundary of the feasible region described by the integer program.
This boundary is determined by equalities of
\tau=0

that are derived from
(in)equalities
{\tau\sim 0}
appearing in the program (where
{\sim}\in\{{=},{\leq}\}
). To ensure we find an optimal solution, it suffices to examine
shifted versions of these boundary equalities,
that is equalities of the form
{\tau+s=0}
,
where
s
ranges over a small set of integer offsets.

Lemma 8.
†
†
margin:

Proof in page
C.1

Let
\varphi(\bm{x})
be a linear-exponential program with divisions, and let
x
be a variable occurring linearly in
\varphi
.
Let
p\coloneqq\textit{mod}(x,\varphi)
,
and let
f(\bm{x})
be a
(x,p)
-monotone function locally to the set of solutions to
\varphi
.
If the instance
(f,\varphi)
has a maximum (analogously, a minimum),
then it has one satisfying an equation
a\cdot x+\tau+r=0
,
where
(a\cdot x+\tau)\in\textup{terms}(\varphi\land x\geq 0)
,
a\neq 0
,
and
r\in[0..\left|a\right|\cdot p-1]
.

By considering
f(x)=x
and considering the minimization problem,

Lemma

8
simplifies to the following corollary.
This corollary, in fact, captures the core argument found in nearly all proofs
of quantifier elimination in Presburger arithmetic (see, e.g.,
[
Wei90
, Lemma 2.6]
).

Corollary 2.
†
†
margin:

Proof in page
C.1

Let
\varphi
be a linear-exponential program with divisions, and let
x
be a variable occurring linearly in
\varphi
. If
\varphi
has a solution, then it has one satisfying an equation
a\cdot x+\tau+r=0
,
where
(a\cdot x+\tau)\in\textup{terms}(\varphi\land x\geq 0)
,
a\neq 0
,
and
r\in[0..\left|a\right|\cdot\textit{mod}(x,\varphi)-1]
.

Locating optimal solutions for non-monotone functions.

Lemma

8
suggests a natural strategy for tackling optimization problems involving non-monotone functions: partition the search space into multiple regions where the function becomes monotone.
This idea is formalized with the subsequent definition of
monotone decomposition
and
Lemma

9
.
It is important to note that, depending on the objective function, constructing such a decomposition with only regions that can be characterized with integer linear-exponential programs (or other desired classes of constraints) can be highly non-trivial or even impossible.
In the next section, we show how to achieve this only in the specific setting needed to solve the ILEP optimization problem.

Definition 1

(Monotone decomposition)
.

A
(i,p)
-monotone decomposition of
{S\subseteq\mathbb{N}^{d}}
for a function
f\colon\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}

is a finite family
R_{1},\dots,R_{t}\subseteq\mathbb{N}^{d}
such that
(i)
S=\bigcup_{j=1}^{t}R_{j}
and (ii) for every
j\in[1..t]
,

R_{j}
is
(i,p)
-periodic
and
f
is
(i,p)
-monotone locally to
R_{j}
.

Lemma 9.
†
†
margin:

Proof in page
C.1

Let
\varphi
be a linear-exponential program with divisions, and
x
be a variable occurring linearly in
\varphi
.
Suppose that the set of solutions to
\varphi
has a
(x,\textit{mod}(x,\varphi))
-monotone
decomposition
R_{1},\dots,R_{t}
for a function
f
,
where each
R_{i}
is the set of solutions of an integer linear-exponential program with divisions
\psi_{i}
in which
x
occurs linearly.
If the instance
(f,\varphi)
has a maximum (analogously, a minimum),
then it has one satisfying an equation

a\cdot x+\tau+r=0

such that
a\neq 0
,
(a\cdot x+\tau)\in\textup{terms}(\psi_{i}\land x\geq 0)

and

r\in[0..\left|a\right|\cdot\textit{mod}(x,\psi_{i})-1]
, for some
i\in[1..t]
.

Remark 2.

Consider
\varphi
,
x
, and
f
as in
Lemma

9
.
By defining
\textit{TP}(x,f,\varphi)
as the set of all equalities
a\cdot x+\tau+r=0

specified in that lemma, we are guaranteed to explore at least one optimal solution (if optimal solutions exists). Notably, when designing a non-deterministic polynomial-time procedure, neither the number of regions nor the number of constraints required to describe each region is inherently restrictive. The key requirement is instead the ability to guess
a single equation
involving the variable
x
targeted for elimination. This means that, while the total number of distinct constraints containing
x
can be at most exponential across regions, the number of constraints independent of
x
can be arbitrarily large (and these need not even be expressible as linear-exponential programs).

Remark 3.

All lemmas in this section were formulated for linear-exponential programs with divisions.
However, upon inspecting their proofs, it should be evident that the only crucial assumption is the
linear occurrence
of
x
.
Indeed, these lemmas could have been stated for any expansion of integer linear programming augmented with arbitrary functions, provided that “occurring linearly” is interpreted as “occurring only within the scope of addition”.

4
Monotone decompositions for ILEP

We now specialize the setting from the previous section, constructing monotone decompositions for the instances that
ElimVars
must be able to handle
in order for
OptILEP
to explore optimal solutions. In doing so, great attention must be paid to ensure that the formulae and ILESLPs computed throughout the procedure remain of polynomial size.
Proving that this is the case will occupy us through the end of
Section

6
.

To simplify the exposition, we work under the following assumptions:

1.

The linear-exponential program
\varphi
in input to
OptILEP
features the variables
x_{1},\dots,x_{n}
.

2.

The goal is to maximize a single variable
x_{m}
, with
m\in[1..n]
. (
Section

6
will relax this assumption to handle both maximization and minimization of general linear-exponential terms.)

3.

The ordering
\theta
guessed at the very beginning of
OptILEP
is
\theta\coloneqq(2^{x_{n}}\geq\dots\geq 2^{x_{1}}\geq 2^{x_{0}}=1)
.

As stated in
Section

2.2
,
OptILEP
iteratively eliminates
x_{n},\dots,x_{1}
.
Throughout the section, we assume that the variables
x_{n},\dots,x_{n-k+1}

have already been successfully eliminated while preserving optimal solutions,
for some
k\in[0..n-1]
.
We focus on the elimination of
x_{n-k}
,
which occurs during the
(k+1)
th iteration of the main loop of
OptILEP
.
More precisely,
we consider the appeal to
ElimVars
during
this
(k+1)
th iteration.
According to
Section

2.2
,
this appeal is preceded by an application of Step I
of the procedure from
[
CMS24
]
(
Lemma

4
),
and is followed by Step III of the same procedure (
Lemma

6
).
With respect to this
(k+1)
th iteration, we define:

4.

The vector
\bm{x}_{k}\coloneqq(x_{n-k},\dots,x_{n})
containing all variables that have been eliminated, plus
x_{n-k}
.

5.

The ordering
\theta_{k}\coloneqq{(2^{x_{n-k}}\geq\dots\geq 2^{x_{0}}=1)}
obtained from
\theta
by removing
the variables that have been eliminated. We also
define
\bm{y}_{k}\coloneqq(x_{0},\dots,x_{n-k})
for the variables in this ordering.
Note that
\bm{y}_{k}
and
\bm{x}_{k}
only share the variable
x_{n-k}
.

6.

The vectors
{\bm{q}_{k}\coloneqq(q_{n-k},\dots,q_{n})}
and
{\bm{r}_{k}\coloneqq(r_{n-k},\dots,r_{n})}
of the
quotient variables
and
remainder variables
introduced during the
(k+1)
th iteration the main loop of
OptILEP
(accordingly to
Lemma

4
).
We assume the variables
q_{1},\dots,q_{n},r_{1},\dots,r_{n}
to be reused at each iteration; e.g.,
\bm{q}_{k-1}=(q_{n-(k-1)},\dots,q_{n})
is the vector of quotient variables used at the
k
th iteration.
Given
\ell\in[0..k]
, we also write
\bm{q}_{[\ell,k]}
for the vector
(q_{n-k},\dots,q_{n-\ell})
.

4.1
Setup

We now introduce a class of circuits that model the evolution of the objective function during the execution of
OptILEP
, and characterize the class of objective functions and systems of constraints for which we design our monotone decomposition.

Linear-Exponential arithmetic circuits.

In
Section

2.1
we gave a bottom-up argument of how the procedure from
[
CMS24
]

constructs ILESLPs. An analogous top-down perspective shows the construction of ILESLPs by progressively
building
Linear-Exponential Arithmetic Circuits
(
LEACs
):

Definition 2

(LEAC)
.

Let
k\in[0..n-1]
and
\ell\in[0..k]
.
An
(k,\ell)
-linear-exponential arithmetic circuit
C
—a
(k,\ell)
-LEAC, in short— is a sequence of assignments
of the form

\displaystyle\hskip 11.38092ptq_{n-i}

\displaystyle\leftarrow\frac{\tau_{n-i}(u,\bm{q}_{[\ell,k]})}{\eta}

\displaystyle\text{for $i$ from $\ell-1$ to $0$},

\displaystyle\hskip 11.38092ptx_{n-i}

\displaystyle\leftarrow\frac{\textstyle\sum_{j={i+1}}^{k}a_{i,j}\cdot 2^{x_{n-j}}}{\mu}+q_{n-i}\cdot 2^{x_{n-k-1}}+r_{n-i}

\displaystyle\hskip-85.35826pt\text{for $i$ from $k$ to $0$},

where each
\tau_{n-i}
is a linear term (with integer coefficients),
every
a_{i,j}
is in
\mathbb{Z}
,
and the denominators
\eta
and
\mu
are positive integers.
We refer to these denominators as
\mu_{C}
and
\eta_{C}
, respectively.
When
k=0
, the sum
\textstyle\sum_{j={i+1}}^{k}a_{i,j}\cdot 2^{x_{n-j}}
equals
0
, and so we are free to update
\mu_{C}
to any positive integer;
we postulate
\mu_{C}\coloneqq 1
in this case.
If
\ell=0
, then
\eta_{C}
is undefined; for technical reasons we postulate
\eta_{C}\coloneqq\mu_{C}
in this case.
Moreover, we define
\xi_{C}\coloneqq\sum\{\left|a_{i,j}\right|:\text{$i\in[0..k]$, $j\in[i+1..k]$}\}
, and write
\textup{vars}(C)
for the set of
free variables

of
C
, i.e.,
u
,
x_{n-k-1}
, and those in the vectors
\bm{q}_{[\ell,k]}
and
\bm{r}_{k}
.

Objective functions.

Consider a
(k,\ell)
-LEAC
C
, and a variable
x_{m}
with
m\in[1..n]
. We denote by
{C}[{x_{m}}]
the (objective) function defined as follows: If
{n-k>m}
, then
{C}[{x_{m}}]
takes as input maps
\nu\colon X\to\mathbb{N}
where
X
is a set of variables featuring
x_{m}
, and
returns
\nu(x_{m})
. Else,
{C}[{x_{m}}]
takes as input maps
\nu\colon X\to\mathbb{N}
such that
\textup{vars}(C)\subseteq X
, and outputs
the number
{C}[{x_{m}}](\nu)
computed as follows:

1:

update
C
: replace each variable
z\in\textup{vars}(C)
with
\nu(z)

2:

evaluate
C

\triangleright

each assignment becomes
y\leftarrow a
where
a
is a number

3:

return
the number assigned to
x_{m}
in
C

The instances.

For our purposes, it suffices to define the monotone decomposition for elements of a set
\bigcup_{k=0}^{n-1}\bigcup_{\ell=0}^{k-1}\mathcal{I}_{k}^{\ell}
,
where
\mathcal{I}_{k}^{\ell}
(defined also for
\ell=k
) is the set of all pairs
(C,\langle{\gamma}\mathbin{;}{\psi}\rangle)
such that:

(i)

C=(y_{1}\leftarrow\rho_{1},\dots,y_{t}\leftarrow\rho_{t})
is a
(k,\ell)
-LEAC such that
\mu_{C}
divides
\eta_{C}
,
as well as all coefficients of the variables
\bm{q}_{[\ell,k]}
occurring in the term
\tau_{n-i}

featured in assignments
q_{n-i}\leftarrow\frac{\tau_{n-i}}{\eta_{C}}
of
C
, with
i\in[0..\ell-1]
.
(Recall that
\eta_{C}\coloneqq\mu_{C}
for
\ell=0
.)

(ii)

The formula
\langle{\gamma}\mathbin{;}{\psi}\rangle
is a
conjunction
of a linear program with divisions
\gamma(u,\bm{q}_{[\ell,k]})

and a linear-exponential program with divisions
\psi
. Inequalities and equalities in
\gamma
are such that all the coefficients of the variables
\bm{q}_{[\ell,k]}

are divisible by
\mu_{C}
. Moreover, for every
q
in
\bm{q}_{[\ell,k]}
,
\gamma
contains an inequality
a\cdot q\geq 0
, for some
a\geq 1
(divisible by
\mu_{C}
).
The system
\psi
is of the form
\chi(\bm{y}_{k-1},\bm{r}_{k})\land\theta_{k}\land(x_{n-k}=q_{n-k}\cdot 2^{x_{n-k-1}}+r_{n-k})\land(u=2^{x_{n-k}-x_{n-k-1}})
.

(We prefer writing
\langle{\gamma}\mathbin{;}{\psi}\rangle
instead of
\gamma\land\psi
as it emphasize more the distinction between
\gamma
and
\psi
.
ElimVars
only updates
\gamma
, treating
\psi
as an invariant used to ensure correctness.)

(iii)

The formula
\langle{\gamma}\mathbin{;}{\psi}\rangle
implies the formula
\Psi(C)
defined as

\displaystyle\bm{0}\leq\bm{r}_{k}<2^{x_{n-k-1}}\land\exists\bm{q}_{[0,\ell-1]}\Big(\bm{0}\leq\bm{q}_{k}\cdot 2^{x_{n-k-1}}+\bm{r}_{k}<2^{x_{n-k}}\land\exists\bm{x}_{k-1}\big(\theta\land\textstyle\bigwedge_{i=1}^{t}(y_{i}=\rho_{i})\big)\Big).

(For
\ell=0
, simply conjoin
\bm{0}\leq\bm{r}_{k}<2^{x_{n-k-1}}
with the formula in the scope of
\exists\bm{q}_{[0,\ell-1]}
.
Analogously, for
k=0
, the subformula
\exists\bm{x}_{k-1}\big(\theta\land\textstyle\bigwedge_{i=1}^{t}(y_{i}=\rho_{i})\big)
becomes
\theta\land\textstyle\bigwedge_{i=1}^{t}(y_{i}=\rho_{i})
.)

We remark that the variables that are quantified in
\Psi(C)
are those assigned to some expression in
C
, excluding
x_{n-k}
.
In essence, elements of
\mathcal{I}_{k}^{\ell}
satisfy certain basic properties that are sufficient to obtain a monotone decomposition. (While our proof relies on all of these properties, it remains unclear whether they are truly necessary for achieving a monotone decomposition.)
For example, the subformula
\exists\bm{x}_{k-1}\big(\theta\land\textstyle\bigwedge_{i=1}^{t}(y_{i}=\rho_{i})\big)

appearing in
\Psi(C)

ensures that from any solution of
\langle{\gamma}\mathbin{;}{\psi}\rangle
, we can assign values to the eliminated variables
x_{n},\dots,x_{n-k+1}

that preserve the ordering
\theta
, simply by following the assignments defined in the LEAC
C
.
This is consistent with the goal of
OptILEP
of finding a solution to the input integer linear-exponential program respecting
\theta
.

4.2
The monotone decomposition

We are now ready to formalize our monotone decomposition:

Proposition 3.

Consider
(C,\langle{\gamma}\mathbin{;}{\psi}\rangle)\in\mathcal{I}_{k}^{\ell}
,
with
\ell<k
,
and
p\coloneqq\textit{mod}(q_{n-\ell},\gamma)
.
The set of solutions to
{\langle{\gamma}\mathbin{;}{\psi}\rangle}

has a
(q_{n-\ell},p)
-monotone decomposition

R_{1},\dots,R_{t}
for the function
{C}[{x_{m}}]
.
Each
R_{i}
is the set of solutions of a linear-exponential program with divisions
\varphi_{i}

satisfying
{\textit{mod}(q_{n-\ell},\varphi_{i})=p}
,
and in which all constraints featuring
q_{n-\ell}

are either from
\gamma\land\gamma[q_{n-\ell}+p\,/\,q_{n-\ell}]
,
or they are inequalities
{\tau\leq 0}
,
where
\tau
is a term (non-deterministically) returned by
Algorithm

2
.

Algorithm 2

Additional terms for the monotone decomposition.

1:

\triangleright

see
Proposition

3
for the definitions of
C
,
\gamma
and
p

2:

(a,d)

\leftarrow

guess
an element from
[-L..L]^{2}
, where
L\coloneqq 3\cdot\mu_{C}\cdot(4\cdot\left\lceil\log_{2}(2\cdot\xi_{C}+\mu_{C})\right\rceil+8)

3:

\lambda\leftarrow\frac{\eta_{C}}{\mu_{C}}

4:

q^{\prime},q^{\prime\prime}

\leftarrow

guess
two elements in
\bm{q}_{k-1}
(they can be equal)

5:

\tau^{\prime}\leftarrow

if

C
assigns an expression
\frac{\tau}{\eta_{C}}
to
q^{\prime}

then

\tau

else

\eta_{C}\cdot q^{\prime}

6:

\tau^{\prime\prime}\leftarrow

if

C
assigns an expression
\frac{\tau}{\eta_{C}}
to
q^{\prime\prime}

then

\tau

else

\eta_{C}\cdot q^{\prime\prime}

7:

if

\ast

then

\tau^{\prime}\leftarrow\tau^{\prime}[q_{n-\ell}+p\,/\,q_{n-\ell}]

\triangleright

\ast
stands for non-deterministic choice

8:

if

\ast

then

\tau^{\prime\prime}\leftarrow\tau^{\prime\prime}[q_{n-\ell}+p\,/\,q_{n-\ell}]

9:

(b\cdot q_{n-\ell}-\rho)

\leftarrow

term obtained from
(a\cdot u+\mu_{C}\cdot(q^{\prime}-q^{\prime\prime})+d)
by simultaneously applying

10:

(b\cdot q_{n-\ell}-\rho)

\leftarrow
the substitutions
[\frac{\tau^{\prime}}{\lambda}\,/\,\mu_{C}\cdot q^{\prime}]
and
[\frac{\tau^{\prime\prime}}{\lambda}\,/\,\mu_{C}\cdot q^{\prime\prime}]

11:

assert
(
b\neq 0
)

\triangleright

else, reject this non-deterministic branch

12:

return

(b\cdot q_{n-\ell}-\rho)

The remainder of this section is dedicated to proving
Proposition

3
. At this stage, we are unable to motivate why the formulae
\varphi_{i}
given in
Proposition

3
suffice for obtaining a monotone decomposition; rather, these the formulae that naturally emerge when trying to build such a decomposition.

Preliminary results.

Before proceeding with the proof of
Proposition

3
, we need a few lemmas.
The first is a small technical result giving sufficient conditions under which an expression
of the form
2^{C}-2^{C/2}-d\cdot C
is non-negative (where
d,C\in\mathbb{R}
).
Ultimately, this lemma plays a role in the definition of the quantity
L

defined in line
2
of
Algorithm

2
.

Lemma 10.
†
†
margin:

Proof in page
C.2

Let
d,C\in\mathbb{R}
with
d\geq 1
and
C\geq 4\cdot\log_{2}(d)+8
. Then,

2^{C}-2^{C/2}-d\cdot C\geq 0
.

The next lemma echoes some ideas firstly used by Semenov for proving the decidability of Presburger arithmetic enriched with the exponential function
[
Sem84
]
. In a nutshell, it establishes that, under appropriate hypotheses, any inequality of the form
\sum_{i=1}^{\ell}a_{i}\cdot 2^{x_{i}}+\mu\cdot y+\mu\cdot d\leq 0
can be reduced to true (in the lemma,
0\leq 0
), false (
1\leq 0
), or a simplified inequality where
the sum
\sum_{i=1}^{\ell}a_{i}\cdot 2^{x_{i}}
is replaced with a single exponential term
a\cdot 2^{x_{1}}
. In our case, this lemma will play a central role in characterizing the regions of our monotone decomposition.

Lemma 11.

Let
E
be an expression
\sum_{i=1}^{\ell}a_{i}\cdot 2^{x_{i}}+\mu\cdot y+\mu\cdot d
, where each
a_{i}
is in
\mathbb{Z}
, and
\mu,d\in\mathbb{N}
.
Let
M,k\in\mathbb{N}
such that

M\geq\max(1+2\cdot\log_{2}(\sum_{i=1}^{\ell}\left|a_{i}\right|+k\cdot\mu),\ 4\cdot\log_{2}(\mu)+8,\ d)
.
Also consider a formula
\psi(x_{1},\dots,x_{\ell},y)
, with
y
ranging over
\mathbb{Z}
and
x_{1},\dots,x_{\ell}
ranging over
\mathbb{N}
, of the form

\psi(x_{1},\dots,x_{\ell},y)\ \coloneqq\ -k\cdot 2^{x_{1}}\leq y\leq k\cdot 2^{x_{1}}\land\bigwedge\nolimits_{i=1}^{\ell-1}(x_{i+1}\sim_{i}x_{i}+d_{i}),

where each pair
(\sim_{i},\,d_{i})
is either
(\geq,\,M)
or is of the form
(=,\,g)
, with
g\in[0..M-1]
.
Let
{{\sim}\in\{\leq,=\}}
.
There is an expression
E^{\prime}

from the set
{\{0,1\}\cup\{a\cdot 2^{x_{1}}+\mu\cdot y+\mu\cdot d\,:\,a\in[-4^{\ell\cdot M}..4^{\ell\cdot M}]\}}
such that the formula
\psi

implies
(E\sim 0\iff E^{\prime}\sim 0)
.

Proof.

First, observe that if
\ell=0
, then we can take
{E^{\prime}\coloneqq E}
.
Hence, below let us assume
\ell\geq 1
.
Note that
\psi
implies the ordering
x_{\ell}\geq x_{\ell-1}\geq\dots\geq x_{1}
.
By induction on
j
from
1
to
\ell
,
we show that for every expression

E_{j}
of the form
{h_{j}\cdot 2^{x_{j}}+\sum_{i=1}^{j-1}a_{i}\cdot 2^{x_{i}}+\mu\cdot y+\mu\cdot d}
,
with
\left|h_{j}\right|\leq 4^{(\ell-j+1)M}
,
there is an expression
E_{j}^{\prime}

such that
\psi
implies
{(E_{j}\sim 0\iff E_{j}^{\prime}\sim 0)}
,
and
E_{j}^{\prime}
belongs to the set

\{0,1\}\cup\{a\cdot 2^{x_{1}}+\mu\cdot y+\mu\cdot d\,:\,a\in[-\left|h_{j}\right|\cdot 4^{(j-1)M}..\left|h_{j}\right|\cdot 4^{(j-1)M}]\}
.
The lemma then follows from the fact that

E
is an expression of the form of
E_{m}
,
setting
a_{m}=h_{m}
;
as indeed
\left|a_{m}\right|\leq 4^{M}
.

base case:
j=1
.

For every expression
E_{1}
of the form
h_{1}\cdot 2^{x_{1}}+\mu\cdot y+\mu\cdot d
, we can take
E_{1}^{\prime}\coloneqq E_{1}
.

induction hypothesis.

Given
j>1
, from every
E_{j-1}=h_{j-1}\cdot 2^{x_{j-1}}+{\sum_{i=1}^{j-2}a_{i}\cdot 2^{x_{i}}+\mu\cdot y+\mu\cdot d}
,
with
\left|h_{j-1}\right|\leq 4^{(\ell-j+2)M}
,
there is an expression
E_{j-1}^{\prime}
from the set

\{0,1\}\cup\{a\cdot 2^{x_{1}}+\mu\cdot y+\mu\cdot d\,:\,a\in[-\left|h_{j-1}\right|\cdot 4^{(j-1)M}..\left|h_{j-1}\right|\cdot 4^{(j-1)M}]\}
,
such that
\psi
implies
(E_{j-1}\sim 0\iff E_{j-1}^{\prime}\sim 0)
.

induction step:
j>1
.

Consider an expression
E_{j}
of the form
h_{j}\cdot 2^{x_{j}}+\sum_{i=1}^{j-1}a_{i}\cdot 2^{x_{i}}+\mu\cdot y+\mu\cdot d
, with
\left|h_{j}\right|\leq 4^{(\ell-j+1)M}
.
If
h_{j}=0
, then we directly obtain
E_{j}^{\prime}
by
applying the induction hypothesis on the expression
\sum_{i=1}^{j-1}a_{i}\cdot 2^{x_{i}}+\mu\cdot y+\mu\cdot d
,
since from the assumption on
M
in
the statement of the lemma, we have
\left|a_{j-1}\right|\leq 4^{M}
.
Below, let us assume then that
h_{j}\neq 0
. We distinguish two cases, depending on whether the constraint
x_{j}\sim_{j-1}x_{j-1}+d_{i}

occurring in
\psi

is
of the form
x_{j}\geq x_{j-1}+M
or
x_{j}=x_{j-1}+g

for some
g\in[0..M-1]
.

case:
x_{j}\geq x_{j-1}+M

occurs in

\psi
.

Intuitively, in this case
\psi
is constraining
x_{j}
to be so large comparatively to
x_{j-1}
that, in any solution to
\psi
,
E_{j}\neq 0
and the sign of
E_{j}

is solely dictated by the sign of
h_{j}
.
When
\sim
from
E\sim 0

is the equality symbol, we can pick
E_{j}^{\prime}=1
, making
E_{j}^{\prime}\sim 0
unsatisfiable.
When
\sim
is instead
\leq
, we set
E_{j}^{\prime}=0
if
h_{j}
is negative,
and
E_{j}^{\prime}=1
otherwise.
To show that
h_{j}
dictates the sign of
E_{j}
,
it suffices to establish that
\psi
implies

2^{x_{j}}>\left|\sum_{i=1}^{j-1}a_{i}\cdot 2^{x_{i}}+\mu\cdot y+\mu\cdot d\right|
.
First, note that
\psi
implies
2^{x_{j}}\geq 2^{M}\cdot 2^{x_{j-1}}
.
As
M\geq d
and
\psi
implies
both
x_{j-1}\geq\dots\geq x_{1}
and
\left|y\right|\leq k\cdot 2^{x_{1}}
,
we have

\displaystyle\left|\,\sum\nolimits_{i=1}^{j-1}a_{i}\cdot 2^{x_{i}}+\mu\cdot y+\mu\cdot d\,\right|

\displaystyle\,\leq\,\sum\nolimits_{i=1}^{j-1}\left|a_{i}\right|2^{x_{i}}+\left|\mu\cdot y\right|+\mu\cdot d

\displaystyle\,\leq\,\left(\sum\nolimits_{i=1}^{j-1}\left|a_{i}\right|+k\cdot\mu+M\cdot\mu\right)\cdot 2^{x_{j-1}}.

Therefore, it suffices to show that
2^{M}>\sum_{i=1}^{j-1}\left|a_{i}\right|+k\cdot\mu+M\cdot\mu
;
or equivalently that
2^{M}-M\cdot\mu>\sum_{i=1}^{j-1}\left|a_{i}\right|+k\cdot\mu
.
Since
M\geq 4\log_{2}(\mu)+8
,
by
Lemma

10

we have
2^{M}-M\cdot\mu\geq 2^{M/2}
. Then,
by
M>2\cdot\log_{2}(\sum_{i=1}^{\ell}\left|a_{i}\right|+k\cdot\mu)
,

2^{M}-\mu\cdot M\geq 2^{M/2}>2^{\lceil\log_{2}(\sum\left|a_{i}\right|+k\cdot\mu)\rceil}\geq\sum\nolimits_{i=1}^{j-1}\left|a_{i}\right|+k\cdot\mu.

case:
x_{j}=x_{j-1}+g

occurs in

\psi
.

Let
E_{j-1}
be the expression obtained from
E_{j}

by replacing
2^{x_{j}}
by
2^{g}\cdot 2^{x_{j-1}}
;
that is,
E_{j-1}=(h_{j}\cdot 2^{g}+a_{j-1})\cdot 2^{x_{j-1}}+\sum_{i=1}^{j-2}a_{i}\cdot 2^{x_{i}}+\mu\cdot y+\mu\cdot d
.
We have
\psi
implies
(E_{j}\sim 0\iff E_{j-1}\sim 0)
.
To conclude the proof,
it suffices to prove that the induction hypothesis can be applied to
E_{j-1}
.
This is the case as soon as
\left|h_{j}\cdot 2^{g}+a_{j-1}\right|\leq 4^{(\ell-j+2)M}
holds, which we show below:

\displaystyle\left|h_{j}\cdot 2^{g}+a_{j-1}\right|

\displaystyle\leq\left|h_{j}\right|\cdot 2^{g}+\left|a_{j-1}\right|

\displaystyle\leq 4^{(\ell-j+1)M}\cdot 2^{M}+2^{M}

\displaystyle\Lbag\text{bounds on $\left|h_{j}\right|$, $g$ and $M$}\Rbag

\displaystyle\leq 2^{2(\ell-j+1)M+2M}

\displaystyle\Lbag\text{recall: $M\geq 8$}\Rbag

\displaystyle\leq 4^{(\ell-j+2)M}.

The set of possible expressions
E^{\prime}
appearing in the conclusion of
Lemma

11
can be significantly reduced by noticing
that if the absolute value of the integer
a
exceeds
\mu\cdot(k+d)
, then the truth of
E^{\prime}\sim 0
becomes independent of the value given to
x_{1}
.
This observation allows us to eliminate the polynomial dependence of
a

on the magnitude of the coefficients
a_{1},\dots,a_{\ell}
in the original expression
E
.
Although not obvious, it turns out that this plays a critical point in the proof of
Theorem

1
. Retaining values of
a
with polynomial dependence on
a_{1},\dots,a_{\ell}
would cause the integers in the LEAC constructed by
OptILEP
to grow polynomially within each variable elimination step. As a result, their bit sizes would become exponential by the end of the procedure.
(In the proof of
Proposition

3
, we will apply
Lemma

11
using a value for the integer
d
that depends only logarithmically on
a_{1},\dots,a_{\ell}
; hence, the resulting values of
a
do in fact retain a logarithmic dependence on these coefficients.)
The next lemma gives the refined set of expressions
E^{\prime}
.

Lemma 12.

Let the expression
E
, the non-negative integers
\mu
,
d
,
M
and
k
, the formula
\psi
, and the symbol
\sim
be defined as in
Lemma

11
.
Let
b\coloneqq\mu\cdot(k+d)
.
There is an expression
E^{\prime}

from the set
\{0,1\}\cup\{a\cdot 2^{x_{1}}+\mu\cdot y+\mu\cdot d\,:\,a\in[-b..b]\}
such that the formula
\psi

implies
(E\sim 0\iff E^{\prime}\sim 0)
.

Proof.

Following

Lemma

11
,
it suffices to take
E^{\prime}
to be an expression of the form
a\cdot 2^{x_{1}}+\mu\cdot y+\mu\cdot d
, where
a\in[-4^{\ell\cdot M}..4^{\ell\cdot M}]
,
and show that if
a
lies outside
[-b..b]
,
then
E^{\prime}
can be rewritten to
0
or
1
.

From its definition,
\psi
implies

\left|\mu\cdot y+\mu\cdot d\right|\leq\mu\cdot(k+d)\cdot 2^{x_{1}}
.
Hence, as soon as
\left|a\right|>b
, the truth of
E^{\prime}\sim 0

is determined by the sign of
a
.
In particular, whenever
\sim
is the symbol
=
, or when
a>0
,
the expression
E^{\prime}
can be replaced with
1
; that is, in this case
\psi
implies
\lnot(E^{\prime}\sim 0)
.
Otherwise,
E^{\prime}
can be replaced with
0
;
and in this case
\psi
implies
E^{\prime}\sim 0
.
∎

Proof of
Proposition

3
.

We are now ready to prove
Proposition

3
.
While long, the proof is divided in several steps and claims.
An intuition of the construction is given after defining various objects
required for the proof; see the paragraph titled “Construction of
\psi_{1},\dots,\psi_{s}
: some intuition”.

Proof.

Throughout the proof, we let
\varphi\coloneqq\langle{\gamma}\mathbin{;}{\psi}\rangle
.
Let
X
be the set of all variables appearing in
\bm{q}_{[\ell,k]}
,
\bm{r}_{k}
,
x_{0},\dots,x_{n-k}
and
u
.
Remark that all variables occurring in
\varphi
are among the set
X
.
The value
{C}[{x_{m}}](\nu)
of objective function
{C}[{x_{m}}]
is defined for every map
\nu\colon X\to\mathbb{N}
, and corresponds to the value taken by
x_{m}
when evaluating
C
on
\nu
.
During the proof, we refer to the variable
q_{n-\ell}
simply as
q
.
By the definition of
\mathcal{I}_{k}^{\ell}
, the variable
q
appears linearly
in
\gamma
and does not appear in
\psi
. Thus, by
Lemma

7

the set of solutions to
\varphi
is
(q,p)
-periodic,
where
p=\textit{mod}(q,\gamma)=\textit{mod}(q,\varphi)
.

Let us begin by considering the (corner) case where
x_{m}
satisfies
n-k\geq m
.
When
n-k>m
, the variable
x_{m}
does not occur in
C
. Consequently, the function
{C}[{x_{m}}]
is constant in the variable
q
(that is,
for any solution
\nu\colon X\to\mathbb{N}

to
\varphi
, the function
{C}[{x_{m}}]
is constant on maps
of the form
\nu+[q\mapsto j]
for
j\in\mathbb{Z}
).
Similarly, when
m=n-k
, the circuit
C
assigns to the variable
x_{m}
the expression
q_{n-k}\cdot 2^{x_{n-k-1}}+r_{n-k}
. As all involved variables (
q_{n-k}
,
x_{n-k-1}
and
r_{n-k}
) belong to
X
and are distinct from
q
, once again we obtain that the function
{C}[{x_{m}}]
is constant in the variable
q
.
We conclude that if
m\leq n-k
,
then
{C}[{x_{m}}]
is
(q,p)
-monotone locally to
\varphi
.
By
Lemma

8
,
for a monotone decomposition it thus suffices to take a single set
R_{1}
given by the set of solutions to
\varphi
.

In the remaining of the proof,
we assume that
x_{m}
satisfies
m>n-k
.
This means that
C
contains an assignment to
x_{m}
,
and that
x_{m}
is not
x_{n-k}
.
We will construct a sequence of linear-exponential programs
\psi_{1},\dots,\psi_{s}

satisfying the following conditions:

I.

The formula
\varphi\land\varphi[q+p\,/\,q]
implies
\psi_{1}\lor\dots\lor\psi_{s}
.

II.

{C}[{x_{m}}]
is
(q,p)
-monotone locally to (the solutions of)
\varphi\land\varphi[q+p\,/\,q]\land\psi_{i}
, for every
i\in[1..s]
.

III.

Each
\psi_{i}
is of the form
\bigwedge_{j=1}^{n_{i}}\psi_{i,j}
, where every
\psi_{i,j}

is an inequality with the following property.
Let
\psi_{i,j}
be
\tau\leq 0
(and note that then
-\tau-1\leq 0
is equivalent to
\lnot\psi_{i,j}
).
If
q
occurs in
\psi_{i,j}
,
both
\tau
and
-\tau-1
are terms
returned by a non-deterministic branch of
the execution of
Algorithm

2
with respect to
C
,
\gamma
and
p
. (This is as required in the statement of the proposition; remark also that in
\varphi\land\varphi[q+p\,/\,q]
, all constraints featuring
q
are from
\gamma\land\gamma[q+p\,/\,q]
.)

Then, the desired
(q,p)
-monotone decomposition
R_{1},\dots,R_{t}

is given by the formulae in the set

\Big\{\varphi\land\varphi[q+p\,/\,q]\land\psi_{i}:i\in[1..s]\Big\}\cup\Big\{\varphi\land\bigwedge\nolimits_{i=1}^{s}\lnot\psi_{i,f(i)}:f\in\mathcal{G}\Big\},

where
\mathcal{G}
is the set of all function
f\colon[1..s]\to\mathbb{N}
such that
f(i)\in[1..n_{i}]
for every
i\in[1..s]
.
Indeed, the function
{C}[{x_{m}}]
is
(q,p)
-monotone locally to the formula
\varphi\land\lnot\varphi[q+p\,/\,q]
,
and so also locally to any formula
\varphi\land\bigwedge_{i=1}^{s}\lnot\psi_{i,f(i)}
; as the latter implies the former by Item
I.
.
Additionally, the sets of solutions of all the formulae
\varphi\land\varphi[q+p\,/\,q]\land\psi_{i}
and
\varphi\land\bigwedge_{i=1}^{s}\lnot\psi_{i,f(i)}
are
(q,p)
-periodic, since
q
occurs linearly in
these formulae, and none of the
\psi_{i}
contains any divisibility constraints.
(A side remark: the number of formulae
\psi_{1},\dots,\psi_{s}
will be exponential in the size of
\varphi
, making the number of constraints in each formula
\bigwedge_{i=1}^{s}\lnot\psi_{i,f(i)}
also exponential. As explained in
Remark

2
,
this is unproblematic: to eliminate
q
, it suffices to guess a
single
constraint from any of these formulae.)

Construction of
\psi_{1},\dots,\psi_{s}
: a preliminary step.

We begin by introducing an
\ell
-LEAC
C^{+p}
that will be associated to the formula
\varphi[q+p\,/\,q]
. We define
C^{+p}
as the
\ell
-LEAC obtained from
C
by replacing
q
with
q+p
, and renaming to
\overline{v}
every variable
v
among
q_{n-\ell+1},\dots,q_{n},x_{n-k+1},\dots,x_{n}
. These are the variables to which
C
assigns an expression, and whose value may depend on the value given to
q
. To clarify, if
C
is defined as

\displaystyle q_{n-i}\leftarrow\frac{\tau_{n-i}}{\eta}

\displaystyle\text{for $i$ from $\ell-1$ to $0$},

\displaystyle x_{n-i}\leftarrow\frac{\textstyle\sum_{j={i+1}}^{k}a_{i,j}\cdot 2^{x_{n-j}}}{\mu}+q_{n-i}\cdot 2^{x_{n-k-1}}+r_{n-i}

\displaystyle\text{for $i$ from $k$ to $0$},

then
C^{+p}
is defined as

\displaystyle\overline{q}_{n-i}\leftarrow\frac{\tau_{n-i}[q+p\,/\,q]}{\eta}

\displaystyle\text{for $i$ from $\ell-1$ to $0$},

\displaystyle x_{n-k}\leftarrow q_{n-k}\cdot 2^{x_{n-k-1}}+r_{n-k},

\displaystyle\overline{x}_{n-i}\leftarrow\frac{\textstyle a_{i,k}\cdot 2^{x_{n-k}}+\sum_{j={i+1}}^{k-1}a_{i,j}\cdot 2^{\overline{x}_{n-j}}}{\mu}+q_{n-i}\cdot 2^{x_{n-k-1}}+r_{n-i}

for
i
from
k-1
to
\ell+1

\displaystyle\overline{x}_{n-\ell}\leftarrow\frac{\textstyle a_{\ell,k}\cdot 2^{x_{n-k}}+\sum_{j={i+1}}^{k-1}a_{\ell,j}\cdot 2^{\overline{x}_{n-j}}}{\mu}+(q_{n-\ell}+p)\cdot 2^{x_{n-k-1}}+r_{n-i}

\displaystyle\overline{x}_{n-i}\leftarrow\frac{\textstyle a_{i,k}\cdot 2^{x_{n-k}}+\sum_{j={i+1}}^{k}a_{i,j}\cdot 2^{\overline{x}_{n-j}}}{\mu}+\overline{q}_{n-i}\cdot 2^{x_{n-k-1}}+r_{n-i}

\displaystyle\text{for $i$ from $\ell-1$ to $0$}.

To simplify the presentation, we introduce
the symbolic aliases:

\displaystyle(z_{1},\dots,z_{2k+1})

\displaystyle\coloneqq(x_{n},x_{n-1},\dots,x_{n-k+1},\overline{x}_{n},\overline{x}_{n-1},\dots,\overline{x}_{n-k+1},x_{n-k}),

\displaystyle(y_{1},\dots,y_{2k+1})

\displaystyle\coloneqq(q_{n},q_{n-1},\dots,q_{n-k+1},\overline{q}_{n},\overline{q}_{n-1},\dots,\overline{q}_{n-\ell+1},(q_{n-\ell}+p),q_{n-\ell-1}\dots,q_{n-k}),

\displaystyle(s_{1},\dots,s_{2k+1})

\displaystyle\coloneqq(r_{n},r_{n-1},\dots,r_{n-k+1},r_{n},r_{n-1},\dots,r_{n-k+1},r_{n-k}).

For every
j\in[1..k]
(resp.
j\in[k+1..2k]
), the variables
y_{j}
and
s_{j}
represent the quotient and remainder variables occurring in the expression assigned to
z_{j}
in
C
(resp.
C^{+p}
).
Note that both
C
and
C^{+p}
include the assignment
x_{n-k}\leftarrow q_{n-k}\cdot 2^{x_{n-k-1}}+r_{n-k}
, which, using of our aliases, is expressed as
z_{2k+1}\leftarrow y_{2k+1}\cdot 2^{x_{n-k-1}}+s_{2k+1}
.
Additionally, we introduce symbolic aliases for all variables to which
C
or
C^{+p}
assign an expression:

(w_{1},\dots,w_{2(k+\ell)+1})\coloneqq(\underbrace{z_{1},\dots,z_{k},y_{1},\dots,y_{\ell}}_{\text{assigned in $C$}},\underbrace{z_{k+1},\dots,z_{2k},y_{k+1},\dots,y_{k+\ell}}_{\text{assigned in $C^{+p}$}},\underbrace{z_{2k+1}}_{\hskip-5.0pt\text{a.k.a.}~x_{n-k}\hskip-5.0pt}).

For
j\in[1..2(k+\ell)+1]
, let
\rho_{j}
denote the expression assigned to the variable
w_{j}

in either
C
or
C^{+p}
. Since
x_{n-k}
is the only variable shared between the two circuits, the definition of
\rho_{j}
is unambiguous. In particular,
\rho_{2(k+\ell)+1}
corresponds to the expression
q_{n-k}\cdot 2^{x_{n-k-1}}+r_{n-k}
.

Recall that
\varphi
implies the formula
\Psi(C)
defined as

\displaystyle\Psi(C)\coloneqq\bm{0}\leq\bm{r}_{k}<2^{x_{n-k-1}}\land\exists\bm{q}_{[0,\ell-1]}:\Big(

\displaystyle\bm{0}\leq\bm{q}_{k}\cdot 2^{x_{n-k-1}}+\bm{r}_{k}<2^{x_{n-k}}\land{}

\displaystyle\hskip-28.45274pt\exists x_{n-k+1}\dots\exists x_{n}\,\,\big(\theta\land\textstyle\bigwedge_{i=1}^{k+\ell}(w_{i}=\rho_{i})\land(w_{2(k+\ell)+1}=\rho_{2(k+\ell)+1})\big)\!\Big).

Similarly, it is simple to see that
\varphi[q+p\,/\,q]

implies the formula
\Psi(C^{+p})
defined as

\displaystyle\Psi(C^{+p})\coloneqq\bm{0}\leq\bm{r}_{k}<2^{x_{n-k-1}}\land\exists\overline{\bm{q}}_{[0,\ell-1]}:\Big(

\displaystyle\bigwedge\nolimits_{j=0}^{\ell-1}\big(0\leq\overline{q}_{n-j}\cdot 2^{x_{n-k-1}}+r_{n-j}<2^{x_{n-k}}\big)\land{}

\displaystyle\big(0\leq q\cdot 2^{x_{n-k-1}}+p\cdot 2^{x_{n-k-1}}+r_{n-\ell}<2^{n-k}\big)\land{}

\displaystyle\bigwedge\nolimits_{j=\ell+1}^{k}\big(0\leq q_{n-j}\cdot 2^{x_{n-k-1}}+r_{n-j}<2^{x_{n-k}}\big)\land{}

\displaystyle\exists\,\overline{x}_{n-k+1}\dots\exists\,\overline{x}_{n}\,\,\big(\overline{\theta}\land\textstyle\bigwedge_{i=k+\ell+1}^{2(k+\ell)+1}(w_{i}=\rho_{i})\big)\!\Big),

where
\overline{\theta}\coloneqq 2^{\overline{x}_{n}}\geq\dots\geq 2^{\overline{x}_{n-k+1}}\geq 2^{x_{n-k}}\geq\dots\geq 2^{x_{0}}=1
.
To show that
\varphi[q+p\,/\,q]
implies
\Psi(C^{+p})
,
observe that
{(\varphi[q+p\,/\,q]\implies\Psi(C^{+p}))}
is syntactically equal
to
{(\varphi\implies\Psi(C))[q+p\,/\,q]}
, except for the names used for the existentially quantified variable. Specifically, every such variable
v
in
\Psi(C)
is replaced with
\overline{v}
in
\Psi(C^{+p})
. Since
{(\varphi\implies\Psi(C))}
is a valid formula, and validity is preserved under substitution and variable renaming, it follows that
{(\varphi[q+p\,/\,q]\implies\Psi(C^{+p}))}

is also valid.

The following claim establishing further properties of
{C^{+p}}[{\overline{x}_{m}}]

follows directly from the fact that
C^{+p}
is obtained from
C
by replacing with
q+p
all occurrences of the variable
q
.

Claim 1.

Let
\nu
and
\nu+[q\mapsto p]
be two solutions to
\varphi
.
Then,
{C}[{x_{m}}](\nu+[q\mapsto p])={C^{+p}}[{\overline{x}_{m}}](\nu)
.

Construction of
\psi_{1},\dots,\psi_{s}
: mixing
x
s and
\overline{x}s
into a single ordering.

Before giving some more intuition on the construction of
\psi_{1},\dots,\psi_{s}
, we need some additional formulae manipulations.
Let
\bm{w}
denote the vector of all variables that appear quantified in the formulae
\Psi(C)
or
\Psi(C^{+p})
.
Specifically, these are the variables
w_{1},\dots,w_{2(k+\ell)}
(note that
w_{2(k+\ell)+1}=x_{n-k}
is a free variable in both formulae).
Observe that, by the definition of
\Psi(C)
and
\Psi(C^{+p})
,
the linear-exponential program
\varphi\land\varphi[q+p\,/\,q]

implies

\exists\bm{w}:(x_{n}\geq x_{n-1}\geq\cdots\geq x_{n-k})\land(\overline{x}_{n}\geq\overline{x}_{n-1}\geq\cdots\geq\overline{x}_{n-k+1}\geq x_{n-k})\land\bigwedge\nolimits_{i=1}^{2(k+\ell)+1}(w_{i}=\rho_{i}).

(6)

Let us denote with
\mathcal{P}
the set of all permutations
\sigma\colon[1..2k+1]\to[1..2k+1]

(on the indices of the variables
z_{1},\dots,z_{2k+1}
)
that satisfy:

•

\sigma^{-1}(1)\leq\sigma^{-1}(2)\leq\dots\leq\sigma^{-1}(k)
, i.e.,
\sigma
respects the ordering
x_{n}\geq\cdots\geq x_{n-k+1}
.

•

\sigma^{-1}(k+1)\leq\sigma^{-1}(k+2)\leq\dots\leq\sigma^{-1}(2k)
, i.e.,
\sigma
respects the ordering
\overline{x}_{n}\geq\cdots\geq\overline{x}_{n-k+1}
.

•

\sigma(2k+1)=2k+1
, i.e., the variable
x_{n-k}
is smaller or equal than any other variable.

The formula in
Equation

6
is equivalent to
\bigvee_{\sigma\in\mathcal{P}}\chi_{\sigma}
, where
\chi_{\sigma}
is defined as

\chi_{\sigma}\coloneqq\exists\bm{w}:(z_{\sigma(1)}\geq z_{\sigma(2)}\geq\cdots\geq z_{\sigma(2k)}\geq z_{\sigma(2k+1)})\land\bigwedge\nolimits_{i=1}^{2(k+\ell)+1}(w_{i}=\rho_{i}).

(7)

Construction of
\psi_{1},\dots,\psi_{s}
: some intuition.

We are now ready to provide the promised intuition behind the construction of
\psi_{1},\dots,\psi_{s}
.
Since
\chi_{\sigma}
includes the equations
{\bigwedge_{i=1}^{2(k+\ell)+1}(w_{i}=\rho_{i})}
, which describe the assignments in the circuits
C
and
C^{+p}
, for every solution to
{\varphi\land\varphi[q+p\,/\,p]}
there is one and only one assignment to the variables in
\bm{w}

that yields a solution to the
quantifier-free part of the formula in
Equation

7
. This assignment is determined by the values computed by the circuits
C
and
C^{+p}
.
The order that
\sigma
induces on the variables
z_{1},\dots,z_{2k+1}

implies either
x_{m}\geq\overline{x}_{m}
or
\overline{x}_{m}\geq x_{m}
.
Therefore, by relying on
1
,
one concludes that
the function
{C}[{x_{m}}]
is
(q,p)
-monotone locally to

\varphi\land\varphi[q+p\,/\,q]\land\chi_{\sigma}
:

Claim 2.
†
†
margin:

Proof in page
C.2

For every
\sigma\in\mathcal{P}
,
the function
{C}[{x_{m}}]
is
(q,p)
-monotone locally to

\varphi\land\varphi[q+p\,/\,q]\land\chi_{\sigma}
.

Clearly, we cannot use the formulae
\chi_{\sigma}
as the desired formulae
\psi_{1},\dots,\psi_{s}
,
as these formulae are quantified over variables not occurring in
\varphi
(we will thus go against
our goal of achieving variable elimination).
Instead, we will further refine and manipulate the ordering in
Equation

7
and, by appealing to
Lemma

11
,
restate it solely in terms of variables that occur (free) in
\varphi
.
This will allow us to push the ordering outside the scope of the quantifiers
\exists\bm{w}
, leading to formulae of the form
\psi\land\exists\bm{w}\bigwedge\nolimits_{i=1}^{2(k+\ell)+1}(w_{i}=\rho_{i})
.
Next, we will show that the function
{C}[{x_{m}}]
is
(q,p)
-monotone locally to
\varphi\land\varphi[q+p\,/\,q]\land\psi\land\exists\bm{w}\bigwedge\nolimits_{i=1}^{2(k+\ell)+1}(w_{i}=\rho_{i})
.
Since
\exists\bm{w}\bigwedge\nolimits_{i=1}^{2(k+\ell)+1}(w_{i}=\rho_{i})
is implied by
\varphi\land\varphi[q+p\,/\,q]
, this ensures that
{C}[{x_{m}}]
is
(q,p)
-monotone locally to
\varphi\land\varphi[q+p\,/\,q]\land\psi
,
as required by Item
II.
.
Moreover, because of how these formulae are constructed, the disjunction over all such formulae
\psi
will still be implied by
{\varphi\land\varphi[q+p\,/\,q]}
, fulfilling Item
I.
.
Finally, we will manipulate
\psi
to meet the structural requirements of Item
III.
.

Construction of
\psi_{1},\dots,\psi_{s}
.

We start by further refining the orderings induced by the permutations in
\mathcal{P}

by quantifying the gaps between variables.
Let
\mathcal{D}
be the set of all functions
{d\colon[1..2k]\to[0..M]}
,
where
M\coloneqq 4\cdot\left\lceil\log_{2}(2\cdot\xi_{C}+\mu_{C})\right\rceil+8
.
For
g\in[0..M-1]
, we write
\sim_{g}
as an alias for
=
,
and
\sim_{M}
as an alias for
\geq
.
For
\sigma\in\mathcal{P}
, the formula
\chi_{\sigma}

in
Equation

7
is equivalent to the formula
\bigvee_{d\in\mathcal{D}}\chi_{\sigma,d}
where

\chi_{\sigma,d}\coloneqq\exists\bm{w}:\bigwedge\nolimits_{j=1}^{2k}\big(z_{\sigma(j)}\sim_{d(j)}z_{\sigma(j+1)}+d(j)\big)\land\bigwedge\nolimits_{i=1}^{2(k+\ell)+1}(w_{i}=\rho_{i}).

(8)

Essentially, every constraint
z_{\sigma(j)}\geq z_{\sigma(j+1)}
from
\chi_{\sigma}

is refined in
\chi_{\sigma,d}
to either
z_{\sigma(j)}\geq z_{\sigma(j+1)}+M
or
z_{\sigma(j)}=z_{\sigma(j+1)}+g
, for some
g\in[0..M-1]
.
Note that each
\chi_{\sigma,d}
implies
\chi_{\sigma}
. Therefore,
by Claim
2
, the function
{C}[{x_{m}}]
is
(q,p)
-monotone locally to
\varphi\land\varphi[q+p\,/\,q]\land\chi_{\sigma,d}
.
Furthermore, the formula
\varphi\land\varphi[q+p\,/\,q]

implies
\bigvee_{\sigma\in\mathcal{P}}\bigvee_{d\in\mathcal{D}}\chi_{\sigma,d}
.

Let
\sigma\in\mathcal{P}
and
d\in\mathcal{D}
.
The next step of the proof involves manipulating the constraints
z_{\sigma(j)}\sim_{d(j)}z_{\sigma(j+1)}+d(j)
from the formula
\chi_{\sigma,d}
.
This manipulation produces a formula
\chi_{\sigma,d}^{\prime}
in which these constraints only feature variables from
\varphi
.
Moreover,
\varphi\land\varphi[q+p\,/\,q]

implies
(\chi_{\sigma,d}\iff\chi_{\sigma,d}^{\prime})
.
The value of
M
introduced when defining
\chi_{\sigma,d}
was chosen
to make this manipulation possible by appealing to
Lemma

11
.
The core step in this manipulation is given in the next claim.

Claim 3.

Let
\sigma\in\mathcal{P}
and
d\in\mathcal{D}
.
The formula

\varphi\land\varphi[q+p\,/\,q]

implies

\forall\bm{w}\,\Big(\bigwedge\nolimits_{i=1}^{2(k+\ell)+1}(w_{i}=\rho_{i})\implies\Big(\bigwedge\nolimits_{j=1}^{2k}\big(z_{\sigma(j)}\sim_{d(j)}z_{\sigma(j+1)}+d(j)\big)\iff\bigwedge\nolimits_{j=1}^{2k}(0\sim_{d(j)}E_{j})\Big)\Big),

where each
E_{j}
(with
j\in[1..2k]
)
is
0
,
1
, or an expression
of the form

a\cdot 2^{x_{n-k}}+\mu_{C}\cdot\big((y_{\sigma(j+1)}-y_{\sigma(j)})\cdot 2^{x_{n-k-1}}+(s_{\sigma(j+1)}-s_{\sigma(j)})\big)+\mu_{C}\cdot d(j),

(9)

for some
a\in[-b..b]
, where
b\coloneqq\mu_{C}\cdot(1+M)
.

Proof of
3
.

The proof is by induction on
t
from
2k+1
to
1
,
with induction hypothesis stating that
\varphi\land\varphi[q+p\,/\,q]

implies
\forall\bm{w}\big(\bigwedge\nolimits_{i=1}^{2(k+\ell)+1}(w_{i}=\rho_{i})\implies\Gamma_{\sigma,d}^{(t)}\,\big)
,
where
\Gamma_{\sigma,d}^{(t)}
is defined as

\bigwedge\nolimits_{j=t}^{2k}\big(z_{\sigma(j)}\sim_{d(j)}z_{\sigma(j+1)}+d(j)\big)\iff\bigwedge\nolimits_{j=t}^{2k}(0\sim_{d(j)}E_{j}),

(10)

for some suitable expressions
E_{j}
having the form described in the statement of the claim.

base case:
t=2k+1
.

This case is trivial, as
\Gamma_{\sigma,d}^{(2k+1)}
is defined as the tautology
(\top\iff\top)
.

induction hypothesis.

For
t\in[1..2k]
,
\varphi\land\varphi[q+p\,/\,q]
implies
\forall\bm{w}(\bigwedge\nolimits_{i=1}^{2(k+\ell)+1}(w_{i}=\rho_{i})\implies\Gamma_{\sigma,d}^{(t+1)})
.

induction step:
t<2k+1
.

From the induction hypothesis,

\varphi\land\varphi[q+p\,/\,q]

implies

\displaystyle\forall\bm{w}\,\Big(\bigwedge\nolimits_{i=1}^{2(k+\ell)+1}(w_{i}=\rho_{i})\implies\Big(

\displaystyle\bigwedge\nolimits_{j=t}^{2k}\big(z_{\sigma(j)}\sim_{d(j)}z_{\sigma(j+1)}+d(j)\big)\iff{}

\displaystyle\bigwedge\nolimits_{j={t+1}}^{2k}\big(0\sim_{d(j)}E_{j}\big)\land\boxed{z_{\sigma(t)}\sim_{d(t)}z_{\sigma(t+1)}+d(t)}\,\Big)\Big),

(11)

for some suitable expressions
E_{j}
adhering to the form described in the statement of the claim.

We construct
E_{t}
by modifying the formula in
Equation

11
,
specifically by only updating the
boxed
occurrence of
{(z_{\sigma(t)}\sim_{d(t)}z_{\sigma(t+1)}+d(t))}

that appears on the right-hand side of the double implication.
After all manipulations, the resulting formula is still implied by
{\varphi\land\varphi[q+p\,/\,q]}
.

We rewrite the
boxed
constraint
(z_{\sigma(t)}\sim_{d(t)}z_{\sigma(t+1)}+d(t))
by replacing the variables
z_{\sigma(t)}
and
z_{\sigma(t+1)}
with the
corresponding expressions featured in the antecedent
\bigwedge\nolimits_{i=1}^{2(k+\ell)+1}(w_{i}=\rho_{i})
of the implication in
Equation

11
. Specifically, if
w_{i_{1}}
is an alias of
z_{\sigma(t)}
and
w_{i_{2}}
is an alias of
z_{\sigma(t+1)}
, then we replace
z_{\sigma(t)}
by
\rho_{i_{1}}
and
z_{\sigma(t+1)}
by
\rho_{i_{2}}
.
By the definition of
C
and
C^{+p}
, the result is a constraint
(0\sim_{d(t)}E)
where
E
is of the form

\sum_{i=t+1}^{2k+1}a_{i}\cdot 2^{z_{\sigma(i)}}+\mu_{C}\cdot\big((y_{\sigma(t+1)}-y_{\sigma(t)})\cdot 2^{x_{n-k-1}}+(s_{\sigma(t+1)}-s_{\sigma(t)})\big)+\mu_{C}\cdot d(t),

(12)

where every
\sum_{i=t+1}^{2k+1}\left|a_{i}\right|
is bounded, in absolute value, by
2\cdot\xi_{C}
.

After the updates, the formula in
Equation

11

is still implied by
\varphi\land\varphi[q+p\,/\,q]
.
Because of the induction hypothesis,
to show this it suffices to see that

•

{w_{i_{1}}=\rho_{i_{1}}\land w_{i_{2}}=\rho_{i_{2}}\land(z_{\sigma(t)}\sim_{d(t)}z_{\sigma(t+1)}+d(t))}
implies
(0\sim_{d(t)}E)
, and

•

{w_{i_{1}}=\rho_{i_{1}}\land w_{i_{2}}=\rho_{i_{2}}\land\lnot(z_{\sigma(t)}\sim_{d(t)}z_{\sigma(t+1)}+d(t))}
implies
\lnot(0\sim_{d(t)}E)
.

Both these implications follows trivially from how
E
is constructed.

We further manipulate the expression
E
from
Equation

12
.
The following three facts hold:

•

The right-hand side of the double implication of the updated formula from
Equation

11
,
now featuring
(0\sim_{d(t)}E)
,
implies (because of the double implication)
the constraints
{\big(z_{\sigma(i)}\sim_{d(i)}z_{\sigma(i+1)}+d(i)\big)}

for every
i\in[t+1..2k]
.

•

Since
\varphi\land\varphi[q+p\,/\,q]
implies the formula in
Equation

11
, we can bound the term
\big((y_{\sigma(t+1)}-y_{\sigma(t)})\cdot 2^{x_{n-k-1}}+(s_{\sigma(t+1)}-s_{\sigma(t)})\big)

occurring in
E
as follows:

-2^{x_{n-k}}<\big((y_{\sigma(t+1)}-y_{\sigma(t)})\cdot 2^{x_{n-k-1}}+(s_{\sigma(t+1)}-s_{\sigma(t)})\big)<2^{x_{n-k}}.

(13)

Indeed, from the definition of
\Psi(C)
and
\Psi(C^{+p})
,
for both
j\in\{\sigma(t),\sigma(t+1)\}
, we have:

–

If
C
and
C^{+p}
do not assign an expression to
y
,
then the formula
\varphi\land\varphi[q+p\,/\,q]
implies
0\leq y_{j}\cdot 2^{x_{n-k-1}}+s_{j}<2^{x_{n-k}}
.

–

If
C
or
C^{+p}
assign an expression
\rho
to
y_{j}
, then
the formula
\varphi\land\varphi[q+p\,/\,q]
implies
\exists y_{j}\,(0\leq y_{j}\cdot 2^{x_{n-k-1}}+s_{j}<2^{x_{n-k}}\land y_{j}=\rho)
. In this case,
observe that since
y_{j}
is (an alias of) a quotient variable among

q_{n},\dots,q_{n-\ell+1},\overline{q}_{n},\dots,\overline{q}_{n-\ell+1}
,
the expression
\rho
only contains variables occurring in
\varphi
(and it does not contain
y_{j}
).
Consequently, the value of
y_{j}
is uniquely determined given a solution to
\varphi\land\varphi[q+p\,/\,q]
.
This means that
\varphi\land\varphi[q+p\,/\,q]
also implies
\forall y_{j}\,(y_{j}=\rho\implies 0\leq y_{j}\cdot 2^{x_{n-k-1}}+s_{j}<2^{x_{n-k}})
.

We conclude that
\varphi\land\varphi[q+p\,/\,q]
implies

\forall\bm{w}\,\Big(\bigwedge\nolimits_{i=1}^{2(k+\ell)+1}(w_{i}=\rho_{i})\implies\bigwedge\nolimits_{j\in\{\sigma(t),\sigma(t+1)\}}0\leq y_{j}\cdot 2^{x_{n-k-1}}+s_{j}<2^{x_{n-k}}\Big),

which allows us to bound
(y_{\sigma(t+1)}-y_{\sigma(t)})\cdot 2^{x_{n-k-1}}+(s_{\sigma(t+1)}-s_{\sigma(t)})

as in
Equation

13
.

•

The integer
M
used to define the map
d
satisfies

M\geq\max\big(1+2\cdot\log_{2}\big(\sum\nolimits_{i=t+1}^{2k+1}\left|a_{i}\right|+\mu_{C}\big),\ 4\cdot\log_{2}(\mu_{C})+8,\ d(t)\big).

Due to the three items above,
we can invoke
Lemma

11

and
Lemma

12

to rewrite
E
as an expression that is either
0
or
1
, or has the form

a\cdot 2^{x_{n-k}}+\mu_{C}\cdot\big((y_{\sigma(t+1)}-y_{\sigma(t)})\cdot 2^{x_{n-k-1}}+(s_{\sigma(t+1)}-s_{\sigma(t)})\big)+\mu_{C}\cdot d(t),

for some
a\in[-b..b]
, where
b\coloneqq\mu_{C}\cdot(1+M)
.
This completes the proof of the claim.
∎

Resuming the proof of
Proposition

3
,
from the chain of equivalences involving
Equations

6
,
7
and
8
and by applying
3
,
we see that
\varphi\land\varphi[q+p\,/\,q]
implies
\bigvee_{\sigma\in\mathcal{P}}\bigvee_{d\in\mathcal{D}}\chi_{\sigma,d}^{\prime}
where

\chi_{\sigma,d}^{\prime}\coloneqq\exists\bm{w}:\bigwedge\nolimits_{j=1}^{2k}(0\sim_{d(j)}E_{\sigma,d,j})\land\bigwedge\nolimits_{i=1}^{2(k+\ell)+1}(w_{i}=\rho_{i}),

(14)

and each
E_{\sigma,d,j}
is an expression having one of the forms given in
3
.
(Below, we simply write
E_{j}
instead of
E_{\sigma,d,j}
, as
\sigma
and
d
will be clear from the context.)
Moreover, as a consequence of Claim
2
, the function
{C}[{x_{m}}]
is
(q,p)
-monotone locally to
\varphi\land\varphi[q+p\,/\,q]\land\chi_{\sigma,d}^{\prime}
.

Given
\sigma\in\mathcal{P}
and
d\in\mathcal{D}
,
we further manipulate the formula
\chi_{\sigma,d}^{\prime}
.
We consider every expression
E_{j}
of the form

a\cdot 2^{x_{n-k}}+\mu_{C}\cdot\big((y_{\sigma(j+1)}-y_{\sigma(j)})\cdot 2^{x_{n-k-1}}+(s_{\sigma(j+1)}-s_{\sigma(j)})\big)+\mu_{C}\cdot d(j)
.
Let us write
L_{\sigma,d,j}
and
R_{\sigma,d,j}
(again, we omit
\sigma
and
d
for simplicity) for the two expressions:

L_{j}\coloneqq a\cdot u+\mu_{C}\cdot(y_{\sigma(j+1)}-y_{\sigma(j)}),\qquad R_{j}\coloneqq\mu_{C}\cdot(s_{\sigma(j+1)}-s_{\sigma(j)})+\mu_{C}\cdot d(j).

Recall that
\varphi
occurs in
\mathcal{I}_{k}
,
and therefore it features the equality
u=2^{x_{n-k}-x_{n-k-1}}
;
so
\varphi\land\varphi[q+p\,/\,q]
implies
\forall\bm{w}:{E_{j}=L_{j}\cdot 2^{x_{n-k-1}}+R_{j}}
.
Moreover, from the definition of
\Psi(C)
,
we see that
\varphi
implies
(0\leq s_{\sigma(j)}<2^{x_{n-k-1}})
and
(0\leq s_{\sigma(j+1)}<2^{x_{n-k-1}})
. We then conclude that
\varphi\land\varphi[q+p\,/\,q]
implies

-\mu_{C}\cdot 2^{x_{n-k-1}}<R_{j}<(\mu_{C}+1)\cdot M\cdot 2^{x_{n-k-1}}
.
Given
r\in[-\mu_{C}..(\mu_{C}+1)\cdot M]
,
we write
\gamma_{\sigma,d,j,r}
(often omitting
\sigma
and
d
, as they will be clear from the context)
for the formula given by:

•

if
\sim_{d(j)}
stands for
=
, then
\gamma_{j,r}\coloneqq\big(L_{j}+r=0\land R_{j}=r\cdot 2^{x_{n-k-1}}\big)
, and,

•

if
\sim_{d(j)}
stands for
\geq
, then
\gamma_{j,r}\coloneqq\big(L_{j}+r\leq 0\land(r-1)\cdot 2^{y}<R_{j}\leq r\cdot 2^{y}\big)
.

From
Lemma

3
,
\varphi\land\varphi[q+p\,/\,q]
implies
\forall\bm{w}\big((0\sim_{d(j)}E_{j})\iff\bigvee_{r=-\mu_{C}}^{(\mu_{C}+1)\cdot M}\gamma_{j,r}\big)
.

We further update the expressions
L_{j}+r
above
by updating
y_{\sigma(j)}
and
y_{\sigma(j+1)}

using the corresponding expressions featured in the formula
\bigwedge\nolimits_{i=1}^{2(k+\ell)+1}(w_{i}=\rho_{i})
, if such expressions exist.
That is, if a variable
w_{i}
in this formula is an alias for
y_{\sigma(j)}
, then we consider the substitution
[\frac{\rho_{i}}{\lambda}\,/\,\mu_{C}\cdot y_{\sigma(j)}]
,
where
\lambda\coloneqq\frac{\eta_{C}}{\mu_{C}}
.
Since
(C,\varphi)\in\mathcal{I}_{k}^{\ell}
, we know that
\mu_{C}
divides
\eta_{C}
,
hence
\lambda
is a positive integer.
Observe moreover that in
L_{j}
,
the variables
y_{\sigma(j)}
and
y_{\sigma(j+1)}
have a coefficient of
\pm\mu_{C}
;
this means that applying the substitution
[\frac{\rho_{i}}{\lambda}\,/\,\mu_{C}\cdot y_{\sigma(j)}]
eliminates
y_{\sigma(j)}
.
If no variable
w_{i}
is an alias for
y_{\sigma(j)}
,
we consider instead the substitution
[\frac{\eta_{C}\cdot y_{\sigma(j)}}{\lambda}\,/\,\mu_{C}\cdot y_{\sigma(j)}]
(applying this substitution to
L_{j}+r

simply scales all coefficients by
\lambda
).
Observe that these substitutions are among those constructed in lines
5
and
7
of
Algorithm

4
.
We handle
y_{\sigma(j+1)}
analogously,
and simultaneously apply the resulting substitutions to
L_{j}+r
.
Let
L_{j,r}^{\prime}
be the resulting expression. It is of the form

\lambda\cdot a\cdot u+\tau(u,\bm{q}_{[\ell,k]})+\lambda\cdot r

(15)

where
\tau(u,\bm{q}_{[\ell,k]})
is a difference
\tau^{\prime}-\tau^{\prime\prime}
of two terms
\tau^{\prime}
and
\tau^{\prime\prime}
having forms among the following:

•

a variable
\eta_{C}\cdot q_{n-i}
with
i\in[\ell..k]
; note that these variables occurs free in
\Psi(C)
and
\Psi(C^{+p})
,

•

the expression
\eta_{C}\cdot(q+p)
; note that
(q+p)
is aliased by one of the variables
y_{1},\dots,y_{2k+1}
, and that no expression in
C
and
C^{+p}

is assigned to
q
.

•

the term
\tau_{n-i}
, for some
i\in[0..\ell-1]
, which occurs in
C
in the expression assigned to
q_{n-i}
,

•

the term
\tau_{n-i}[q+p\,/\,q]
, for some
i\in[0..\ell-1]
, which occurs in
C^{+p}
in the expression for
\overline{q}_{n-i}
.

Lastly, let
\gamma_{j,r}^{\prime}
be the formula obtained from
\gamma_{j,r}
by replacing
L_{j}+r
with
L_{j,r}^{\prime}
, and rewriting
L_{j,r}^{\prime}=0
as
L_{j,r}^{\prime}\leq 0\land-L_{j,r}^{\prime}\leq 0
. Note that no variable from
\gamma_{j,r}^{\prime}
occurs in the vector
\bm{w}
.
We are now in the position of establishing the following two results,
which almost complete the proof of
Proposition

3
.

Claim 4.

The formula
\varphi\land\varphi[q+p\,/\,q]
implies
\bigvee_{\sigma\in\mathcal{P}}\bigvee_{d\in\mathcal{D}}\bigwedge_{j=1}^{2k}\bigvee_{r=-\mu_{C}}^{(\mu_{C}+1)\cdot M}\gamma_{\sigma,d,j,r}^{\prime}
.

Claim 5.

For every
\sigma\in\mathcal{P}
and every
d\in\mathcal{D}
,
the function
{C}[{x_{m}}]
is
(q,p)
-monotone locally to the set of solutions of the formula
\varphi\land\varphi[q+p\,/\,q]\land\bigwedge_{j=1}^{2k}\bigvee_{r=-\mu_{C}}^{(\mu_{C}+1)\cdot M}\gamma_{\sigma,d,j,r}^{\prime}
.

Proof of Claims
4
–
5
.

We have already
established
that:

1.

The formula
\varphi\land\varphi[q+p\,/\,q]
implies
\bigvee_{\sigma\in\mathcal{P}}\bigvee_{d\in\mathcal{D}}\chi_{\sigma,d}^{\prime}
, where

\chi_{\sigma,d}^{\prime}
is as in
Equation

14
.

2.

For every
\sigma\in\mathcal{P}
and
d\in\mathcal{D}
,
\varphi\land\varphi[q+p\,/\,q]
implies
\forall\bm{w}\big((0\sim_{d(j)}E_{j})\iff\bigvee_{r=-\mu_{C}}^{(\mu_{C}+1)\cdot M}\gamma_{j,r}\big)
.

3.

The function
{C}[{x_{m}}]
is
(q,p)
-monotone locally to
{\varphi\land\varphi[q+p\,/\,q]\land\chi_{\sigma,d}^{\prime}}
.

Every formula
\gamma_{j,r}^{\prime}
is
obtained from
\gamma_{j,r}
by “applying”
the equalities from
{\bigwedge\nolimits_{i=1}^{2(k+\ell)+1}(w_{i}=\rho_{i})}
as substitutions.
Together with Item
2
above,
we thus conclude that
\chi_{\sigma,d}^{\prime}
is equivalent to

\exists\bm{w}:\Big(\Big(\bigwedge\nolimits_{j=1}^{2k}\bigvee\nolimits_{r=-\mu_{C}}^{(\mu_{C}+1)\cdot M}\gamma_{j,r}^{\prime}\Big)\land\bigwedge\nolimits_{i=1}^{2(k+\ell)+1}(w_{i}=\rho_{i})\Big).

The variables
\bm{w}
do not occur in any formula
\gamma_{j,r}^{\prime}
, and so the above formula is equivalent to

\Big(\bigwedge\nolimits_{j=1}^{2k}\bigvee\nolimits_{r=-\mu_{C}}^{(\mu_{C}+1)\cdot M}\gamma_{j,r}^{\prime}\Big)\land\exists\bm{w}:\bigwedge\nolimits_{i=1}^{2(k+\ell)+1}(w_{i}=\rho_{i}).

The two claims then follows from Items
1
and
3
, together with the fact that
the formula
\varphi\land\varphi[q+p\,/\,q]
implies
\Psi(C)\land\Psi(C^{+p})
,
which in turn implies
{\exists\bm{w}\bigwedge\nolimits_{i=1}^{2(k+\ell)+1}(w_{i}=\rho_{i})}
by definition.
∎

At last, let us define the formulae
\psi_{1},\dots,\psi_{s}
.
They correspond to all the linear-exponential systems occurring as disjuncts
of the disjunctive normal form of the formulae
\bigwedge_{j=1}^{2k}\bigvee_{r=-\mu_{C}}^{(\mu_{C}+1)\cdot M}\gamma_{\sigma,d,j,r}^{\prime}
, for every
\sigma\in\mathcal{P}
and
d\in\mathcal{D}
.
Directly from
4
and
5
, we conclude that these formulae satisfy the desired conditions in Items
I.
and
II.
.
To complete the proof, it suffices to show that the condition in Item
III.
is also satisfied.

Every constraint occurring in the formulae
\psi_{1},\dots,\psi_{s}

that features the variable
q
is of the form
\pm L_{\sigma,d,j,r}^{\prime}\leq 0
,
where
L_{\sigma,d,j,r}^{\prime}
is an expression as in
Equation

15
.
Therefore, the term
\pm L_{\sigma,d,j,r}^{\prime}

is of the form
\lambda\cdot a^{\prime}\cdot u+\tau^{\prime}(u,\bm{q}_{[\ell,k]})+\lambda\cdot r^{\prime}
where

•

a^{\prime}\in\mathbb{Z}
belongs to the set
[-\mu_{C}\cdot(1+M)..\mu_{C}\cdot(1+M)]
.
(Recall:
M\coloneqq 4\cdot\left\lceil\log_{2}(2\cdot\xi_{C}+\mu_{C})\right\rceil+8
.)

•

\tau^{\prime}(u,\bm{q}_{[\ell,k]})
is the term obtained from
(y_{\sigma(j+1)}-y_{\sigma(j)})
or
(y_{\sigma(j)}-y_{\sigma(j+1)})
by applying suitable substitutions.
These are among the substitutions considered in lines
5
–
8
of
Algorithm

2
.

•

r^{\prime}\in\mathbb{Z}
belongs to
[-(\mu_{C}+1)\cdot M..(\mu_{C}+1)\cdot M]
.

Note that
\lnot(\pm L_{\sigma,d,j,r}^{\prime}\leq 0)

is equivalent to
\mp L_{\sigma,d,j,r}^{\prime}+1\leq 0
.
Then, in order to cover
\lnot(\pm L_{\sigma,d,j,r}^{\prime}\leq 0)

with terms
\lambda\cdot a^{\prime}\cdot u+\tau^{\prime}(u,\bm{q}_{[\ell,k]})+\lambda\cdot r^{\prime}

as above, it suffices to increase the interval for the integers
r^{\prime}
to
[-(\mu_{C}+1)\cdot M-1..(\mu_{C}+1)\cdot M+1]
.
It is then easy to see that Item
III.
holds.
In fact, for simplicity of the presentation,
Algorithm

2
uses slightly larger ranges for
a^{\prime}
and
r^{\prime}
(these integers are called
a
and
d
in the pseudocode, respectively, see line
2
).
Indeed, since
\mu_{C}\geq 1
and
M\geq 8
, both
\mu_{C}\cdot(1+M)
and
(\mu_{C}+1)\cdot M+1
are bounded by
3\cdot\mu_{C}\cdot M
.
∎

5
An efficient variable elimination that preserves optimal solutions

Building on our monotone decomposition,
this section instantiates
Algorithm

1
(
ElimVars
) into the optima-preserving variable elimination procedure that was promised in
Section

2.2
. We also provide the proofs of correctness and complexity
of this algorithm.

The pseudocode of the instantiation of
ElimVars
is given on page
3
. The instantiation is obtained by
(i)
defining
the inputs of
ElimVars
,

(ii)
providing an algorithm for computing the test points
and
(iii)
implementing the elimination discipline.
Following the arguments in
Section

4
, achieving the first two points is simple. In particular, appealing to the notation from
Section

4
:

•

The
inputs
of
ElimVars
are triples
(\bm{q}_{k-1},{C}[{x_{m}}],\langle{\gamma}\mathbin{;}{\psi}\rangle)
, for every
k\in[0..n-1]
, where
{(C,\langle{\gamma}\mathbin{;}{\psi}\rangle)}
belongs to
\mathcal{I}_{k}^{0}
.
(The procedure will thus eliminate the variables
q_{n-k+1},\dots,q_{n}
, but not the variable
q_{n-k}
. This is consistent with the sketch of the procedure from
[
CMS24
]
given in
Section

2
, where the latter variable is eliminated only after Step III.)

•

The pseudocode of the procedure for computing the test points is given in
Algorithm

4
. Briefly, the procedure first (non-deterministically) computes a term
(a\cdot q-\tau)
stemming from the monotone decomposition of
Proposition

3
,
and then returns an equality
{(a\cdot q=\tau-s)}
, where
s
is a non-negative shift
that suffices to explore optimal solutions, by
Lemma

9
.

The implementation of an efficient elimination discipline is a more complex
task. As noted in the introduction (
Example

2
), the naïve approach of
rewriting a formula
\gamma
as
{\gamma[\frac{\tau-s}{a}\,/\,q]\land(\left|a\right|\mathrel{|}\tau-s)}
, where
(a\cdot q=\tau-s)
is a test point, would lead
to exponential growth in the bit length of integer coefficients, during the
execution of
ElimVars
. In
[
CMS24
]
, this problem is
avoided by extending Bareiss’ algorithm for Gaussian elimination
[
Bar68
]

to
integer linear programs. While our elimination discipline is also based on Bareiss’
algorithm, it is different from the one in
[
CMS24
]
. In
particular, we do not introduce “slack variables”, and add
some technical machinery to handle the additional test points required by the monotone
decomposition (those computed by
Algorithm

2
).

5.1
Efficient elimination discipline: the high-level idea

Algorithm 3

Instantiation of
ElimVars
(
Algorithm

1
).

1:

(\bm{q}_{k-1},{C}[{x_{m}}],\langle{\gamma}\mathbin{;}{\psi}\rangle)\colon
a triple such that
(C,\langle{\gamma}\mathbin{;}{\psi}\rangle)
belongs to
\mathcal{I}_{k}^{0}
, for some
k\in[0..n-1]
.

2:

while
some variable from
\bm{q}_{k-1}
appears in
\gamma
or in
\textup{vars}(C)

do

3:

(a\cdot q=\tau)\leftarrow\textbf{guess}\text{ an element in }\textit{TP}(\bm{q}_{k-1},{C}[{x_{m}}],\langle{\gamma}\mathbin{;}{\psi}\rangle)

\triangleright

Algorithm

4

4:

({C}[{x_{m}}],\langle{\gamma}\mathbin{;}{\psi}\rangle)\leftarrow\textit{Elim}({C}[{x_{m}}],\langle{\gamma}\mathbin{;}{\psi}\rangle,a\cdot q=\tau)

\triangleright

Algorithm

5

5:

return

(f,\varphi)

Algorithm 4

TP
: Non-deterministic generation of the test points for ILEP.

1:

(\bm{q}_{k-1},{C}[{x_{m}}],\langle{\gamma}\mathbin{;}{\psi}\rangle)\colon
a triple such that
(C,\langle{\gamma}\mathbin{;}{\psi}\rangle)
belongs to
\mathcal{I}_{k}^{\ell}
, with
\ell<k
.

2:

p\leftarrow\textit{mod}(q_{n-\ell},\gamma)

3:

(a\cdot q_{n-\ell}-\tau)\leftarrow{}

guess
a term with
a\neq 0
that is either from
\textup{terms}(\gamma\land\gamma[q_{n-\ell}+p\,/\,q_{n-\ell}])
,

4:

(a\cdot q_{n-\ell}-\tau)\leftarrow{}

guess
or computed using
Algorithm

2
with respect to
(C,\gamma,p)
.

5:

s\leftarrow\text{{guess} an element in }[0..\left|a\right|\cdot p-1]
