return

(a\cdot q_{n-\ell}=\tau-s)

Algorithm 5

Elim
: An efficient elimination discipline for ILEP.

1:

({C}[{x_{m}}],\langle{\gamma}\mathbin{;}{\psi}\rangle)

:

a pair such that
(C,\langle{\gamma}\mathbin{;}{\psi}\rangle)\in\mathcal{I}_{k}^{\ell}
, with
\ell<k
;

a\cdot q_{n-\ell}=\tau

:

an equality returned by
Algorithm

4
on input
(\bm{q}_{k-1},{C}[{x_{m}}],\langle{\gamma}\mathbin{;}{\psi}\rangle)
.

2:

if

a<0

then

(a,\tau)\leftarrow(-a,-\tau)

\triangleright

consider
-a\cdot q_{n-\ell}=-\tau
instead

3:

\lambda\leftarrow\frac{\eta_{C}}{\mu_{C}}
;
\alpha\leftarrow\frac{a}{\mu_{C}}

4:

\gamma\leftarrow\gamma[\frac{\tau}{\alpha}\,/\,\mu_{C}\cdot q_{n-\ell}]\land(a\mathrel{|}\tau)

5:

assert
(in every equality
\tau=0
of
\gamma
, the constant of the term
\tau
is divisible by
\lambda
)

6:

\triangleright

here and below,
assert
(
false
) causes the non-deterministic branch to reject

7:

update each equality
\tau=0
in
\gamma
:

8:

\bullet
divide all integers appearing in the term
\tau
by
\lambda

9:

update each inequality
\tau\leq 0
in
\gamma
:

10:

\bullet
divide all variable coefficients in the term
\tau
by
\lambda

11:

\bullet
replace the constant
c
of the term
\tau
with
\lceil{\frac{c}{\lambda}}\rceil

12:

update
C
:

13:

\bullet
for every
i\in[0..\ell-1]
, consider the assignment
q_{n-i}\leftarrow\frac{\tau_{n-i}}{\eta_{C}}
in
C

14:

-

assert
(the constant of the term
\tau_{n-i}[\frac{\tau}{\alpha}\,/\,\mu_{C}\cdot q_{n-\ell}]
is divisible by
\lambda
)

15:

-
replace
q_{n-i}\leftarrow\frac{\tau_{n-i}}{\eta_{C}}
with
q_{n-i}\leftarrow\frac{\tau_{n-i}^{\prime}}{a}
, where the term
\tau_{n-i}^{\prime}
is obtained

16:

-
from the term
\tau_{n-i}[\frac{\tau}{\alpha}\,/\,\mu_{C}\cdot q_{n-\ell}]
by dividing all integers by
\lambda

17:

\bullet
prepend the assignment
q_{n-\ell}\leftarrow\frac{\tau}{a}

18:

return

\left({C}[{x_{m}}],\langle{\gamma}\mathbin{;}{\psi}\rangle\right)

\triangleright

(C,\langle{\gamma}\mathbin{;}{\psi}\rangle)
belongs to
\mathcal{I}_{k}^{\ell+1}

The pseudocode of our elimination discipline is given in
Algorithm

5
.
We now discuss the overall idea behind this procedure.
Consider one of its inputs: a pair
({C}[{x_{m}}],\langle{\gamma}\mathbin{;}{\psi}\rangle)
,
where
(C,\langle{\gamma}\mathbin{;}{\psi}\rangle)\in\mathcal{I}_{k}^{\ell}
with
\ell<k
,
and an equality
a\cdot q_{n-\ell}=\tau
returned by
Algorithm

4
on input
(\bm{q}_{k-1},{C}[{x_{m}}],\langle{\gamma}\mathbin{;}{\psi}\rangle)
.

Let us briefly explain why the naïve approach of eliminating the variable
q_{n-\ell}
from
\gamma
by performing the substitution
[\frac{\tau}{a}\,/\,q_{n-\ell}]
leads to an exponential growth.
Assuming
a>0
, this substitution rewrites an inequality
b\cdot q_{n-\ell}\leq\tau^{\prime}
as
b\cdot\tau\leq a\cdot\tau^{\prime}
. Let
c
and
d
denote the coefficients of another variable, say
y
, in
\tau
and
\tau^{\prime}
, respectively.
Then, in the term
b\cdot\tau-a\cdot\tau^{\prime}
, the coefficient of
y
is
b\cdot c-a\cdot d
. In essence, this shows that the coefficients of the variables in
\gamma

may grow quadratically within each elimination of one of the variables in
\bm{q}_{k-1}
. As a result, by the end of
ElimVars
, the naïve approach may produce a linear program with coefficients of exponential bit size.

The above explosion can be avoided by observing that the variable coefficient
b\cdot c-a\cdot d
is exactly the one we would obtain when performing a naïve version of the Gaussian elimination procedure for putting a matrix with entries over
\mathbb{Z}
in echelon form. Building on Bareiss’s observation
[
Bar68
]
, we see that these growing variable coefficients accumulate common factors as we iteratively eliminate variables. Divisions by these common factors after each variable elimination keep variable coefficients of polynomial bit size. (These divisions are performed in lines
7
–
9
of
Algorithm

5
.)
While variable coefficients evolve as in Gaussian elimination,
the
constants
of the terms do not. In particular, the shift performed in line
5
of
5
disrupt any structure in the constants. This however does not pose a problem.
Inequalities of the form
\lambda\cdot\rho+c\leq 0
(where
\lambda\geq 1
is the common factor,
\rho
a term, and
c
is the integer constant) can be rewritten as
\rho+\left\lceil\frac{c}{\lambda}\right\rceil\leq 0
.
For equalities
\lambda\cdot\rho+c=0
instead, observe that they are unsatisfiable when
c
is not a multiple of
\lambda
, and otherwise they can be rewritten as
\rho+\frac{c}{\lambda}=0
.

5
implements this reasoning in lines
5
–
9
, where the positive integer
\lambda
defined in line
3
represents the common factor.
(We will clarify why this common factor is exactly the ratio
\frac{\eta_{C}}{\mu_{C}}
of the two denominators of the
(k,\ell)
-LEAC
C
in
Section

5.4
.)
A similar argument can be made for updates performed to the circuit
C
, by seeing each assignment
q\leftarrow\frac{\tau}{\eta_{C}}
as the equality
\eta_{C}\cdot q=\tau
;
see line
12
of
12
.

Further technical details must be added to the above picture to keep the complexity of
OptILEP
in check. The main problem arises when
Algorithm

4
produces a test point by appealing to
Algorithm

2
. Such test points depend on the assignments featured in the circuit
C
.
This dependence makes it challenging to maintain the delicate “Gaussian-elimination-style evolution” that variable coefficients must have throughout
ElimVars
.
The solution
Algorithm

5
implements starts from the observation that all non-zero coefficients of the quotient variables
\bm{q}_{k}
in
the term in line
9
of
9
are (before substitutions) only
\pm\mu_{C}
. Together with the constraints imposed by
\mathcal{I}_{k}^{\ell}
, which ensure that all coefficients of the variables
\bm{q}_{[\ell,k]}
are divisible by
\mu_{C}
,
this observation will enable us to give a variation of Bareiss algorithm,
from which we can prove that
ElimVars
, and later
OptILEP
, run in non-deterministic polynomial time.

Before moving to a more technical analysis of
ElimVars
,
let us note that the overwhelming presence of
\mu_{C}
in both
9
and elements of
\mathcal{I}_{k}^{\ell}
implies the following property of
Algorithm

4
:

Lemma 13.
†
†
margin:

Proof in page
C.5

Given in input a triple
(\bm{q}_{k-1},{C}[{x_{m}}],\langle{\gamma}\mathbin{;}{\psi}\rangle)
with
(C,\langle{\gamma}\mathbin{;}{\psi}\rangle)\in\mathcal{I}_{k}^{\ell}

Algorithm

4
guesses in line
3

a linear term
a\cdot q_{n-\ell}-\tau(u,\bm{q}_{[\ell+1,k]})
in which all coefficients of
\bm{q}_{[\ell,k]}

are divisible by
\mu_{C}
.

5.2
Correctness of
ElimVars

The integration of the machinery from Bareiss algorithm to keep the growth of the coefficients in check has a “presentational drawback”: the arguments for establishing
the complexity of the algorithm now mix with those needed to prove its correctness, as we must ensure that this machinery is implemented correctly.

To ease the presentation, we structure this and the next three (
Sections

5.3
,
5.4
and
5.5
) as follows. In the current section, we isolate the key property necessary for the correct implementation of Bareiss’s machinery.
This property is formalized in
6
.
Assuming this claim to hold, we then establish the correctness of
ElimVars
.

Sections

5.3
and
5.4

develop the arguments needed to prove
6
,
while also setting up the properties required for the complexity analysis.
More precisely,
Section

5.3
presents a variation of Bareiss algorithm in which the evolution of variable coefficients precisely mirrors that of
ElimVars
. We state a series of results characterizing this evolution;
their proofs are deferred to
Section

C.3

—these proofs involve a detour to linear algebra and Bareiss algorithm,
and we prefer to keep the focus of this section on
ElimVars
. In
Section

5.4
we formalize the connection between this variation of Bareiss algorithm and
ElimVars
, and use it to prove
6
. Finally, in
Section

5.5

we leverage this connection to analyze the complexity of
ElimVars
.

Here is the aforementioned key property related to Bareiss algorithm:

Claim 6.

The following property is true across all the executions of
Algorithm

5
performed in all non-deterministic branches of
ElimVars
, on any of its inputs.
In all equalities and inequalities of the formula
{\gamma[\frac{\tau}{\alpha}\,/\,\mu_{C}\cdot q_{n-\ell}]}
computed in line
4
, and in terms
{\tau_{n-i}[\frac{\tau}{\alpha}\,/\,\mu_{C}\cdot q_{n-\ell}]}
computed in line
12
,
all coefficients of the variables
\bm{q}_{[\ell+1,k]}
are divisible by
\eta_{C}
, and all coefficients of
u
are divisible by
\frac{\eta_{C}}{\mu_{C}}
.

The following lemma establishes the correctness of
ElimVars
.

Lemma 14.

There is a non-deterministic procedure with the following specification:

Input:

\bm{q}_{k-1}

:

the vector of quotient variables
q_{n-(k-1)},\dots,q_{n}
; (for any
k
)

{C}[{x_{m}}]

:

objective function, where
C
is a
(k,0)
-LEAC;

\langle{\gamma}\mathbin{;}{\psi}\rangle

:

linear exponential program with divisions,

such that the pair
(C,\langle{\gamma}\mathbin{;}{\psi}\rangle)
belongs to
\mathcal{I}_{k}^{0}
.

Output of each branch (
\beta
):

{C_{\beta}^{\prime}}[{x_{m}}]

:

objective function, where
C_{\beta}^{\prime}
is a
(k,k)
-LEAC;

\langle{\gamma_{\beta}^{\prime}}\mathbin{;}{\psi}\rangle

:

linear exponential program with divisions,

such that
(C_{\beta}^{\prime},\langle{\gamma_{\beta}^{\prime}}\mathbin{;}{\psi}\rangle)
belongs to
\mathcal{I}_{k}^{k}
.

The procedure ensures the satisfaction of the following two properties:

•

Equivalence:

The formulae
\exists\bm{q}_{k-1}\,\gamma

and
\bigvee_{\beta}\gamma_{\beta}^{\prime}
are equivalent.
Consider a branch
\beta
,
and let
q_{n-(k-1)}\leftarrow\frac{\tau_{n-(k-1)}}{\eta},\dots,q_{n}\leftarrow\frac{\tau_{n}}{\eta}
be the assignments
to the variables
\bm{q}_{k-1}
occurring in
C_{\beta}^{\prime}
.
Given a solution
\nu\colon\{u,q_{n-k}\}\to\mathbb{N}
to
\gamma_{\beta}^{\prime}
,
the map
\nu+\sum_{i=0}^{k-1}[q_{n-i}\mapsto\frac{\nu(\tau_{n-i})}{\eta}]
is a solution to
\gamma
.

•

Preservation of maximum:
if
\max\{{C}[{x_{m}}](\nu):\text{$\nu$ is a solution to $\langle{\gamma}\mathbin{;}{\psi}\rangle$}\}
exists,
then it is equal to

\max\{{C_{\beta}^{\prime}}[{x_{m}}](\nu):\text{$\beta$ is a branch, $\nu$ is a solution to $\langle{\gamma_{\beta}^{\prime}}\mathbin{;}{\psi}\rangle$}\}
.

Proof (assuming
6
).

By induction on
\ell=k,\dots,0
, with induction
hypothesis:

induction hypothesis.

The specification given
in
Lemma

14
holds when executing the
while
loop
of
Algorithm

1
(
ElimVars
) on a triple
(\bm{q}_{k-1},{C}[{x_{m}}],\langle{\gamma}\mathbin{;}{\psi}\rangle)
where
(C,\langle{\gamma}\mathbin{;}{\psi}\rangle)

belongs to
\mathcal{I}_{k}^{\ell}
. That is to say,

•

Output:
the output of each branch
\beta

is
({C_{\beta}^{\prime}}[{x_{m}}],\langle{\gamma_{\beta}^{\prime}}\mathbin{;}{\psi}\rangle)
, with

(C_{\beta}^{\prime},\langle{\gamma_{\beta}^{\prime}}\mathbin{;}{\psi}\rangle)\in\mathcal{I}_{k}^{k}
.

•

Equivalence:

The formulae
\exists\bm{q}_{[\ell,k-1]}\gamma
and
\bigvee_{\beta}\gamma_{\beta}^{\prime}
are equivalent. Consider a branch
\beta
,
and let
q_{n-(k-1)}\leftarrow\frac{\tau_{n-(k-1)}}{\eta},\dots,q_{n-\ell}\leftarrow\frac{\tau_{n-\ell}}{\eta}
be the assignments
to the variables
\bm{q}_{[\ell,k-1]}
in
C_{\beta}^{\prime}
.
Given a solution
\nu\colon\{u,q_{n-k}\}\to\mathbb{N}
to
\gamma_{\beta}^{\prime}
,
the map
{\nu+\sum_{i=\ell}^{k-1}[q_{n-i}\mapsto\frac{\nu(\tau_{n-i})}{\eta}]}
is a solution to
\gamma
.

•

Preservation of maximum:
if
\max\{{C}[{x_{m}}](\nu):\text{$\nu$ is a solution to $\langle{\gamma}\mathbin{;}{\psi}\rangle$}\}
exists, then it
is equal to
\max\{{C_{\beta}^{\prime}}[{x_{m}}](\nu):\text{$\beta$ is a
branch, $\nu$ is a solution to $\langle{\gamma_{\beta}^{\prime}}\mathbin{;}{\psi}\rangle$}\}
.

Observe that setting
\ell=0
in the above induction hypothesis yields the
statement of
Lemma

14
.

base case:
\ell=k
.

In this case, we have
(C,\langle{\gamma}\mathbin{;}{\psi}\rangle)\in\mathcal{I}_{k}^{k}
. By
definition of
\mathcal{I}_{k}^{k}
(page
(i)
) this means that

C
is a
(k,k)
-LEAC, and
\gamma
only features the variables

q_{n-k}
and
u
. Therefore, no variable in
\bm{q}_{k-1}
occurs in
these objects, and the condition of the
while
loop of
ElimVars
fails. The algorithm then returns

({C}[{x_{m}}],\langle{\gamma}\mathbin{;}{\psi}\rangle)
, and all requirements stated in
the induction hypothesis are trivially satisfied.

induction step:
\ell\in{[0..k-1]}
.

Let
\varphi\coloneqq\langle{\gamma}\mathbin{;}{\psi}\rangle
.
By definition of
\mathcal{I}_{k}^{\ell}
, the linear program
\gamma
features an inequality
b\cdot q_{n-\ell}\geq 0
, for some
b\geq 1
. Let
p\coloneqq\textit{mod}(q_{n-\ell},\gamma)
. Since
q_{n-\ell}
belongs to
\bm{q}_{k-1}
,
the body of the
while
loop of
ElimVars
executes. The call
to
Algorithm

4
(non-deterministically) returns an equality
a\cdot q_{n-\ell}=\tau-s
such that
(i)

a\neq 0
,
(ii)

(a\cdot q_{n-\ell}-\tau)
is either from
\textup{terms}(\gamma\land\gamma[q_{n-\ell}+p\,/\,q_{n-\ell}])
or it is computed
using
Algorithm

2
with respect to
(C,\gamma,p)
,
and
(iii)

s\in[0..\left|a\right|\cdot p-1]
. Again by definition
of
\mathcal{I}_{k}^{\ell}
, the variables occurring in
\tau
are from the vector
\bm{q}_{[\ell+1,k]}=(q_{n-k},\dots,q_{n-(\ell+1)})
.

From
Corollary

2
(which concerns satisfiability)
and
Proposition

3

and
Lemma

9
(which concern optimization) we
conclude that if
\varphi
has a solution (analogously,
if
({C}[{x_{m}}],\varphi)
has a maximum), then it has
one satisfying an equality
a\cdot q_{n-\ell}=\tau-s
returned
by
Algorithm

4
.
The lemma is therefore implied by the induction hypothesis (applied to elements in
\mathcal{I}_{k}^{\ell+1}
) together with the following claim (below, for brevity we write
\rho
instead of
\tau-s
):

Claim 7.

Given in input
({C}[{x_{m}}],\varphi)
and
an equality
a\cdot q_{n-\ell}=\rho

computed by
Algorithm

4
,

Algorithm

5
behaves as follows:

1.

If the statements in the
assert
commands in lines
5
and
14
are not satisfied, then the algorithm rejects.
In this case the formula
\exists q_{n-\ell}:\varphi\land(a\cdot q_{n-\ell}=\rho)
is unsatisfiable.

2.

Else,
the algorithm
returns
({C^{\prime}}[{x_{m}}],\langle{\gamma^{\prime}}\mathbin{;}{\psi}\rangle)

such that
(C^{\prime},\langle{\gamma^{\prime}}\mathbin{;}{\psi}\rangle)\in\mathcal{I}_{k}^{\ell+1}
.
In this case:

(a)

The formula
\langle{\gamma^{\prime}}\mathbin{;}{\psi}\rangle

is equivalent to
\exists q_{n-\ell}:\varphi\land(a\cdot q_{n-\ell}=\rho)
.

(b)

Consider a solution
\nu\colon X\setminus\{q_{n-\ell}\}\to\mathbb{N}
to
\langle{\gamma^{\prime}}\mathbin{;}{\psi}\rangle
,
and let
\nu^{\prime}\coloneqq\nu+[q_{n-\ell}\mapsto\frac{\nu(\rho)}{a}]
. For each
i\in[0..\ell-1]
, given the assignments
q_{n-i}\leftarrow\frac{\tau_{n-i}}{\eta_{C}}
and
q_{n-i}\leftarrow\frac{\tau_{n-i}}{\eta_{C^{\prime}}}
from
C
and
C^{\prime}
, respectively,
we have
\frac{\nu^{\prime}(\tau_{n-i})}{\eta_{C}}=\frac{\nu(\tau_{n-i}^{\prime})}{\eta_{C^{\prime}}}
. Moreover,
{C^{\prime}}[{x_{m}}](\nu)={C}[{x_{m}}](\nu^{\prime})
.

Let us prove
7
.
Observe that line
2

rewrites the equality
a\cdot q_{n-\ell}=\rho

and
-a\cdot q_{n-\ell}=-\rho
whenever
a<0
, hence forcing the coefficient of
q_{n-\ell}
to be positive.
Hence, in what follows we assume without loss of generality that
a>0
(hence
\alpha=\frac{a}{\mu_{C}}
in line
3
is positive).

Let us consider
Item

1
of the claim. Assume that one of the
assert
commands in lines
5
and
14

is not satisfied. In the case of line
5
,
this means that an equation
\tau^{\prime}=0
from

\gamma[\frac{\rho}{\alpha}\,/\,\mu_{C}\cdot q_{n-\ell}]

is such that the constant
c
of
\tau^{\prime}
is not divisible by
\lambda
.
Since
\lambda=\frac{\eta_{C}}{\mu_{C}}
,
assuming
6
,

\tau^{\prime}=0
can be written as
\lambda\cdot\tau^{\prime\prime}+c=0
.
But then, whenever the variables in
\tau^{\prime\prime}
are evaluated to some integers, this equation is asserting that a multiple of
\lambda
is equal to
-c
; contradicting the fact that
c
is not divisible by
\lambda
. This implies that
\gamma[\frac{\rho}{\alpha}\,/\,\mu_{C}\cdot q_{n-\ell}]
is unsatisfiable, and thus so is
\exists q_{n-\ell}:\varphi\land(a\cdot q_{n-\ell}=\rho)
.
The argument is similar when the
assert

command of line
14

is not satisfied. Indeed, consider
i\in[0..\ell-1]

such that
q_{n-i}\leftarrow\frac{\tau_{n-i}}{\eta_{C}}
occurs in
C
,
and the constant
of the term
\tau_{n-i}[\frac{\tau}{\alpha}\,/\,\mu_{C}\cdot q_{n-\ell}]

is not divisible by
\lambda
.
From the definition of
\mathcal{I}_{k}^{\ell}
(more precisely, the definition of
\Psi(C)
),
\varphi
implies
\exists q_{n-i}:\eta_{C}\cdot q_{n-i}=\tau_{n-i}
,
which in turn means that

\exists q_{n-\ell}:\varphi\land(a\cdot q_{n-\ell}=\rho)

implies
\exists q_{n-i}:\alpha\cdot\eta_{C}\cdot q_{n-i}=\tau_{n-i}[\frac{\tau}{\alpha}\,/\,\mu_{C}\cdot q_{n-\ell}]
.
By definition,
\lambda
is a divisor of
\eta_{C}
.
Hence, assuming
6
,
in the equality
\alpha\cdot\eta_{C}\cdot q_{n-i}=\tau_{n-i}[\frac{\tau}{\alpha}\,/\,\mu_{C}\cdot q_{n-\ell}]
all variable coefficients are divisible by
\lambda=\frac{\eta_{C}}{\mu_{C}}
, but the constant term is not.
As in the previous case, this means that
\alpha\cdot\eta_{C}\cdot q_{n-i}=\tau_{n-i}[\frac{\tau}{\alpha}\,/\,\mu_{C}\cdot q_{n-\ell}]

is unsatisfiable, and thus so is
\exists q_{n-\ell}:\varphi\land(a\cdot q_{n-\ell}=\rho)
.
This completes the proof of the first of the two items in
7
.

We move to
Item

2
. Assume that the
assert
commands in lines
5
and
14
are satisfied,
and therefore that the algorithm returns some pair
{({C^{\prime}}[{x_{m}}],\langle{\gamma^{\prime}}\mathbin{;}{\psi}\rangle)}
.
We first show that
\langle{\gamma^{\prime}}\mathbin{;}{\psi}\rangle

is equivalent to
\exists q_{n-\ell}:\varphi\land(a\cdot q_{n-\ell}=\rho)
.
From
(C,\langle{\gamma}\mathbin{;}{\psi}\rangle)\in\mathcal{I}_{k}^{\ell}
,
in all (in)equality from
\gamma
the coefficients of
q_{n-\ell}
are divisible by
\mu_{C}
.
This means that the substitution
[\frac{\tau}{\alpha}\,/\,\mu_{C}\cdot q_{n-\ell}]
performed in line
4

eliminates
q_{n-\ell}
.
Therefore, the formula
\gamma[\frac{\tau}{\alpha}\,/\,\mu_{C}\cdot q_{n-\ell}]\land(a\mathrel{|}\tau)
is a linear program with divisions
over the variables
\bm{q}_{[\ell+1,k]}
and
u
.
Clearly, this formula is equivalent to
\exists q_{n-\ell}:\gamma\land(a\cdot q_{n-\ell}=\rho)
, proving
Item

2a
.
Since
\psi
does not contain the variable
q_{n-\ell}
,
it thus suffices to show that transformation in lines
5
–
9
,
which produces
\gamma^{\prime}
from
\gamma[\frac{\tau}{\alpha}\,/\,\mu_{C}\cdot q_{n-\ell}]\land(a\mathrel{|}\tau)
, preserves formula equivalence.
These lines rely on the following two equivalences, that hold for any linear term
\tau^{\prime}

(since such terms evaluate to integers):

\displaystyle\lambda\cdot\tau^{\prime}=0

\displaystyle\iff\tau^{\prime}=0

(since
\lambda\neq 0
)

\displaystyle\lambda\cdot\tau^{\prime}+c\leq 0

\displaystyle\iff\tau^{\prime}+\left\lceil\frac{c}{\lambda}\right\rceil\leq 0

(since
\lambda\geq 1
)

To ensure that lines
5
–
9
correctly implement these equivalences, we need to verify that all divisions performed in these lines are without remainder.
Assuming
6
,
the coefficients of the variables
\bm{q}_{[\ell+1,k]}
in
(in)equalities of
\gamma[\frac{\tau}{\alpha}\,/\,\mu_{C}\cdot q_{n-\ell}]
are divisible by
\eta_{C}
,
while the coefficients of
u
are divisible by
\lambda=\frac{\eta_{C}}{\mu_{C}}
.
Since the
assert
command of line
5
is satisfied,
the constants occurring in equalities are also divisible by
\lambda
.
It follows that the divisions performed in lines
7
and
9
are without remainder.
(Note that this also shows that
\gamma^{\prime}
is a linear program with divisions in variables
\bm{q}_{[\ell+1,k]}
and
u
.)

Next, we show that the returned pair
{({C^{\prime}}[{x_{m}}],\langle{\gamma^{\prime}}\mathbin{;}{\psi}\rangle)}
is such that
(C^{\prime},\langle{\gamma^{\prime}}\mathbin{;}{\psi}\rangle)\in\mathcal{I}_{k}^{\ell+1}
.
Recall that, by
Lemma

13
, the equality
a\cdot q_{n-\ell}=\rho
computed by
Algorithm

4
is such that
a\cdot q_{n-\ell}-\rho
is a linear term featuring variables
u
and
\bm{q}_{[\ell,k]}
, in which the coefficients of the variables
\bm{q}_{[\ell,k]}
divisible by
\mu_{C}
.
Below,
Items

(i)
,
(ii)
and
(iii)
refers to the
items characerizing
\mathcal{I}_{k}^{\ell}
(page
(i)
).

•

Item

(i)
:
we must prove that
C^{\prime}

is a
(k,\ell+1)
-LEAC such that
\mu_{C^{\prime}}
divides
\eta_{C^{\prime}}
,
as well as all coefficients of the variables
\bm{q}_{[\ell+1,k]}
occurring in the term
\tau_{n-i}^{\prime}

featured in assignments
q_{n-i}\leftarrow\frac{\tau_{n-i}^{\prime}}{\eta_{C^{\prime}}}
of
C^{\prime}
, with
i\in[0..\ell]
.

The circuit
C^{\prime}
is constructed in line
12
.
This line
updates all assignments
q_{n-i}\leftarrow\frac{\tau_{n-i}}{\eta_{C}}
in
C
, where
i\in[0..\ell-1]
,
and prepends the assignment
q_{n-\ell}\leftarrow\frac{\tau}{a}
.
The denominator of all these assignments is
\eta_{C^{\prime}}=a
, which is divisible by
\mu_{C^{\prime}}=\mu_{C}
.
Recall that, from
(C,\langle{\gamma}\mathbin{;}{\psi}\rangle)\in\mathcal{I}_{k}^{\ell}
, the term
\tau_{n-i}

is a linear term in variables
\bm{q}_{[\ell,k]}
and
u
,
in which the coefficient of the variable

is divisible by
\mu_{C}
.
From

, we conclude that
\tau_{n-i}[\frac{\tau}{\alpha}\,/\,\mu_{C}\cdot q_{n-\ell}]

is a linear term in variables
\bm{q}_{[\ell+1,k]}
and

.
Therefore,
C^{\prime}
is a
(k,\ell+1)
-LEAC.
Lastly, assuming
6
,
the coefficients of the variables
\bm{q}_{[\ell+1,k]}
in the term
\tau_{n-i}[\frac{\tau}{\alpha}\,/\,\mu_{C}\cdot q_{n-\ell}]
are divisible by
\eta_{C}
,
and the coefficient of

is divisible by
\lambda=\frac{\eta_{C}}{\mu_{C}}
.
Since the
assert
command of line

is satisfied,
the constant of this term is also divisible by
\lambda
.
We conclude that the divisions by
\lambda

performed to compute
\tau_{n-i}^{\prime}
are without remainder,
and that in this term the coefficients of the variables in
\bm{q}_{[\ell+1,k]}
are divisible by
\mu_{C^{\prime}}
.

•

Item

(ii)
:

We have already shown that
\gamma^{\prime}
is a linear program with divisions in variables
\bm{q}_{[\ell+1,k]}
and
u
.
It thus suffices to show that
\gamma^{\prime}
satisfies the following properties:
(i)
every coefficient of the variables in
\bm{q}_{[\ell+1,k]}
is divisible by
\mu_{C^{\prime}}
, and
(ii)
for each
q
in
\bm{q}_{[\ell+1,k]}
,
\gamma^{\prime}
contains an inequality of the form
a\cdot q\geq 0
for some
a\geq 1
.

For the first property,
let us go back to the fact that the coefficients of the variables
\bm{q}_{[\ell+1,k]}
in
(in)equalities of
\gamma[\frac{\tau}{\alpha}\,/\,\mu_{C}\cdot q_{n-\ell}]
are divisible by
\eta_{C}
(
6
).
Following the (remainder-less) divisions by
\lambda=\frac{\eta_{C}}{\mu_{C}}
performed in lines
7
and
9
,
we conclude that in all (in)equalities of
\gamma^{\prime}
the coefficients of the variables in
\bm{q}_{[\ell+1,k]}
are divisible by
\mu_{C^{\prime}}=\mu_{C}
.

For the second property, recall that
for every
q
in
\bm{q}_{[\ell+1,k]}
,
\gamma
features an inequality of the form
b\cdot q\geq 0
for some
b\geq 1
.
In
\gamma^{\prime}
this inequality is transformed into
\frac{\alpha\cdot b}{\lambda}\cdot q\geq 0
,
where
\frac{\alpha\cdot b}{\lambda}
is positive and, assuming
6
,
an integer.

•

Item

(iii)
:

For brevity, let
C=(y_{1}\leftarrow\rho_{1},\dots,y_{t}\leftarrow\rho_{t})

and
C^{\prime}={(y_{0}\leftarrow\rho_{0}^{\prime},\dots,y_{t}\leftarrow\rho_{t}^{\prime})}
,
where
y_{0}\leftarrow\rho_{0}^{\prime}
is an alias for the assignment
q_{n-\ell}\leftarrow\frac{\rho}{a}
that the algorithm prepend to
C
in line
12
, in order to define
C^{\prime}
.
We must show that
\langle{\gamma^{\prime}}\mathbin{;}{\psi}\rangle
implies
\Psi(C^{\prime})
, that is,

\displaystyle\bm{0}\leq\bm{r}_{k}<2^{x_{n-k-1}}\land\exists\bm{q}_{[0,\ell]}\Big(\bm{0}\leq\bm{q}_{k}\cdot 2^{x_{n-k-1}}+\bm{r}_{k}<2^{x_{n-k}}\land\exists\bm{x}_{k-1}\big(\theta\land\textstyle\bigwedge_{i=0}^{t}(y_{i}^{\prime}=\rho_{i}^{\prime})\big)\Big).

We have already established that
\langle{\gamma^{\prime}}\mathbin{;}{\psi}\rangle

is equivalent to
\exists q_{n-\ell}:\varphi\land(a\cdot q_{n-\ell}=\rho)
.
Since
\varphi
implies
\Psi(C)
, we then conclude that
\langle{\gamma^{\prime}}\mathbin{;}{\psi}\rangle

implies

\displaystyle\bm{0}\leq\bm{r}_{k}<2^{x_{n-k-1}}\land\exists\bm{q}_{[0,\ell]}\Big(

\displaystyle\bm{0}\leq\bm{q}_{k}\cdot 2^{x_{n-k-1}}+\bm{r}_{k}<2^{x_{n-k}}

\displaystyle{}\land\exists\bm{x}_{k-1}\big(\theta\land\textstyle\bigwedge_{i=1}^{t}(y_{i}=\rho_{i})\land(a\cdot q_{n-\ell}=\rho)\big)\Big).

Hence, as line
12
does not modify the assignments in
C

that feature variables in
\bm{x}_{k-1}
,
to conclude that
\langle{\gamma^{\prime}}\mathbin{;}{\psi}\rangle
implies
\Psi(C^{\prime})

it suffices to show that, for every
i\in[0..\ell-1]
,

a\cdot q_{n-\ell}=\rho\implies\big(\eta_{C}\cdot q_{n-i}=\tau_{n-i}\iff a\cdot q_{n-i}=\tau_{n-i}^{\prime}\big),

(16)

where
\tau_{n-i}
is the term such that
q_{n-i}\leftarrow\frac{\tau_{n-i}}{\eta_{C}}
occurs in
C
, and
\tau_{n-i}^{\prime}
is the term
such that
q_{n-i}\leftarrow\frac{\tau_{n-i}^{\prime}}{a}
occurs in
C^{\prime}
.
Recall that
\alpha=\frac{a}{\mu_{C}}\geq 1
,
\lambda=\frac{\eta_{C}}{\mu_{C}}\geq 1
, and that all divisions performed in line
12
are without remainder. Then,

Equation

16
follows from the equivalences below:

\displaystyle(\eta_{C}\cdot q_{n-i}-\tau_{n-i})[{\textstyle\frac{\rho}{\alpha}}\,/\,\mu_{C}\cdot q_{n-\ell}]

\displaystyle={}

\displaystyle\alpha\cdot\eta_{C}\cdot q_{n-i}-(\tau_{n-i}[{\textstyle\frac{\rho}{\alpha}}\,/\,\mu_{C}\cdot q_{n-\ell}])

\displaystyle\Lbag\text{by def.~of substitution}\Rbag

\displaystyle={}

\displaystyle\frac{\alpha\cdot\eta_{C}}{\lambda}\cdot q_{n-i}-\tau_{n-i}^{\prime}

\displaystyle\Lbag\text{division by $\lambda$}\Rbag

\displaystyle={}

\displaystyle a\cdot q_{n-i}-\tau_{n-i}^{\prime}

\displaystyle\Lbag\text{from $\textstyle\alpha=\frac{a}{\mu_{C}}$, $\textstyle\lambda=\frac{\eta_{C}}{\mu_{C}}$}\Rbag

This concludes the proof that
(C^{\prime},\langle{\gamma^{\prime}}\mathbin{;}{\psi}\rangle)
belongs to
\mathcal{I}_{k}^{\ell+1}
. To conclude the
proof
7
, it remains to show
Item

2b
.
Consider a solution
\nu\colon X\setminus\{q_{n-\ell}\}\to\mathbb{N}
to
\langle{\gamma^{\prime}}\mathbin{;}{\psi}\rangle
,
and let
\nu^{\prime}\coloneqq\nu+[q_{n-\ell}\mapsto\frac{\nu(\rho)}{a}]
.
Since
\langle{\gamma^{\prime}}\mathbin{;}{\psi}\rangle

is equivalent to
{\exists q_{n-\ell}:\varphi\land(a\cdot q_{n-\ell}=\rho)}
, it follows that
\nu^{\prime}
is a valid assignment of variables into
\mathbb{N}
.
By definition, for each
i\in[0..\ell-1]
,
evaluating
C^{\prime}
on
\nu
assigns to the variable
q_{n-i}
the (non-negative) integer
\frac{\nu(\tau_{n-i}^{\prime})}{a}
.
Directly from
Equation

16
, we have
\frac{\nu(\tau_{n-i}^{\prime})}{a}=\frac{\nu^{\prime}(\tau_{n-i})}{\eta_{C}}
. Moreover, since
C
and
C^{\prime}
agree on all the assignments to all variables in
\bm{x}_{k-1}
, we conclude that

{C}[{x_{m}}](\nu)={C}[{x_{m}}](\nu^{\prime})
.
∎

5.3
A variation of Bareiss algorithm

We now introduce our variation of Bareiss algorithm.
As already stated, Bareiss algorithm is commonly used to
calculate the echelon form of a matrix with integer entries.
For a formal definition of echelon form, we refer the reader to standard linear algebra textbooks (e.g.,
[
HK71
]
). However, below we do not rely on this definition, as we characterize every single entry that the manipulated matrix has throughout the procedure, in terms of the original ones.

Some notation.

Consider a
m\times d
integer matrix
B_{0}
:

B_{0}\coloneqq\begin{pmatrix}b_{1,1}&\ldots&b_{1,d}\\
\vdots&\ddots&\vdots\\
b_{m,1}&\ldots&b_{m,d}\end{pmatrix}.

Let
\ell\in[0..\min(m,d)]
.
We write
b^{(\ell)}_{i,j}
(with
(i,j)\in[1..m]\times[1..d]
) and
b^{(\ell)}_{r\leftarrow j}
(with
(r,j)\in[1..\ell]\times[1..d]
)
to denote the following sub-determinants of
B_{0}
:

b_{i,j}^{(\ell)}\coloneqq\det\begin{pmatrix}b_{1,1}&\ldots&b_{1,\ell}&b_{1,j}\\
\vdots&\ddots&\vdots&\vdots\\
b_{\ell,1}&\ldots&b_{\ell,\ell}&b_{\ell,j}\\
b_{i,1}&\ldots&b_{i,\ell}&b_{i,j}\end{pmatrix},\qquad b_{r\leftarrow j}^{(\ell)}\coloneqq\det\begin{pmatrix}b_{1,1}&\ldots&b_{1,r-1}&b_{1,j}&b_{1,r+1}&\dots&b_{1,\ell}\\
\vdots&\ddots&\vdots&\vdots&\vdots&\ddots&\vdots\\
b_{\ell,1}&\ldots&b_{\ell,r-1}&b_{\ell,j}&b_{\ell,r+1}&\dots&b_{\ell,\ell}\end{pmatrix}.

Let us fix
k\in[0..\min(m,d)]
(this quantity corresponds to the number of iterations the algorithm will perform).
For every
\ell\in[1..k]
,
we define
\lambda_{0}\coloneqq 1
and
{\lambda_{\ell}\coloneqq b_{\ell,\ell}^{(\ell-1)}}
.
(This notation is chosen intentionally: we will later show that
\left|\lambda_{\ell}\right|
corresponds to the integer
\lambda
computed by
Algorithms

4
and
5
when applied to inputs featuring pairs from
\mathcal{I}_{k}^{\ell}
.)
Throughout this section, we assume that every
\lambda_{\ell}
is non-zero.

Let us moreover fix a positive integer
\mu
(we will later set
\mu
to the integer
\mu_{C}
of the LEAC
C
in input of
Algorithms

4
and
5
),
fix in
g\in[k..d]
(an index of a column in the
B_{0}
),
and consider the diagonal matrix
U_{g}\coloneqq\mathrm{diag}(\mu,\dots,\mu,1,\dots,1)

having
\mu
in all positions
(i,i)
with
i\in[1..g]
, and
1
in positions
(i,i)
with
i\in[g+1..d]
.

The algorithm.

The input matrix is of the form

B_{0}^{\prime}\ \coloneqq\ B_{0}\cdot U_{g}\ =\ \begin{pmatrix}\mu\cdot b_{1,1}&\ldots&\mu\cdot b_{1,g}&b_{1,g+1}&\dots&b_{1,d}\\
\vdots&\ddots&\vdots\\
\mu\cdot b_{m,1}&\ldots&\mu\cdot b_{m,g}&b_{m,g+1}&\dots&b_{m,d}\end{pmatrix}.

(17)

The algorithm iteratively constructs a sequence of matrices
B_{1}^{\prime},\dots,B_{k}^{\prime}
as follows.
Consider
{\ell\in[0..k-1]}
, and let
B_{\ell}^{\prime}
be the matrix

B_{\ell}^{\prime}\coloneqq\begin{pmatrix}h_{1,1}&\ldots&h_{1,d}\\
\vdots&\ddots&\vdots\\
h_{m,1}&\ldots&h_{m,d}\end{pmatrix}.

The matrix
B_{\ell+1}^{\prime}
is constructed from
B_{\ell}^{\prime}

by applying the following transformation

01:

let

\pm
be the sign of
h_{\ell+1,\ell+1}
, and
\alpha\coloneqq\frac{\pm h_{\ell+1,\ell+1}}{\mu}

\triangleright

this division is without remainder

02:

multiply the row
\ell+1
of
B_{\ell}
by
\pm 1

03:

for
every row
i
except row
\ell+1

do

04:

let

\beta\coloneqq\frac{h_{i,\ell+1}}{\mu}

\triangleright

this division is without remainder

05:

multiply the
i
th row of
B_{\ell}^{\prime}
by
\alpha

\triangleright

B_{\ell}^{\prime}(i,\ell+1)
is now
\alpha\cdot g_{i,\ell+1}

06:

subtract
\pm\beta\cdot(h_{\ell+1,1},\dots,h_{\ell+1,d})
from the
i
th row of
B_{\ell}^{\prime}

07:

divide each entry of the
i
th row of
B_{\ell}^{\prime}
by
\left|\lambda_{\ell}\right|

\triangleright

these divisions are without remainder

Characterization of the entries of the matrices.

The following three lemmas fully characterize all entries in the matrices
B_{1}^{\prime},\dots,B_{k}^{\prime}
in terms of the entries of
B_{0}^{\prime}
.
Their proofs are given in
Section

C.4
,
after introducing the necessary background on the (classical) Bareiss algorithm.

Lemma 15.
†
†
margin:

Proof in page
C.4

For all
\ell\in[0..k-1]
,
the
(\ell+1)
th row of
B_{\ell+1}^{\prime}
is obtained by multiplying the
(\ell+1)
th
row of
B_{\ell}^{\prime}
by the sign of its
(\ell+1)
th entry.

Lemma 16.
†
†
margin:

Proof in page
C.4

Consider
\ell\in[0..k]
and
i\in[\ell+1..m]
, and let
\pm
be the sign of
\lambda_{\ell}
. Then:

1.

For every
j\in[1..g]
,
the entry in position
(i,j)
of the matrix
B_{\ell}^{\prime}
is

\pm\mu\cdot b^{(\ell)}_{i,j}
.

(In particular, this entry is zero whenever
j\leq\ell
.)

2.

For every
j\in[g+1..d]
,
the entry in position
(i,j)
of the matrix
B_{\ell}^{\prime}
is

\pm b^{(\ell)}_{i,j}
.

Lemma 17.
†
†
margin:

Proof in page
C.4

Consider
\ell\in[1..k]

and
i\in[1..\ell]
, and let
\pm
be the sign of
\lambda_{\ell}
. Then:

1.

For every
j\in[1..g]
,
the entry in position
(i,j)
of
B_{\ell}^{\prime}
is

\pm\mu\cdot b^{(\ell)}_{i\leftarrow j}
.

(In particular,
this entry is zero if
j\leq\ell
and
i\neq j
,
and it is instead
\pm\mu\cdot b^{(\ell-1)}_{\ell,\ell}
when
i=j
.)

2.

For every
j\in[g+1..d]
,
the entry in position
(i,j)
of
B_{\ell}^{\prime}
is

\pm b^{(\ell)}_{i\leftarrow j}
.

The next lemma provides an alternative algorithm to reconstruct the matrix
B_{\ell}^{\prime}
starting from its first
\ell
rows and from
B_{0}^{\prime}
, without constructing
B_{1}^{\prime},\dots,B_{\ell-1}^{\prime}
. In the next section, this lemma will turn out useful when analyzing the terms computed by
Algorithm

2
.

Lemma 18.
†
†
margin:

Proof in page
C.4

Let
\ell\in[0..k]
and
i\in[\ell+1..m]
.
Consider the following transformation applied to
B_{0}^{\prime}
:

1:

multiply the
i
th row of
B_{0}^{\prime}
by
\left|\lambda_{\ell}\right|

2:

for

r
in
[1..\ell]

do

subtract
b_{i,r}\cdot\bm{u}_{r}
to the
i
th row of
B_{0}^{\prime}
, where
\bm{u}_{r}
is the
r
th row of
B_{\ell}^{\prime}

After the transformation, the
i
th rows of
B_{0}^{\prime}
and
B_{\ell}^{\prime}
are equal.

5.4
How coefficients evolve as
ElimVars
executes, and proof of
6

We now prove that the evolution of the variable coefficients during
ElimVars
mirrors that of the matrix entries in our variation of Bareiss algorithm.
(For brevity, in this section by Bareiss algorithm we always mean this variation.)
This is done by setting up a sequence of matrices
M_{0},M_{1},\dots
, where
M_{\ell}
snapshots
the coefficients of the variables
\bm{q}_{k}
and
u
in the (in)equalities of
\gamma
or in the terms of the circuit
C
, after
\ell
iterations the
while
loop in
ElimVars
. We then show that
M_{\ell}
can alternatively be obtained from
M_{0}
by performing
\ell
iterations of Bareiss algorithm. In doing so, we also establish
6
.

Throughout the section, let

(\bm{q}_{k-1},{C}[{x_{m}}],\langle{\gamma}\mathbin{;}{\psi}\rangle)
be the triple in input to
ElimVars
,
with
{(C,\langle{\gamma}\mathbin{;}{\psi}\rangle)\in\mathcal{I}_{k}^{0}}
, for some
k\in[0..n-1]
.
Also, let
\mu\coloneqq\mu_{C}
.
Since
Algorithm

5
does not modify the formula
\psi
,
each non-deterministic branch of
ElimVars
can be identified with a sequence of pairs

(C,\gamma)=(C_{0},\gamma_{0})\xrightarrow{e_{0}}(C_{1},\gamma_{1})\xrightarrow{e_{1}}(C_{2},\gamma_{2})\dots\xrightarrow{e_{j-1}}(C_{j},\gamma_{j})\xrightarrow{e_{j}}\dots

(18)

where
e_{\ell}
is the equality computed by
Algorithm

4
during the
(\ell+1)
th iteration of
ElimVars
, and
C_{\ell}
and
\gamma_{\ell}
are the circuit
C
and formula
\gamma
at the completion of the
\ell
th iteration of
ElimVars
, respectively.
At this point, the length of this sequence is unknown.
It might be short (e.g., if one of the
assert
commands of
Algorithm

5
fails and the algorithm rejects), or even infinite —we are not assuming
6
, and thus cannot guarantee that variables are eliminated correctly.
Nonetheless, we will prove that in non-rejecting runs, each iteration of the
while
loop in
ElimVars
eliminates one of the variables
\bm{q}_{k-1}
.
Because of this, we truncate the above sequence to some
j\in[0..k]
, and focus our analysis on this finite prefix.

Towards defining the matrices.

Let us fix an enumeration

(\rho_{1}\sim_{1}0),(\rho_{2}\sim_{2}0),\,\dots\,,\,(\rho_{t}\sim_{t}0)

(19)

of all the equalities and inequalities of
\gamma_{0}
(that is, each
\sim_{i}
belongs to
\{=,\leq\}
). Observe that
Algorithm

5
constructs
\gamma_{\ell+1}
from
\gamma_{\ell}
by simply applying a substitution (line
4
), adding a divisibility constraint (again line
4
), and performing some integer divisions. Since substitutions are applied locally to each constraints,
this implies not only that the number of (in)equalities in
\gamma_{0},\gamma_{1},\dots,\gamma_{j}

does not change, but that in fact there is a one-to-one mapping between
(in)equalities of
\gamma_{0}
and those in
\gamma_{\ell}
. That is, there is an enumeration of the (in)equalities of
\gamma_{\ell}
such that the
i
th element of the enumeration
is the inequality obtained from
\rho_{i}\sim_{i}0
by applying all the substitutions and divisions performed by
Algorithm

5
during the first
\ell
iterations of
ElimVars
.
We will denote such a one-to-one mapping, from (in)equalities of
\gamma_{0}
to those in
\gamma_{\ell}
, as
\Lambda_{\ell}
(
\Lambda_{0}
is the identity).

Let us now look at the equality
e_{\ell}
(with
\ell\in[0..j-1]
).
Following
Algorithm

4
, this equality is of the form
a\cdot q=\tau-s
, where

s
is the shift introduced in line
5
of
Algorithm

4
, and

a\cdot q-\tau
is a term of one of the following two types:

I.

A term from
\textup{terms}(\gamma_{\ell}\land\gamma_{\ell}[q+p\,/\,q])
.
Note that the substitution
[q+p\,/\,q]
affects only the constants of (in)equalities. Consequently, there is an (in)equality
\rho^{\prime}\sim 0
in
\gamma_{\ell}
where the term
\rho
has the same
variable coefficients as
a\cdot q-\tau
.
In this case, we define the
generator

g_{\ell}

of

e_{\ell}

to be the (in)equality
\rho\sim 0
of
\gamma_{0}
such that
\Lambda_{\ell}(\rho\sim 0)=(\rho^{\prime}\sim 0)
.

II.

A term computed using
Algorithm

2
with respect to
(C_{\ell},\gamma_{\ell},p)
.
This is a term obtained by simultaneously applying two substitutions
to a term
\rho
of the form
{a\cdot u+\mu_{C_{\ell}}\cdot(q^{\prime}-q^{\prime\prime})+d}
, with
a,d\in\mathbb{Z}
and
q^{\prime},q^{\prime\prime}
from
\bm{q}_{k-1}
.
We define the
generator

g_{\ell}

of

e_{\ell}
to be the equality
\rho=0
.

We say that
e_{\ell}
is of
Type
I.
or
Type
II.
, depending on which of the two cases above it falls under.

The matrix associated to
\gamma_{0}
.

Each of the matrices
M_{0},\dots,M_{j}
we define have
j+t
rows (where
t
is the number of equalities and inequalities in
\gamma_{0}
) and
k+2
columns. For every
i\in[0..k]
, the
(i+1)
th column contains coefficients of the variable
q_{n-i}
. The
(k+2)
th column contains coefficients of
u
.

\begin{array}[]{ccccccl}q_{n}&q_{n-1}&\dots&q_{n-k+1}&q_{n-k}&u\\[5.0pt]
\ \mu\cdot b_{1,1}&\mu\cdot b_{1,2}&\dots&\mu\cdot b_{1,k}&\mu\cdot b_{1,k+1}&b_{1,k+2}&\text{coefficients of $g_{0}$}\\[4.0pt]
\ \mu\cdot b_{2,1}&\mu\cdot b_{2,2}&\dots&\mu\cdot b_{2,k}&\mu\cdot b_{2,k+1}&b_{2,k+2}&\text{coefficients in $g_{1}$}\\[4.0pt]
\vdots\\[4.0pt]
\ \mu\cdot b_{j,1}&\mu\cdot b_{j,2}&\dots&\mu\cdot b_{j,k}&\mu\cdot b_{j,k+1}&b_{j,k+2}&\text{coefficients in $g_{j-1}$}\\[4.0pt]
\ \mu\cdot b_{j+1,1}&\mu\cdot b_{j+1,2}&\dots&\mu\cdot b_{j+1,k}&\mu\cdot b_{j+1,k+1}&b_{j+1,k+2}&\text{coefficients in $\rho_{1}$}\\[4.0pt]
\vdots\\[4.0pt]
\ \mu\cdot b_{j+t,1}&\mu\cdot b_{j,2}&\dots&\mu\cdot b_{j+t,k}&\mu\cdot b_{j+t,k+1}&b_{j+t,k+2}&\text{coefficients in $\rho_{t}$}\\[4.0pt]
\end{array}$

Figure 2:
Structure of the matrix
M_{0}
. Observe that all coefficients of the variables in
\bm{q}_{k}
have
\mu
as a common factor.
This is because
(C,\langle{\gamma}\mathbin{;}{\psi}\rangle)
belongs to
\mathcal{I}_{k}^{0}
.

Inthematrix
M_0
,
for
i ∈[1..j]
,
the
i
throwstoresthecoefficientsofthevariables
q
_k
and
u
occurringinthegenerator
g_i-1
of
e_i-1
.
Theremaining
t
rowsstorethecoefficientsofthevariables
q
_k
and
u
occurringin
(
in
)
equalitiesof
γ
_0
:
followingtheenumerationin

Equation

19
,
the
(j+r)
throwstoresthevariablecoefficientsof
ρ
_r
.
Thestructureofthematrix
M_0
isillustratedin

Section

5.4
.

The matrices
M_{1},\dots,M_{j}
.

Let
\ell\in[1..j]
.
At the
\ell
th iteration of the
while
loop of
ElimVars
,

Algorithm

5

prepends
a single assignment
q\leftarrow\frac{\tau}{a}

to the circuit
C_{\ell-1}
(where
\pm a\cdot q=\pm\tau
is
e_{\ell-1}
, with
\pm
being the sign of
a
), and modifies all other assignments to variables from
\bm{q}_{k-1}
so that the denominator of the assigned expression becomes
a
. This means that
\eta_{C_{\ell}}=a
, which we abbreviate as
\eta_{\ell}
.
Note that then
C_{\ell}
features
\ell
assignments to variables in
\bm{q}_{k-1}
,
and the remaining assignments are the original ones from
C_{0}
, featuring the variables
\bm{x}_{k}
. In particular,
\mu_{C_{\ell}}=\mu
.
We define the matrix
M_{\ell}
as follows:

i.

For
i\in[1..\ell]
, let
q\leftarrow\frac{\tau}{\eta_{\ell}}
be the
(\ell-(i-1))
th assignment in
C_{\ell}
. The
i
th row of
M_{\ell}
contains the coefficients of the variables
\bm{q}_{k}
and
u
from the term
\eta_{\ell}\cdot q-\tau
.

ii.

For every
i\in[\ell+1..j]
, if
e_{i-1}
is of Type
I.
, then the
i
th row of
M_{\ell}
contains the coefficients of the variables
\bm{q}_{k}
and
u
occurring in the (in)equality
\Lambda_{\ell}(g_{i-1})
.

iii.

For
i\in[\ell+1..j]
, if
e_{i-1}
is of Type
II.
, then let
\rho=0
be
g_{i-1}
.
The
i
th row of
M_{\ell}
contains the coefficients of the variables
\bm{q}_{k}
and
u
from the term obtained from
\rho
as follows:

1:

multiply every integer in
\rho
by the quotient of the division of
\eta_{\ell}
by
\mu

2:

for

r
in
[1..\ell]

do

\rho\leftarrow\rho[\tau\,/\,\eta_{\ell}\cdot q]
,
where
q\leftarrow\frac{\tau}{\eta_{\ell}}
is the
r
th assignment in
C_{\ell}

(We will later see that this is in fact a simultaneous substitution.)

iv.

For every
i\in[1..t]
, the
(j+i)
th row of
M_{\ell}
contains the coefficients of the variables
\bm{q}_{k}
and
u
in the term of the (in)equality
\Lambda_{\ell}(\rho_{i}\sim_{i}0)
.

The following lemma is immediate:

Lemma 19.

Let
\ell\in[0..j]
. For every assignment
q\leftarrow\frac{\tau}{\eta_{\ell}}
in
C_{\ell}
, with
q
in
\bm{q}_{k}
,
there is a row in
M_{\ell}
whose entries encode the coefficients that
\bm{q}_{k}
and
u
have in
\eta_{\ell}\cdot q-\tau
.
Similarly, for every (in)equality
\rho\sim 0
in
\gamma_{\ell}
,
there is a row of
M_{\ell}
whose entries encode the coefficients that
\bm{q}_{k}
and
u
have in
\rho
.

Proof.

For
\ell=0
,
C_{0}
has no assignments to variables in
\bm{q}_{k}
, and
the
i
th entry in the enumeration of
Equation

19
is in row
j+i
. For
\ell\geq 1
, the lemma follows from Items (
i.
) and (
iv.
) above.
∎

Correspondence between
ElimVars
and Bareiss algorithm.

The matrix
M_{0}
has the form required to run (our) Bareiss algorithm. Let us denote by
B_{0}
the
{(j+t)\times(k+2)}
integer matrix such that
M_{0}=B_{0}\cdot\mathrm{diag}(\mu,\dots,\mu,1)
,
and by
b_{ij}
the entry of
B_{0}
in position
(i,j)
; as in
Section

5.4
.
We also use the notation
b^{(\ell)}_{i,j}
and
b^{(\ell)}_{r\leftarrow j}
to denote the sub-determinants of
B_{0}

analogous to those in
Section

5.3
, and define
\lambda_{0}\coloneqq 1
and
\lambda_{\ell}\coloneqq b^{(\ell-1)}_{\ell,\ell}
.
We write
B_{1}^{\prime},\dots,B_{j}^{\prime}
for the sequence of matrices iteratively constructed by Bareiss algorithm, starting from the matrix
{B_{0}^{\prime}\coloneqq M_{0}}
.
The next lemma establishes the key correspondence between these matrices and
M_{1},\dots,M_{j}
.

Lemma 20.

Consider
\ell\in[0..j]
. Then,
M_{\ell}=B_{\ell}^{\prime}
,
\frac{\eta_{\ell}}{\mu}=\left|\lambda_{\ell}\right|\neq 0
, and if
\ell\geq 1
, then
6
holds when
restricted to
Algorithm

5
having as input

(C_{\ell-1}[x_{m}],\langle{\gamma_{\ell-1}}\mathbin{;}{\psi}\rangle)
and the equality
e_{\ell-1}
.

Proof.

The proof is by induction on
\ell\in[0..j]
.

base case:
\ell=0
.

By definition,
M_{0}=B_{0}^{\prime}
. Since
C_{0}
is a
(k,0)
-LEAC,
\eta_{0}=\mu
, and so
\frac{\eta_{0}}{\mu}=1=\lambda_{0}
.

induction hypothesis.

For
\ell\geq 1
,
M_{\ell-1}=B_{\ell-1}^{\prime}
and

\frac{\eta_{\ell-1}}{\mu}=\left|\lambda_{\ell-1}\right|\neq 0
.
Moreover, if
\ell\geq 2
,
then
6
holds when
restricted to
Algorithm

5
having as input

(C_{\ell-2}[x_{m}],\langle{\gamma_{\ell-2}}\mathbin{;}{\psi}\rangle)

and the equality
e_{\ell-2}
.

induction step:
\ell\geq 1
.

By induction hypothesis,
6
holds
whenever
Algorithm

5
is called on the inputs

(C_{r}[x_{m}],\langle{\gamma_{r}}\mathbin{;}{\psi}\rangle)
and the equality
e_{r}
,
for every
r\in[0..\ell-2]
.
In particular, following the correctness arguments given in the proof of
Lemma

14
,
we conclude that
ElimVars
is correct for the first
\ell-1
iterations,
and
(C_{\ell-1}[x_{m}],\langle{\gamma_{r}}\mathbin{;}{\psi}\rangle)
thus belong to
\mathcal{I}_{k}^{\ell-1}
. Indeed,
to obtain correctness for the first
\ell-1
iterations, it suffices to restrict
6

to the first
\ell-1
calls of
Algorithm

5
, featuring the equalities
e_{0},\dots,e_{\ell-2}
.

Our first goal is to prove that the variable coefficients of the equality
e_{\ell-1}
are
stored in the
\ell
th row of
M_{\ell-1}
.
Note that since
(C_{\ell-1}[x_{m}],\langle{\gamma_{r}}\mathbin{;}{\psi}\rangle)\in\mathcal{I}_{k}^{\ell-1}
,

Algorithm

4
outputs an equality of the form
a\cdot q_{n-(\ell-1)}=\tau
with
a\neq 0
, and that, by definition, this equality is
e_{\ell-1}
.

Claim 8.

The
\ell
th row of
M_{\ell-1}
contains the variable coefficients of
the term
a\cdot q_{n-(\ell-1)}-\tau
.

Proof.

If
e_{\ell-1}
is of Type
I.
,
then by definition the
\ell
th row of
M_{\ell-1}
contains the coefficients of the variables
\bm{q}_{k}
and
u
occurring in the
(in)equality
\rho^{\prime}\sim 0
given by
\Lambda_{\ell-1}(g_{\ell-1})
.
(In particular, for
\ell=1
, we have
\rho^{\prime}\sim 0
equal to
g_{0}
.)
By definition of
\Lambda_{\ell-1}
, the terms
\rho^{\prime}
and
a\cdot q_{n-(\ell-1)}-\tau
share the same variable coefficients.

If
e_{\ell-1}
is of Type
II.
,
then
g_{\ell-1}
is an equality
\rho=0
where
\rho
is of the form
{b\cdot u+\mu\cdot(q^{\prime}-q^{\prime\prime})+d}
, with
b,d\in\mathbb{Z}
and
q^{\prime},q^{\prime\prime}
from
\bm{q}_{k}
.
Moreover, inspecting
Algorithm

2
,
we see that the variable coefficients of
a\cdot q_{n-(\ell-1)}-\tau

corresponds to the ones obtained from
b\cdot u+\mu\cdot(q^{\prime}-q^{\prime\prime})+d
by simultaneously applying two substitutions
\nu_{1}
and
\nu_{2}
.
The substitution
\nu_{1}
has one of the following forms
(recall that
\frac{\eta_{\ell-1}}{\mu}=\left|\lambda_{\ell-1}\right|
, by induction hypothesis):

1.

[\frac{\eta_{\ell-1}\cdot q^{\prime}}{\left|\lambda_{\ell-1}\right|}\,/\,\mu\cdot q^{\prime}]
;
this is the case when
C_{\ell-1}
does not assign any expression to
q^{\prime}
, and the
guess
in
line
7
returns false.

2.

[\frac{\eta_{\ell-1}\cdot q^{\prime}+\eta_{\ell-1}\cdot p}{\left|\lambda_{\ell-1}\right|}\,/\,\mu\cdot q^{\prime}]
,
with
p\coloneqq\textit{mod}(q_{n-(\ell-1)},\gamma_{\ell-1})
;
this is the case when
q^{\prime}=q_{n-(\ell-1)}
,
C_{\ell-1}
does not assign any expression to
q^{\prime}
, and the
guess
in
line
7
returns true.

3.

[\frac{\tau^{\prime}}{\left|\lambda_{\ell-1}\right|}\,/\,\mu\cdot q^{\prime}]
;
in this case
C_{\ell-1}
features
q^{\prime}\leftarrow\frac{\tau^{\prime}}{\eta_{\ell-1}}
and the
guess
in
line
7
returns false.

4.

[\frac{\tau^{\prime}\sigma}{\left|\lambda_{\ell-1}\right|}\,/\,\mu\cdot q^{\prime}]

where
\sigma
is the substitution
[q_{n-(\ell-1)}+p\,/\,q_{n-(\ell-1)}]
;
in this case
C_{\ell-1}
features
q^{\prime}\leftarrow\frac{\tau^{\prime}}{\eta_{\ell-1}}
and the
guess
in
line
7
returns true.

Observe that the coefficients of
\bm{q}_{k}
and
u

are the same in
\tau^{\prime}
and
\tau^{\prime}\sigma
, and that moreover
these terms do not contain variables to which
C_{\ell-1}
assigns some expressions (because
C_{\ell-1}
is a
(k,\ell-1)
-LEAC).
We also note that the only effect that the substitutions in
Items

1
and
2
have on the variable coefficients of
\rho
is to multiply them by
\left|\lambda_{\ell-1}\right|
, because after this multiplication,
\left|\lambda_{\ell-1}\right|\cdot\mu\cdot q^{\prime}
is replaced by
\eta_{\ell-1}\cdot q^{\prime}
or
\eta_{\ell-1}\cdot q^{\prime}+\eta_{\ell-1}\cdot p
, but
\eta_{\ell-1}=\left|\lambda_{\ell-1}\right|\cdot\mu
.

An analysis similar to the one above can be performed for
\nu_{2}
(simply change
q^{\prime}
for
q^{\prime\prime}
, and line
7
for line
8
, in the items above).
From the definition of simultaneous substitution, we then conclude that
the variables coefficients of
a\cdot q_{n-(\ell-1)}-\tau

are exactly those in the term obtained from
\rho

by simultaneously applying all substitutions of the form
[\frac{\rho^{\prime}}{\left|\lambda_{\ell-1}\right|}\,/\,\mu\cdot q]
,
where
q\in\{q_{n},\dots,q_{n-(\ell-2)}\}
and
q\leftarrow\frac{\rho^{\prime}}{\eta_{\ell-1}}
is an assignment in
C_{\ell-1}
.
For
\ell=1
, this list of substitutions is empty, and indeed by definition of
M_{0}
, the
\ell
th row contains the variable coefficients of
\rho
.
For
\ell>1
, these substitutions correspond to the transformation
applied to
\rho
in Item (
iii.
) of the definition of
M_{\ell-1}
, in order to define its
\ell
th column.
The claim then holds.
∎

We now analyze
M_{\ell}
and
B_{\ell}^{\prime}
row by row, showing that the two matrices are equal. In the process, we will also prove the other statements in the lemma. Below, we write
\pm
for the sign of the coefficient
a\neq 0
in the term
a\cdot q_{n-(\ell-1)}-\tau
, and define
\alpha\coloneqq\frac{\pm a}{\mu}

(by
Lemma

13
this division is without remainder). Let us also write

\displaystyle q_{n-(\ell-2)}\leftarrow\frac{\tau_{n-(\ell-2)}(u,\bm{q}_{[\ell-1,k]})}{\eta_{\ell-1}},\quad\dots\ ,\quad q_{n}\leftarrow\frac{\tau_{n}(u,\bm{q}_{[\ell-1,k]})}{\eta_{\ell-1}}

for the sequence of all the assignments to variables in
\bm{q}_{k}
featured in
C_{\ell-1}
(this is a prefix of all the assignments in
C_{\ell-1}
, since this circuit is a
(k,\ell-1)
-LEAC).

The following sequence of Claims summarizes our analysis.
As their proofs are rather similar (each crucially relying on
Lemmas

15
,
16
,
17
and
18
)
below we only provide a detailed proof of
10
,
deferring the proofs of the remaining claims to
Section

C.6
.

Claim 9.
†
†
margin:

Proof in page

The

Moreover,

and

.

Claim 10.

Let
i\in[1..\ell-1]
. The
i

and

are equal.
Moreover, in all terms

, with

,
all coefficients of the variables

are divisible by

, and all coefficients of

are divisible by

.

Claim 11.
†
†
margin:

Proof in page

Let
i\in[j+1..j+t]
. The
i
th rows of
M_{\ell}
and
B_{\ell}^{\prime}
are equal.
Moreover, in all equalities and inequalities
{\gamma_{\ell-1}[\frac{\pm\tau}{\alpha}\,/\,\mu\cdot q_{n-{\ell-1}}]}
,
all coefficients of the variables
\bm{q}_{[\ell,k]}
are divisible by
\eta_{\ell-1}
, and all coefficients of

are divisible by

.

Claim 12.
†
†
margin:

Proof in page
C.6

Let
i\in[\ell+1..j]
. The
i
th rows of
M_{\ell}
and
B_{\ell}^{\prime}
are equal.

Proof of
10
.

By definition (Item (
i.
)),
the matrix
M_{\ell}
includes, in these rows,
the variable coefficients of the assignments in
C_{\ell}
, ranging from the
(\ell-1)
th assignment to the
2
nd one (in this reverse order). The corresponding rows in
M_{\ell-1}
contain the variable coefficients of the terms
{(\eta_{\ell-1}\cdot q_{n}-\tau_{n})}
to

{(\eta_{\ell-1}\cdot q_{n-(\ell-2)}-\tau_{n-(\ell-2)})}
, which arise from the assignments in
C_{\ell-1}
.
Let
i\in[1..\ell-1]
.
Since line
14
of
Algorithm

5

prepends one assignment to
C_{\ell-1}
and only updates the rest, the
i
th row of
M_{\ell}
corresponds precisely
to the term obtained by running line
14

on the assignment
q_{n-(i-1)}\leftarrow\frac{\tau_{n-(i-1)}}{\eta_{\ell-1}}
. We will analyze this update below.
At the same time, since the variable coefficients of this assignment are stored in the
i
th row of
B_{\ell-1}^{\prime}
, we can alternatively track how Bareiss algorithm updates this row when computing
B_{\ell}^{\prime}
from
B_{\ell-1}^{\prime}
.
We will then deduce that the
i
th rows of
M_{\ell}
and
B_{\ell}^{\prime}
are equal.

Let us examine how line
14
updates
the assignment
q_{n-(i-1)}\leftarrow\frac{\tau_{n-(i-1)}}{\eta_{\ell-1}}
. Let us write
\tau_{n-(i-1)}
as
\beta\cdot\mu\cdot q_{n-(\ell-1)}+\tau^{\prime}
.
Line
14
first constructs the term
\tau_{n-(i-1)}[\frac{\pm\tau}{\alpha}\,/\,\mu\cdot q_{n-(\ell-1)}]
. The substitution multiplies
\tau_{n-(i-1)}
by
\alpha\geq 1
, to then replace
\alpha\cdot\mu\cdot q_{n-(\ell-1)}
by
\pm\tau
. The resulting term is

\pm\beta\cdot\tau+\alpha\cdot\tau^{\prime}

Note that multiplying the denominator by
\alpha
yields
\alpha\cdot\eta_{\ell-1}=\frac{\pm a}{\mu}\cdot\eta_{\ell-1}=\pm{a}\cdot\frac{\eta_{\ell-1}}{\mu}
. So,
at this intermediate stage of line
14
, the assignment can be viewed as

q_{n-(i-1)}\leftarrow\frac{\pm\beta\cdot\tau+\alpha\cdot\tau^{\prime}}{\pm{a}\cdot\frac{\eta_{\ell-1}}{\mu}}\,.

(20)

In the upcoming analysis of the update performed by Bareiss algorithm, we will show that every variable coefficient in the term
\pm\beta\cdot\tau+\alpha\cdot\tau^{\prime}
is divisible by
\frac{\eta_{\ell-1}}{\mu}
. This implies that the constant of the term must also be divisible by
\frac{\eta_{\ell-1}}{\mu}
; otherwise the expression in
Equation

20
would fail to evaluate to an integer under any variable assignment.
This explains the
assert
command of line
14
.
Line
14

concludes by dividing every integer in the term
\pm\beta\cdot\tau+\alpha\cdot\tau^{\prime}

by
\frac{\eta_{\ell-1}}{\mu}
, and setting the denominator to
\pm a
. Let us write
(\pm\beta\cdot\tau+\alpha\cdot\tau^{\prime})/\frac{\eta_{\ell-1}}{\mu}
for the term resulting from these divisions. The circuit
C_{\ell}
thus
features the assignment

q_{n-(i-1)}\leftarrow\frac{(\pm\beta\cdot\tau+\alpha\cdot\tau^{\prime})/\frac{\eta_{\ell-1}}{\mu}}{\pm{a}}\,,

and the
i
th row of
M_{\ell}
stores the variable coefficients of
\pm a\cdot q_{n-(i-1)}-\big((\pm\beta\cdot\tau+\alpha\cdot\tau^{\prime})/\frac{\eta_{\ell-1}}{\mu}\big)
.

We now turn to Bareiss algorithm.
By induction hypothesis, the
i
th row of
B_{\ell-1}^{\prime}
contains the variable coefficients of the term
\eta_{\ell-1}\cdot q_{n-(i-1)}-(\beta\cdot\mu\cdot q_{n-(\ell-1)}+\tau^{\prime})
.
The algorithm first multiplies this row by
\alpha
,
resulting in the variable coefficients of
\alpha\cdot\eta_{\ell-1}\cdot q_{n-(i-1)}-\alpha(\beta\cdot\mu\cdot q_{n-(\ell-1)}+\tau^{\prime})
.
Next, the algorithm subtracts to this row the quantity
\pm(-\beta)\cdot\bm{r}_{\ell}
, where
\bm{r}_{\ell}
is the
\ell
th row of
B_{\ell-1}^{\prime}
. By
8
,

\bm{r}_{\ell}
holds the variable coefficients of
a\cdot q_{n-(\ell-1)}-\tau
. Hence, after this subtraction, the
i
th row contains the variable coefficients of the term

\displaystyle\alpha\cdot\eta_{\ell-1}\cdot q_{n-(i-1)}-\alpha(\beta\cdot\mu\cdot q_{n-(\ell-1)}+\tau^{\prime})-\pm(-\beta)\cdot(a\cdot q_{n-(\ell-1)}-\tau)

\displaystyle={}

\displaystyle\alpha\cdot\eta_{\ell-1}\cdot q_{n-(i-1)}-(\pm\beta\cdot\tau+\alpha\cdot\tau^{\prime}).

Lastly, each entry of the
i
th row is divided by
\left|\lambda_{\ell-1}\right|=\frac{\eta_{\ell-1}}{\mu}
.
Thanks to
Lemma

17
, we know that these divisions are exact, since the results correspond to sub-determinants of the matrix
B_{0}^{\prime}
.
Moreover, because
q_{n-(i-1)}
does not appear in neither
\tau
nor
\tau^{\prime}
,
we conclude that every variable coefficient in
\pm\beta\cdot\tau+\alpha\cdot\tau^{\prime}
is divisible by
\frac{\eta_{\ell-1}}{\mu}
. Therefore, the divisions performed in line
14

of
Algorithm

5
are also without remainder.
Since
\alpha\cdot\eta_{\ell-1}=\pm a\cdot\frac{\eta_{\ell-1}}{\mu}
, we conclude that the
i
th row of
B_{\ell}^{\prime}
holds the variables coefficients of
\pm a\cdot q_{n-(i-1)}-\big((\pm\beta\cdot\tau+\alpha\cdot\tau^{\prime})/\frac{\eta_{\ell-1}}{\mu}\big)
. That is, the
i
th rows of
M_{\ell}
and
B_{\ell}^{\prime}
coincide.

To complete the proof, let us address the second statement of the claim.
Consider once more the term
{\tau_{n-(i-1)}[\frac{\pm\tau}{\alpha}\,/\,\mu\cdot q_{n-(\ell-1)}]}
, that is,

\pm\beta\cdot\tau+\alpha\cdot\tau^{\prime}
. We have already established that all variable coefficients of this term are divisible by
\frac{\eta_{\ell-1}}{\mu}
; in particular, this shows the second statement of the claim for the variable
u
. As for the remaining variables,
Lemma

17

guarantee that, once divided by
\frac{\eta_{\ell-1}}{\mu}
, their coefficients are still divisible by
\mu
.
Therefore, in
\pm\beta\cdot\tau+\alpha\cdot\tau^{\prime}
,
all coefficients of variables other than
u
are divisible by
\eta_{\ell-1}
.
∎

Lemma

20
follows:
Claims
9

to
12

imply that
M_{\ell}=B_{\ell}^{\prime}
.

9

establishes
\frac{\eta_{\ell}}{\mu}=\left|\lambda_{\ell}\right|\neq 0
.
Claims
10

and
11
imply that

6
holds
when
restricted to
Algorithm

5
having as input
the pair
(C_{\ell-1}[x_{m}],\langle{\gamma_{\ell-1}}\mathbin{;}{\psi}\rangle)
and the equality
e_{\ell-1}
.
∎

†
†
margin:

Proof in page
C.7

6
follows as a corollary of
Lemma

20
; thus completing the proof of correctness of
ElimVars
.

5.5
Complexity of
ElimVars

In addition to being crucial for establishing the correctness of
ElimVars
,
Lemma

20
allows us to obtain a refined complexity analysis of the procedure.
The next lemma summarizes this analysis.

Lemma 21.
†
†
margin:

Proof in page
C.8

The algorithm from
Lemma

14
runs in non-deterministic polynomial time.
Consider its execution on an input
(\bm{q},{C}[{x_{m}}],\langle{\gamma}\mathbin{;}{\psi}\rangle)
,
where
(C,\langle{\gamma}\mathbin{;}{\psi}\rangle)
belongs to
\mathcal{I}_{k}^{0}
,
and define:

\displaystyle L

\displaystyle\coloneqq 3\cdot\mu_{C}\cdot(4\cdot\left\lceil\log_{2}(2\cdot\xi_{C}+\mu_{C})\right\rceil+8),

\displaystyle Q

\displaystyle\coloneqq\max\{\left|b\right|:\text{$b\in\mathbb{Z}$ is a coefficient of~$q_{n-k}$ or of a variable in $\bm{q}$, in a term from $\textup{terms}(\gamma)$}\},

\displaystyle\qquad U

\displaystyle\coloneqq\max\{\left|a\right|:\text{$a=L$ or $a\in\mathbb{Z}$ is a coefficient of $u$ in a term from~$\textup{terms}(\gamma)$}\},

\displaystyle R

\displaystyle\coloneqq\max\{\left|d\right|:\text{$d=L$ or $d\in\mathbb{Z}$ is a constant of a term from~$\textup{terms}(\gamma)$}\}.

In each non-deterministic branch
\beta
,
the algorithm returns a pair
({C^{\prime}}[{x_{m}}],\langle{\gamma^{\prime}}\mathbin{;}{\psi}\rangle)
such that:

1.

\gamma^{\prime}
features
k
constraints more than
\gamma
, they are all divisibility constraints.

2.

The circuits
C
and
C^{\prime}
assign the same expressions to
x_{n-k},\dots,x_{n}
(in particular,
\mu_{C}=\mu_{C^{\prime}}
).

3.

In terms
\tau
either from
\textup{terms}(\gamma^{\prime})
or in assignments
q_{n-i}\leftarrow\frac{\tau}{\eta_{C^{\prime}}}
of
C^{\prime}
(where
{i\in[0..k-1]}
),

•

the coefficient of the variable
q_{n-k}
is
\mu_{C}\cdot c
, for some
c\in\mathbb{Z}
with
\left|c\right|\leq(k+1)^{k+1}\big(\frac{Q}{\mu_{C}}\big)^{k+1}
;

•

the absolute value of the coefficient of the variable
u
is bounded by
(k+1)^{k+1}\big(\frac{Q}{\mu_{C}}\big)^{k}U
;

•

the absolute value of the constant is bounded by
{\frac{((k+1)\cdot Q)^{2(k+2)^{2}}}{(\mu_{C})^{2k^{2}}}\cdot\textit{mod}(\gamma)\cdot R}
.

4.

The positive integer
\textit{mod}(\gamma^{\prime})
divides
c\cdot\textit{mod}(\gamma)
,
for some positive integer
c\leq\frac{(k\cdot Q)^{k^{2}}}{(\mu_{C})^{k(k-1)}}
.

5.

We have
\eta_{C^{\prime}}=\mu_{C}\cdot g
, for some positive integer
g\leq k^{k}\big(\frac{Q}{\mu_{C}}\big)^{k}
.

Proof idea.

The bounds follow by applying
Lemma

20

in conjunction with
Lemmas

16
and
17
,
and recalling that the Leibniz formula for determinants yields
\left|\det(A)\right|\leq d^{d}\cdot\prod_{i=1}^{d}\alpha_{i}

for any
d\times d
integer matrix
A
in which the entries of the
i
th column are bounded, in absolute value, by
\alpha_{i}\in\mathbb{N}
.
∎

6
Proof of Theorem
1

In this section, we complete the proof of Theorem
1
:
we define the procedure
OptILEP
for solving the integer
linear-exponential programming optimization problem,
to then show that the procedure runs in non-deterministic polynomial time
and returns an ILESLP encoding an (optimal) solution, if one exists.
The section is divided into four parts. We begin with an overview
of the procedure, expanding on the brief summary provided in
Section

2.2
.
As part of this overview, we introduce a slight variant of LEACs, which we refer to as
PreLEAC
s.
In
Section

6.2
we present the full
correctness proof of
OptILEP
, followed by its complexity analysis in
Section

6.3
.
The pseudocode of
OptILEP
considers the setting of maximizing a single variable
x
subject to an integer linear-exponential program.
In
Section

6.4
we show (using rather standard arguments), how to extend the procedure to the optimization (maximization or minimization)
of arbitrary linear-exponential terms, completing the proof of Theorem
1
.

6.1
Overview of
OptILEP

Algorithm 6

OptILEP
: Exploration of optimal solutions for ILEP.

1:

\varphi(\bm{x})

:

integer linear-exponential program;

w

:

a variable from
\bm{x}
(to be maximized)

2:

An ILESLP
\sigma_{\beta}
.

3:

C\leftarrow\emptyset

\triangleright

the empty
0
-
PreLEAC
. The objective function is
{C}[{w}]

4:

let

x_{0}
be a fresh variable

5:

\theta

\leftarrow

guess
ordering
{2^{x_{n}}\geq{\ldots}\geq 2^{x_{1}}\geq 2^{x_{0}}=1}
, where
x_{1},\dots,x_{n}
is a permutation of
\bm{x}

6:

\triangleright

below, we write
x_{m}
for the variable among
x_{1},\dots,x_{n}
corresponding to
w

7:

\bm{r}

\leftarrow
empty vector of (remainder) variables

8:

while

\theta
is not the ordering
2^{x_{0}}=1

do

9:

2^{x}

\leftarrow
leading exponential term of
\theta

10:

2^{y}

\leftarrow
second-leading exponential term of
\theta

11:

(\gamma(q_{x},\bm{q},u),\psi(\bm{y},r_{x},\bm{r}^{\prime}))

\leftarrow
apply the algorithm from
Lemma

4
on
(\varphi,\theta)

12:

\triangleright

(q_{x},\bm{q})
quotient variables,
(r_{x},\bm{r}^{\prime})
new remainder variables,
u
proxy for
2^{x-y}

13:

update

:

add the assignment

, and replace each variable in
\bm{r}

\bm{r}=\bm{q}\cdot 2^{y}+\bm{r}^{\prime}
stemming from the above call of the algorithm form

\triangleright

15:

: replace each (in)equality
\tau\sim 0

({C}[{x_{m}}],\langle{\gamma^{\prime}}\mathbin{;}{\psi^{\prime}}\rangle)

\textsc{ElimVars}(\bm{q},\,{C}[{x_{m}}],\,\langle{\gamma}\mathbin{;}{\psi^{\prime}}\rangle)

on

\triangleright

\leftarrow

\triangleright

0

20:

\leftarrow
least upper bound of

in

\triangleright

default:
\infty

21:

then

h\leftarrow\ell+\textit{mod}(\gamma^{\prime\prime})

\leftarrow

guess
a value in
[\ell..h]
such that
\gamma^{\prime\prime}(v)
is true

23:

update

: translate into a

following
Remark

4
, replacing

for

and

for

24:

\leftarrow

(r_{x},\bm{r}^{\prime})

\leftarrow

\psi\land\psi^{\prime\prime}

remove
2^{x}
from
\theta

(

is true)

: replace

and every variable in
\bm{r}
with
0
