C

\triangleright

C
is an ILESLP encoding a solution to
\varphi

Step I

Step II

Step IV

The pseudocode of
OptILEP
is given in
Algorithm

6
.
Echoing
Section

2.2
,
the procedure starts by guessing an ordering
\theta
of the form
2^{x_{n}}\geq\dots\geq 2^{x_{1}}\geq 2^{x_{0}}=1
, where
x_{1},\dots,x_{n}
are the variables appearing input
(\varphi,w)
of
OptILEP
,
see lines
3
–
7
.
These lines also initialize the
remainder variables

\bm{r}
(as described in
Section

2
),
and the circuit
C
, which will ultimately become the ILESLP encoding the computed
solution. After this initialization step, the procedure enters its main loop.

Let
2^{x}
and
2^{y}
be the leading and second-leading exponential terms of
\theta
, respectively (as in lines
9
and
10
).
As mentioned in
Section

2.2
, the main loop of the procedure
eliminates
x
by mirroring the four steps
of the procedure from
[
CMS24
]
, with the key difference that Step II is replaced by our
optimum-preserving procedure
ElimVars
. We refer the reader back to
Section

2

for a refresher, particularly on the specifications of Steps I and III,
which we treat here as black boxes.

As discussed in
Section

2
,
Step I “divides” all constraints in
\varphi
by
2^{y}
,
non-deterministically computing from
\varphi
and
\theta

a pair of formulae of the form
(\gamma(q_{x},\bm{q},u),\psi(\bm{y},r_{x},\bm{r}^{\prime}))
, where, in particular,
\gamma
is a linear program with divisions.
As described in the specification of Step I given by
Lemma

4
,
\varphi
and
(\gamma,\psi)
are “coupled”
by the system featuring the equalities
x=q_{x}\cdot 2^{y}+r_{x}
and

\bm{r}=\bm{q}\cdot 2^{y}+\bm{r}^{\prime}
(
Equation

3
), with
q_{x}
and
\bm{q}

quotient variables
, and
r_{x}
and
\bm{r}^{\prime}
fresh remainder variables.
The change of variables given by this system
must be applied also to the circuit
C
;
this is done in line
13
.

The goal of Step II is to eliminate the variables
\bm{q}
from
\gamma
.
We preserve optimal solutions while eliminating these variables
by appealing to our instantiation of
ElimVars
.
However, according to
Lemma

14
,
a correct invocation to this algorithm requires that its input
belong to
\mathcal{I}_{k}^{0}
(for some
k
).
Accordingly, lines
14
–
16

perform the necessary manipulations on
\gamma
and
\psi

to ensure this condition is met.

After eliminating the variables
\bm{q}
and appropriately updating the circuit
C
via
ElimVars
, line
18

applies Step III from
[
CMS24
]
. This eliminates
the variables
x
and
u
(where
u
is the proxy for
2^{x-y}
).
According to the specification in
Lemma

6
,
this step transforms the linear program with divisions
\gamma^{\prime}(q_{x},u)
produced as output of
ElimVars
into a pair consisting of a linear program with divisions
\gamma^{\prime\prime}(q_{x})

and linear-exponential program with divisions
\psi^{\prime\prime}(y,r_{x})
.

Step IV (lines
19
–
23
) eliminates
q_{x}
. In
[
CMS24
]
, this is achieved by checking
whether the linear program with divisions
\gamma^{\prime\prime}
is satisfiable, and
replacing it with
\top
if so. In contrast,
OptILEP
instead works by trying
“all” the solutions to
\gamma^{\prime\prime}
. More precisely, since
\gamma^{\prime\prime}
is
univariate, all of its inequalities can be rewritten in the form
\ell^{*}\leq q_{x}
or
q_{x}\leq h^{*}
, with
\ell^{*},h^{*}\in\mathbb{Z}
. Then, every solution to

\gamma^{\prime\prime}
must lie in the interval
[\ell..h]
, where
\ell
is either
0
or
the largest such integer
\ell^{*}
, and
h
is either
\infty
or the smallest
such integer
h^{*}
. If
h\neq\infty
, we can (non-deterministically) test all
values in this interval (in the complexity proof we will show that both
\ell

and
h
have polynomial bit size). If instead
h=\infty
, in the correctness
proof we will show that either no optimal solution exists, or the
objective function is independent of
q_{x}
.
To cover the latter case, the algorithm updates

h
from
\infty
to
\ell+\textit{mod}(\gamma^{\prime\prime})
(line
21
),
ensuring that at least one solution of
\gamma^{\prime\prime}
is explored.
After eliminating
q_{x}
, the body of the loop terminates with a small “Step V” (lines
24
–
26
),
which prepares
\varphi
for the next iteration,
and updates
\theta
by removing
2^{x}
(making
2^{y}
the new leading exponential term).

In the above overview, we have not elaborated on
the structure of the circuit
C
during the procedure.
According to
Lemma

14
,

C
must be a
(k,0)
-LEAC when
ElimVars
is called, and it evolves into a
(k,k)
-LEAC
by the time this algorithm terminates.
From this information, we know that
C
must become
a
(k,0)
-LEAC precisely when line
13
executes.
Prior to this line, however,
C
contains no quotient variable,
and instead has the structure given in the following definition:

Definition 3

(
PreLEAC
)
.

Let
k\in[0..n]
.
A
k
-
PreLEAC

C
is a sequence of assignments

\displaystyle\hskip 11.38092ptx_{n-i}

\displaystyle\leftarrow\frac{\textstyle\sum_{j={i+1}}^{k}a_{i,j}\cdot 2^{x_{n-j}}}{\mu}+r_{n-i}

\displaystyle\hskip-28.45274pt\text{for $i$ from $k-1$ to $0$},

where every
a_{i,j}
is in
\mathbb{Z}
,
and the denominator
\mu
is a positive integer.

We transfer the notation used for LEACs also to
PreLEAC
s.
In particular, we refer to the denominator
\mu
as
\mu_{C}
, postulating
\mu_{C}\coloneqq 1
when
k=0
(note that
C
is the empty sequence in this case). We also define
\xi_{C}\coloneqq\sum\{\left|a_{i,j}\right|:\text{$i\in[0..k-1]$, $j\in[i+1..k]$}\}
, and write
\textup{vars}(C)
for the set of
free variables
of
C
, that is,
x_{n-k}
and the variables
r_{n-i}
.
Lastly, for a variable
x_{m}
with
m\in[0..n]
,
we write
{C}[{x_{m}}]
for the function analogous
to the one defined for LEACs on page
2
.

It is easy to verify that, starting from
C
being a
k
-
PreLEAC
,
line
13
of
OptILEP
produces a
(k,0)
-LEAC.
More interesting is the transformation that occurs in line
23
,
where the variables
u
and
q_{n-k}
are removed from the
(k,k)
-LEAC
returned by
OptILEP
.
The following remark describes this transformation,
which yields a
(k+1)
-
PreLEAC
.

Remark 4
(From
(k,k)
-LEACs to
(k+1)
-
PreLEAC
s)
.

Let
k\in[0..n-1]
. Let
C
be a
(k,k)
-LEAC

\displaystyle\hskip 11.38092ptq_{n-i}

\displaystyle\leftarrow\frac{b_{n-i}\cdot u+c_{n-i}\cdot q_{n-k}+d_{n-i}}{\eta}

\displaystyle\text{for $i$ from $k-1$ to $0$},

\displaystyle\hskip 11.38092ptx_{n-i}

\displaystyle\leftarrow\frac{\textstyle\sum_{j={i+1}}^{k}a_{i,j}\cdot 2^{x_{n-j}}}{\mu}+q_{n-i}\cdot 2^{x_{n-k-1}}+r_{n-i}

\displaystyle\hskip-85.35826pt\text{for $i$ from $k$ to $0$},

such that
\mu
divides
\eta
. Let
\lambda=\frac{\eta}{\mu}
.
By replacing
u
for
2^{x_{n-k}-x_{n-k-1}}
, assigning an integer
v
to
q_{n-k}
, and substituting the expressions for
q_{n-(k-1)},\dots,q_{n}
into the expressions for
x_{n-k},\dots,x_{n}
, one transforms
C
into the following
(k+1)
-
PreLEAC

C^{\prime}
:

\displaystyle\hskip 11.38092ptx_{n-k}

\displaystyle\leftarrow\frac{\eta\cdot v\cdot 2^{x_{n-k-1}}}{\eta}+r_{n-k}

\displaystyle\hskip 11.38092ptx_{n-i}

\displaystyle\leftarrow\frac{\textstyle(c_{n-i}\cdot v+d_{n-i})\cdot 2^{x_{n-k-1}}+(\lambda\cdot a_{i,k}+b_{n-i})\cdot 2^{x_{n-k}}+\sum_{j={i+1}}^{k-1}\lambda\cdot a_{i,j}\cdot 2^{x_{n-j}}}{\eta}+r_{n-i}

\displaystyle\hskip-85.35826pt\text{for $i$ from $k-1$ to $0$}.

Note that
\textup{vars}(C^{\prime})=\textup{vars}(C)\setminus\{u,q_{n-k}\}
, and when evaluating
C
and
C^{\prime}
on a map
\nu\colon\textup{vars}(C)\to\mathbb{N}
satisfying
{\nu(q_{n-k})=v}
and
\nu(u)=2^{\nu(x_{n-k})-\nu(x_{n-k-1})}
, the values taken by
x_{n-k},\dots,x_{n}
coincide,
that is,
{C}[{x_{n-i}}](\nu)={C^{\prime}}[{x_{n-i}}](\nu)
for every
i\in[0..k]
.

6.2
Correctness of
OptILEP

The next proposition states that
OptILEP
correctly solves ILEP.

Proposition 4.

There is a non-deterministic procedure with the following specification:

Input:

\varphi(\bm{x})

:

an integer linear-exponential program;

w

:

a variable occurring in
\bm{x}
(to be maximized).

Output of each branch (
\beta
):

\sigma_{\beta}

:

an ILESLP.

The algorithm ensures that,
if
\varphi
is satisfiable (resp., the problem of maximizing
x
subject to
\varphi
has a solution),
then there a branch
\beta
such that
\left\llbracket\sigma_{\beta}\right\rrbracket
is a solution
(resp., an optimal solution) to
\varphi
.

Proof.

Let
\varphi_{0}(\bm{x})
be the linear-exponential program in input of
OptILEP
, and let
n
denote the number of variables in
\varphi_{0}
.
In line
5
, the algorithm guesses an ordering
2^{x_{n}}\geq{\ldots}\geq 2^{x_{1}}\geq 2^{x_{0}}=1
, where
x_{1},\dots,x_{n}
is a permutation of
\bm{x}
, and
x_{0}
is a fresh variable.
Let
\Theta
be the set of all such ordering. Clearly,
\varphi_{0}
is equivalent to
\bigvee_{\theta\in\Theta}(\varphi_{0}\land\theta)
. To prove the proposition, it suffices to show, for a given
\theta\in\Theta
, that if
\varphi_{0}\land\theta
is satisfiable (resp., the problem of maximizing
x
subject to
\varphi_{0}\land\theta
has a solution),
then, in a non-deterministic branch
\beta
, the algorithm returns an ILESLP
\sigma_{\beta}
such that
\left\llbracket\sigma_{\beta}\right\rrbracket
is a solution
(resp., an optimal solution) to
\varphi_{0}\land\theta
.
Therefore, throughout the proof we fix the ordering guessed in line
5

to be some
\theta_{0}\coloneqq{(2^{x_{n}}\geq{\ldots}\geq 2^{x_{1}}\geq 2^{x_{0}}=1)}
from
\Theta
. Moreover, let
x_{m}
(for some
m\in[1..n]
) denote the variable to be maximized, with respect to the order
\theta_{0}
.

Throughout the proof, we write
S_{k}
for the set of all triples
(C,\theta,\varphi)
that represent the state of
OptILEP
in any of its non-deterministic branches at the point when the execution reaches line
8
(the condition of the only
while
loop of the procedure) for the
(k+1)
th time.
Since we fix the ordering
\theta_{0}
, in particular
S_{0}=\{(\emptyset,\theta_{0},\varphi_{0})\}
, where
\emptyset
is the empty sequence assigned to
C
in line
3
.

We show that the
while
loop of
OptILEP
enjoys the following loop invariant:

loop invariant.

Let
k\in\mathbb{N}
. For every
(C,\theta,\varphi)\in S_{k}
:

I_{1}
.

\theta
is the ordering
\theta_{k}\coloneqq(2^{x_{n-k}}\geq\dots\geq 2^{x_{1}}\geq 2^{x_{0}}=1)
.

I_{2}
.

\varphi
is a linear-exponential program with divisions featuring variables
\bm{y}_{k}\coloneqq(x_{0},\dots,x_{n-k})
and
remainder variables

\bm{r}_{k-1}\coloneqq(r_{n-(k-1)},\dots,r_{n})
. The remainder variables do not occur in exponentials, and for every
i\in[0..k-1]
,
\varphi
implies
r_{n-i}<2^{x_{n-k}}
.

I_{3}
.

C
is a
k
-
PreLEAC
of the form
(x_{n-(k-1)}\leftarrow\frac{\tau_{n-(k-1)}}{\mu_{C}}+r_{n-(k-1)}\,,\,\dots\,,\,x_{n}\leftarrow\frac{\tau_{n}}{\mu_{C}}+r_{n})
.

I_{4}
.

Given a solution
\nu\colon\textup{vars}(\varphi\land\theta)\to\mathbb{N}
to
\varphi\land\theta
, the map

\nu+\textstyle\sum_{i=0}^{k-1}[x_{n-i}\mapsto g_{n-i}]
,
where
g_{n-i}
is the value taken by
x_{n-i}
when evaluating
{C}[{x_{m}}]
on
\nu
, is a solution to
\varphi_{0}\land\theta_{0}
.

Moreover:

I_{5}
.

\exists\bm{x}_{k-1}(\varphi_{0}\land\theta_{0})
is equivalent to
\exists\bm{r}_{k-1}\bigvee_{(C,\theta,\varphi)\in S_{k}}(\varphi\land\theta)
, where
\bm{x}_{k-1}\coloneqq(x_{n-(k-1)},\dots,x_{n})
.

I_{6}
.

If
\max\{\nu(x_{m}):\text{$\nu$ is a solution to $\varphi_{0}\land\theta_{0}$}\}
exists, then it
is equal to

\max\{{C}[{x_{m}}](\nu):\text{$(C,\theta,\varphi)\in S_{k}$ and $\nu$ is a solution to $\varphi\land\theta$}\}.

(For
k\in[0..n]
, the loop invariant assumes a fixed set of variables
r_{0},\dots,r_{n}

that are reused across different non-deterministic branches and across iterations of the
while
loop.
This assumption is without loss of generality.)

Let
(C,\theta,\varphi)\in S_{n}
.
The loop invariant implies that
\theta
is
2^{x_{0}}=1
.
This causes the condition in the
while
loop to fail, terminating the loop.
(In particular, we have
S_{k}=\emptyset
for all
k>n
.)
Moreover, from Item

I
2

.
, we conclude that the only solution to
\varphi\land\theta

is the map assigning
0
to every variable.
Accordingly, the algorithm checks whether this is indeed a solution (line
27
), and if so, replaces all free variables in
C
with
0
.
Since
C
is a
n
-
PreLEAC
, this results in a sequence of the form

\displaystyle\hskip 11.38092ptx_{n-i}

\displaystyle\leftarrow\frac{a_{i,n}+\textstyle\sum_{j={i+1}}^{n-1}a_{i,j}\cdot 2^{x_{n-j}}}{\mu_{C}}

\displaystyle\hskip-28.45274pt\text{for $i$ from $n-1$ to $0$},

which can easily be represented as an ILESLP.
The proposition then follows from Items

I
5

.
and

I
6

.
.
Therefore, to complete the proof, it suffices to verify that the loop invariant holds.

The invariant is trivially true for
S_{0}
. (In particular,
\bm{r}_{-1}
and
\bm{x}_{-1}
are empty in this case, and formulae like
\exists\bm{x}_{-1}(\varphi_{0}\land\theta_{0})
simplify to just
\varphi_{0}\land\theta_{0}
.)
Hence, let us assume that the loop invariant is true when the execution reaches line
8
for the
(k+1)
th time,
with
k\in[0..n-1]
, and show that the invariant still holds when the algorithm comes back to this line
for the
(k+2)
th time.

Consider
(C,\theta,\varphi)\in S_{k}
, and
let
T_{k+1}
be the set of those triples from
S_{k+1}
that are constructed by (non-deterministially)
running the body of the
while
loop starting from
(C,\theta,\varphi)
. More precisely, we will show that each triple in
T_{k+1}
satisfies Items

I
1

.
–

I
4

.
, and that moreover

I_{5}^{\prime}
.

\exists x_{n-k}\exists\bm{r}_{k-1}(\varphi\land\theta)
is equivalent to
\exists\bm{r}_{k}\bigvee_{(C^{\prime},\theta^{\prime},\varphi^{\prime})\in T_{k+1}}(\varphi^{\prime}\land\theta^{\prime})
.

I_{6}^{\prime}
.

If
\max\{{C}[{x_{m}}](\nu):\text{$\nu$ is a solution to $\varphi\land\theta$}\}
exists, then it
is equal to

\max\{{C^{\prime}}[{x_{m}}](\nu):\text{$(C^{\prime},\theta^{\prime},\varphi^{\prime})\in T_{k+1}$ and $\nu$ is a solution to $\varphi^{\prime}\land\theta^{\prime}$}\}.

We divide the proof following the five steps identified in
Section

6.1
:
Step I (lines
9
–
13
),
Step II (lines
14
–
17
),
Step III (line
18
),
Step IV (lines
19
–
23
) and
Step V (lines
24
–
26
).

Step I (lines
9
–
13
).

By Item

I
1

.
,
\theta
is
\theta_{k}
, and in it the leading and second-leading exponential terms are
2^{x_{n-k}}
and
2^{x_{n-k-1}}
, respectively.
Following the pseudocode of
OptILEP
, throughout the proof we write
x
for
x_{n-k}
, and
y
for
x_{n-k-1}
.
According to Item

I
2

.
, within
\varphi(\bm{y}_{k},\bm{r}_{k-1})
the variables from
\bm{r}_{k-1}
do not occur in exponentials, and moreover
\varphi
implies
\bm{r}_{k-1}<2^{x}
. In line
11
,
OptILEP
invokes the algorithm from
Lemma

4
on the pair
(\varphi,\theta)
. Let
E_{1}
be the set of pairs
(\gamma,\psi)
returned by this algorithm across its non-deterministic branches. By
Lemma

4
, each
\gamma
is a linear program with division in variables
\bm{q}_{k}\coloneqq(q_{n-k},\dots,q_{n})
(called
quotient variables
) and
u
, whereas
each
\psi
is a linear-exponential program with divisions in variables
\bm{y}_{k+1}
and
\bm{r}^{\prime}\coloneqq(r_{n-k}^{\prime},\dots,r_{n}^{\prime})
, the latter being fresh
remainder variables
not occurring in exponentials, and such that
\psi
implies
\bm{r}^{\prime}<2^{y}
. Again as in the pseudocode,
we often write
q_{x}
for
q_{n-k}
,
and
r_{x}
for
r_{n-k}^{\prime}
.
The system

\left[\begin{matrix}x\\
r_{n-(k-1)}\\
\vdots\\
r_{n}\end{matrix}\right]=\left[\begin{matrix}q_{x}\\
q_{n-(k-1)}\\
\vdots\\
q_{n}\end{matrix}\right]\cdot 2^{y}+\left[\begin{matrix}r_{x}\\
r_{n-(k-1)}^{\prime}\\
\vdots\\
r_{n}^{\prime}\end{matrix}\right],

(21)

yields a one-to-one correspondence between the solutions of

\varphi\land\theta
and those of
\bigvee_{(\gamma,\psi)\in E_{1}}\Phi(\gamma,\psi)
,
where
\Phi(\gamma,\psi)\coloneqq\big(\gamma\land\psi\land(u=2^{x-y})\land(x=q_{x}\cdot 2^{y}+r_{x})\land\theta\big)
.
This correspondence is the identity for the variables these two formulae share (i.e., the variables in
\bm{y}_{k}
).

In line
13
,
C
is updated following the system described in
Equation

21
. Let
C^{\prime}
be the resulting sequence.
By Item

I
3

.
,

C
is a
k
-
PreLEAC
, and therefore
C^{\prime}
takes the form:

\displaystyle\hskip 11.38092ptx_{n-i}

\displaystyle\leftarrow\frac{\tau_{n-i}}{\mu}+q_{n-i}\cdot 2^{y}+r_{n-i}^{\prime}

\displaystyle\hskip-28.45274pt\text{for $i$ from $k$ to $0$},

(22)

where each
\tau_{n-i}
is a term of the form
\textstyle\sum_{j={i+1}}^{k}a_{i,j}\cdot 2^{x_{n-j}}
,
with each
a_{i,j}
in
\mathbb{Z}
,
and
\mu
is a positive integer.
In other words,
C^{\prime}
is a
(k,0)
-LEAC.
The claim below follows directly from the one-to-one correspondence given by
Equation

21
.

Claim 13.

The following properties hold:

1.

The formulae
\exists\bm{r}_{k-1}(\varphi\land\theta)
and
\exists\bm{q}_{k}\exists u\exists\bm{r}^{\prime}\textstyle\bigvee_{(\gamma,\psi)\in E_{1}}\Phi(\gamma,\psi)
are equivalent.

2.

Consider
(\gamma,\psi)\in E_{1}
, and let
\nu\colon\textup{vars}(\Phi(\gamma,\psi))\to\mathbb{N}
be a solution to
\Phi(\gamma,\psi)
. Then, the map
{\nu+\textstyle\sum_{i=0}^{k-1}[r_{n-i}\mapsto\nu(q_{n-i})\cdot 2^{\nu(y)}+\nu(r_{n-i}^{\prime})]}
is a solution to
\varphi\land\theta
.

3.

If
\max\{{C}[{x_{m}}](\nu):\text{$\nu$ is a solution to $\varphi\land\theta$}\}
exists, it
is equal to

\max\{{C^{\prime}}[{x_{m}}](\nu):\text{$\nu$ is a solution to $\Phi(\gamma,\psi)$, for some $(\gamma,\psi)\in E_{1}$}\}.

Step II (lines
14
–
17
).

Fix
(\gamma,\psi)\in E_{1}
.
In a nutshell, Step II removes the quotient variables
\bm{q}_{k-1}=(q_{n-(k-1)},\dots,q_{n})
from
\gamma
.
Let
\widetilde{\gamma}
be the formula obtained from
\gamma
by performing the updates in lines
14

and
15
;

\widetilde{\gamma}
and
\gamma
are equivalent, as all variables range over
\mathbb{N}
and
\mu_{C^{\prime}}\geq 1
.
Let
\psi^{\prime}
be the formula in line
16
,
that is,
\psi^{\prime}\coloneqq(\psi\land\theta\land({x=q_{x}\cdot 2^{y}+r_{x}})\land(u=2^{x-y}))
.
By definition, the three formulae
\langle{\widetilde{\gamma}}\mathbin{;}{\psi^{\prime}}\rangle
,

\langle{\gamma}\mathbin{;}{\psi^{\prime}}\rangle
and
\Phi(\gamma,\psi)
are equivalent.
We show that
(C^{\prime},\langle{\widetilde{\gamma}}\mathbin{;}{\psi^{\prime}}\rangle)
is in
\mathcal{I}_{k}^{0}
; and hence that the call to
ElimVars
performed in line
17
adheres to the specification of this algorithm (from
Lemma

14
).
Below, we refer to the Items
(i)
–
(iii)
characterizing
\mathcal{I}_{k}^{0}
(page
(i)
):

•

Item

(i)
:
We have already seen that
C^{\prime}
is a

(k,0)
-LEAC.

•

Item

(ii)
:
By definition,
\widetilde{\gamma}
is a linear
exponential program with divisions, in variables
u
and
\bm{q}_{k}
, and in which
all inequalities and equalities are such that the coefficients of the
variables
\bm{q}_{k}
are divisible by
\mu_{C^{\prime}}
. Moreover,
\widetilde{\gamma}
contains
an inequality
\mu_{C^{\prime}}\cdot q\geq 0
for every

in
\bm{q}_{k}
.
Lastly, the formula
\psi^{\prime}
trivially satisfies the conditions specified in
Item

(ii)
.

•

Item

(iii)
:

We need to show that
\langle{\widetilde{\gamma}}\mathbin{;}{\psi^{\prime}}\rangle
implies the formula
\Psi(C^{\prime})
given by

\displaystyle\bm{0}\leq\bm{r}^{\prime}<2^{y}\land\bm{0}\leq\bm{q}_{k}\cdot 2^{y}+\bm{r}^{\prime}<2^{x}\land\exists\bm{x}_{k-1}\big(\theta_{0}\land\textstyle\bigwedge_{i=0}^{k}(x_{n-i}=\rho_{n-i})\big),

where
\rho_{n-i}
is the expression assigned to
x_{n-i}
in
C^{\prime}

(following

22
).

Consider a solution
\nu\colon\textup{vars}(\langle{\widetilde{\gamma}}\mathbin{;}{\psi^{\prime}}\rangle)\to\mathbb{N}
to
\langle{\widetilde{\gamma}}\mathbin{;}{\psi^{\prime}}\rangle
.
This is also a solution to
\Phi(\gamma,\psi)
. By definition,
\psi
implies
\bm{0}\leq\bm{r}^{\prime}<2^{y}
.
From the one-to-one correspondence given by
Equation

21
,
the map
\nu^{\prime}\coloneqq\nu+\sum_{i=0}^{k-1}[r_{n-i}\mapsto\nu(q_{n-i})\cdot 2^{\nu(y)}+\nu(r_{n-i}^{\prime})]
is a solution to
\varphi\land\theta
.
By Item

I
2

.
,

\varphi
implies
\bm{r}_{k-1}<2^{x}
.
Therefore,
\langle{\widetilde{\gamma}}\mathbin{;}{\psi^{\prime}}\rangle

.

Lastly, we see that
\langle{\widetilde{\gamma}}\mathbin{;}{\psi^{\prime}}\rangle

also implies
\exists\bm{x}_{k-1}\big(\theta_{0}\land\textstyle\bigwedge_{i=0}^{k}(x_{n-i}=\rho_{n-i})\big)
.
Indeed, by Item

I
4

.
,
the map
{\nu^{\prime\prime}\coloneqq\nu^{\prime}+\sum_{i=0}^{k-1}[x_{n-i}\mapsto\frac{\nu^{\prime}(\tau_{n-i})}{\mu_{C}}+\nu^{\prime}(r_{n-i})]}

\varphi_{0}\land\theta_{0}
.
Together with the fact that

{x=q_{x}\cdot 2^{y}+r_{x}}

this means that

implies
\exists\bm{x}_{k-1}\big(\theta_{0}\land\bigwedge_{i=0}^{k}(x_{n-i}=\frac{\tau_{n-i}}{\mu_{C}}+q_{n-i}\cdot 2^{y}+r_{n-i}^{\prime})\big)
.
By definition,
\rho_{n-i}=(\frac{\tau_{n-i}}{\mu_{C}}+q_{n-i}\cdot 2^{y}+r_{n-i}^{\prime})
.

Therefore,
(C^{\prime},\langle{\widetilde{\gamma}}\mathbin{;}{\psi^{\prime}}\rangle)\in\mathcal{I}_{k}^{0}
. In line
17
,
OptILEP
calls
ElimVars
, eliminating the quotient variables
\bm{q}_{k-1}
.
Let us denote by
E_{2}(\gamma,\psi)
the set of all triples

(C^{\prime\prime},\gamma^{\prime},\psi^{\prime})

such that

{({C^{\prime\prime}}[{x_{m}}],\langle{\gamma^{\prime}}\mathbin{;}{\psi^{\prime}}\rangle)}
is a pair
returned by a non-deterministic execution of
ElimVars
with as input the formulae
computed from
\gamma
and

in

–

.
Then, by direct application of

, we obtain:

Claim 14.

Let
(\gamma,\psi)\in E_{1}
. The following properties hold:

1.

The formulae
\exists\bm{q}_{k-1}\Phi(\gamma,\psi)
and
\bigvee_{(C^{\prime\prime},\gamma^{\prime},\psi^{\prime})\in E_{2}(\gamma,\psi)}\langle{\gamma^{\prime}}\mathbin{;}{\psi^{\prime}}\rangle
are equivalent.

2.

Consider
(C^{\prime\prime},\gamma^{\prime},\psi^{\prime})\in E_{2}(\gamma,\psi)
.
Let
q_{n-(k-1)}\leftarrow\frac{\tau_{n-(k-1)}^{\prime\prime}}{\eta},\dots,q_{n}\leftarrow\frac{\tau_{n}^{\prime\prime}}{\eta}
be the assignments
to the variables
\bm{q}_{k-1}
occurring in
C^{\prime\prime}
.
Let
\nu\colon\textup{vars}(\langle{\gamma^{\prime}}\mathbin{;}{\psi^{\prime}}\rangle)\to\mathbb{N}
be a solution to
\langle{\gamma^{\prime}}\mathbin{;}{\psi^{\prime}}\rangle
. Then, the map

\nu+\sum_{i=0}^{k-1}[q_{n-i}\mapsto\frac{\nu(\tau_{n-i}^{\prime\prime})}{\eta}]
is a solution to
\Phi(\gamma,\psi)
.

3.

If
\max\{{C^{\prime}}[{x_{m}}](\nu):\text{$\nu$ is a solution to $\Phi(\gamma,\psi)$}\}
exists, then it is equal to

\max\{{C^{\prime\prime}}[{x_{m}}](\nu):\text{$\nu$ is a solution to $\langle{\gamma^{\prime}}\mathbin{;}{\psi^{\prime}}\rangle$, for some $(C^{\prime\prime},\gamma^{\prime},\psi^{\prime})\in E_{2}(\gamma,\psi)$}\}.

4.

For every
(C^{\prime\prime},\gamma^{\prime},\psi^{\prime})\in E_{2}(\gamma,\psi)
, the pair
(C^{\prime\prime},\langle{\gamma^{\prime}}\mathbin{;}{\psi^{\prime}}\rangle)
belongs to
\mathcal{I}_{k}^{k}
.

Step III (line
18
).

Let

(C^{\prime\prime},\gamma^{\prime},\psi^{\prime})\in E_{2}(\gamma,\psi)
, for some
(\gamma,\psi)\in E_{1}
.
Step III eliminates
x
and
u
from
\gamma^{\prime}
by applying the algorithm from
Lemma

6
, which non-deterministically returns a pair
(\gamma^{\prime\prime},\psi^{\prime\prime})
. We write
E_{3}(\gamma^{\prime})
for the set of all such resulting pairs.
By
14
.
4
,
\gamma^{\prime}
is a linear program with divisions in variables
q_{x}
and
u
. So, by
Lemma

6
,

\gamma^{\prime\prime}
is a linear program with divisions in the single variable
q_{x}
,
and
\psi^{\prime\prime}
is a linear-exponential program with divisions in the variables
y
and
r_{x}
.
Moreover, the equation
x=q_{x}\cdot 2^{y}+r_{x}

yields a one-to-one correspondence between the solutions
of
\gamma^{\prime}\land(u=2^{x-y})\land(x=q_{x}\cdot 2^{y}+r_{x})

and those of
\bigvee_{(\gamma^{\prime\prime},\psi^{\prime\prime})\in E_{3}(\gamma^{\prime})}(\gamma^{\prime\prime}\land\psi^{\prime\prime})
.
This correspondence is the identity for the variables these two
formulae share (that is,
y
,
q_{x}
and
r_{x}
).
Roughly speaking, this one-to-one correspondence allows us to remove
x

and
u
without changing the set of solutions.

Recall that
\psi^{\prime}\coloneqq(\psi\land\theta\land({x=q_{x}\cdot 2^{y}+r_{x}})\land(u=2^{x-y}))
, and observe
that
\theta\land(u=2^{x-y})
is equal to
\theta_{k+1}\land(u=2^{x-y})
, because
u=2^{x-y}
implies

2^{x}\geq 2^{y}
(as
u
ranges over
\mathbb{N}
).
Then, the claim below follows immediately from the one-to-one correspondence given by the equation
x=q_{x}\cdot 2^{y}+r_{x}
.

Claim 15.

Let
(\gamma,\psi)\in E_{1}

and
(C^{\prime\prime},\gamma^{\prime},\psi^{\prime})\in E_{2}(\gamma,\psi)
.
The following properties hold:

1.

The formulae
\exists x\exists u\langle{\gamma^{\prime}}\mathbin{;}{\psi^{\prime}}\rangle
and
\textstyle\bigvee_{(\gamma^{\prime\prime},\psi^{\prime\prime})\in E_{3}(\gamma^{\prime})}(\gamma^{\prime\prime}\land\psi^{\prime\prime}\land\psi\land\theta_{k+1})
are equivalent.

2.

Let
(\gamma^{\prime\prime},\psi^{\prime\prime})\in E_{3}(\gamma^{\prime})
, and
\nu\colon\textup{vars}(\gamma^{\prime\prime}\land\psi^{\prime\prime}\land\psi\land\theta_{k+1})\to\mathbb{N}
be a solution to
{\gamma^{\prime\prime}\land\psi^{\prime\prime}\land\psi\land\theta_{k+1}}
. Define
g\coloneqq\nu(q_{x})\cdot 2^{\nu(y)}+\nu(r_{x})
.
The map
{\nu+[x\mapsto g]+[u\mapsto 2^{g-\nu(y)}]}
is a solution to
\langle{\gamma^{\prime}}\mathbin{;}{\psi^{\prime}}\rangle
.

3.

If
\max\{{C^{\prime\prime}}[{x_{m}}](\nu):\text{$\nu$ is a solution to $\langle{\gamma^{\prime}}\mathbin{;}{\psi^{\prime}}\rangle$}\}
exists, it
is equal to

\displaystyle\max\{{C^{\prime\prime}}[{x_{m}}](\nu):{}

for some
(\gamma^{\prime\prime},\psi^{\prime\prime})\in E_{3}(\gamma^{\prime})
, the map
\nu
is a solution to

\displaystyle\hskip 14.22636pt\text{the formula~$\gamma^{\prime\prime}\land\psi^{\prime\prime}\land\psi\land\theta_{k+1}\land(u=2^{x-y})\land(x=q_{x}\cdot 2^{y}+r_{x})$}\}.

(In
Item

3
, the constraints
u=2^{x-y}
and
x=q_{x}\cdot 2^{y}+r_{x}
are added to handle the fact that

still appears as a free variable of

. This discrepancy is resolved in line

.)

Step IV (lines
19
–
23
).

Let
(C^{\prime\prime},\gamma^{\prime},\psi^{\prime})\in E_{2}(\gamma,\psi)
and
(\gamma^{\prime\prime},\psi^{\prime\prime})\in E_{3}(\gamma^{\prime})
, for some
(\gamma,\psi)\in E_{1}
.
Step IV removes the quotient variable
q_{x}
from the linear program with divisions
\gamma^{\prime\prime}
,
and translates the
(k,k)
-LEAC into a
(k+1)
-
PreLEAC
.
Let us treat each equality
\tau=0
in
\gamma^{\prime\prime}
as a conjunction of two inequalities:
\tau\leq 0\land-\tau\leq 0
.
Since
\gamma^{\prime\prime}
contains only the variable
q_{x}
, every inequality in it is either of the form
a\leq b\cdot q_{x}
or
b\cdot q_{x}\leq a
, with
b
non-negative.
Let us update
\gamma^{\prime\prime}
by rewriting these as
\left\lceil\frac{a}{b}\right\rceil\leq q_{x}
and
q_{x}\leq\left\lfloor\frac{a}{b}\right\rfloor
, respectively. Line
19
computes the greatest non-negative integer
\ell
such that
\ell\leq q_{x}
occurs in
\gamma^{\prime\prime}
, while Line
20

computes the smallest integer
h
such that
q_{x}\leq h
occurs in
\gamma^{\prime\prime}
.
By default,
\ell
and
h
are initialized as
0
and
\infty
, respectively, so in particular we always have
\ell\geq 0
.
If
h=\infty
, the algorithm updates it to
\ell+\textit{mod}(\gamma^{\prime\prime})
in line
21
, ensuring that at least one solution to
\gamma^{\prime\prime}
is explored (if one exists).
Let us write
B(\gamma^{\prime\prime})
for the set
\{v\in[\ell..h]:\text{$\gamma^{\prime\prime}(v)$ holds}\}
.
Step IV concludes by guessing
v\in B(\gamma^{\prime\prime})
(line
22
), to then translating
C^{\prime\prime}
into a
(k+1)
-
PreLEAC
following
Remark

4
. If
B(\gamma^{\prime\prime})
is empty then
\gamma^{\prime\prime}
is unsatisfiable; in this case the
guess
instruction fails, and the non-deterministic branch of the algorithm rejects.

Let us write
E_{4}(C^{\prime\prime},\gamma^{\prime\prime})
for the set of all circuits obtained from
C^{\prime\prime}
when running line
23
, with respect to some
v\in B(\gamma^{\prime\prime})
.
We show the following claim (whose proof clarifies why it is sufficient to restrict
q_{x}
to values in
[\ell..h]
in order to explore optimal solutions).

Claim 16.

Let
(\gamma,\psi)\in E_{1}
,
(C^{\prime\prime},\gamma^{\prime},\psi^{\prime})\in E_{2}(\gamma,\psi)

and
(\gamma^{\prime\prime},\psi^{\prime\prime})\in E_{3}(\gamma^{\prime})
.
We have:

1.

If
B(\gamma^{\prime\prime})
is non-empty, then

\exists q_{x}(\gamma^{\prime\prime}\land\psi^{\prime\prime}\land\psi\land\theta_{k+1})

is equivalent to
\psi^{\prime\prime}\land\psi\land\theta_{k+1}
.

2.

Let
v\in B(\gamma^{\prime\prime})
, and let
\nu\colon\textup{vars}(\psi^{\prime\prime}\land\psi\land\theta_{k+1})\to\mathbb{N}
be a solution to
{\psi^{\prime\prime}\land\psi\land\theta_{k+1}}
. Then, the map
{\nu+[q_{x}\mapsto v]}
is a solution to
\gamma^{\prime\prime}\land\psi^{\prime\prime}\land\psi\land\theta_{k+1}
.

3.

If

M\coloneqq\max\{{C^{\prime\prime}}[{x_{m}}](\nu):{}\text{for some $(\gamma^{\prime\prime},\psi^{\prime\prime})\in E_{3}(\gamma^{\prime})$, the map~$\nu$ is a solution to the formula }

\gamma^{\prime\prime}\land\psi^{\prime\prime}\land\psi\land\theta_{k+1}\land(u=2^{x-y})\land(x=q_{x}\cdot 2^{y}+r_{x})\}
exists, it
is equal to

\max\{{C^{*}}[{x_{m}}](\nu):\text{$\nu$ is a solution to $\psi^{\prime\prime}\land\psi\land\theta_{k+1}$, for some $C^{*}\in E_{4}(C^{\prime\prime},\gamma^{\prime\prime})$}\}.

Proof.

In the formula
\gamma^{\prime\prime}\land\psi^{\prime\prime}\land\psi\land\theta_{k+1}
,
the variable
q_{x}
only occurs in
\gamma^{\prime\prime}
.
Then, the left-to-right direction of
Item

1
follows from
Corollary

2
,
whereas the right-to-left direction holds from
Item

2
,
which in turn follows directly from the definition of
B(\gamma^{\prime\prime})
.

We prove
Item

3
.
Let
\Psi\coloneqq(\gamma^{\prime\prime}\land\psi^{\prime\prime}\land\psi\land\theta_{k+1}\land(u=2^{x-y})\land(x=q_{x}\cdot 2^{y}+r_{x}))
.
We divide the proof depending on the variable
x_{m}
we are maximizing:

case:
m<n-k
.

Suppose
M
exists.
The circuit
C^{\prime\prime}
does not feature an assignment to the variable
x_{m}
, and the same is true for every
C^{*}\in E_{4}(C^{\prime\prime},\gamma^{\prime\prime})
.
For both
C^{\prime\prime}
and
C^{*}
, given a map
\nu
from their free variables plus
x_{m}
to
\mathbb{N}
, we have
{C^{\prime\prime}}[{x_{m}}](\nu)=\nu(x_{m})={C^{*}}[{x_{m}}](\nu)
. Let
\nu
be a solution to
\Psi
.
Since
\nu
is also a solution to
{\psi^{\prime\prime}\land\psi\land\theta_{k+1}}
,
we have
\max\{{C^{*}}[{x_{m}}](\nu):\nu
is a solution to
{\psi^{\prime\prime}\land\psi\land\theta_{k+1}}
, for some
C^{*}\in E_{4}(C^{\prime\prime},\gamma^{\prime\prime})\}\geq M
.

Ad absurdum
, assume
{C^{*}}[{x_{m}}](\nu^{*})>M
, for some solution
{\nu^{*}\colon\textup{vars}(\psi^{\prime\prime}\land\psi\land\theta_{k+1})\to\mathbb{N}}
to
\psi^{\prime\prime}\land\psi\land\theta_{k+1}
, and
C^{*}\in E_{4}(C^{\prime\prime},\gamma^{\prime\prime})
.
By definition,
C^{*}
is constructed from
C^{\prime\prime}
by replacing
q_{x}
with some
{v\in B(\gamma^{\prime\prime})}
, and
u
with
2^{x-y}
.
By
Item

2
,
\nu^{\prime}\coloneqq\nu^{*}+[q_{x}\mapsto v]

is a solution to
\gamma^{\prime\prime}\land\psi^{\prime\prime}\land\psi\land\theta_{k+1}
. Define
g\coloneqq\nu^{\prime}(q_{x})\cdot 2^{\nu^{\prime}(y)}+\nu^{\prime}(r_{x})
.
By
15
.
2
,
the map
{\nu^{\prime\prime}\coloneqq\nu^{\prime}+[x\mapsto g]+[u\mapsto 2^{g-\nu^{\prime}(y)}]}
is a solution to
\langle{\gamma^{\prime}}\mathbin{;}{\psi^{\prime}}\rangle
.
Since
\psi^{\prime}
implies
u=2^{x-y}\land x=q_{x}\cdot 2^{y}+r_{x}
,
we conclude that
\nu^{\prime\prime}
is a solution to
\Psi
.
We have
{C^{\prime\prime}}[{x_{m}}](\nu^{\prime\prime})=\nu^{\prime\prime}(x_{m})=\nu^{*}(x_{m})={C^{*}}[{x_{m}}](\nu^{*})>M
, contradicting
the fact that
M
is maximal.
Therefore,
Item

3
holds.

case:
m\geq n-k
.

First, let us consider the case where
h\neq\infty
when defined in line
20
. Then, all the solutions to
\gamma^{\prime\prime}
lie in the interval
[\ell..h]
. The set
E_{4}(C^{\prime\prime},\gamma^{\prime\prime})
is
constructed to consider all these solutions, and therefore
Item

3
follows.

Suppose instead that
h=\infty
in line
20
.
In this case, we establish
Item

3
by showing that
M
does not exist.
Consider an arbitrary solution
\nu
to
\Psi
.
Let
p\coloneqq\textit{mod}(\gamma^{\prime\prime})
. Since
h=\infty
, we note that increasing
\nu(q_{x})
by any positive multiple of
p
, and increasing
\nu(x)
and
\nu(u)
accordingly, still yields a solution to
\Psi
.
More precisely, if we want to increase the
\nu(q_{x})

by
i\cdot p
, with
i\in\mathbb{N}
,
the resulting map is

\nu+[q_{x}\mapsto i\cdot p]+[x\mapsto i\cdot p\cdot 2^{\nu(y)}]+[u\mapsto(2^{i\cdot p\cdot 2^{\nu(y)}}-1)\cdot 2^{\nu(x)-\nu(y)}]
.
By definition,
C^{\prime\prime}
contains the assignment
x\leftarrow q_{x}\cdot 2^{y}+r_{x}
. Therefore, arbitrarily increasing
\nu(q_{x})
results in arbitrarily large values that
x
evaluates to in
{C^{\prime\prime}}[{x_{m}}]
.

Let
\nu
be a solution to
\Psi
.
To complete the proof, it suffices to show that the value taken by
x
when evaluating
{C^{\prime\prime}}[{x_{m}}]
on
\nu
is at most
{C^{\prime\prime}}[{x_{m}}](\nu)
,
in other words, that
{C^{\prime\prime}}[{x_{n-k}}](\nu)\leq{C^{\prime\prime}}[{x_{m}}](\nu)
.
From
15
.
2
,
\nu
is also a solution to
\langle{\gamma^{\prime}}\mathbin{;}{\psi^{\prime}}\rangle
. By
14
.
4
,
{(C^{\prime\prime},\langle{\gamma^{\prime}}\mathbin{;}{\psi^{\prime}}\rangle)}
belongs to
\mathcal{I}_{k}^{k}
. By definition of
\mathcal{I}_{k}^{k}
,

\langle{\gamma^{\prime}}\mathbin{;}{\psi^{\prime}}\rangle
implies
{\exists\bm{q}_{k-1}\exists\bm{x}_{k-1}\big(\theta_{0}\land\textstyle\bigwedge_{i=1}^{t}(y_{i}=\rho_{i})\big)}
,
where
(y_{1}\leftarrow\rho_{1},\dots,y_{t}\leftarrow\rho_{t})=C^{\prime\prime}
.
The variables in
\bm{q}_{k-1}
and
\bm{x}_{k-1}
are all among
y_{1},\dots,y_{t}
, meaning that there is exactly one evaluation for these variables
for which
\theta_{0}\land\textstyle\bigwedge_{i=1}^{t}(y_{i}=\rho_{i})
is satisfied: the values that these variables take when
{C^{\prime\prime}}[{x_{m}}]
is evaluated on
\nu
.
Since
m\geq n-k
, the ordering
\theta_{0}
implies
x_{n-k}\leq x_{m}
.
Hence, when evaluating
{C^{\prime\prime}}[{x_{m}}]
on
\nu
, the value taken by
x
is at most
{C^{\prime\prime}}[{x_{m}}](\nu)
, as required.
∎

Step V (lines
24
–
26
).

These lines simply prepare the linear-exponential system for the next loop iteration.
Let
(\gamma,\psi)\in E_{1}
,
(C^{\prime\prime},\gamma^{\prime},\psi^{\prime})\in E_{2}(\gamma,\psi)
,
(\gamma^{\prime\prime},\psi^{\prime\prime})\in E_{3}(\gamma^{\prime})
and
{C^{*}\in E_{4}(C^{\prime\prime},\gamma^{\prime\prime})}
.
Line
24
sets
\bm{r}^{\prime}
as the remainder variables for the next iterations of the loop (in this proof,
\bm{r}_{k}
).
Line
25
sets
\varphi^{*}\coloneqq\psi\land\psi^{\prime\prime}
as the formula for the next iteration, whereas line
26
updates
\theta
to
\theta_{k+1}
. This concludes the body of the
while
loop, and
T_{k+1}
is the set of all possible triples
(C^{*},\theta_{k+1},\varphi^{*})
.

Let us now complete the proof by showing that all items in the loop invariant are satisfied.

•

Item

I
1

.
:

\theta_{k+1}
is indeed the ordering required by this item.

•

Item

I
2

.
:
In
\varphi^{*}=\psi\land\psi^{\prime\prime}
, both
\psi
and
\psi^{\prime\prime}
are linear-exponential programs with divisions in variables
\bm{y}_{k+1}
and
\bm{r}^{\prime}
. Moreover,
\psi
(which was defined in Step I), implies
\bm{r}^{\prime}<2^{y}
, as required.

•

Item

I
3

.
:
This follows directly from the manipulation performed in line
23
to construct
C^{*}
, recalling that
C^{\prime\prime}
is a
(k,k)
-LEAC.
Below, let
C^{*}=(x_{n-k}\leftarrow\frac{\tau_{n-k}^{*}}{\mu^{*}}+r_{n-k}^{\prime}\,,\,\dots\,,\,x_{n}\leftarrow\frac{\tau_{n}^{*}}{\mu^{*}}+r_{n}^{\prime})
.

•

Item

I
4

.
:
We must show that, given a solution
\nu\colon\textup{vars}(\varphi^{*}\land\theta_{k+1})\to\mathbb{N}
to
\varphi^{*}\land\theta_{k+1}
, the map
\nu+\textstyle\sum_{i=0}^{k}[x_{n-i}\mapsto g_{n-i}]
,
where
g_{n-i}
is the value taken by
x_{n-i}
when evaluating
{C^{*}}[{x_{m}}]
on
\nu
, is a solution to
\varphi_{0}\land\theta_{0}
.
In a nutshell, this is shown by appealing to
the second Items in Claims
13
–
16
, to
then apply the induction hypothesis.
Indeed, observe that starting from
\nu
, these Items
construct a solution
\nu^{*}
for
\varphi\land\theta
.
Recall that the initial circuit
C

is a
k
-
PreLEAC

\displaystyle\hskip 11.38092ptx_{n-i}

\displaystyle\leftarrow\frac{\tau_{n-i}}{\mu}+r_{n-i}

\displaystyle\hskip-28.45274pt\text{for $i$ from $k-1$ to $0$}.

This circuit is then manipulated into the circuit
C^{\prime}

\displaystyle x

\displaystyle\leftarrow q_{x}\cdot 2^{y}+r_{x},

\displaystyle\hskip 11.38092ptx_{n-i}

\displaystyle\leftarrow\frac{\tau_{n-i}}{\mu}+q_{n-i}\cdot 2^{y}+r_{n-i}^{\prime}

\displaystyle\hskip-28.45274pt\text{for $i$ from $k-1$ to $0$},

and
C^{\prime\prime}
is constructed from
C^{\prime}

by adding assignments
q_{n-i}\leftarrow\frac{\tau_{n-i}^{\prime\prime}(u,q_{x})}{\eta}
for every
i\in[0..k-1]
.
Lastly,
C^{*}
is essentially obtained from
C^{\prime\prime}

by replacing
q_{x}
the integer
v
from line
22
, and
u
with
2^{x-y}
.
By induction hypothesis, the map obtained from
\nu^{*}

by adding
\sum_{i=0}^{k-1}[x_{n-i}\mapsto t_{n-i}]
,
where
t_{n-i}
is the value taken by
x_{n-i}
when evaluating
{C}[{x_{m}}]
on
\nu^{*}

is a solution to
\varphi_{0}\land\theta_{0}
.
Then, because of the updates required to obtain
C^{*}
from
C
, it suffices to show that

\displaystyle\nu^{*}(x)

\displaystyle=v\cdot 2^{\nu(y)}+\nu(r_{x}),

\displaystyle\nu^{*}(r_{n-i})

\displaystyle=\frac{\tau_{n-i}^{\prime\prime}(2^{\nu^{*}(x_{n})-\nu(y)},v)}{\eta}\cdot 2^{\nu(y)}+\nu(r_{n-i}^{\prime})

for
i
from
k-1
to
0
.

This follows directly from the identities below:

\displaystyle\nu^{\prime}

\displaystyle=\nu+[q_{x}\mapsto v]

\displaystyle\Lbag\text{\lx@cref{creftypecap\lx@tilde refnum}{claim:optilep:correcntess:stepIV}.\ref{claim:optilep:correcntess:stepIV:i2}}\Rbag

\displaystyle\nu^{\prime\prime}

\displaystyle=\nu^{\prime}+[x\mapsto g]+[u\mapsto 2^{g-\nu(y)}],\text{\quad where }g\coloneqq v\cdot 2^{\nu(y)}+\nu(r_{x})

\displaystyle\Lbag\text{\lx@cref{creftypecap\lx@tilde refnum}{claim:optilep:correcntess:stepIII}.\ref{claim:optilep:correcntess:stepIII:i2}}\Rbag

\displaystyle\nu^{\prime\prime\prime}

\displaystyle=\nu^{\prime\prime}+\textstyle\sum_{i=0}^{k-1}[q_{n-i}\mapsto\frac{\nu^{\prime\prime}(\tau_{n-i}^{\prime\prime})}{\eta}]

\displaystyle\Lbag\text{\lx@cref{creftypecap\lx@tilde refnum}{claim:optilep:correcntess:stepII}.\ref{claim:optilep:correcntess:stepII:i2}}\Rbag

\displaystyle\nu^{*}

\displaystyle=\nu^{\prime\prime\prime}+\textstyle\sum_{i=0}^{k-1}[r_{n-i}\mapsto\nu^{\prime\prime\prime}(q_{n-i})\cdot 2^{\nu(y)}+\nu(r_{n-i}^{\prime})]

\displaystyle\Lbag\text{\lx@cref{creftypecap\lx@tilde refnum}{claim:optilep:correcntess:stepI}.\ref{claim:optilep:correcntess:stepI:i2}}\Rbag

Let us now show Item

I
5

′

.
; then Item

I
5

.

follows directly from the induction hypothesis:

\displaystyle\exists x\exists\bm{r}_{k-1}(\varphi\land\theta)

\displaystyle\iff{}

\displaystyle\exists x\exists\bm{q}_{k}\exists u\exists\bm{r}^{\prime}\textstyle\bigvee_{(\gamma,\psi)\in E_{1}}\Phi(\gamma,\psi)

\displaystyle\Lbag\text{\lx@cref{creftypecap\lx@tilde refnum}{claim:optilep:correcntess:stepI}.\ref{claim:optilep:correcntess:stepI:i1}}\Rbag

\displaystyle\iff{}

\displaystyle\exists x\exists q_{x}\exists u\exists\bm{r}^{\prime}\textstyle\bigvee_{\begin{subarray}{c}(\gamma,\psi)\in E_{1},\,(C^{\prime\prime},\gamma^{\prime},\psi^{\prime})\in E_{2}(\gamma,\psi)\end{subarray}}\langle{\gamma^{\prime}}\mathbin{;}{\psi^{\prime}}\rangle

\displaystyle\Lbag\text{\lx@cref{creftypecap\lx@tilde refnum}{claim:optilep:correcntess:stepII}.\ref{claim:optilep:correcntess:stepII:i1}}\Rbag

\displaystyle\iff{}

\displaystyle\exists q_{x}\exists\bm{r}^{\prime}\textstyle\bigvee_{\begin{subarray}{c}(\gamma,\psi)\in E_{1},\,(C^{\prime\prime},\gamma^{\prime},\psi^{\prime})\in E_{2}(\gamma,\psi),\,(\gamma^{\prime\prime},\psi^{\prime\prime})\in E_{3}(\gamma^{\prime})\end{subarray}}\ (\gamma^{\prime\prime}\land\psi^{\prime\prime}\land\psi\land\theta_{k+1})

\displaystyle\Lbag\text{\lx@cref{creftypecap\lx@tilde refnum}{claim:optilep:correcntess:stepIII}.\ref{claim:optilep:correcntess:stepIII:i1}}\Rbag

\displaystyle\iff{}

\displaystyle\exists\bm{r}^{\prime}\textstyle\bigvee_{\begin{subarray}{c}(\gamma,\psi)\in E_{1},\,(C^{\prime\prime},\gamma^{\prime},\psi^{\prime})\in E_{2}(\gamma,\psi),\,(\gamma^{\prime\prime},\psi^{\prime\prime})\in E_{3}(\gamma^{\prime})\,\text{s.t.}\,B(\gamma^{\prime\prime})\neq\emptyset\end{subarray}}\ (\psi^{\prime\prime}\land\psi\land\theta_{k+1})

\displaystyle\Lbag\text{\lx@cref{creftypecap\lx@tilde refnum}{claim:optilep:correcntess:stepIV}.\ref{claim:optilep:correcntess:stepIV:i1}}\Rbag

\displaystyle\iff{}

\displaystyle\exists\bm{r}^{\prime}\textstyle\bigvee_{(C^{*},\theta_{k+1},\varphi^{*})\in T_{k+1}}\ (\varphi^{*}\land\theta_{k+1})

\displaystyle\Lbag\text{def.~of $T_{k+1}$}\Rbag

Lastly, we show Item

I
6

′

.
, which implies Item

I
6

.

by induction hypothesis. Suppose that
M\coloneqq\max\{{C}[{x_{m}}](\nu):\text{$\nu$ is a solution to $\varphi\land\theta$}\}
exists.
Then,

\displaystyle M

\displaystyle=\max\{{C^{\prime}}[{x_{m}}](\nu):\text{$\nu$ is a solution to $\Phi(\gamma,\psi)$, for some $(\gamma,\psi)\in E_{1}$}\}

\displaystyle\hskip-85.35826pt\Lbag\text{\lx@cref{creftypecap\lx@tilde refnum}{claim:optilep:correcntess:stepI}.\ref{claim:optilep:correcntess:stepI:i3}}\Rbag

\displaystyle=\max\{{C^{\prime\prime}}[{x_{m}}](\nu):\text{$\nu$ is a solution to $\langle{\gamma^{\prime}}\mathbin{;}{\psi^{\prime}}\rangle$, for some $(\gamma,\psi)\in E_{1}$,}

\displaystyle\hphantom{= \max\lx@text@lbrace${C^{\prime\prime}}[{x_{m}}]$ (\nu) : \text{$\nu$ is a solution to $\langle{\gamma^{\prime}}\mathbin{;}{\psi^{\prime}}\rangle$}}\hskip 22.76228pt\text{and $(C^{\prime\prime},\gamma^{\prime},\psi^{\prime})\in E_{2}(\gamma,\psi)$}\}

\displaystyle\hskip-85.35826pt\Lbag\text{\lx@cref{creftypecap\lx@tilde refnum}{claim:optilep:correcntess:stepII}.\ref{claim:optilep:correcntess:stepII:i3}}\Rbag

\displaystyle=\max\{{C^{\prime\prime}}[{x_{m}}](\nu):\text{$\nu$ is a solution to $\gamma^{\prime\prime}\land\psi^{\prime\prime}\land\psi\land\theta_{k+1}\land(u=2^{x-y})\land(x=q_{x}\cdot 2^{y}+r_{x})$}

\displaystyle\hskip 59.75095pt\text{for some~$(\gamma,\psi)\in E_{1}$, $(C^{\prime\prime},\gamma^{\prime},\psi^{\prime})\in E_{2}(\gamma,\psi)$, and $(\gamma^{\prime\prime},\psi^{\prime\prime})\in E_{3}(\gamma^{\prime})$}\}

\displaystyle\hskip-85.35826pt\Lbag\text{\lx@cref{creftypecap\lx@tilde refnum}{claim:optilep:correcntess:stepIII}.\ref{claim:optilep:correcntess:stepIII:i3}}\Rbag

\displaystyle=\max\{{C^{*}}[{x_{m}}](\nu):\text{$\nu$ is a solution to $\psi^{\prime\prime}\land\psi\land\theta_{k+1}$, for some $(\gamma,\psi)\in E_{1}$,}

\displaystyle\hskip 59.75095pt\text{ $(C^{\prime\prime},\gamma^{\prime},\psi^{\prime})\in E_{2}(\gamma,\psi)$, $(\gamma^{\prime\prime},\psi^{\prime\prime})\in E_{3}(\gamma^{\prime})$, and $C^{*}\in E_{4}(C^{\prime\prime},\gamma^{\prime\prime})$}\}

\displaystyle\hskip-85.35826pt\Lbag\text{\lx@cref{creftypecap\lx@tilde refnum}{claim:optilep:correcntess:stepIV}.\ref{claim:optilep:correcntess:stepIV:i3}}\Rbag

\displaystyle=\max\{{C^{*}}[{x_{m}}](\nu):\text{$\nu$ is a solution to $\varphi^{*}\land\theta_{k+1}$, for some $(C^{*}\!,\theta_{k+1},\varphi^{*})\in T_{k+1}$\lx@text@rbrace}

\displaystyle\hskip-46.94687pt\Lbag\text{def.~of~$T_{k+1}$}\Rbag

Therefore, the loop invariant holds, completing the proof of the proposition.
∎

6.3
Complexity of
OptILEP

We now provide the complexity analysis of
OptILEP
,
which requires tracking several parameters of linear-exponential systems.
For a linear-exponential program with divisibilities
\varphi
, we track:

•

The parameters
\#{\varphi}
,
\lVert{\varphi}\rVert_{1}
and
\textit{mod}(\varphi)
, defined in the preliminaries (page
•
‣
I
).

•

The
linear norm

\lVert{\varphi}\rVert_{\!\mathfrak{L}}\coloneqq\max\{\lVert{\tau}\rVert_{\!\mathfrak{L}}:\text{$\tau$ is a term appearing in an equality or inequality of $\varphi$}\}
.

Given a linear-exponential term
\tau={\sum\nolimits_{i=1}^{n}\big(a_{i}\cdot x_{i}+b_{i}\cdot 2^{x_{i}}+\sum\nolimits_{j=1}^{n}c_{i,j}\cdot(x_{i}\bmod 2^{x_{j}})\big)+d}
,
we define its linear norm as
\lVert{\tau}\rVert_{\!\mathfrak{L}}\coloneqq\max\{\left|a_{i}\right|,\left|c_{i,j}\right|:i,j\in[1..n]\}
.

•

Consider an ordering of exponentiated variables
\theta\coloneqq(\theta(\bm{x})\coloneqq{2^{x_{n}}\geq 2^{x_{n-1}}\geq\dots\geq 2^{x_{0}}=1})
.
Let
\bm{r}
be the variables from
\varphi
that are not in
\bm{x}
.
We track the set of the
least significant terms

\displaystyle\textit{lst}(\varphi,\theta)\coloneqq\big\{\pm\rho:{}

\rho
is the least significant part of a term
\tau
appearing in

\displaystyle\text{an equality or inequality $\tau\sim 0$ of $\varphi$,
with respect to $\theta$\,}\big\}.

The
least significant part
of a term
a\cdot 2^{x_{n}}+b\cdot x_{n}+\tau^{\prime}(x_{0},\dots,x_{n-1},\bm{r})
with respect to
\theta

is defined as the term
b\cdot x_{n}+\tau^{\prime}
.

For a
k
-
PreLEAC

C
, we track
the growth of the parameters
\mu_{C}
and
\xi_{C}
.

Remark 5.

From the above parameters one can bound the sizes of
\varphi
and
C
: the size of
\varphi(\bm{x})
is in
\text{poly}(\#{\varphi},\#\bm{x},\log\lVert{\varphi}\rVert_{1},\log\textit{mod}(\varphi))
,
and the size of a
k
-
PreLEAC

C
is in
\text{poly}(k,\log\mu_{C},\log\xi_{C})
.

The complexity of Steps I and III from
[
CMS24
]
.

For the complexity analysis of lines
11
and
18
of
OptILEP
, which correspond to Steps I and III of
[
CMS24
]
, we refer directly to the analysis carried out in
[
CMS24
]
. (We remind the reader that
Appendix

B
gives more information on these two steps).
This analysis is reported in the following two lemmas.

Lemma 22
[
CMS24
]
.

The algorithm from
Lemma

4
(Step I) runs in non-deterministic polynomial time.
Consider its execution on an input
(\theta,\varphi)
where
\theta(\bm{x})
is an ordering of exponentiated variables and
\varphi(\bm{x},\bm{r})
is a linear exponential program with divisions. In each non-deterministic branch
\beta
, the algorithm returns a pair
(\gamma,\psi)
, where
\gamma(q_{x},\bm{q},u)
is a linear program with divisions and
\psi(\bm{y},r_{x},\bm{r}^{\prime})
a linear-exponential program with divisions,
such that (for every
\ell,s,a,c,d\geq 1
):

\text{if\ }\begin{cases}\#\textit{lst}(\varphi,\theta)&\hskip-5.0pt\leq\ell\\
\#\varphi&\hskip-5.0pt\leq s\\
\lVert{\varphi}\rVert_{\!\mathfrak{L}}&\hskip-5.0pt\leq a\\
\lVert{\varphi}\rVert_{1}&\hskip-5.0pt\leq c\\
\textit{mod}(\varphi)&\hskip-5.0pt\hskip 3.0pt\mathrel{|}\hskip 2.0ptd\end{cases}\text{\ then\ }\begin{cases}\#\textit{lst}(\psi,\theta^{\prime})&\hskip-5.0pt\leq\ell+2\cdot k\\
\#\psi&\hskip-5.0pt\leq s+6\cdot k+2\cdot\ell\\
\lVert{\psi}\rVert_{\!\mathfrak{L}}&\hskip-5.0pt\leq 3\cdot a\\
\lVert{\psi}\rVert_{1}&\hskip-5.0pt\leq 4\cdot c+5\\
\textit{mod}(\psi)&\hskip-5.0pt\hskip 3.0pt\mathrel{|}\hskip 2.0ptd\\
\end{cases}\text{and\ }\begin{cases}\#\gamma&\hskip-5.0pt\leq s+2\cdot k\\
\lVert{\gamma[2^{u}\,/\,u]}\rVert_{\!\mathfrak{L}}&\hskip-5.0pt\leq 3\cdot a\\
\lVert{\gamma}\rVert_{1}&\hskip-5.0pt\leq 2\cdot c+3\\
\textit{mod}(\gamma)&\hskip-5.0pt\hskip 3.0pt\mathrel{|}\hskip 2.0ptd\end{cases}

where
\theta^{\prime}
is the ordering obtained from
\theta
by removing its largest term
2^{x}
, and
k\coloneqq 1+\#{\bm{r}}
.

Below,
\phi
denotes Euler’s totient function.
Recall that given a positive integer
a
,

\displaystyle\phi(a)\coloneqq\textstyle\prod_{i=1}^{k}\big((p_{i}-1)\cdot p_{i}^{e_{i}-1}\big),\text{ where $p_{1}^{e_{1}}\cdots p_{k}^{e_{k}}$ is the prime factorization of $a$.}

(23)

Lemma 23
[
CMS24
]
.

The algorithm from
Lemma

6
(Step III) runs in non-deterministic polynomial time.
Consider its execution on an input linear program with divisions
\gamma^{\prime}
.
In each non-deterministic branch
\beta
, the algorithm returns a pair
(\gamma^{\prime\prime},\psi^{\prime\prime})
,
where
\gamma^{\prime\prime}
is a linear program with divisions and
\psi^{\prime\prime}
is a linear-exponential program
with divisions, such that (for every
s,a,c,d\geq 1
):

\text{if}\,\begin{cases}\#\gamma^{\prime}&\hskip-9.0pt\leq s\\
\lVert{\gamma^{\prime}[2^{u}\,/\,u]}\rVert_{\!\mathfrak{L}}&\hskip-9.0pt\leq a\\
\lVert{\gamma^{\prime}}\rVert_{1}&\hskip-9.0pt\leq c\\
\textit{mod}(\gamma^{\prime})&\hskip-9.0pt\hskip 3.0pt\mathrel{|}\hskip 2.0ptd\end{cases}\text{ \ then}\,\begin{cases}\#\gamma^{\prime\prime}&\hskip-9.0pt\leq s+2\\
\lVert{\gamma^{\prime\prime}}\rVert_{\!\mathfrak{L}}&\hskip-9.0pt\leq a\\
\lVert{\gamma^{\prime\prime}}\rVert_{1}&\hskip-9.0pt\leq\max(2^{5}c^{3},c\cdot d)\\
\textit{mod}(\gamma^{\prime\prime})&\hskip-9.0pt\hskip 3.0pt\mathrel{|}\hskip 2.0pt{\rm lcm}(d,\phi(d))\\
\end{cases}\text{ \ and}\,\begin{cases}\#\psi^{\prime\prime}&\hskip-9.0pt\leq 3\\
\lVert{\psi^{\prime\prime}}\rVert_{\!\mathfrak{L}}&\hskip-9.0pt\leq 1\\
\lVert{\psi^{\prime\prime}}\rVert_{1}&\hskip-9.0pt\leq 12+4\,{\cdot}\log(\max(c,d))\\
\textit{mod}(\psi^{\prime\prime})&\hskip-9.0pt\hskip 3.0pt\mathrel{|}\hskip 2.0pt\phi(d)\end{cases}

The complexity of performing one iteration of the main loop is given in the next lemma:

Lemma 24.

Consider the execution of
Algorithm

6
on an linear-exponential program
\varphi(x_{1},\dots,x_{n})
, with
n\geq 1
. Let
(\varphi,\theta,C)
be the system, circuit, and ordering obtained after the
k
th iteration
of the
while
loop of line
8
.
The
(k+1)
th iteration of the
while
loop runs in non-deterministic polynomial time in the bit sizes of
\varphi
and
C
.
Each non-deterministic execution of the loop updates the triple
(\varphi,\theta,C)

into a triple
(\varphi^{\prime},\theta^{\prime},C^{\prime})
such that (for every
\ell,s,a,c,d\geq 1
):

if

\displaystyle\begin{cases}\#\textit{lst}(\varphi,\theta)&\hskip-5.0pt\leq\ell\\
\#\varphi&\hskip-5.0pt\leq s\\
\lVert{\varphi}\rVert_{\!\mathfrak{L}}&\hskip-5.0pt\leq a\\
\lVert{\varphi}\rVert_{1}&\hskip-5.0pt\leq c\\
\textit{mod}(\varphi)&\hskip-5.0pt\hskip 3.0pt\mathrel{|}\hskip 2.0ptd\end{cases}

then

\displaystyle\begin{cases}\#\textit{lst}(\varphi^{\prime},\theta^{\prime})&\hskip-5.0pt\leq\ell+2\cdot k+3\\
\#\varphi^{\prime}&\hskip-5.0pt\leq s+6\cdot k+2\cdot\ell+3\\
\lVert{\varphi^{\prime}}\rVert_{\!\mathfrak{L}}&\hskip-5.0pt\leq 3\cdot a\\
\lVert{\varphi^{\prime}}\rVert_{1}&\hskip-5.0pt\leq 12+4\cdot\max(c,\log\beta)\\
\textit{mod}(\varphi^{\prime})&\hskip-5.0pt\hskip 3.0pt\mathrel{|}\hskip 2.0pt{\rm lcm}{(d,\phi(\alpha\cdot d))}\\
\xi_{C^{\prime}}&\hskip-5.0pt\leq\xi_{C}\cdot(3\cdot k\cdot a)^{k}+2^{6}(k+1)\cdot\beta^{4}\\
\mu_{C^{\prime}}&\hskip-5.0pt\leq\mu_{C}\cdot(3\cdot k\cdot a)^{k},\end{cases}

with
\alpha\in[1..(3\cdot k\cdot\mu_{C}\cdot a)^{k^{2}}]
, and
\beta\coloneqq d\cdot\big(2^{7}(k+1)\cdot\mu_{C}\cdot\max(c,\log(\xi_{C}+\mu_{C}))\big)^{3(k+2)^{2}}
.

Proof.

Throughout the proof,
\mu
is short for
\mu_{C}
.
We analyze how the parameters evolve over the five steps of the while loop of
OptILEP
.

Step I (lines
9
–
13
).

Let
\gamma
and
\psi
be the systems computed by the algorithm in line
11
.
Directly from
Lemma

22
, we derive the following bounds on their parameters:

\begin{cases}\#\textit{lst}(\psi,\theta^{\prime})&\hskip-5.0pt\leq\ell+2\cdot k\\
\#\psi&\hskip-5.0pt\leq s+6\cdot k+2\cdot\ell\\
\lVert{\psi}\rVert_{\!\mathfrak{L}}&\hskip-5.0pt\leq 3\cdot a\\
\lVert{\psi}\rVert_{1}&\hskip-5.0pt\leq 4\cdot c+5\\
\textit{mod}(\psi)&\hskip-5.0pt\hskip 3.0pt\mathrel{|}\hskip 2.0ptd\\
\end{cases}\text{and\ }\begin{cases}\#\gamma&\hskip-5.0pt\leq s+2\cdot k\\
\lVert{\gamma[2^{u}\,/\,u]}\rVert_{\!\mathfrak{L}}&\hskip-5.0pt\leq 3\cdot a\\
\lVert{\gamma}\rVert_{1}&\hskip-5.0pt\leq 2\cdot c+3\\
\textit{mod}(\gamma)&\hskip-5.0pt\hskip 3.0pt\mathrel{|}\hskip 2.0ptd\end{cases}

Note that the updates performed to
C
in line
13

do not change the values of
\xi_{C}
and
\mu
.

Step II (lines
14
–
17
).

From
Lemma

4
, line
14
adds
k+1
inequalities of the form
q\geq 0
to
\gamma
(one for each quotient variable). The following line
15

multiplies all (in)equalities in
\gamma
by
\mu
.
Therefore, when the program reaches line
17
,
the formula
\gamma
satisfies the following:

\begin{cases}\#\gamma&\leq s+3k+1\\
\lVert{\gamma[2^{u}\,/\,u]}\rVert_{\!\mathfrak{L}}&\leq 3\cdot\mu\cdot a\\
\lVert{\gamma}\rVert_{1}&\leq\mu\cdot(2\cdot c+3)\\
\textit{mod}(\gamma)&\hskip 3.0pt\mathrel{|}\hskip 2.0ptd\end{cases}

Observe that the formula
\psi^{\prime}
constructed in line
16

has bit size polynomial in
\psi^{\prime}
. The procedure
ElimVars
does not update this formula (
Lemma

14
), moreover
\psi^{\prime}
is not used again within the loop.
Therefore, no further analysis on
\psi^{\prime}
is necessary.
Together with
\psi^{\prime}
, the call to
ElimVars
in line
17

returns a linear program with divisions
\gamma^{\prime}
and a
(k,k)
-LEAC
C^{*}
.
(More precisely, we have
(C^{*},\langle{\gamma^{\prime}}\mathbin{;}{\psi^{\prime}}\rangle)\in\mathcal{I}_{k}^{k}
.)
We bound the parameters of these two objects using
Lemma

21
.
In order to simplify the analysis,
let us define
M\coloneqq 2^{6}\cdot\mu\cdot\max(c,\log(\xi_{C}+\mu))
.
The values
L,Q,U
and
R
defined in
Lemma

21
are all bounded by
M
.
Furthermore, from the bound on
\lVert{\gamma[2^{u}\,/\,u]}\rVert_{\!\mathfrak{L}}
, we have
Q\leq 3\cdot\mu\cdot a
,
and therefore
\frac{Q}{\mu}\leq 3\cdot a
.

number of constraints in
\gamma^{\prime}
:

Directly from
Lemma

21
.
1
,
\#{\gamma^{\prime}}\leq s+4\cdot k+1
.

linear norm of
\gamma^{\prime}
:

Since
(C^{*},\langle{\gamma^{\prime}}\mathbin{;}{\psi^{\prime}}\rangle)\in\mathcal{I}_{k}^{k}
, the system
\gamma^{\prime}
only features the variable
u

and the quotient variable
q_{n-k}
. By
Lemma

21
.
3
, the linear norm of
\gamma^{\prime}
is thus:

\displaystyle\lVert{\gamma^{\prime}}\rVert_{\!\mathfrak{L}}

\displaystyle\leq\max{\Big({\textstyle\mu\cdot(k+1)^{k+1}\Big(\frac{Q}{\mu}\Big)^{k+1},(k+1)^{k+1}\Big(\frac{Q}{\mu}\Big)^{k}U}\Big)}

\displaystyle\leq\max{\Big(\mu\cdot(3\cdot a\cdot(k+1))^{k+1},(k+1)^{k+1}(3\cdot a)^{k}M\Big)}

\displaystyle\leq(3\cdot(k+1)\cdot a)^{k+1}M.

\displaystyle\Lbag\text{as $\mu\leq M$}\Rbag

1
-norm of
\gamma^{\prime}
:

First, by
Lemma

21
.
3
the bound on the constants of the terms from
\textup{terms}(\gamma^{\prime})
is

\displaystyle{\frac{((k+1)\cdot Q)^{2(k+2)^{2}}}{\mu^{2k^{2}}}\cdot\textit{mod}(\gamma)\cdot R}

\displaystyle\leq{}

\displaystyle((k+1)\cdot M)^{3(k+2)^{2}}d.

\displaystyle\Lbag\text{as $R,Q\leq M$ and $\textit{mod}(\gamma)\leq d$}\Rbag

Let us define
N\coloneqq 3\cdot((k+1)\cdot M)^{3(k+2)^{2}}
.
Terms in
\textup{terms}(\gamma^{\prime})
are of the form
b_{1}\cdot q+b_{2}\cdot 2^{u}+b_{3}
, where
\left|b_{1}\right|
and
\left|b_{2}\right|
are bounded by
\lVert{\gamma^{\prime}}\rVert_{\!\mathfrak{L}}\leq(3\cdot(k+1)\cdot a)^{k+1}M
. Therefore,
\lVert{\gamma^{\prime}}\rVert_{1}\leq N\cdot d
.

modulus of
\gamma^{\prime}
:

By
Lemma

21
.
4
,
\textit{mod}{(\gamma^{\prime})}\mathrel{|}\alpha\cdot\textit{mod}(\gamma)
, for some positive integer
\alpha\leq(3\cdot k\cdot\mu\cdot a)^{k^{2}}
.
(Observe that then
\bmod(\gamma^{\prime})\leq(k\cdot M)^{k^{2}}d
; we will silently use this fact when computing the bounds in
Equation

24
below.)

denominator
\eta_{C^{*}}
:

Lemma 25.
†
†
margin:

Proof in page
C.9

Algorithm

6
runs in non-deterministic polynomial time.
Consider its execution on an integer linear-exponential program
\varphi(x_{1},\ldots,x_{n})
with

. Let

\displaystyle\begin{cases}\#\textit{lst}(\varphi,\theta)&\leq\ell\\
\#\varphi&\leq s\\
\lVert{\varphi}\rVert_{\!\mathfrak{L}}&\leq a\\
\lVert{\varphi}\rVert_{1}&\leq c\\

\displaystyle\begin{cases}\#\textit{lst}(\varphi_{k},\theta_{k})&\leq\ell+3\cdot k^{2}\\

\lVert{\varphi_{k}}\rVert_{\!\mathfrak{L}}&\leq 3^{k}a\\
\lVert{\varphi_{k}}\rVert_{1}&\leq 3^{8(k+1)}c\\
\textit{mod}(\varphi_{k})&\leq 3^{2\cdot{k}^{8}}a^{2\cdot{k}^{7}}\\
\xi_{C_{k}}&\leq 3^{8(k+2)^{8}}c^{8(k+2)^{7}}\\
\mu_{C_{k}}&\leq 3^{k^{3}}a^{k^{2}}.\end{cases}

Proof sketch.

The proof is by induction on

, assuming as the induction hypothesis that the bounds stated hold at the
k
th iteration of the loop.
This hypothesis, combined with

24
, is sufficient to establish all bounds except for the one given to

,

the

,

such that

6.4
Maximization and minimization of arbitrary linear-exponential terms

Together,
Sections

6.2
and
6.3

establish
Theorem

1
for the case of maximizing a variable
x
under a linear-exponential program. We now complete the proof of
Theorem

1
by extending the argument to general linear-exponential objective functions, and to include the case of minimization.

Let us consider first the maximization problem

\text{maximize $\tau(\bm{x})$ subject to $\varphi(\bm{x})$},

(26)

where
\tau
is a linear-exponential term, and
\varphi
an integer linear-exponential program. Let
z
be a variable not in
\bm{x}
.
Since variables in ILEP range over
\mathbb{N}
, a common approach to solving
this problem is to distinguish two cases based on the sign of
\tau(\bm{x})
in the optimal solution The variable
z
is used to represent the value of
\tau(\bm{x})
, adjusting its sign accordingly when assuming
\tau
to be negative. Here is the corresponding pseudocode:

1:

if
(
maximize

z

subject to

\varphi(\bm{x})\land z=\tau(\bm{x})
) has an optimal solution
\sigma_{1}

then

return

\sigma_{1}

2:

if

\varphi(\bm{x})\land\tau(\bm{x})\geq 0
is satisfiable
then

return
“no optimal solution exists”

3:

if
(
minimize

z

subject to

\varphi(\bm{x})\land-z=\tau(\bm{x})
) has an optimal solution
\sigma_{2}

then

return

\sigma_{2}

4:

return
“
\varphi
is unsatisfiable”

From
Sections

6.2
and
6.3
, we know that the maximization problem in line
1

admits an optimal solution, then it has
one representable with a polynomial-size ILESLP.
Regarding the minimization problem in line
3
,
since
z
ranges over
\mathbb{N}
, a minimal solution
is guaranteed to exist as soon as
\varphi(\bm{x})\land-z=\tau(\bm{x})

is satisfiable. Again from
Sections

6.2
and
6.3

when
\varphi(\bm{x})\land-z=\tau(\bm{x})
is satisfiable,
then there is
a
solution representable with a polynomial-size ILESLP
\sigma
.
Then, to solve the minimization problem in line
3
,
we can consider the equivalent maximization problem “
maximize

\left\llbracket\sigma\right\rrbracket(z)-z

subject to

\varphi(\bm{x})\land-z=\tau(\bm{x})
”,
since
\left\llbracket\sigma\right\rrbracket(z)-z
attains its maximum precisely when
z
is minimal.
Given that
\left\llbracket\sigma\right\rrbracket(z)-z
is non-negative, this problem
can be reformulated as “
maximize

w

subject to

\varphi(\bm{x})\land(-z=\tau(\bm{x}))\land(w=\left\llbracket\sigma\right\rrbracket(z)-z)
”,
where
w
is a fresh variable.
Of course,
\left\llbracket\sigma\right\rrbracket(z)
may not be representable in binary
using polynomially many bits.
Instead, we incorporate directly the ILESLP
\sigma

directly into the constraints of the linear-exponential program.
To do so, we first rename every variable
y
occurring in
\sigma
as
y^{\prime}

to avoid conflicts with the variables
\bm{x}
,
z
and
w
.
Let
\sigma
be now of the form
(y_{0}^{\prime}\leftarrow\rho_{1},\dots,y_{t}\leftarrow\rho_{t})
. We then solve the following maximization problem

maximize

w

subject to

\varphi(\bm{x})\land(-z=\tau(\bm{x}))\land(w=z^{\prime}-z)\land\bigwedge_{i=1}^{t}(y_{i}^{\prime}=\rho_{i})
.

From
Sections

6.2
and
6.3
,
if this problem has an optimal solution,
then it has one representable with a polynomial-size ILESLP.
We conclude that the same holds for the problem in
Equation

26
.

We can treat the minimization problem

\text{minimize $\tau(\bm{x})$ subject to $\varphi(\bm{x})$},

in a similar way. Again following the sign
of
\tau
, this problem is solved as follows:

1:

if
(
maximize

z

subject to

\varphi(\bm{x})\land-z=\tau(\bm{x})
) has an optimal solution
\sigma_{1}

then

return

\sigma_{1}

2:

if

\varphi(\bm{x})\land\tau(\bm{x})<0
is satisfiable
then

return
“no optimal solution exists”

3:

if
(
minimize

z

subject to

\varphi(\bm{x})\land z=\tau(\bm{x})
) has an optimal solution
\sigma_{2}

then

return

\sigma_{2}

4:

return
“
\varphi
is unsatisfiable”

We already know that if one of the optimization problems in the code above has an optimal solution,
then it has one representable by a polynomial-size ILESLP.
This concludes the proof of
Theorem

1
.

Part II
Deciding properties of ILESLPs

In this second part of the paper, we discuss algorithms
for deciding
\textup{{Nat}}_{\scalebox{0.7}{{{ILESLP}}}}
and
\textup{{Div}}_{\scalebox{0.7}{{{ILESLP}}}}
(
Sections

7
and
8
, respectively),
and for computing ILESLPs representing terms of the form
{(x\bmod 2^{y})}
(
Section

9
).
This part is almost completely independent of
Part

I
,
the sole exception being an appeal to
Lemma

10

when proving that
\textup{{Nat}}_{\scalebox{0.7}{{{ILESLP}}}}
is in
P
.
We refer the reader to section
Section

1.1

for the definition of ILESLPs.

Some notation and an auxiliary lemma.

Let
\sigma\coloneqq(x_{0}\leftarrow\rho_{0},\dots,x_{n}\leftarrow\rho_{n})
be an ILESLP.
We write
e(\sigma)
(respectively,
d(\sigma)
) for the absolute value of the product of all numerators
m\neq 0
(respectively, denominators
g
) occurring in rational constants
\frac{m}{g}
of the scaling expressions
\frac{m}{g}\cdot x_{j}
in
\sigma
. By convention, this product is defined to be
1
when taken over an empty set.
Given an expression
{E\coloneqq\sum_{j\in J}a_{j}\cdot 2^{x_{j}}}
,
where
J\subseteq[0..n]
and each
a_{j}
is an integer,
we write
\left\llbracket\sigma\right\rrbracket(E)
for the number obtained by
evaluating

E
on
\sigma
, that is,
\left\llbracket\sigma\right\rrbracket(E)\coloneqq\sum_{j\in J}a_{j}\cdot 2^{\left\llbracket\sigma\right\rrbracket(x_{j})}
.
The next auxiliary lemma recasts
\sigma
into a form that is
more amenable to our subsequent algorithms.

Lemma 27.
†
†
margin:

Proof in page
D

Consider an ILESLP
\sigma\coloneqq(x_{0}\leftarrow\rho_{0},\dots,x_{n}\leftarrow\rho_{n})
and let
i\in[0..n]
.
One can compute, in time polynomial in the size of
\sigma
,
an expression
E_{i}
of the form

\sum_{j=0}^{i-1}a_{i,j}\cdot 2^{x_{j}}

such that

\left\llbracket\sigma\right\rrbracket(E_{i})=d(\sigma)\cdot\left\llbracket\sigma\right\rrbracket(x_{i})
.
For every
j\in[0..i-1]
, the coefficient
a_{i,j}
is
(i)
an integer
whose absolute value is bounded by
2^{i}\cdot e(\sigma)\cdot d(\sigma)
, and

(ii)
non-zero only if
\left\llbracket\sigma\right\rrbracket(x_{j})\geq 0
.

Proof sketch.

Given
i\in[0..n]
,
let
\sigma_{i}
denote the ILESLP
(x_{0}\leftarrow\rho_{0},\dots,x_{i}\leftarrow\rho_{i})
obtained by truncating
\sigma
after
i+1
assignments.
We remark that
d(\sigma_{i})
divides
d(\sigma_{j})
for every
i\leq j
.

Inductively on
i
, one shows
that it is possible to compute
a vector of rational numbers
\bm{b}_{i}=(b_{i,0},\dots,b_{i,i-1})
satisfying

\left\llbracket\sigma\right\rrbracket(x_{i})=\sum_{j=0}^{i-1}b_{i,j}\cdot 2^{\left\llbracket\sigma\right\rrbracket(x_{j})}
,
where each
b_{i,j}
is of the form
\frac{m}{d(\sigma_{i})}
for some
m\in\mathbb{Z}
satisfying
\left|m\right|\leq 2^{i}\cdot e(\sigma_{i})\cdot d(\sigma_{i})
,
and
m\neq 0
only if
\left\llbracket\sigma\right\rrbracket(x_{j})\geq 0
.
With this result at hand, the expression
E_{i}
in the statement of the lemma is computed by multiply all these rational numbers by
d(\sigma)
, as to make them all integers. In particular, if
b_{i,j}=\frac{m}{d(\sigma_{i})}
, then in
E_{i}
the coefficient of
2^{x_{j}}
is
a_{i,j}\coloneqq m\cdot\frac{d(\sigma)}{d(\sigma_{i})}
.
Hence,
\left|a_{i,j}\right|\leq 2^{i}\cdot e(\sigma_{i})\cdot d(\sigma_{i})\cdot\frac{d(\sigma)}{d(\sigma_{i})}\leq 2^{i}\cdot e(\sigma)\cdot d(\sigma)
.
Note that the bit size of each
a_{i,j}
is thus polynomial in the size of
\sigma
.
With this in mind, the fact that the whole computation can be performed in polynomial time follows immediately from the inductive proof.
∎

7
Deciding
\textup{{Nat}}_{\scalebox{0.7}{{{ILESLP}}}}
in polynomial time

Algorithm 7

A polynomial-time algorithm for
\textup{{Nat}}_{\scalebox{0.7}{{{ILESLP}}}}
.

1:

ILESLP
\sigma\coloneqq(x_{0}\leftarrow\rho_{0},\dots,x_{n}\leftarrow\rho_{n})
.

2:

C\leftarrow 8\cdot(\text{bit size of $\sigma$})+8

3:

let
, for
i\in[0..n]
,
E_{i}
be an expression
\sum_{j=0}^{i-1}a_{j}\cdot 2^{x_{j}}
, with all
a_{j}\in\mathbb{Z}
,
and
\left\llbracket\sigma\right\rrbracket(x_{i})=\frac{\sum_{j=0}^{i-1}a_{j}\cdot 2^{\left\llbracket\sigma\right\rrbracket(x_{j})}}{d(\sigma)}

4:

M

\leftarrow
empty map from
[0..n]^{2}
to
{[-C..C]\cup\{-\infty,\infty\}}

5:

for

i
from
0
to
n

do

6:

M(i,i)\leftarrow 0

7:

let

\{\ell_{0}=0,\dots,\ell_{m}\}
maximal subset of
[0..i-1]
such that
M(\ell_{k},\ell_{k-1})\geq 0
for every
k\in[1..m]

8:

for

j
from
0
to
i-1

do

9:

E\leftarrow E_{i}-E_{j}

\triangleright

E
is of the form
\sum_{k=0}^{m}c_{k}2^{x_{\ell_{k}}}

10:

for

k

from

m

to

1

do

11:

if

M(\ell_{k},\ell_{k-1})\leq C

then

replace
2^{x_{\ell_{k}}}
with
2^{M(\ell_{k},\ell_{k-1})}2^{x_{\ell_{k-1}}}
in
E

12:

else

13:

a\leftarrow
coefficient of
2^{x_{\ell_{k}}}
in
E

14:

if

a>0

then

M(i,j)\leftarrow+\infty

15:

if

a<0

then

M(i,j)\leftarrow-\infty

16:

if

a\neq 0

then

break

17:

if

E
is of the form
h\cdot 2^{x_{0}}
for some
h\in\mathbb{Z}

then

18:

if

\frac{h}{d(\sigma)}\in[-C..C]

then

M(i,j)\leftarrow\frac{h}{d(\sigma)}

19:

else

M(i,j)\leftarrow{\textbf{if}\,\frac{h}{d(\sigma)}\,{>}\,0\,\textbf{then}\,{+\infty}\,\textbf{else}\,{-\infty}}

20:

M(j,i)\leftarrow-M(i,j)

21:

return
true
if
M(n,0)\geq 0

else
false

\textup{{Nat}}_{\scalebox{0.7}{{{ILESLP}}}}

Input:

An ILESLP
\sigma
.

Question:

Is
\left\llbracket\sigma\right\rrbracket_{\bullet}\geq 0
?

The pseudocode of our procedure for deciding
\textup{{Nat}}_{\scalebox{0.7}{{{ILESLP}}}}
is given in
Algorithm

7
.
In a nutshell, given an ILESLP
\sigma=({x_{0}\leftarrow\rho_{0}},\dots,x_{n}\leftarrow\rho_{n})
,
the algorithm constructs a map
M
with the following property: for every
i,j\in[0..n]
, the entry
M(i,j)
stores the value of the difference
\left\llbracket\sigma\right\rrbracket(x_{i})-\left\llbracket\sigma\right\rrbracket(x_{j})
up to a certain threshold
C
defined in line
2
.
If the absolute value of this difference exceeds the threshold, then
M(i,j)
is instead equal to
+\infty
or
-\infty
,
depending on the sign of the difference.
After constructing
M
, the algorithm checks whether
M(n,0)\geq 0
to decide if
\left\llbracket\sigma\right\rrbracket(x_{n})\geq 0
.

Following
Lemma

27
, for every
i\in[0..n]
, the algorithm starts by “flattening” the expression
\rho_{i}
of
x_{i}

into the form
\frac{E_{i}}{d(\sigma)}
, where
E_{i}=\sum_{k=0}^{i-1}a_{k}\cdot 2^{x_{k}}
with
a_{0},\dots,a_{i-1}
integers (line
3
).
The computation of
M(i,j)
with
j<i
occurs at the
(i+1)
th iteration of the loop of line
5
and
(j+1)
th iteration of the loop of line
8
.
To compute
M(i,j)
, the expression
E\coloneqq E_{i}-E_{j}
is considered (line
9
).
This is again of the form
\sum_{k=0}^{i-1}b_{k}\cdot 2^{x_{k}}
, where

b_{k}\neq 0
only if
\left\llbracket\sigma\right\rrbracket(x_{k})\geq 0
(again by
Lemma

27
).
Since all entries of
M
involving variables
x_{0},\dots,x_{i-1}
are already computed in the earlier iterations, we can reduce
E
to an expression
\sum_{k=0}^{m}c_{k}\cdot 2^{x_{\ell_{k}}}
,
where
\ell_{0},\dots,\ell_{m}\in[0..i-1]
are the indices of the variables
x_{\ell}
with
\left\llbracket\sigma\right\rrbracket(x_{\ell})\geq 0
, in ascending order (see line
7
).

Intuitively, if
\left\llbracket\sigma\right\rrbracket(x_{\ell_{m}})
is large enough compared to
\left\llbracket\sigma\right\rrbracket(x_{\ell_{m-1}})
, then the sign of
\sum_{k=0}^{m}c_{k}\cdot 2^{\left\llbracket\sigma\right\rrbracket(x_{\ell_{k}})}
is solely determined by the sign of the integer
c_{m}
(assuming
c_{m}\neq 0
).
The threshold
C
has been chosen to capture this idea of
x_{\ell_{m}}
being “large enough”.
In particular, one can show that for every
k\in[1..m]
, if
\left\llbracket\sigma\right\rrbracket(x_{\ell_{k}})-\left\llbracket\sigma\right\rrbracket(x_{\ell_{k-1}})>C
and
c_{k}\neq 0
,
then
\left|c_{k}2^{\left\llbracket\sigma\right\rrbracket(x_{\ell_{k}})}\right|>\left|\sum_{j=0}^{k-1}c_{j}2^{\left\llbracket\sigma\right\rrbracket(x_{\ell_{j}})}\right|+d(\sigma)\cdot C
.
So, if
M(\ell_{m},\ell_{m-1})>C
and
c_{m}\neq 0
, we have
M(i,j)=\pm\infty

(lines
13
–
16
).
Otherwise, if
\left\llbracket\sigma\right\rrbracket(x_{\ell_{m}})
is small compared to
\left\llbracket\sigma\right\rrbracket(x_{\ell_{m-1}})
, that is,
M(\ell_{m},\ell_{m-1})\leq C
,
we replace
2^{x_{\ell_{m}}}
with
2^{M(\ell_{m},\ell_{m-1})}\cdot 2^{x_{\ell_{m-1}}}
in
E
, and iterate the same reasoning; now on variables
x_{\ell_{m-1}}
and
x_{\ell_{m-2}}
.
At the end of the loop of line
10
, either
M(i,j)
has been set to
\pm\infty
, or we have reduced
E
into an expression of the form
h\cdot 2^{x_{0}}
.
In the latter case, we have
\left\llbracket\sigma\right\rrbracket(x_{i})-\left\llbracket\sigma\right\rrbracket(x_{j})=\frac{h\cdot 2^{\left\llbracket\sigma\right\rrbracket(x_{0})}}{d(\sigma)}=\frac{h}{d(\sigma)}
.
If
\frac{h}{d(\sigma)}
belongs to
[-C..C]
, the algorithm sets
M(i,j)=\frac{h}{d(\sigma)}
(line
18
). Else,
M(i,j)
is set to
\pm\infty
, according to the sign of
\frac{h}{d(\sigma)}
.

To prove that
Algorithm

7
decides
\textup{{Nat}}_{\scalebox{0.7}{{{ILESLP}}}}
in polynomial time,
the key observation is that
C
is linear in the bit size of
\sigma
,
and thus so is the bit size of the integers
2^{M(\ell_{k},\ell_{k-1})}
computed in line
11
.

We now formalize the above explanation, proving correctness and polynomial
running time of
Algorithm

7
. The correctness proof centers on the
semantics of the map
M
.

Lemma 28.

Given an input ILESLP
\sigma\coloneqq(x_{0}\leftarrow\rho_{0},\dots,x_{n}\leftarrow\rho_{n})
,

Algorithm

7
constructs a map

{M:[0..n]^{2}\to[-C..C]\cup\{-\infty,\infty\}}
,
where
C\coloneqq 8\cdot(\textnormal{bit size of $\sigma$})+8
.
For all
i,j\in[0..n]
,
this map satisfies

M(i,j)=T_{C}(\left\llbracket\sigma\right\rrbracket(x_{i})-\left\llbracket\sigma\right\rrbracket(x_{j}))
, where
T_{C}
is the truncation function

T_{C}(g)\coloneqq\begin{cases}g&\text{if }g\in[-C..C]\\
-\infty&\text{if }g<-C\\
+\infty&\text{if }g>C\end{cases}\hskip 56.9055pt\text{for every }g\in\mathbb{Z}.

Proof.

The map
M
is initialized as empty in line
4
.
Let
E_{0},\dots,E_{n}
be the expressions computed in line
3
, following
Lemma

27
. They satisfy
\left\llbracket\sigma\right\rrbracket(E_{i})=d(\sigma)\cdot\left\llbracket\sigma\right\rrbracket(x_{i})
, for every
i\in[0..n]
.
We prove by induction on
i\in\mathbb{N}
that after the
(i+1)
th iteration of the outer
for
loop of line
5
, the map
M
satisfies

M(j,k)=T_{C}(\left\llbracket\sigma\right\rrbracket(x_{j})-\left\llbracket\sigma\right\rrbracket(x_{k}))

for every
j,k\in[0..i]
.

base case:
i=0
.

In the first iteration,
line
6
sets
M(0,0)=0
as required. The inner loop of line
8
does not execute for
i=0
as the range of
j
is empty.

induction hypothesis.

For every
j,k\in[0..i-1]
,

M(j,k)=T_{C}(\left\llbracket\sigma\right\rrbracket(x_{j})-\left\llbracket\sigma\right\rrbracket(x_{k}))
.

induction step:
i\geq 1
.

In the
(i+1)
th iteration, the algorithm sets
M(i,j)
and
M(j,i)
for all
j\in[0..i]
.
Line
6
correctly sets
M(i,i)=0
. For each
j\in[0..i-1]
, the inner loop of line
8
computes
M(i,j)
and
M(j,i)
. As
M(j,i)=-M(j,i)
by line
20
, it suffices to show that the computed
M(i,j)=T_{C}(\left\llbracket\sigma\right\rrbracket(x_{i})-\left\llbracket\sigma\right\rrbracket(x_{j}))
for all
j\in[0..i-1]
.

In line
7
,
the algorithm computes the maximal subset of indices
\{\ell_{0}=0,\dots,\ell_{m}\}\subseteq[0..i-1]
such that
M(\ell_{k},\ell_{k-1})\geq 0
for every
k\in[1..m]
.
By the induction hypothesis,
M(\ell_{k},\ell_{k-1})=T_{C}(\left\llbracket\sigma\right\rrbracket(x_{\ell_{k}})-\left\llbracket\sigma\right\rrbracket(x_{\ell_{k-1}}))
,
which implies
0=\left\llbracket\sigma\right\rrbracket(x_{\ell_{0}})\leq\left\llbracket\sigma\right\rrbracket(x_{\ell_{1}})\leq\ldots\leq\left\llbracket\sigma\right\rrbracket(x_{\ell_{m}})
. Moreover, by the maximality of this subset,
the variables
x_{\ell_{0}},\dots,x_{\ell_{m}}
are exactly those among
x_{0},\dots,x_{i-1}
for which
\left\llbracket\sigma\right\rrbracket
is non-negative.

Computation of
M(i,j)
:

This computation occurs at the
(j+1)
th iteration of the inner
for
loop of line
8
, with
j\in[0..i-1]
.
Let
E
be the expression
E_{i}-E_{j}
as in line
9
.
By
Lemma

27
,

E
is of the form
\sum_{k=0}^{i-1}a_{k}\cdot 2^{x_{k}}
where

1.

each
a_{k}
is an integer whose absolute value is bounded by
2^{n+1}\cdot e(\sigma)\cdot d(\sigma)
,

2.

if
a_{k}\neq 0
, then
\left\llbracket\sigma\right\rrbracket(x_{k})\geq 0
.

The second property above implies that
E
can be written as

\sum_{k=0}^{m}a_{\ell_{k}}\cdot 2^{x_{\ell_{k}}}
.
From the definition of
E_{i}
and
E_{j}
, we also have
\left\llbracket\sigma\right\rrbracket(E)=d(\sigma)\cdot(\left\llbracket\sigma\right\rrbracket(x_{i})-\left\llbracket\sigma\right\rrbracket(x_{j}))
.

The algorithm now enters the inner
for
loop of line
10
, iterating
k
from
m
down to
1
.
This loop progressively rewrites the expression
E
.
Let
E^{(t)}
denote for the value of
E
after the
t
th iteration of the loop.
During the
t
th iteration, the value of the variable
k

is
m-t+1
.
The following two claims (proved later) define the behavior of this loop:

Claim 18.

If the
t
th iteration of the loop of line
10

completes without executing the
break
statement of line
16
,
then
E^{(t)}
is of the form
c_{\ell_{m-t}}\cdot 2^{x_{\ell_{m-t}}}+\sum_{k=0}^{m-t-1}a_{\ell_{k}}\cdot 2^{x_{\ell_{k}}}
,
where
c_{\ell_{m-t}}
is an integer satisfying
\left|c_{\ell_{m-t}}\right|<2^{tC+n+2}\cdot e(\sigma)\cdot d(\sigma)
.
Moreover,
\left\llbracket\sigma\right\rrbracket(E^{(t)})=\left\llbracket\sigma\right\rrbracket(E)
.

Claim 19.

If the
t
th iteration of the loop of line
10

executes the
break
statement of line
16
,
then
\left|\left\llbracket\sigma\right\rrbracket(E)\right|>d(\sigma)\cdot C
,
and
\left\llbracket\sigma\right\rrbracket(E)
and the coefficient
of
2^{x_{\ell_{m-t+1}}}
in
E^{(t-1)}

have the same sign.

Using these two claims, we can now verify that
M(i,j)
is computed correctly. If the
break
statement of
line
16
is executed during some iteration
t

of the loop of line
10
, then
by
19
, we have
\left|\frac{\left\llbracket\sigma\right\rrbracket(E)}{d(\sigma)}\right|>C
, and
\frac{\left\llbracket\sigma\right\rrbracket(E)}{d(\sigma)}
has the same sign as that of the coefficient
of
2^{x_{\ell_{m-t+1}}}
in
E^{(t-1)}
.
This coefficient is the integer
a
referenced in
line
13
, and
since the
break
statement was executed,
a\neq 0
.
Accordingly, lines
14
and
15

set the value of
M(i,j)
to
\pm\infty
, following the sign of
a
.
Hence,
M(i,j)=T_{C}(\frac{\left\llbracket\sigma\right\rrbracket(E)}{d(\sigma)})=T_{C}(\left\llbracket\sigma\right\rrbracket(x_{i})-\left\llbracket\sigma\right\rrbracket(x_{j}))
, as required.

Suppose now that the
break
statement of
line
16
is never executed:
the loop of line
10
terminates after
m
iterations,
and the expression
E^{(m)}
is defined.
By
18
, this expression
is of the form
c_{\ell_{0}}\cdot 2^{x_{\ell_{0}}}
,
for some integer
c_{\ell_{0}}
(the bound on
c_{\ell_{0}}
given in
18
will be later used in the runtime analysis in
Lemma

1
).
Since
\ell_{0}=0
and
\left\llbracket\sigma\right\rrbracket(x_{0})=0
,
we have
\left\llbracket\sigma\right\rrbracket(E^{(m)})=c_{\ell_{0}}
.
Recall that
\left\llbracket\sigma\right\rrbracket(E_{m})=\left\llbracket\sigma\right\rrbracket(E)=d(\sigma)\cdot(\left\llbracket\sigma\right\rrbracket(x_{i})-\left\llbracket\sigma\right\rrbracket(x_{j}))
, so

M(i,j)
must be set to
T_{C}(\frac{c_{\ell_{0}}}{d(\sigma)})
.
This is exactly what the algorithm does in lines
17
–
19
.

To complete the induction step, it is now sufficient to
prove Claims
18
and
19
.

Proof of
18
.

For simplicity, let
B\coloneqq 2^{n+1}\cdot e(\sigma)\cdot d(\sigma)
.
Note that
B\in[2..2^{3\cdot(\text{bit size of $\sigma$})+1}]
;
in particular,
B<2^{C}
.
The proof is by induction on
t
.

base case:
t=0
.

Before the first iteration of the loop, we have
E^{(0)}=E
. Recall that
E
is an expression of the form
\sum_{k=0}^{i-1}a_{k}\cdot 2^{x_{k}}
where each
a_{k}
is an integer whose absolute value is bounded by
B
. Thus,
\left|a_{0}\right|<2\cdot B
, as required.

induction hypothesis.

If the
(t-1)
th iteration of the loop of line
10

completes without executing the
break
statement of line
16
,
then the expression
E^{(t-1)}
is of the form
{c_{\ell_{m-t+1}}\cdot 2^{x_{\ell_{m-t+1}}}+\sum_{k=0}^{m-t}a_{\ell_{k}}\cdot 2^{x_{\ell_{k}}}}
,
where
c_{\ell_{m-t+1}}\in\mathbb{Z}
satisfies
\left|c_{\ell_{m-t+1}}\right|<2^{(t-1)C+1}\cdot B
.
Moreover,
\left\llbracket\sigma\right\rrbracket(E^{(t-1)})=\left\llbracket\sigma\right\rrbracket(E)
.

induction step:
t\geq 1
.

Suppose that the
t
th iteration of the loop of line
10

completes without executing the
break
statement of line
16
.
Then, the same must hold for the
(t-1)
th iteration,
and so the induction hypothesis applies.
Since the
break
statement is not executed, there are two options:

1.

The condition of the
if
statement in line
11
is true, i.e.,
M(\ell_{m-t+1},\ell_{m-t})\leq C
, or

2.

M(\ell_{m-t+1},\ell_{m-t})=+\infty
and the coefficient
c_{\ell_{m-t+1}}
of
2^{x_{\ell_{m-t+1}}}
in
E^{(t-1)}
is zero.

In the second case, no update is needed:
E^{(t-1)}
is already of the form
\sum_{k=0}^{m-t}a_{\ell_{k}}\cdot 2^{x_{\ell_{k}}}
, and we have
E^{(t)}=E^{(t-1)}
.
In the first case,
E^{(t)}
is constructed from
E^{(t-1)}
by replacing

2^{x_{\ell_{m-t+1}}}
by
2^{M(\ell_{m-t+1},\ell_{m-t})}\cdot 2^{x_{\ell_{m-t}}}
;
see line
11
.
This removes
2^{x_{\ell_{m-t+1}}}
,
and modifies the coefficient of
2^{x_{\ell_{m-t}}}

from
a_{\ell_{m-t}}
to

c_{\ell_{m-t}}\coloneqq(a_{\ell_{m-t}}+c_{\ell_{m-t+1}}\cdot 2^{M(\ell_{m-t+1},\ell_{m-t})})
.
The coefficients of the terms
2^{x_{\ell_{k}}}
with
k\in[0..m-t-1]
are unchanged.
As
{M(\ell_{m-t+1},\ell_{m-t})\geq 0}
, we have
c_{\ell_{m-t}}\in\mathbb{Z}
.
Finally, we bound the absolute value of
c_{\ell_{m-t}}

as follows:

\displaystyle\left|c_{\ell_{m-t}}\right|

\displaystyle=\left|a_{\ell_{m-t}}\right|+\left|c_{\ell_{m-t+1}}\right|\cdot 2^{M(\ell_{m-t+1},\ell_{m-t})}

\displaystyle\leq B+\left|c_{\ell_{m-t+1}}\right|\cdot 2^{C}

\displaystyle\hskip-19.91684pt\Lbag\text{bounds on $a_{\ell_{m-t}}$ and $M(\ell_{m-t+1},\ell_{m-t})$}\Rbag

\displaystyle\leq B+(2^{(t-1)C+1}\cdot B-1)\cdot 2^{C}

\displaystyle\Lbag\text{by induction hypothesis}\Rbag

\displaystyle<2^{C}+(2^{(t-1)C+1}\cdot B-1)\cdot 2^{C}

\displaystyle\Lbag\text{from $B<2^{C}$}\Rbag

\displaystyle<2^{tC+1}\cdot B.

Proof of
19
.

If the
t
th iteration of the loop of line
10

executes the
break
statement of line
16
,
then the first
t-1
iterations completed without executing the
break
statement.
By
18
,

E^{(t-1)}
is of the form
c_{\ell_{m-t+1}}\cdot 2^{x_{\ell_{m-t+1}}}+\sum_{k=0}^{m-t}a_{\ell_{k}}\cdot 2^{x_{\ell_{k}}}
, and
\left\llbracket\sigma\right\rrbracket(E^{(t-1)})=\left\llbracket\sigma\right\rrbracket(E)
.
Since the
t
th iteration executes the
break
statement, we have:

1.

M(\ell_{m-t+1},\ell_{m-t})=+\infty
(from the condition of the
if
statements of line
11
),

2.

c_{\ell_{m-t+1}}\neq 0
(from the condition of the
if
statement of line
16
).

We show that

2^{\left\llbracket\sigma\right\rrbracket(x_{\ell_{m-t+1}})}>\left|\sum\nolimits_{k=0}^{m-t}a_{\ell_{k}}\cdot 2^{\left\llbracket\sigma\right\rrbracket(x_{\ell_{k}})}\right|+d(\sigma)\cdot C.

(27)

From the definition of
E^{(t-1)}
,
and the fact that
