\left\llbracket\sigma\right\rrbracket(E^{(t-1)})=\left\llbracket\sigma\right\rrbracket(E)
and
c_{\ell_{m-t+1}}\neq 0
, this inequality implies
19
.

To prove
Equation

27
, we first note that
M(\ell_{m-t+1},\ell_{m-t})=+\infty

implies, from the induction hypothesis in the main proof, that
\left\llbracket\sigma\right\rrbracket(x_{\ell_{m-t+1}})>\left\llbracket\sigma\right\rrbracket(x_{\ell_{m-t}})+C
.
Also, by definition of the indices
\ell_{0},\dots,\ell_{m}
,

\left\llbracket\sigma\right\rrbracket(x_{\ell_{m-t}})\geq\left\llbracket\sigma\right\rrbracket(x_{\ell_{k}})\geq 0
for every
k\in[0..m-t-1]
.
Hence,
2^{\left\llbracket\sigma\right\rrbracket(x_{\ell_{m-t+1}})}>2^{C}\cdot 2^{\left\llbracket\sigma\right\rrbracket(x_{\ell_{m-t}})}
and
\big(\sum\nolimits_{k=0}^{m-t}\left|a_{\ell_{k}}\right|\big)\cdot 2^{\left\llbracket\sigma\right\rrbracket(x_{\ell_{m-t}})}\geq\left|\sum\nolimits_{k=0}^{m-t}a_{\ell_{k}}\cdot 2^{\left\llbracket\sigma\right\rrbracket(x_{\ell_{k}})}\right|
,
which in turn implies that
Equation

27

holds as soon as we prove
2^{C}\geq\big(\sum\nolimits_{k=0}^{m-t}\left|a_{\ell_{k}}\right|\big)+d(\sigma)\cdot C
.
To show this inequality, we establish that

2^{C/2}\geq\sum\nolimits_{k=0}^{m-t}\left|a_{\ell_{k}}\right|

and
C\geq 4\cdot\log_{2}(d(\sigma))+8
;
the inequality then follows from
Lemma

10
.
We have

\displaystyle 2\cdot\left\lceil\log_{2}(\max(1,{\textstyle\sum}_{k=0}^{m-t}\left|a_{\ell_{k}}\right|))\right\rceil+4\left\lceil\log_{2}(d(\sigma))\right\rceil+8

\displaystyle\leq{}

\displaystyle 2\cdot\left\lceil\log_{2}(n\cdot 2^{n+1}\cdot e(\sigma)\cdot d(\sigma))\right\rceil+4\left\lceil\log_{2}(d(\sigma))\right\rceil+8

\displaystyle\hskip-42.67912pt\Lbag\text{bound on each $a_{\ell_{k}}$}\Rbag

\displaystyle\leq{}

\displaystyle 2\cdot(\underline{n+1}+\underline{\left\lceil\log_{2}(n)\right\rceil+\left\lceil\log_{2}(e(\sigma))\right\rceil+\left\lceil\log_{2}(d(\sigma))\right\rceil})+4\cdot\underline{\left\lceil\log_{2}(d(\sigma))\right\rceil}+8

\displaystyle\leq{}

\displaystyle 8\cdot(\text{bit size of $\sigma$})+8=C.

\displaystyle\hskip-199.16928pt\Lbag\text{each \lx@text@underline{underlined} quantity is $\leq(\text{bit size of $\sigma$})$}\Rbag

Therefore, both
2^{C/2}\geq\sum\nolimits_{k=0}^{m-t}\left|a_{\ell_{k}}\right|

and
C\geq 4\cdot\log_{2}(d(\sigma))+8
hold.
∎

This completes the proof of
Lemma

28
.
∎

See
1

Proof.

Let
\sigma=(x_{0}\leftarrow\rho_{0},\dots,x_{n}\leftarrow\rho_{n})
be the input ILESLP of Algorithm
7
.

Correctness:

By line
21
, the algorithm returns true if and only if
{M(n,0)}\geq 0
.
Recall that
\left\llbracket\sigma\right\rrbracket(x_{0})=0
. It then follows from
Lemma

28
that

M(n,0)=T_{C}(\left\llbracket\sigma\right\rrbracket(x_{n}))
.
Since
C>0
,
T_{C}(\left\llbracket\sigma\right\rrbracket(x_{n}))\geq 0
if and only if
\left\llbracket\sigma\right\rrbracket(x_{n})\geq 0
.
Therefore,
M(n,0)\geq 0
if and only if
\left\llbracket\sigma\right\rrbracket(x_{n})\geq 0
;
showing the correctness of the algorithm.

Complexity:

To prove that
Algorithm

7
runs in polynomial time, observe that:

•

The bit length of
C
is bounded logarithmically in the bit size of
\sigma
.

•

The expressions
E_{0},\dots,E_{n}
are computed in polynomial time following
Lemma

27
.

•

The map
M
requires only
O(n^{2}\log_{2}{C})
space.

•

All
for
loops (lines
5
,
8
and
10
) iterate on intervals of size linear in the bit size of
\sigma
.

Following these observations, the only remaining crucial point is showing that repeated executions of line
11
, locally to one iteration of the
for
loop of line
8
, do not cause the integers in the expression
E

to grow superpolynomially.
This property is already established in Claim
18
of the proof of
Lemma

28
. In particular, the absolute value of each integer in
E
is bounded by
2^{nC+n+2}\cdot e(\sigma)\cdot d(\sigma)
;
the bit length of this number is polynomial in the bit size of
\sigma
.
With this key observation, it follows that all remaining operations performed by the algorithm run in polynomial time.
∎

8
Deciding
\textup{{Div}}_{\scalebox{0.7}{{{ILESLP}}}}
in
\textup{{P}}^{\textup{{factoring}}}

\textup{{Div}}_{\scalebox{0.7}{{{ILESLP}}}}

Input:

An ILESLP
\sigma
, and
g\in\mathbb{N}_{\geq 1}
encoded in binary.

Question:

Is
\left\llbracket\sigma\right\rrbracket_{\bullet}
divisible by
g
?

We describe a procedure that, given an ILESLP
\sigma=(x_{0}\leftarrow\rho_{0},\dots,x_{n}\leftarrow\rho_{n})
and
g\in\mathbb{N}_{\geq 1}
encoded in binary,
outputs (the binary encoding of) the remainder of
\left\llbracket\sigma\right\rrbracket_{\bullet}
modulo
g
. The decision problem
\textup{{Div}}_{\scalebox{0.7}{{{ILESLP}}}}
is solved by checking if the remainder in output is
0
. The pseudocode of this procedure is shown in
Algorithm

8
.

We denote by
\nu_{\sigma}\colon\mathbb{N}_{\geq 1}\to\mathbb{N}_{\geq 1}
the function
{\nu_{\sigma}(x)\coloneqq\phi(\textit{odd}(x\cdot d(\sigma)))}
, where
\textit{odd}(a)
denotes the largest odd factor of
a\in\mathbb{N}_{\geq 1}
, and
\phi
denotes Euler’s totient function (see
Equation

23
on page
23
for the formal definition).
We denote by
\nu^{k}_{\sigma}
the
k
th iterate of
\nu_{\sigma}
, that is,
\nu^{0}_{\sigma}(x)\coloneqq x
and
\nu^{k+1}_{\sigma}(x)\coloneqq\nu_{\sigma}(\nu^{k}_{\sigma}(x))
for every
k\in\mathbb{N}
.

Algorithm

8
constructs a map
M
with the following property: for every
i,k\in[0..n]
with
i+k\leq n
, the entry
M(i,k)
stores the value of
\left\llbracket\sigma\right\rrbracket(x_{i})\bmod\nu_{\sigma}^{k}(g)
.
Once the map is opportunely populated, it returns the value
M(n,0)

corresponding to
\left\llbracket\sigma\right\rrbracket_{\bullet}\bmod g
(line
14
).

Algorithm 8

A
\textup{{FP}}^{\textup{{factoring}}}
algorithm for computing
\left\llbracket\sigma\right\rrbracket_{\bullet}\bmod g
.

1:

ILESLP
\sigma\coloneqq(x_{0}\leftarrow\rho_{0},\dots,x_{n}\leftarrow\rho_{n})
, and
g\in\mathbb{N}_{\geq 1}
encoded in binary.

2:

M\leftarrow
empty map from
[0..n]^{2}
to
\mathbb{N}

3:

for

k
form
0
to
n

do

M(0,k)\leftarrow 0

4:

for

i
from
1
to
n

do

5:

let

a_{0},\dots,a_{i-1}\in\mathbb{Z}
such that
\left\llbracket\sigma\right\rrbracket(x_{i})=\frac{\sum_{j=0}^{i-1}a_{j}\cdot 2^{\left\llbracket\sigma\right\rrbracket(x_{j})}}{d(\sigma)}

6:

for

k
from
0
to
(n-i)

do

7:

h\leftarrow\nu_{\sigma}^{k}(g)\cdot d(\sigma)

\triangleright

requires factorization oracle

8:

let

m,q\in\mathbb{N}
such that
q=\textit{odd}(h)
and
h=2^{m}\cdot q

9:

for

j
from
0
to
i-1

do

10:

b\leftarrow

if

\left\llbracket\sigma\right\rrbracket(x_{j})\geq m

then

0

else

2^{\left\llbracket\sigma\right\rrbracket(x_{j})}

\triangleright

uses the algorithm for
\textup{{Nat}}_{\scalebox{0.7}{{{ILESLP}}}}

11:

c\leftarrow 2^{M(j,k+1)}\bmod q

12:

let

r_{j}
be the (only) value in
[0..h-1]
such that
2^{m}
divides
r_{j}-b
, and
q
divides
r_{j}-c

13:

M(i,k)\leftarrow(\frac{1}{d(\sigma)}\sum_{j=0}^{i-1}a_{j}\cdot r_{j})\bmod\nu_{\sigma}^{k}(g)

14:

return

M(n,0)

The core of the algorithm is the
for
loop of line
4
. During its
i
th iteration, this loop populates the entries
M(i,0),\dots,M(i,n-i)
.
(The base case of
i=0
is handled in line
3
, as
\left\llbracket\sigma\right\rrbracket(x_{0})=0
.)
Similarly to
Algorithm

7
and following
Lemma

27
, in line
5
the algorithm “flattens” the expression associated to
x_{i}
into one of the form
\frac{1}{d(\sigma)}\sum_{j=0}^{i-1}a_{j}\cdot 2^{x_{j}}
, with
a_{1},\dots,a_{i-1}
integers.
Let
h\coloneqq\nu_{\sigma}^{k}(g)\cdot d(\sigma)
.

To compute
M(i,k)
(during the
(k+1)
th iteration of the loop of line
6
) we reason modulo
\nu_{\sigma}^{k}(g)
:

\displaystyle M(i,k)

\displaystyle=\textstyle\frac{1}{d(\sigma)}\cdot\sum_{j=0}^{i-1}a_{j}\cdot 2^{\left\llbracket\sigma\right\rrbracket(x_{j})}

\displaystyle=\textstyle\frac{1}{d(\sigma)}\cdot\sum_{j=0}^{i-1}a_{j}\cdot(2^{\left\llbracket\sigma\right\rrbracket(x_{j})}\bmod h),

where the last number is an integer, because the sum
\sum_{j=0}^{i-1}a_{j}\cdot 2^{\left\llbracket\sigma\right\rrbracket(x_{j})}
is divisible by
d(\sigma)
(as
\sigma
is an ILESLP). To compute
2^{\left\llbracket\sigma\right\rrbracket(x_{j})}\bmod h
, we appeal to the Chinese Remainder Theorem (CRT). Write
h=2^{m}\cdot q
with
m,q\in\mathbb{N}
and
q
odd. Compute
{b\coloneqq 2^{\left\llbracket\sigma\right\rrbracket(x_{j})}\bmod 2^{m}}
and
{c\coloneqq 2^{\left\llbracket\sigma\right\rrbracket(x_{j})}\bmod q}
,
and then use CRT to find the only
r\in[0..h-1]
such that
r\equiv b\bmod{2^{m}}
, and
r\equiv c\bmod{q}
(line
12
).

The value
b
is computed via
Algorithm

7
(line
10
).
To compute
c
, we see that
2^{\phi(q)}\bmod q=1
, by Euler’s theorem.
Therefore,
2^{\left\llbracket\sigma\right\rrbracket(x_{j})}
and
2^{\left\llbracket\sigma\right\rrbracket(x_{j})\bmod\phi(q)}

have the same reminder modulo
q
.
We have
\phi(q)=\nu_{\sigma}^{k+1}(g)
by definition, and so

M(j,k+1)=\left\llbracket\sigma\right\rrbracket(x_{j})\bmod\phi(q)
.
Note that
j<i
, and so
M(j,k+1)
has been populated in a previous iteration of the loop of line
4
;
the algorithm thus constructs
c
by computing
2^{M(j,k+1)}\bmod q

(line
11
).

Regarding the complexity of
Algorithm

8
, most of its operations can be implemented in polynomial time. The map
M
requires polynomial space,
since
\nu_{\sigma}^{k}(g)
is bounded by
d(\sigma)^{k}\cdot g
.
Moreover, while
2^{M(j,k+1)}
can in principle be of exponential bit size, computing it modulo
q
can be done in polynomial time in the bit size of
M(j,k+1)
and
q
by relying on the exponentiation-by-squaring method
[
BW08
, Ch. 1.4]
.
The only difficulty stems from the computation
of
\nu_{\sigma}^{k}(g)
.
For this we use the integer factorization oracle in order to compute Euler’s totient function, following
Equation

23
.

We now formalize the above arguments into a full proof of correctness and runtime analysis.

See
2

Proof.

Consider as input an ILESLP
\sigma=(x_{0}\leftarrow\rho_{0},\dots,x_{n}\leftarrow\rho_{n})
and
g\in\mathbb{N}_{\geq 1}
.
We show that
Algorithm

8
computes
\left\llbracket\sigma\right\rrbracket_{\bullet}\bmod g
, and runs in polynomial time with a factoring oracle.
For simplicity of the exposition,
let us see the map
M
as an
(n+1)\times(n+1)
matrix over
\mathbb{N}
, with indices for rows and columns in
[0..n]
. The matrix is initially empty, and the algorithm only populates it in a “triangular way”,
only filling the entries
(i,k)
such that
i+k\leq n
.
Upon completion of the algorithm, we will have
M(i,k)=(\left\llbracket\sigma\right\rrbracket{x_{i}}\bmod\nu_{\sigma}^{k}(g))
, for every such entry
(i,k)
.
Figure

3
depicts this matrix.

Since
\phi(a)\leq a
for all
a\in\mathbb{N}_{\geq 1}
, we have
\nu_{\sigma}^{k}(g)\leq d(\sigma)^{k}g
.
As a result, the number of bits required to store
M
is in
O(n^{2}\log_{2}(d(\sigma)^{n}g))
.
We prove by induction on
i\in\mathbb{N}
that, during the
i
-th iteration of the
for
loop of line
4
, the algorithm correctly fills the matrix representing
M
up to the
i
-th row, and
does so in polynomial time, assuming access to a factoring oracle.

base case:
i=0
.

(i.e., before the loop of line
4
starts), the
0
-th row of the matrix is already populated with all
0
s (line
3
).

induction hypothesis.

The first
(i-1)
th rows of the matrix
M
are correctly populated. That is, for each
j\in[0..i-1]
and
k\in[0..n-j]
, we have
M(j,k)=\left\llbracket\sigma\right\rrbracket{x_{k}}\bmod\nu_{\sigma}^{j}(g)
.

\begin{array}[]{cccccc}\nu_{\sigma}^{0}(g)=g&\nu_{\sigma}(g)&\nu_{\sigma}^{2}(g)&\nu_{\sigma}^{3}(g)&\nu_{\sigma}^{4}(g)\\[3.0pt]
0&0&0&0&0&x_{0}\\
\left\llbracket\sigma\right\rrbracket{x_{1}}\bmod g&\left\llbracket\sigma\right\rrbracket{x_{1}}\bmod\nu_{\sigma}(g)&\left\llbracket\sigma\right\rrbracket{x_{1}}\bmod\nu_{\sigma}^{2}(g)&\left\llbracket\sigma\right\rrbracket{x_{1}}\bmod\nu_{\sigma}^{3}(g)&{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\times}&x_{1}\\
\left\llbracket\sigma\right\rrbracket{x_{2}}\bmod g&\left\llbracket\sigma\right\rrbracket{x_{2}}\bmod\nu_{\sigma}(g)&\left\llbracket\sigma\right\rrbracket{x_{2}}\bmod\nu_{\sigma}^{2}(g)&{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\times}&{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\times}&x_{2}\\
-&-&{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\times}&{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\times}&{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\times}&x_{3}\\
-&{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\times}&{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\times}&{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\times}&{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\times}&x_{4}\\
\end{array}

Figure 3:
Illustration of how the matrix
M
looks like after
2
iterations of the loop of line
4
(in the case of
n=4
). To fill the next row, we only need the values in the rows above it. Entries with
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\times}
are left empty in the algorithm as they are not needed to perform the computation of
\left\llbracket\sigma\right\rrbracket(x_{n})\bmod g
.

induction step:
i\geq 1
.

The
i
th iteration of the loop handles the variable
x_{i}
.
Line
5
computes
integers
a_{0},\dots,a_{i-1}
such that
\left\llbracket\sigma\right\rrbracket(x_{i})=\frac{1}{d(\sigma)}\sum_{j=0}^{i-1}a_{j}2^{\left\llbracket\sigma\right\rrbracket(x_{j})}
.
Following
Lemma

27
, this computation can be
performed in polynomial time.
Next, for each
k\in[0..n-i]
, the inner
for
loop of line
6
computes
\left\llbracket\sigma\right\rrbracket(x_{i})\bmod\nu_{\sigma}^{k}(g)
, storing the in
M(i,k)
(see line
13
).
In order to compute
\left\llbracket\sigma\right\rrbracket(x_{i})\bmod\nu_{\sigma}^{k}(g)
, the algorithm uses the identity

\left\llbracket\sigma\right\rrbracket(x_{i})\bmod\nu_{\sigma}^{k}(g)\ =\ {\left(\frac{1}{d(\sigma)}\cdot\sum\nolimits_{j=0}^{i-1}a_{j}\cdot\big(2^{\left\llbracket\sigma\right\rrbracket(x_{j})}\bmod\nu_{\sigma}^{k}(g)\cdot d(\sigma)\big)\right)\bmod\nu_{\sigma}^{k}(g)}.

(28)

We discuss the
(k+1)
th iteration of the inner
for
loop of line
6
, analyzing it line by line.

line
7
.

The algorithm computes
\nu_{\sigma}^{k}(g)
. The integer factorization oracle is used to factorize the arguments of Euler’s totient function, to then compute the result of this function via
Equation

23
.
Since
\nu_{\sigma}^{k}(g)
is bounded by
d(\sigma)^{k}g
, the computation is polynomial time except the oracle calls. The value
h=\nu_{\sigma}^{k}(g)\cdot d(\sigma)
is then computed in polynomial time.

line
8
.

The algorithm factorizes
h
as
h=2^{m}\cdot q
, where
q\in\mathbb{N}
is the largest odd divisor of
h
. This is done by repeatedly dividing
h
by
2
until it becomes odd to compute
q
; which takes polynomial time. Note that
2^{m}
and
q
are coprime.

The values
m
and
q
will be used to compute the value
r_{j}\coloneqq 2^{\left\llbracket\sigma\right\rrbracket(x_{j})}\bmod h
, following
Equation

28
, in the
(j+1)
th iteration of the inner
for
loop of line
9
.
To do this, we first compute
b\coloneqq 2^{\left\llbracket\sigma\right\rrbracket(x_{j})}\bmod 2^{m}
and
c\coloneqq 2^{\left\llbracket\sigma\right\rrbracket(x_{j})}\bmod q
. Then, by the CRT, we compute the unique
r_{j}\in[0..h-1]
such that
r_{j}\equiv b\bmod 2^{m}
and
r_{j}\equiv c\bmod q
.

We analyse lines
10
–
12
locally to the
(j+1)
th iteration of the
for
loop of line
10
(
j\in[0..i-1]
).

line
10
.

This line computes
b=2^{\left\llbracket\sigma\right\rrbracket(x_{j})}\bmod 2^{m}
.
If
\left\llbracket\sigma\right\rrbracket(x_{j})\geq m
then clearly
b=0
, and otherwise
b=2^{\left\llbracket\sigma\right\rrbracket(x_{j})}
.
The comparison
\left\llbracket\sigma\right\rrbracket(x_{j})\geq m
can be performed in polynomial time using
Algorithm

7
.
Moreover, when we set
b=2^{\left\llbracket\sigma\right\rrbracket(x_{j})}
, its value is less than
2^{m}
, so its bit size remains polynomial in the input size.

line
11
.

This line computes
c=2^{\left\llbracket\sigma\right\rrbracket(x_{j})}\bmod q
. First, recall that Euler’s theorem states that if two numbers
a
and
q
are coprime, then
a^{\phi(q)}
is congruent to
1
modulo
q
. Since
q
is odd, we thus have
c=2^{\left\llbracket\sigma\right\rrbracket(x_{j})\bmod\phi(q)}\bmod q
.
From the induction hypothesis,
we have already set
M(j,k+1)
to
\left\llbracket\sigma\right\rrbracket(x_{j})\bmod\phi(q)
in previous iterations of the loop of line
4
.
Therefore, to compute
c

it only remains to compute
2^{M(j,k+1)}\bmod q

in polynomial time; which can be done by relying on the
exponentiation-by-squaring method
[
BW08
, Ch. 1.4]
.

line
12
.

Given
b
and
c
, the algorithm replies on (a constructive version of) the CRT to compute
r_{j}
in polynomial time.
More precisely, for this computation one can use the extended Euclidean algorithm to compute two integers
\ell_{1}
and
\ell_{2}
such that
\ell_{1}\cdot 2^{m}+\ell_{2}\cdot q=1

(
\ell_{1}
and
\ell_{2}
exist by Bézout’s identity).
Then,
r_{j}\coloneqq(b\cdot\ell_{2}\cdot q+c\cdot\ell_{1}\cdot 2^{m})\bmod h
.

When the
for
loop of line
9
ends, the algorithm has computed
r_{j}
for every
{j\in[0..i-1]}
.
Line
13

executes, populating
M(i,k)
in polynomial time following the formula in
Equation

28
.

From the analysis above, we conclude that the algorithm is correct.
Regarding the running time, note that each loop in the procedure iterates only over natural numbers bounded by
n
(which is bounded by the bit size of
\sigma
).
Furthermore, all other operations performed by the algorithm run in polynomial time,
except for line
7
,
which relies on the integer factorization oracle to compute
\nu_{\sigma}^{k}(g)
.
So,
Algorithm

8
runs in polynomial time with a factoring oracle, and
\textup{{Div}}_{\scalebox{0.7}{{{ILESLP}}}}
is in
\textup{{P}}^{\textup{{factoring}}}
.
∎

We can avoid appealing to the factorization oracle by
providing
Algorithm

8
with the set

\mathbb{P}(\sigma,g)\coloneqq\{p\text{ prime}:p\text{ divides either $d(\sigma)$ or $\nu_{\sigma}^{k}(g)$, for some $k\in[0..n-2]$}\}.

(29)

Observe that this set only contains polynomially many primes of polynomial bit size with respect to the sizes of
\sigma
and
g
, since
\nu_{\sigma}^{k}(g)\leq d(\sigma)^{k}\cdot g
.

Lemma 29.

Algorithm

8
runs in polynomial time, when provided with the set
\mathbb{P}(\sigma,g)
(or any superset of this set) as an additional input.

Proof.

Following the proof of
Lemma

2
,
the only line that requires the factoring oracle is line
7
.
In particular, this line asks to compute
\nu_{\sigma}^{k}(g)
, for a value of
k
that ranges in
[0..n-1]
.
By induction on
k\in[0..n-1]
, we show that knowing
\mathbb{P}(\sigma,g)
suffices to compute this number in polynomial time.

base case:
k=0
.

Since
\nu_{\sigma}^{0}(g)=g
, and
g
is part of the input, this case is trivial.

induction hypothesis.

For
k\geq 1
, the positive integer
\nu_{\sigma}^{k-1}(g)
can be computed in polynomial time in the sizes of
\sigma
and
g
, by relying on
\mathbb{P}(\sigma,g)
.

induction step:
k\geq 1
.

By induction hypothesis, we can compute
\nu_{\sigma}^{k-1}(g)
in polynomial time. Observe that
k-1\leq n-2
, and therefore
all prime divisors of
\nu_{\sigma}^{k-1}(g)
occur in
\mathbb{P}(\sigma,g)
.
Recall that this set has size polynomial in the sizes of
\sigma
and
g
.
We iterate through
\mathbb{P}(\sigma,g)
in order to find all prime divisors of
\nu_{\sigma}^{k-1}(g)
,
as well as those of
d(\sigma)
. With this set of primes at hand, we can efficiently compute the prime factorization of
{\textit{odd}(\nu_{\sigma}^{k-1}(g)\cdot d(\sigma))}
.
Finally, we compute
\nu_{\sigma}^{k}(g)=\phi(\textit{odd}(\nu_{\sigma}^{k-1}(g)\cdot d(\sigma)))
using
Equation

23
.
∎

9
Computing an ILESLP representing
x\bmod 2^{y}

Computation of
x\bmod 2^{y}

Input:

An ILESLP
\sigma
and two of its variables
x
and
y
.

Output:

An ILESLP
\xi
such that
\left\llbracket\xi\right\rrbracket_{\bullet}=\left\llbracket\sigma\right\rrbracket(x)\bmod 2^{\left\llbracket\sigma\right\rrbracket(y)}
.

Algorithm

9
describes a procedure for solving the above problem.
It builds on
Algorithms

7
and
8
,
inheriting a polynomial running time given either a factoring oracle or access to the set

\mathbb{P}(\sigma,\nu_{\sigma}(1))
.

Algorithm 9

An
\textup{{FP}}^{\textup{{factoring}}}
algorithm for computing
x\bmod 2^{y}
.

1:

ILESLP
\sigma\coloneqq(x_{0}\leftarrow\rho_{0},\dots,x_{n}\leftarrow\rho_{n})
, and two variables
x
and
y
in
\sigma
.

2:

if

\left\llbracket\sigma\right\rrbracket(y)\leq 0

then

return
the ILESLP
(x_{0}\leftarrow 0)

3:

let

a_{0},\dots,a_{n-1}\in\mathbb{Z}
such that
\left\llbracket\sigma\right\rrbracket(x)=\frac{\sum_{j=0}^{n-1}a_{j}\cdot 2^{\left\llbracket\sigma\right\rrbracket(x_{j})}}{d(\sigma)}

4:

I\leftarrow\{j\in[0..n-1]:\left\llbracket\sigma\right\rrbracket(x_{j})<\left\llbracket\sigma\right\rrbracket(y)\}

\triangleright

each comparison resolved with
Algorithm

7

5:

let

S=\sum_{j\in I}a_{j}\cdot 2^{x_{j}}
and
L=\sum_{j\in[0..n-1]\setminus I}a_{j}\cdot 2^{x_{j}}

6:

A\leftarrow\sum_{j\in I}\left|a_{j}\right|

7:

Perform binary search to find
q\in[-A..A]

satisfying
0\leq\left\llbracket\sigma\right\rrbracket(S)-q\cdot 2^{\left\llbracket\sigma\right\rrbracket(y)}<2^{\left\llbracket\sigma\right\rrbracket(y)}

8:

\triangleright

each iteration of binary search uses
Algorithm

7

9:

let

r
be the residue of
\left\llbracket\sigma\right\rrbracket(L)\cdot 2^{-\left\llbracket\sigma\right\rrbracket(y)}+q
modulo
d(\sigma)
\triangleright

uses
Algorithm

8

10:

return
an ILESLP
\xi
such that
\left\llbracket\xi\right\rrbracket_{\bullet}=\frac{1}{d(\sigma)}\cdot\big(\left\llbracket\sigma\right\rrbracket(S)+(r-q)\cdot 2^{\left\llbracket\sigma\right\rrbracket(y)}\big)

Lemma 30.

Given an ILESLP
\sigma
and two of its variables
x
and
y
,
Algorithm

9
returns an ILESLP
\xi
such that
\left\llbracket\xi\right\rrbracket_{\bullet}=\left\llbracket\sigma\right\rrbracket(x)\bmod 2^{\left\llbracket\sigma\right\rrbracket(y)}
. The algorithm runs in polynomial time with a factoring oracle.

Proof.

We analyze the runtime and correctness of the algorithm line by line,
providing the underlying intuition throughout.
Below, let
\sigma\coloneqq(x_{0}\leftarrow\rho_{0},\dots,x_{n}\leftarrow\rho_{n})
.
Recall that
d(\sigma)
is a positive integer.

line
2
.

Over the reals, given
a,m\in\mathbb{R}
with
m\neq 0
,

(a\bmod m)
is defined as
a-m\cdot\left\lfloor\frac{a}{m}\right\rfloor
. In particular,
(a\bmod 2^{\ell})=0
whenever
\ell\leq 0
. Accordingly, line
2
checks whether
\left\llbracket\sigma\right\rrbracket(y)\leq 0
, and in that case the algorithm returns an ILESLP that encodes the number
0
.
This line can be implemented in polynomial time by appealing to
Algorithm

7
.
Below, assume
\left\llbracket\sigma\right\rrbracket(y)\geq 1
.

line
3
.

As done in
Algorithms

7
and
8
, this line computes in polynomial time (
Lemma

27
) an expression
E\coloneqq\sum_{j=0}^{n-1}a_{j}\cdot 2^{x_{j}}
, where
a_{0},\ldots,a_{n-1}\in\mathbb{Z}
, and
\left\llbracket\sigma\right\rrbracket(x)=\frac{E}{d(\sigma)}
.

lines
4
and
5
.

The algorithm sorts the monomial
a\cdot 2^{z}
in the expression
E
depending on the comparison
\left\llbracket\sigma\right\rrbracket(z)<\left\llbracket\sigma\right\rrbracket(y)
.
This is done by computing the set
I\subseteq[0..n-1]
of indices
j
of variables
x_{j}
such that
\left\llbracket\sigma\right\rrbracket(x_{j})<\left\llbracket\sigma\right\rrbracket(y)
. Then,
E
can be rearranged as
L+S
, where

S\coloneqq\sum_{j\in I}a_{j}\cdot 2^{x_{j}}
contains the exponentials
2^{z}
that are “small” comparatively to
2^{\left\llbracket\sigma\right\rrbracket(y)}
, and
L\coloneqq\sum_{j\in[0..n-1]\setminus I}a_{j}\cdot 2^{x_{j}}

contains those that are “large”.
In particular,
2^{\left\llbracket\sigma\right\rrbracket(y)}
divides
\left\llbracket\sigma\right\rrbracket(L)
.
The set
I
can be constructed in polynomial time, by
appealing
n
times to
Algorithm

7
.

line
6
.

This line computes (in polynomial time)
A\coloneqq\sum_{j\in I}\left|a_{j}\right|
. Observe that:

\left|\left\llbracket\sigma\right\rrbracket(S)\right|=\sum\nolimits_{j\in I}\left|a_{j}\right|\cdot 2^{\left\llbracket\sigma\right\rrbracket(x_{j})}\leq A\cdot 2^{\left\llbracket\sigma\right\rrbracket(y)}.

(30)

line
7
.

This line computes the quotient
q
of the division of
\left\llbracket\sigma\right\rrbracket(S)
by
2^{\left\llbracket\sigma\right\rrbracket(y)}
;
formally, the only integer satisfying
0\leq\left\llbracket\sigma\right\rrbracket(S)-q\cdot 2^{\left\llbracket\sigma\right\rrbracket(y)}<2^{\left\llbracket\sigma\right\rrbracket(y)}
.
By
Equation

30
, we know that
q\in[-A..A]
.
Since
A
has bit size polynomial in the size of
\sigma
, we can compute
q
in polynomial time
by performing binary search on the interval
[-A..A]
, appealing to
Algorithm

7
.
For the sake of completeness, let us briefly explain how the search is implemented.
Suppose knowing that the required
q
belongs to
[\ell..u]
, where
l,u\in\mathbb{Z}
.
Initially,
[\ell..u]=[-A..A]
. Let
v\coloneqq\left\lceil\frac{\ell+u}{2}\right\rceil
. Then,

•

If
\left\llbracket\sigma\right\rrbracket(S)-v\cdot 2^{\left\llbracket\sigma\right\rrbracket(y)}<0
, then we can restrict the search to
[\ell..v]
.

•

If
\left\llbracket\sigma\right\rrbracket(S)-v\cdot 2^{\left\llbracket\sigma\right\rrbracket(y)}>2^{\left\llbracket\sigma\right\rrbracket(y)}
, then we can restrict the search to
[v..u]
.

•

If none of the previous two cases hold, then
v
is the required
q
.

The conditions in the first two cases above are checked in polynomial time using
Algorithm

7
.
Specifically, by following the operations in the expression
S-v\cdot 2^{y}
,
it is simple to extend the ILESLP
\sigma
into a new ILESLP
\sigma^{\prime}
such that
\left\llbracket\sigma^{\prime}\right\rrbracket_{\bullet}=\left\llbracket\sigma\right\rrbracket(S)-v\cdot 2^{\left\llbracket\sigma\right\rrbracket(y)}
. One can then apply
Algorithm

7
to
\sigma^{\prime}

to check the first of the two cases (the second case is handled similarly).

From the definition of the expression
E
, we have:

\left\llbracket\sigma\right\rrbracket(x)=\frac{\left\llbracket\sigma\right\rrbracket(E)}{d(\sigma)}=\frac{\left\llbracket\sigma\right\rrbracket(L)+\left\llbracket\sigma\right\rrbracket(S)}{d(\sigma)}=\frac{(\left\llbracket\sigma\right\rrbracket(L)+q\cdot 2^{\left\llbracket\sigma\right\rrbracket(y)})+(\left\llbracket\sigma\right\rrbracket(S)-q\cdot 2^{\left\llbracket\sigma\right\rrbracket(y)})}{d(\sigma)}.

(31)

line
9
.

This line computes the residue
r
of
\left\llbracket\sigma\right\rrbracket(L)\cdot 2^{-\left\llbracket\sigma\right\rrbracket(y)}+q
modulo
d(\sigma)
.
This is done by constructing, in polynomial time, an ILESLP
\sigma^{\prime}
encoding
\left\llbracket\sigma\right\rrbracket(L)\cdot 2^{-\left\llbracket\sigma\right\rrbracket(y)}+q
, and then calling
Algorithm

8
on
\sigma^{\prime}
and
d(\sigma)
.
Thus, this line can be implemented in polynomial time with access to the factoring oracle
—this is the only line of the algorithm requiring the oracle.
To construct
\sigma^{\prime}
, recall that
the expression
L=\sum_{j\in[0..n-1]\setminus I}a_{j}\cdot 2^{x_{j}}

is such that
\left\llbracket\sigma\right\rrbracket(x_{j})\geq\left\llbracket\sigma\right\rrbracket(y)
for every
j\in[0..n-1]\setminus I
.
By following the operations in the expression
{L^{\prime}\coloneqq\sum_{j\in[0..n-1]\setminus I}a_{j}\cdot 2^{x_{j}-y}}
, we can extend
\sigma
into an ILESLP
\sigma^{\prime\prime}

such that
\left\llbracket\sigma^{\prime\prime}\right\rrbracket_{\bullet}=\left\llbracket\sigma\right\rrbracket(L^{\prime})=\left\llbracket\sigma\right\rrbracket(L)\cdot 2^{-\left\llbracket\sigma\right\rrbracket(y)}
.
Finally,
\sigma^{\prime\prime}
can be further extended to produce the desired
\sigma^{\prime}
.

Following
Equation

31
, we see that:

\displaystyle\left\llbracket\sigma\right\rrbracket(x)

\displaystyle=\frac{(\left\llbracket\sigma\right\rrbracket(L)+q\cdot 2^{\left\llbracket\sigma\right\rrbracket(y)})+(\left\llbracket\sigma\right\rrbracket(S)-q\cdot 2^{\left\llbracket\sigma\right\rrbracket(y)})}{d(\sigma)}

\displaystyle=\frac{(\left\llbracket\sigma\right\rrbracket(L)+q\cdot 2^{\left\llbracket\sigma\right\rrbracket(y)}-r\cdot 2^{\left\llbracket\sigma\right\rrbracket(y)})+(\left\llbracket\sigma\right\rrbracket(S)-q\cdot 2^{\left\llbracket\sigma\right\rrbracket(y)}+r\cdot 2^{\left\llbracket\sigma\right\rrbracket(y)})}{d(\sigma)}

\displaystyle=\frac{\left\llbracket\sigma\right\rrbracket(L)+q\cdot 2^{\left\llbracket\sigma\right\rrbracket(y)}-r\cdot 2^{\left\llbracket\sigma\right\rrbracket(y)}}{d(\sigma)}+\frac{\left\llbracket\sigma\right\rrbracket(S)-q\cdot 2^{\left\llbracket\sigma\right\rrbracket(y)}+r\cdot 2^{\left\llbracket\sigma\right\rrbracket(y)}}{d(\sigma)}

\displaystyle=\frac{\left\llbracket\sigma\right\rrbracket(L^{\prime})+q-r}{{d(\sigma)}}\cdot 2^{\left\llbracket\sigma\right\rrbracket(y)}+\frac{\left\llbracket\sigma\right\rrbracket(S)+(r-q)\cdot 2^{\left\llbracket\sigma\right\rrbracket(y)}}{d(\sigma)}.

(32)

By definition of
r
,

\frac{\left\llbracket\sigma\right\rrbracket(L^{\prime})+q-r}{{d(\sigma)}}
is an integer.
Then, since
\left\llbracket\sigma\right\rrbracket(x)
and
\left\llbracket\sigma\right\rrbracket(y)
are both integers, and the latter is positive,

Equation

32
shows that
\ell\coloneqq\frac{\left\llbracket\sigma\right\rrbracket(S)+(r-q)\cdot 2^{\left\llbracket\sigma\right\rrbracket(y)}}{d(\sigma)}
is an integer.

line
10
.

From
Equation

32
, we conclude that
\left\llbracket\sigma\right\rrbracket(x)\bmod 2^{\left\llbracket\sigma\right\rrbracket(y)}=\ell\bmod 2^{\left\llbracket\sigma\right\rrbracket(y)}
.
We will now show that
\ell\in[0..2^{\left\llbracket\sigma\right\rrbracket(y)}-1]
, which implies that
\ell

is in fact
\left\llbracket\sigma\right\rrbracket(x)\bmod 2^{\left\llbracket\sigma\right\rrbracket(y)}
.
Accordingly, line
10

of the algorithm constructs (and returns) an ILESLP
\xi
encoding
\ell
.
Clearly,
\xi
can be constructed in polynomial time
by extending
\sigma
, following the operations in the expression
\frac{1}{d(\sigma)}\cdot\big(S+(r-q)\cdot 2^{y}\big)
.
Recall that
(\left\llbracket\sigma\right\rrbracket(S)-q\cdot 2^{\left\llbracket\sigma\right\rrbracket(y)})\in[0..2^{\left\llbracket\sigma\right\rrbracket(y)}-1]

and
r\in[0..d(\sigma)-1]
,
by definition of
q
and
r
, respectively.
Then,

0\,\leq\,\left\llbracket\sigma\right\rrbracket(S)-q\cdot 2^{\left\llbracket\sigma\right\rrbracket(y)}\,\leq\,\left\llbracket\sigma\right\rrbracket(S)+(r-q)\cdot 2^{\left\llbracket\sigma\right\rrbracket(y)},

and
\left\llbracket\sigma\right\rrbracket(S)+(r-q)\cdot 2^{\left\llbracket\sigma\right\rrbracket(y)}\,<\,2^{\left\llbracket\sigma\right\rrbracket(y)}+r\cdot 2^{\left\llbracket\sigma\right\rrbracket(y)}\,\leq\,d(\sigma)\cdot 2^{\left\llbracket\sigma\right\rrbracket(y)}
.
Therefore, by definition of
\ell
,
we conclude that
\ell\in[0..2^{\left\llbracket\sigma\right\rrbracket(y)}-1]
.
∎

Lastly, we show that
\left\llbracket\sigma\right\rrbracket(x)\bmod 2^{\left\llbracket\sigma\right\rrbracket(y)}
is computable in polynomial time given
\mathbb{P}(\sigma,\nu_{\sigma}(1))
.

Lemma 31.

Algorithm

9
runs in polynomial time
when provided
\mathbb{P}(\sigma,\nu_{\sigma}(1))
as an additional input.

Proof.

As explained during the proof of
Lemma

30
,
only line
9
requires the factoring oracle.
This line requires computing the residue of

\left\llbracket\sigma\right\rrbracket(L)\cdot 2^{-\left\llbracket\sigma\right\rrbracket(y)}+q
modulo
d(\sigma)
,
where
L\coloneqq\sum_{j\in[0..n-1]\setminus I}a_{j}\cdot 2^{x_{j}}

defined in line
5
is such that

\left\llbracket\sigma\right\rrbracket(x_{j})\geq\left\llbracket\sigma(y)\right\rrbracket
for every
j\in[0..n-1]\setminus I
.
Therefore,

\displaystyle\left\llbracket\sigma\right\rrbracket(L)\cdot 2^{-\left\llbracket\sigma\right\rrbracket(y)}+q\ =\ \sum\nolimits_{j\in[0..n-1]\setminus I}a_{j}\cdot 2^{\left\llbracket\sigma\right\rrbracket(x_{j})-\left\llbracket\sigma\right\rrbracket(y)}+q.

Since all
a_{j}
and
q
have a bit size polynomial in the size of
\sigma
,
it suffices to show how to compute
2^{\left\llbracket\sigma\right\rrbracket(x_{j})-\left\llbracket\sigma\right\rrbracket(y)}\bmod d(\sigma)

in polynomial time. The arguments are similar as those in
Section

8
.

Let
m\in\mathbb{N}
be such that
d(\sigma)=2^{m}\cdot\textit{odd}(d(\sigma))
. By the CRT,
2^{\left\llbracket\sigma\right\rrbracket(x_{j})-\left\llbracket\sigma\right\rrbracket(y)}\bmod d(\sigma)
can be computed in polynomial time given the following two values:

b\coloneqq 2^{\left\llbracket\sigma\right\rrbracket(x_{j})-\left\llbracket\sigma\right\rrbracket(y)}\bmod 2^{m}

and
c\coloneqq 2^{\left\llbracket\sigma\right\rrbracket(x_{j})-\left\llbracket\sigma\right\rrbracket(y)}\bmod\textit{odd}(d(\sigma))
,

The value
b
can be computed in polynomial time by appealing to
Algorithm

7
.
This is done as for the identically named value “
b
” in line
10
of
Algorithm

8
; see the proof
of
Lemma

2
.

By definition, the set
\mathbb{P}(\sigma,\nu_{\sigma}(1))
contains all prime factors of
d(\sigma)
. Therefore, we can compute
t\coloneqq\nu_{\sigma}(1)=\phi(\textit{odd}(d(\sigma)))

in polynomial time using
Equation

23
.
For obtaining the value
c
, we then first derive the residue
r\coloneqq(\left\llbracket\sigma\right\rrbracket(x_{j})\bmod t)
and the residue
s\coloneqq(\left\llbracket\sigma\right\rrbracket(y)\bmod t)
in polynomial time using
Algorithm

8
.
Afterwards,
c={\big(2^{(r-s)\bmod t}\bmod\textit{odd}(d(\sigma))\big)}

is computed in polynomial time using the exponentiation-by-squaring method.
∎

Part III
On the complexity of ILEP

We combine the results of the two previous parts of the paper
to show
Corollary

1
, i.e.,
that the optimization problem for integer linear-exponential programs is in
NPO-cmp
.
The first section of this part of the paper introduces the class
NPO-cmp
.
The second section proves the corollary.

10
The complexity class
NPO-cmp

We briefly recall the notion of an optimization problem.
An
optimization problem

\mathcal{P}
is characterized by a quintuple
(I,U,\mathrm{sol},m,\mathrm{goal})
where:

•

I
is the set of instances of
\mathcal{P}
,

•

U
is a set (or,
universe
) containing all possible solutions,

•

\mathrm{sol}\colon I\to 2^{U}
assigns each input instance
x\in I
to the set of its solutions
\mathrm{sol}(x)
,

•

m\colon I\times U\rightharpoonup\mathbb{Z}
is the
measure function
, a partial function defined for every
x\in I
and
y\in\mathrm{sol}(x)
,

•

\mathrm{goal}\in\{\min,\max\}
specifies a minimization or a maximization objective.

For
x\in I
,
the set of
optimal solutions
of
x
is defined as

\mathrm{opt}(x)\coloneqq\{y\in\mathrm{sol}(x):m(x,y)=\mathrm{goal}\{m(x,z):z\in\mathrm{sol}(x)\}\}.

The computational task associated to
\mathcal{P}
is the following:

Input:

An instance
x\in I
.

Output:

An element
y\in\mathrm{opt}(x)
if
\mathrm{opt}(x)\neq\emptyset
, otherwise
reject
.

Below, we assume the elements of the sets
I
and
U
to be endowed with a notion of size
\left|\cdot\right|
.
We define
NPO-cmp
as the class of all optimization problems
\mathcal{P}=(I,U,\mathrm{sol},m,\mathrm{goal})
such that:

1.

The sets
I
and
U
are recognizable in polynomial time.

2.

Given in input
x\in I
and
y\in U
, checking
y\in\mathrm{sol}(x)
is in
P
.

3.

m
is computable, and checking
m(x,y_{1})\leq m(x,y_{2})
, given
x\in I
and
y_{1},y_{2}\in\mathrm{sol}(x)
, is in
P
.

4.

There is a polynomial
q\colon\mathbb{N}\to\mathbb{N}
such that, for all
x\in I
,
\mathrm{short}(x)\coloneqq\{{y\in\mathrm{sol}(x)}:{\left|y\right|\leq q(\left|x\right|)}\}
satisfies:

(a)

if
\mathrm{sol}(x)\neq\emptyset
then
\mathrm{short}(x)\neq\emptyset
, and

(b)

if
\mathrm{opt}(x)\neq\emptyset
then
\mathrm{opt}(x)\cap\mathrm{short}(x)\neq\emptyset
.

5.

Given an instance
x\in I
, deciding
{\mathrm{sol}(x)\neq\emptyset\land\mathrm{opt}(x)=\emptyset}
is in
NP
.

It is worth noting that some authors prefer replacing Properties (
4
) and (
5
) above with the simpler

4’.

There is a polynomial
q\colon\mathbb{N}\to\mathbb{N}
such that
\left|y\right|\leq q(\left|x\right|)
for every
x\in I
and
y\in\mathrm{sol}(x)
,

which in particular implies the finiteness of
\mathrm{sol}(x)
(see, e.g., the definition of
NPO
in
[
AMC+99
]
).
The only difference between Properties (
4
) and (
5
) and Property (
4’.
) lies in whether only small solutions are considered:
if an optimization problem
(I,U,\mathrm{sol},m,\mathrm{goal})
is in
NPO-cmp
,
then the problem
(I,U,\mathrm{short},m,\mathrm{goal})
, where
\mathrm{short}
is the function required by Property (
4
), is also in
NPO-cmp
and satisfies Property (
4’.
).
In other words, Property (
4’.
) reflects the idea that only polynomial size solutions are reasonable solutions.
Our rationale for preferring the more wordy Properties (
4
) and (
5
) is that they provide a nice blueprint for
organizing the results in the previous parts of the paper.
This modest goal is indeed the main purpose behind the class
NPO-cmp
;
as stated in the introduction, we make no presumption on the naturality of this class in a broader context.

Aside from the differences between Properties (
4
) and (
5
) and Property (
4’.
),
starting from the definition of
NPO-cmp
, one obtains the class
NPO
by
replacing Property (
3
) with the stronger

3’.

m
is computable in polynomial time, assuming a binary encoding for the integer in output.

Therefore, every problem in
NPO
belongs to
NPO-cmp
.

Expanding on the discussion in
Section

1.3
,
we see that, when
\mathcal{P}
belongs to
NPO
, Properties (
3’.
)
and (
4’.
) ensure that, for any input
x\in I
, one can compute in polynomial time two integers
a
and
b

such that for every (short) solution
y\in\mathrm{sol}(x)
we have
{m(x,y)\in[a..b]}
.
(Implicitly, this step assumes knowing the polynomial
q
in Property (
4’.
), as well as a polynomial bounding the runtime of
m
.)
One can then search for the optimal solution by performing binary search: at each iteration, the interval
[a..b]
shrinks in half following the answer to the query
\exists y\in U:y\in\mathrm{sol}(x)\land m(x,y)\geq\frac{b-a}{2}
. By Properties (
2
), (
3’.
) and (
4’.
), this query is solvable in
NP
.
Using this approach, it follows that
NPO
problems can be solved by polynomial-time Turing machines with access to an
NP
oracle, that is,
\textup{{NPO}}\subseteq\textup{{FP}}^{\textup{{NP}}}
. (In fact,
\textup{{NPO}}=\textup{{FP}}^{\textup{{NP}}}
for a suitable model of computation characterizing
NPO
, see
[
Kre88
,
CP89
]
.)

In the case of
NPO-cmp
, Property (
4
)
ensures that
{\{m(x,y):y\in\mathrm{short}(x)\}}
is a set of exponentially many integers containing the optimal value for
m
(if one exists). However,
NPO-cmp
does not fix any representation on the integers returned by
m
(we only know that one such representation exists, since
m
is computable). Therefore, the size of these integers is unknown, and there is no guarantee that binary search can be performed on this set.
Instead of an inclusion within
\textup{{FP}}^{\textup{{NP}}}
, we have
{\textup{{NPO-cmp}}\subseteq\textup{{FNP}}^{\textup{{NP}}}}
. Indeed: a polynomial-time non-deterministic Turing machine with access to an
NP
oracle
can solve an
NPO-cmp
problem in the following simple way:

1:

Check that the input
x
belongs to
I
; if not,
reject

\triangleright

In
P
by Property (
1
).

2:

Query the
NP
oracle to determine if
{\mathrm{sol}(x)\neq\emptyset\land\mathrm{opt}(x)=\emptyset}
holds; if the answer is
yes
,
reject

3:

\triangleright

This query can be solved in
NP
by Property (
5
).

4:

Guess a string
y
of length
q(\left|x\right|)
, where
q
is the polynomial in Property (
4
)

5:

Check
y\in U
and
y\in\mathrm{sol}(x)
; if not,
reject

\triangleright

In
P
by Properties (
1
) and (
2
).

6:

Query the
NP
oracle to determine if there exists
z\in\mathrm{short}(x)
such that
m(x,z)>m(x,y)
(assuming
\mathrm{goal}=\max
); if the answer is
yes
,
reject

7:

\triangleright

This query can be solved in
NP
because
\left|z\right|\leq q(\left|x\right|)
, and checking whether
z\in\mathrm{sol}(x)
and

8:

m(x,z)>m(x,y)
can be done in polynomial time by Properties (
2
) and (
3
).

9:

return

y

11
ILEP is in
NPO-cmp

We now prove that the optimization problem for integer linear-exponential programs is in
NPO-cmp
(
Corollary

1
). Let us first define the objects
I,U,\mathrm{sol}
and
m
, noting that
\mathrm{goal}
is simply
\min
or
\max
:

•

I
is the set of all pairs
(\tau,\varphi)
where
\tau
is a linear-exponential term (the objective function) and
\varphi
is an integer linear-exponential program. The size
\left|(\tau,\varphi)\right|
of
(\tau,\varphi)\in I
is the sum of the sizes of
\tau
and
\varphi
.

•

U\coloneqq\{(\sigma,\mathbb{P}(\sigma)):\sigma\text{ is a ILESLP}\}
, where
\mathbb{P}(\sigma)\coloneqq\mathbb{P}(\sigma,d(\sigma)\cdot\nu_{\sigma}(1))
and

–

d(\sigma)
is the product of all denominators occurring in rational constants of scaling expressions in
\sigma

(as defined at the beginning of
Part

II
);

–

\nu_{\sigma}
is the function
\nu_{\sigma}(x)\coloneqq\phi(\textit{odd}(x\cdot d(\sigma)))
, as defined in
Section

8
;

–

\mathbb{P}(\sigma,g)
is the set of primes defined in
Equation

29
on page
29
.

The size
\left|(\sigma,\mathbb{P}(\sigma))\right|
of
(\sigma,\mathbb{P}(\sigma))\in U

is the sum of the bit sizes of
\sigma
and
\mathbb{P}(\sigma)
.

•

Given
(\tau,\varphi)\in I
, we define
\mathrm{sol}(\tau,\varphi)
as the set of all
(\sigma,\mathbb{P}(\sigma))\in U
with the following property. Let
{\sigma=(x_{0}\leftarrow\rho_{0},\,\dots\,,\,x_{n}\leftarrow\rho_{n})}
. Then,

(a)

the set
\{x_{0},\dots,x_{n}\}
contains (at least) all variables in
\tau
and in
\varphi
;

(b)

each variable
x
occurring in
\varphi
or
\tau
is such that
\left\llbracket\sigma\right\rrbracket(x)\geq 0
;

(c)

the map assigning to each
x
in
\varphi
the value
\left\llbracket\sigma\right\rrbracket(x_{i})
is a solution of
\varphi
.

•

Given
(\tau,\varphi)\in I
and
(\sigma,\mathbb{P}(\sigma))\in\mathrm{sol}(\tau,\varphi)
, we define
m((\tau,\varphi),\sigma)
as the integer
\tau(\sigma)
obtained by
evaluating
\tau
, replacing each variable
x
occurring in it with
\left\llbracket\sigma\right\rrbracket(x)
.

Let us prove that these objects satisfy the five properties of
NPO-cmp
.

Property (
1
).

The set
I
is clearly recognizable in polynomial time.
We show that the same is true for the set
U
—this is the content of
Proposition

1
(
Section

1.2
):

See
1

Proof.

Consider a pair
(\sigma,S)
, where
S
is a set of positive integers, and
\sigma\coloneqq(x_{0}\leftarrow\rho_{0},\,\dots\,,\,x_{n}\leftarrow\rho_{n})
is a LESLP
(both objects are clearly recognizable in polynomial time). We first check that
S=\mathbb{P}(\sigma)
.
Recall that, given
g\in\mathbb{N}_{\geq 1}
,

\mathbb{P}(\sigma,g)\coloneqq\{p\text{ prime}:p\text{ divides $d(\sigma)$ or $\nu_{\sigma}^{k}(g)$, for some $k\in[0..n-2]$}\}
;
and
\mathbb{P}(\sigma)=\mathbb{P}(\sigma,d(\sigma)\cdot\nu_{\sigma}(1))
.
Here,
\nu_{\sigma}^{k}
stands for the
k
th iterate of the function
\nu_{\sigma}
.
To check
S=\mathbb{P}(\sigma)
, we first
we use the polynomial time algorithm for primality testing
[
AKS04
]
to verify that all elements of
S
are primes.
Afterwards, we check that these primes are exactly those appearing in the prime factorization of
d(\sigma)
or of numbers of the form
\nu^{k}_{\sigma}(d(\sigma)\cdot\nu_{\sigma}(1))
,
with
k\in[0..n-2]
.
For this second step, the arguments are similar to those in the proof of
Lemma

29
.
Below, we give the pseudocode of a polynomial time procedure preforming this step:

1:

assert

S
contains all prime divisors of
d(\sigma)

2:

compute
\nu_{\sigma}(1)=\phi(\textit{odd}(d(\sigma)))
by relying on the prime divisors of
d(\sigma)

\triangleright

see
Equation

23

3:

m_{0}\leftarrow d(\sigma)\cdot\nu_{\sigma}(1)

\triangleright

m_{i}=\nu_{\sigma}^{i}(d(\sigma)\cdot\nu_{\sigma}(1))

4:

for

k
from
0
to
n-2

do

5:

assert

S
contains all prime divisors of
m_{k}

6:

if

k\neq n-2

then

7:

compute
\nu_{\sigma}(m_{k})=\phi(\textit{odd}(m_{k}\cdot d(\sigma)))
by relying on the prime divisors of
d(\sigma)
and
m_{k}

8:

m_{k+1}\leftarrow\nu_{\sigma}(m_{k})

9:

assert
every prime in
S
divide
\prod_{i=0}^{k-2}m_{i}

10:

return
true

After establishing
S=\mathbb{P}(\sigma)
,
we determine
whether the LESLP
\sigma
is actually an ILESLP.
We recall the snippet of code from
Section

1.2

that solves this problem:

1:

for

i=1
to
n

do

2:

if

\rho_{i}
is of the form
2^{x}

then

assert

\left\llbracket\sigma\right\rrbracket(x)\geq 0

3:

if

\rho_{i}
is of the form
\frac{m}{g}\cdot x

then

assert

\frac{g}{\gcd(m,g)}
divides
\left\llbracket\sigma\right\rrbracket(x)

4:

return
true

By
Lemma

1
, the condition in the
assert
command of line
2
can be checked in polynomial time.
To show that the same is true for the
assert
command of line
3
, first observe that
\frac{g}{\gcd(m,g)}

is a divisor of
d(\sigma)
. Then, the statement follows from
Lemma

29
, as soon as we show the following claim:

Claim 20.

for every
a,b\in\mathbb{N}_{\geq 1}
such that
a
is a divisor of
b
,

\mathbb{P}(\sigma,a)\subseteq\mathbb{P}(\sigma,b)
.

Proof of
20
.

It suffices to show that
\nu_{\sigma}^{k}(a)
divides
\nu_{\sigma}^{k}(b)
. The proof is by induction on
k
.

base case:
k=0
.

Since
\nu_{\sigma}^{0}(x)=x
, the statements follows trivially.

induction hypothesis.

Given
k\geq 1
,
\nu_{\sigma}^{k-1}(a)
divides
\nu_{\sigma}^{k-1}(b)
.

induction step:
k\geq 1
.

By definition of
odd
,

\textit{odd}(a\cdot d(\sigma))
divides
\textit{odd}(b\cdot d(\sigma))
.
Moreover, one of the basic properties of
Euler’s totient function
\phi
is that
\phi(g)
divides
\phi(c)
whenever
g
divides
c
(this follows directly from the definition of
\varphi
). Hence,
\nu_{\sigma}(a)
divides
\nu_{\sigma}(b)
.
By induction hypothesis,
\nu_{\sigma}^{k-1}(\nu_{\sigma}(a))
divides
\nu_{\sigma}^{k-1}(\nu_{\sigma}(b))
;
in other words,
\nu_{\sigma}^{k}(a)
divides
\nu_{\sigma}^{k}(b)
.
∎

This concludes the proof of
Proposition

1
.
∎

Property (
2
).

We start with an auxiliary lemma which we will also use to show Property (
3
).

Lemma 32.

There is a polynomial time procedure with the following specification:

Input:

(\sigma,\mathbb{P}(\sigma))\in U
and a linear-exponential term
\tau
featuring variables
X
from
\sigma
.

Output:

An ILESLP
\xi
.

Under the assumption that
\left\llbracket\sigma\right\rrbracket(x)\geq 0
for all
x\in X
,
the algorithm ensures that
\left\llbracket\xi\right\rrbracket_{\bullet}
is the integer obtained
by evaluating
\tau
by replacing all
x\in X
with
\left\llbracket\sigma\right\rrbracket(x)
.

Proof.

Let
X=\{y_{1},\dots,y_{\ell}\}
, and
\tau
be the term

\sum_{i=1}^{\ell}\big(a_{i}\cdot y_{i}+b_{i}\cdot 2^{y_{i}}+\sum_{j=1}^{\ell}c_{i,j}\cdot(y_{i}\bmod 2^{y_{j}})\big)+d
.
Here is the pseudocode of the algorithm:

for

(i,j)\in[1..\ell]\times[1..\ell]

do

let

\xi_{i,j}
be the ILESLP computed by
Algorithm

9
on input
(\sigma,y_{i},y_{j},\mathbb{P}(\sigma))

Rename variables in
\xi_{i,j}
to be distinct from those in
\sigma
;

let

z_{i,j}
be the variable in the last assignment of
\xi_{i,j}

\triangleright

the one encoding
\left\llbracket\xi_{i,j}\right\rrbracket_{\bullet}

Extend
\sigma
by appending the assignments in
\xi_{i,j}

return
an ILESLP for the expression
\sum_{i=1}^{\ell}\big(a_{i}\cdot y_{i}+b_{i}\cdot 2^{y_{i}}+\sum_{j=1}^{\ell}c_{i,j}\cdot z_{i,j}\big)+d

Each call to
Algorithm

9
runs in polynomial time (
Lemma

31
and
20
), and by
Lemma

30

it produces an ILESLP
\xi_{i,j}
with

\left\llbracket\xi_{i,j}\right\rrbracket_{\bullet}=(\left\llbracket\sigma\right\rrbracket(y_{i})\bmod 2^{\left\llbracket\sigma\right\rrbracket(y_{j})})
.
Upon reaching line
6
,
the (augmented) ILESLP
\sigma
is of polynomial size,
and contains not only the initial assignments,
but also all those added by the
for
loop of line
1

—specifically, assignments to variables
z_{i,j}

satisfying
\left\llbracket\sigma\right\rrbracket(z_{i,j})=(\left\llbracket\sigma\right\rrbracket(y_{i})\bmod 2^{\left\llbracket\sigma\right\rrbracket(y_{j})})
. The expression in line
6

involves
O(\ell^{2})
additions, exponentiations, and multiplications by integer constants. So, line
6
can be implemented in polynomial time by appending,
to
\sigma
, a suitable sequence of assignments corresponding to these operations.
∎

The following proposition (first stated in
Section

1.2
) implies Property (
2
).

See
2

Proof.

Checking condition
Item

(a)
in the definition of
\mathrm{sol}
can clearly be done in polynomial time; let us write
X
for the
variables occurring in
\varphi
or
\tau
. For
Item

(b)
,
given a variable
x\in X
,
we can decide
\left\llbracket\sigma\right\rrbracket(x)\geq 0
in polynomial time
by appealing to
Algorithm

7
(see
Lemma

1
).
For
Item

(c)
, we need to check whether the map
\nu
assigning to each
x\in X
the value
\left\llbracket\sigma\right\rrbracket(x)
satisfies
\varphi
.
Let
\tau\leq 0
be an inequality in
\varphi

(equalities
\tau=0
are treated analogously by viewing them
as conjunctions
{\tau\leq 0\land-\tau\leq 0}
).
By
Lemma

32
, we can construct, in polynomial time,
an ILESLP
\xi
such that
\left\llbracket\xi\right\rrbracket_{\bullet}
is the integer
\tau(\sigma)
obtained
by evaluating
\tau
on
\nu
. Next, we append an assignment
{y\leftarrow-1\cdot x}
to
\xi
, where
x
is the variable in the last assignment of
\xi
(the one encoding
\left\llbracket\xi\right\rrbracket_{\bullet}
), and
y
is a fresh variable. Then,
\tau(\sigma)\leq 0
if and only if
\left\llbracket\xi\right\rrbracket_{\bullet}\geq 0
,
and we can check whether
\left\llbracket\xi\right\rrbracket_{\bullet}\geq 0
in polynomial time
via
Algorithm

7
.∎

Property (
3
).

The map
m
is clearly computable. Consider an instance
(\tau,\varphi)\in I
and
two of its solutions
(\sigma_{1},\mathbb{P}(\sigma_{1})),(\sigma_{2},\mathbb{P}(\sigma_{2}))\in\mathrm{sol}(\tau,\varphi)
. An algorithm to decide
\tau(\sigma_{1})\leq\tau(\sigma_{2})
is the following:

1:

Construct a polynomial-size ILESLP
\xi
such that
\left\llbracket\xi\right\rrbracket_{\bullet}=\tau(\sigma_{2})-\tau(\sigma_{1})

2:

return
true
if

\left\llbracket\xi\right\rrbracket_{\bullet}\geq 0

else
false

The ILESLP
\xi
in line
1
is computed in polynomial time by relying on the algorithm in
Lemma

32
.
The check
\left\llbracket\xi\right\rrbracket_{\bullet}\geq 0
is performed in polynomial time by
appealing to
Algorithm

7
.

Property (
4
).

By
Theorem

1
(proven in
Part

I
),
if an instance
(\tau,\varphi)\in I
has an (optimal) solution, then it has one representable with a polynomial-size ILESLP
\sigma
.
We remark that our proof of this theorem is constructive, meaning that it allows one to explicitly derive a suitable monotonic polynomial
h_{1}
, such that,
for every
(\tau,\varphi)\in I
, the corresponding ILESLP
\sigma

from
Theorem

1
has (when it exists)
size bounded by
h_{1}(\left|(\tau,\varphi)\right|)
.
Moreover, the set of primes
\mathbb{P}(\sigma)
has size polynomial in
\sigma
(see page
29
, and note that the definition of
\mathbb{P}(\sigma)
is constructive).
Let
h_{2}
be a monotonic polynomial bounding the size of
\mathbb{P}(\sigma)
given the size of any ILESLP
\sigma
. Setting
{q(x)\coloneqq h_{1}(x)+h_{2}(h_{1}(x))}

results in the polynomial required by Property (
4
):
given an instance
(\tau,\varphi)\in I
, if an (optimal) solution exists,
then there is
(\sigma,\mathbb{P}(\sigma))\in\mathrm{sol}(\tau,\varphi)
such that

\sigma
has size
s
bounded by
h_{1}(\left|(\tau,\varphi)\right|)
,
and
\mathbb{P}(\sigma)
has size bounded by
h_{2}(s)\leq h_{1}(h_{1}(\left|(\tau,\varphi)\right|))
. Then,
\left|(\sigma,\mathbb{P}(\sigma))\right|\leq q(\left|(\tau,\varphi)\right|)
.

Property (
5
).

Informally, this property asks for an
NP
procedure to check whether the input instance is
unbounded
, that is, it has
an infinite sequence of solutions in which the value of the objective function
strictly increases (assuming
\mathrm{goal}=\max
; the argument we give is analogous for
\mathrm{goal}=\min
). We reason similarly to how this property is proven in ILP.
Let
q
and
\mathrm{short}
be the polynomial and function from Property (
4
).
From the above discussion, we have an explicit definition for
q
,
and this polynomial is monotonic.
Let
(\tau,\varphi)\in I
.
We show that
{\mathrm{sol}(\tau,\varphi)\neq\emptyset\land\mathrm{opt}(\tau,\varphi)=\emptyset}

holds if and only if the following integer linear-exponential program is feasible:

\varphi\,\land\,\tau\geq\lVert{\tau}\rVert_{1}\cdot 2^{z_{s}}\,\land\,z_{1}=2^{s}\,\land\,\bigwedge\nolimits_{i=2}^{s}z_{i}=2^{z_{i}}\,,

(33)

where
s\coloneqq q(\left|(\tau,\varphi)\right|)+3
and
z_{1},\dots,z_{s}
are variables not occurring in
\varphi
or
\tau
. The size of this linear-exponential program is polynomial in
\left|(\tau,\varphi)\right|
, and its feasibility can be decided in
NP
by
Theorem

1
(or, alternatively, the original algorithm from
[
CMS24
]
); Property (
5
) follows.
Below, let
\xi
be the ILESLP
{\xi\coloneqq(z_{-1}\leftarrow 0,\,z_{0}\leftarrow 2^{z_{-1}},\,z_{1}\leftarrow 2^{s}\cdot z_{0},\,z_{2}\leftarrow 2^{z_{1}},\,\dots,\,z_{s}\leftarrow 2^{z_{s}},\,z_{s+1}\leftarrow\lVert{\tau}\rVert_{1}\cdot z_{s})}
,
and observe that in any solution to
Equation

33
,
the value taken by the term
\lVert{\tau}\rVert_{1}\cdot 2^{z_{s}}
is exactly
\left\llbracket\xi\right\rrbracket_{\bullet}
.

For the left-to-right direction of the double implication, supposes
{\mathrm{sol}(\tau,\varphi)\neq\emptyset\land\mathrm{opt}(\tau,\varphi)=\emptyset}
. As stated above, this means that there is an infinite sequence of solutions of
\varphi
in which the value of
\tau
strictly increases.
Therefore, there is a solution for which the value of
\tau

exceeds
\left\llbracket\xi\right\rrbracket_{\bullet}
,
and this implies the feasibility of
Equation

33
.

For the right-to-left direction, we consider the contrapositive.
Assume that either
\mathrm{sol}(\tau,\varphi)=\emptyset
or
\mathrm{opt}(\tau,\varphi)\neq\emptyset
. If
\mathrm{sol}(\tau,\varphi)=\emptyset
then
\varphi
is infeasible and therefore so is
Equation

33
.
If instead
\mathrm{opt}(\tau,\varphi)\neq\emptyset
, then by Property (
4
)
we have
\mathrm{opt}(\tau,\varphi)\cap\mathrm{short}(\tau,\varphi)\neq\emptyset
.
To show that
Equation

33
is infeasible,
it suffices to show that
for every
(\sigma,\mathbb{P}(\sigma))\in\mathrm{short}(\tau,\varphi)
,
the integer
\tau(\sigma)
obtained by evaluating
\tau
on
\sigma

is strictly smaller than
\left\llbracket\xi\right\rrbracket_{\bullet}
.
Let
\bm{x}\coloneqq(x_{1},\dots,x_{n})
be the variables occurring in
\tau
.
By definition,
\tau(\bm{x})\leq\lVert{\tau}\rVert_{1}\cdot 2^{\max(x_{1},\dots,x_{n})}
for all values given to
\bm{x}
among the natural numbers.
Consider then
(\sigma,\mathbb{P}(\sigma))\in\mathrm{short}(\tau,\varphi)
,
with
\sigma=(y_{0}\leftarrow\rho_{0},\dots,y_{m}\leftarrow\rho_{m})
.
By definition of
\mathrm{short}
, we have
m\leq q(\left|(\tau,\varphi)\right|)
.
We show that, for every
i\in[1..m]
,
\left|\left\llbracket\sigma\right\rrbracket(y_{i})\right|\leq\left\llbracket\xi\right\rrbracket(z_{i})
.
Together with the fact that
\left\llbracket\xi\right\rrbracket(z_{j-1})<\left\llbracket\xi\right\rrbracket(z_{j})
for every
j\in[1..s]
, this implies
\tau(\sigma)\leq\lVert{\tau}\rVert_{1}\cdot 2^{\left\llbracket\xi\right\rrbracket_{\bullet}}
, concluding the proof.

base case:
i=1
.

The expression
\rho_{1}
has one of the following forms:
0
,
a\cdot y_{0}
(for some
a\in\mathbb{Q}
),
y_{0}+y_{0}
, or
2^{y_{0}}
.
Since
\left\llbracket\sigma\right\rrbracket(y_{0})=0
, we have
\left\llbracket\sigma\right\rrbracket(y_{1})\in\{0,1\}
.
On the other hand,
\left\llbracket\xi\right\rrbracket(z_{1})=2^{s}>1
.

induction hypothesis.

Given
i\geq 2
, we have
\left|\left\llbracket\sigma\right\rrbracket(y_{j})\right|\leq\left\llbracket\xi\right\rrbracket(z_{j})
for every
j\in[1..i-1]
.

induction step:
i\geq 2
.

The expression
\rho_{i}
has one of the following forms:
0
,
a\cdot y_{j}
,
y_{j}+y_{k}
or
2^{y_{j}}
, where
j,k\in[1..i-1]
. Let
\ell\coloneqq\max\{\left|\left\llbracket\sigma\right\rrbracket(y_{j})\right|:j\in[1..i-1]\}
, and
k\in[1..i-1]
be such that
\left|\left\llbracket\sigma(y_{k})\right\rrbracket\right|=\ell
. Then,
\left\llbracket\sigma\right\rrbracket(y_{i})\leq\max(\left|a\right|\cdot\ell,2^{\ell})
.
We show that
\max(\left|a\right|\cdot\ell,2^{\ell})\leq\left\llbracket\xi\right\rrbracket(z_{i})
:

•

2^{\ell}\leq\left\llbracket\xi\right\rrbracket(z_{i})
:

By induction hypothesis,
{\ell=\left|\left\llbracket\xi\right\rrbracket(y_{k})\right|\leq\left\llbracket\xi\right\rrbracket(z_{k})}
. By definition of
\xi
,
\left\llbracket\xi\right\rrbracket(z_{j-1})<\left\llbracket\xi\right\rrbracket(z_{j})
for every
j\in[1..s]
, and therefore

\left\llbracket\xi\right\rrbracket(z_{k})\leq\left\llbracket\xi\right\rrbracket(z_{i-1})
.
Moreover, by definition
\left\llbracket\xi\right\rrbracket(z_{i})=2^{\left\llbracket\xi\right\rrbracket(z_{i-1})}
.
Therefore,
2^{\ell}\leq 2^{\left\llbracket\xi\right\rrbracket(z_{k})}\leq 2^{\left\llbracket\xi\right\rrbracket(z_{i-1})}=\left\llbracket\xi\right\rrbracket(z_{i})
.

•

\left|a\right|\cdot\ell\leq\left\llbracket\xi\right\rrbracket(z_{i})
:

Since
0\leq\ell\leq\left\llbracket\xi\right\rrbracket(z_{i-1})
(from the previous point in the proof), it suffices to show
\left|a\right|\cdot\left\llbracket\xi\right\rrbracket(z_{i-1})\leq 2^{\left\llbracket\xi\right\rrbracket(z_{i-1})}
. This inequality is trivial for
a=0
.
Else, by
Lemma

10
,
we see that the inequality is true as soon as
\left\llbracket\xi\right\rrbracket(z_{i-1})\geq 4\cdot\log_{2}(\left|a\right|)+8
.
Observe that the bit size of
a
is bounded by the bit size of
\sigma
,
and therefore
\log_{2}(\left|a\right|)\leq q(\left|(\tau,\varphi)\right|)
.
By definition of
\xi
, we also have
\left\llbracket\xi\right\rrbracket(z_{i-1})\geq\left\llbracket\xi\right\rrbracket(z_{1})=2^{q(\left|(\tau,\varphi)\right|)+3}
. Then,

4\cdot\log_{2}(\left|a\right|)+8\leq 4\cdot q(\left|(\tau,\varphi)\right|)+8\leq 2^{q(\left|(\tau,\varphi)\right|)+3}\leq\left\llbracket\xi\right\rrbracket(z_{i-1}).

This completes the proof of
Corollary

1
.

Part IV
Appendices

Appendix A
The Sequential Squaring Assumption and ILESLPs

This appendix contains a detour on the
time-lock puzzle
introduced in
[
RSW96
]
,
which we use to establish
a lower bound for the problem
\textup{{Div}}_{\scalebox{0.7}{{{ILESLP}}}}
from
Section

1.2

(or, equivalently, the problem of deciding if an LESLP is an ILESLP) in terms of a well-established cryptographic assumption.

Basic number theory concepts for cryptography.

A prime
p
is said to be
safe
whenever
\frac{p-1}{2}
is also prime.
A number
b
is a
quadratic residue
modulo
N
whenever it is congruent to
r^{2}
modulo
N
, for some
r\in[0..N-1]
; if
b
is also in
[0..N-1]
, then it is a quadratic residue
of

N
.
Given two distinct safe primes
p
and
q
,
the set of all quadratic residues modulo
N\coloneqq p\cdot q
forms a multiplicative cyclic subgroup of order
\frac{(p-1)\cdot(q-1)}{4}
; the key point being that it is then possible
to generate all quadratic residues of
N
starting from any of them, but
it is impossible to do so in time polynomial in the bit sizes of
p
and
q
.
A function
f\colon\mathbb{N}\to(0,1)
is said to be
negligible
if for every
c\in\mathbb{N}
there is an integer
M_{c}
such that

f(n)<\frac{1}{n^{c}}
for every
n>M_{c}
.

The time-lock puzzle.

We give a brief description of the time-lock puzzle from
[
RSW96
]
,
referring the reader to that paper for a full account on the problem and its applications.
The objective is to encrypt a message
M
in a way that gives not only strong guarantees
on the minimum amount of time any adversary must spend to decrypt it,
but also some (mild) guarantees on the maximum time a strong adversary would take.
As usual in the computational model of cryptography, adversaries are modelled as probabilistic polynomial-time Turing machines, and we moreover assume to know a reasonably tight upper bound
S
on the number of squaring per second that these adversaries can perform, modulo any number.
At our disposal, we also have a pair of symmetric-key cryptographic algorithms (
Encrypt
,
Decrypt
);
these algorithms are known to the adversary.
Besides minor changes that we will discuss later,
[
RSW96
]
proposes the following protocol for encrypting
M
:

1:

p,q

\leftarrow
two distinct safe primes such that
\frac{p-1}{2}
and
\frac{q-1}{2}
are both congruent to
3
modulo
8

2:

T\leftarrow S\cdot t

\triangleright

t
: number of seconds the puzzle must last. Given in input with
M
and
S

3:

generate a secret key
K\in[0..N-1]
for the pair of algorithms (
Encrypt
,
Decrypt
)

4:

C\leftarrow\textsc{Encrypt}(K,M)

5:

E\leftarrow 2^{T}\bmod\phi(N)

\triangleright

use exponentiation-by-squaring method
[
BW08
, Ch. 1.4]
…

6:

D\leftarrow(K+2^{E})\mod N

\triangleright

…twice

7:

return

(N,D,C,T)

To solve the time-lock puzzle, an adversary must retrieve the message
M
.
Except for trying to compute
K
from
C
—which is infeasible, since secure symmetric-key cryptographic algorithms exists (the simplest of all being one-time pad)—
the only way for the adversary to retrieve
M
is to extract
K
from
D
, and then run
\textsc{Decrypt}(K,C)
.
That can be done by computing
y\coloneqq 2^{2^{T}}\bmod N
via repeated squaring, to then subtract it from
D
(modulo
N
). The key cryptographic assumption implying the security of the time-lock puzzle thus focuses on the computation of
y
:

Conjecture 1
(Sequential Squaring Assumption)
.

There is a polynomial

P\colon\mathbb{N}\to\mathbb{N}

such that for every probabilistic polynomial-time adversary

\mathcal{A}
,
there exists a negligible function

{\text{negl}\colon\mathbb{N}\to(0,1)}
such that for all
\lambda\in\mathbb{N}
(in unary):

\left|\,\text{Pr}\hskip-2.5pt\left[\,b=b^{\prime}:\begin{array}[]{rl}(p,q,N)&\leftarrow\textsc{GenMod}(1^{\lambda})\\
T&\xleftarrow{\$}P(2^{\lambda})\\
b&\xleftarrow{\$}\{0,1\}\\
\text{if }b=0&\text{then }y\coloneqq 2^{2^{T}}\bmod N\\
\text{if }b=1&\text{then }y\xleftarrow{\$}\mathbb{QR}_{N}\\
b^{\prime}&\leftarrow\mathcal{A}(1^{\lambda},N,T,y)\end{array}\right]-\frac{1}{2}\,\right|\leq\text{negl}(\lambda).

In
1
,
\lambda
is a security parameter that governs the bit sizes of the primes
p
and
q
, the “time limit”
T
of the time-puzzle, and the runtime of the adversary
\mathcal{A}
.
The function
GenMod
is a probabilistic polynomial-time algorithm that returns a triple
(p,q,N)

where
p
and
q
are distinct safe primes such that
\frac{p-1}{2}
and
\frac{q-1}{2}
are congruent to
3
modulo
8
(i.e., those computed in line
1
of the protocol), and
N\coloneqq p\cdot q
.
The arrow
\xleftarrow{\$}
stands for uniform sampling.
In a nutshell,
1
states that adversaries can only distinguish between a
y
computed as
(2^{2^{T}}\bmod N)
and one randomly sampled among the quadratic residues of
N
(denoted
\mathbb{QR}_{N}
above) with negligible probability.

Proposition 5.

1
implies that
\textup{{Div}}_{\scalebox{0.7}{{{ILESLP}}}}
is not in
BPP
.

Proof.

Suppose that
\textup{{Div}}_{\scalebox{0.7}{{{ILESLP}}}}
is in BPP. A simple adversary
\mathcal{A}
breaking the binary sequential squaring assumption is defined as follows. Given the input
(1^{\lambda},N,T,y)
,
\mathcal{A}
constructs the ILESLP
\sigma
:

x_{0}\leftarrow 0,\ \ x_{1}\leftarrow 2^{x_{0}},\ \ x_{2}\leftarrow T\cdot x_{1},\ \ x_{3}\leftarrow 2^{x_{2}},\ \ x_{4}\leftarrow 2^{x_{3}},\ \ x_{5}\leftarrow-y\cdot x_{1},\ \ x_{6}\leftarrow x_{4}+x_{5},

which evaluates to
\left\llbracket\sigma\right\rrbracket_{\bullet}=2^{2^{T}}-y
.
The adversary then invokes the BPP algorithm for
\textup{{Div}}_{\scalebox{0.7}{{{ILESLP}}}}
with inputs
\sigma
and
N
.
If the algorithm returns true,
\mathcal{A}
outputs
0
; otherwise, it outputs
1
.
∎

On the security of the time-lock puzzle.

As mentioned above, the protocol (and thus the cryptographic assumption)
considered in
[
RSW96
]
differ very slightly from the one reported here.
In particular, the protocol in
[
RSW96
]
allows in line
5
to use exponentiation
x^{E}
instead of
2^{E}
, where
x\in[2..N-1]
is randomly chosen.
Correspondingly, the cryptographic assumption in
1
would sample uniformly at random
x
in
[2..N-1]
to then define
y\coloneqq x^{2^{T}}\bmod N
in the case of
b=0
.
The fact that the protocol is believed to be secure then
stems from the fact that
factoring
is not believed to be in
BPP
and that, with high probability,
the period of the sequence
x_{0},x_{1},x_{2},\dots
, where
x_{i}\coloneqq x^{2^{i}}\bmod N
, is large
comparatively to
N
.
(Note that we can assume
\textsc{GenMod}(1^{\lambda})
to return an
N
in
\Omega(2^{\lambda})
, hence the period of the sequence is also large comparatively to
T
.)
As remarked in
[
RSW96
]
, we can fix
x=2
as long as we guarantee this period to still be large.
This is ensured by the constraints on the primes
p
and
q
imposed in line
1
of the protocol (which are absent in
[
RSW96
]
), as we explain below.

Denote by
\lambda\colon\mathbb{N}_{\geq 1}\to\mathbb{N}_{\geq 1}
the Carmichael function.
Given a positive integer
n
, this function returns the smallest positive integer
m

such that
a^{m}\equiv 1\pmod{n}
holds for every
a
coprime with
n
.

Theorem 2
[
BBS86
]
.

Let
N\coloneqq p\cdot q
, with
p
and
q
distinct safe primes such that
\frac{p-1}{2}
and
\frac{q-1}{2}
are congruent to
3
modulo
8
. The period of the sequence

x_{0},x_{1},\dots
, where
x_{i}=2^{2^{i}}\bmod N
, is
\lambda(\lambda(N))
.

Proof.

Denote by
\pi
the period of the sequence
x_{0},x_{1},\dots
,
and by
\text{ord}_{M}(x)
the (multiplicative) order of
x
modulo
M
(assuming
x
and
M
coprime).
From the definition of the Charmichael function one can show that
\frac{\lambda(N)}{2}=\frac{p-1}{2}\cdot\frac{q-1}{2}
.
In particular, since both
\frac{p-1}{2}
and
\frac{q-1}{2}
are odd,
\text{ord}_{\lambda(N)/2}(2)
is well-defined.
In
[
BBS86
]
the following results are established:

1.

Under the sole assumptions that
p
and
q
are distinct primes that are congruent to
3
modulo
4
,
and that
2
is a quadratic residue modulo
N
,
we have
\pi\mathrel{|}\lambda(\lambda(N))
. (See
[
BBS86
, Theorem 6]
.)

2.

Under the same assumptions as Item
1
, and further assuming
\text{ord}_{\lambda(N)/2}(2)=\lambda(\lambda(N))
and
\text{ord}_{N}(2)=\frac{\lambda(N)}{2}
, it holds that
\lambda(\lambda(N))\mathrel{|}\pi
. (See
[
BBS86
, Theorem 7]
.)

3.

Under the sole assumption that
p
and
q
are distinct safe primes that are congruent to
3
modulo
4
,
and that
2
is a quadratic residue with respect to
at most
one among
\frac{p-1}{2}
and
\frac{q-1}{2}
,
then
\text{ord}_{\lambda(N)/2}(2)=\lambda(\lambda(N))
. (See
[
BBS86
, Theorem 8]
.)

The theorem then follows as soon as we establish that all the above assumptions are covered
by “
p
and
q
are distinct safe primes such that
\frac{p-1}{2}
and
\frac{q-1}{2}
are congruent to
3
modulo
8
”:

Assumption:
“
p
and
q
are distinct are congruent to
3
modulo
4
”
.

This assumption is equivalent to asking
\frac{p-1}{2}
and
\frac{q-1}{2}
to be odd, which they are, since they are congruent to
3
modulo
8
.

Assumption:
“
2
is a quadratic residue modulo
N
”
.

First,
remark that
2
is a quadratic residue modulo
p\cdot q

if and only if
2
is a quadratic residue modulo
p
and modulo
q
.
The left-to-right direction of this double implication is trivial.
For the right-to-left direction, suppose
r^{2}\equiv 2\pmod{p}
and
s^{2}\equiv 2\pmod{q}
.
By the Chinese remainder theorem, there is
z\in[0..p\cdot q-1]
such that
z\equiv r\pmod{p}
and
z\equiv s\pmod{q}
. Then,
z^{2}-2
is divisible by both
p
and
q
,
and so, by coprimality of
p
and
q
, it is also divisible by
p\cdot q
;
i.e.,
2
is a quadratic residue of
p\cdot q
.

Let us then show that
2
is a quadratic residue modulo
p
(same arguments for
q
).
The second supplement to the law of quadratic reciprocity (see the entry for
“quadratic reciprocity, law of”
in
[
Nel08
]
)
states that

2
is a quadratic residue modulo
p
if and only if
{p\equiv\pm 1\pmod{8}}
.
Note that if
p\equiv 1\pmod{8}
, then
\frac{p-1}{2}\equiv 0\pmod{4}

and therefore
p
cannot be a safe prime.
The congruence
{p\equiv-1\pmod{8}}
is instead equivalent to
{\frac{p-1}{2}\equiv 3\pmod{4}}

(and there are safe primes satisfying these pairs of constraints, take e.g.
p=7
).
Since we are assuming
{\frac{p-1}{2}\equiv 3\pmod{8}}
, we also have
{\frac{p-1}{2}\equiv 3\pmod{4}}
; as required.

Observe that we have now shown that the assumptions of Item
1
apply.

Assumption:
\text{\rm ord}_{N}(2)=\frac{\lambda(N)}{2}
.

Since
p
and
q
are safe primes, the set of quadratic residues modulo
N
forms a multiplicative cyclic group of order
\frac{(p-1)\cdot(q-1)}{4}=\frac{\lambda(N)}{2}
; meaning in particular that all quadratic residues have the same order (i.e.,
\frac{\lambda(N)}{2}
).
From the previous point,
2
is a quadratic residue modulo
N
.

Assumption:
“
2
is a quadratic residue with respect to at most one among
\frac{p-1}{2}
and
\frac{q-1}{2}
”
.

Again

from the second supplement to the law of quadratic reciprocity,

2
is a quadratic residue modulo
\frac{p-1}{2}
if and only if
\frac{p-1}{2}\equiv\pm 1\pmod{8}
.
We are however imposing
\frac{p-1}{2}\equiv 3\pmod{8}
,
so
2
is not a quadratic residue modulo
\frac{p-1}{2}
(nor modulo
\frac{q-1}{2}
).

This is the last assumption we needed to show: it completes the assumptions in Item
3
and, following the conclusion of that item, also the assumptions in Item
2
.
∎

One last point:
1
requires
GenMod
to run in probabilistic polynomial-time, and so we also need to check that the set of numbers
p
that are safe primes satisfying
{\frac{p-1}{2}\equiv 3\pmod{8}}

is not only infinite, but also not too sparse. However, already whether there are infinitely many safe primes is not known.
The usual (well-corroborated) assumption in cryptography is that among the first
n
integers,
\Omega\big(\frac{n}{\log(n)^{2}}\big)
are safe primes. Following Dirichlet’s theorem, it is natural to expect such a bound to hold also for the safe primes
p
satisfying
\frac{p-1}{2}\equiv 3\pmod{8}
:

Conjecture 2.

Among the first
n\in\mathbb{N}_{\geq 1}
positive integers,

\Omega\big(\frac{n}{\log(n)^{2}}\big)
are safe primes numbers
p
that satisfy
\frac{p-1}{2}\equiv 3\pmod{8}
.

The bound in
2
is also implied by the well-known Bateman-Horn conjecture.
Taken together,
Theorem

2
,
2
, and the assumption that
factoring
is not in
BPP
provide a rationale for believing that
1
holds.

Proof that the Bateman-Horn conjecture implies
2
.

Define the maps
f_{1}(x)\coloneqq 8\cdot x+3
and
{f_{2}(x)\coloneqq 2\cdot f_{1}(x)+1}
.
Note that the subset of
[1..n]
we are interested in is

S(n)\coloneqq{\{f_{2}(i):i\in\mathbb{N}\text{ and both }f_{1}(i)\text{ and }f_{2}(i)\text{ are prime}\}}\cap[1..n].

We first assume Dickson’s conjecture and show that it implies that
S(n)
is infinite.
We will later appeal to Bateman–Horn conjecture (which implies Dickson’s conjecture) to obtain an estimation on
\#{S(n)}
.
Recall that Dickson’s conjecture
states that, for any given finite family
f_{1},\dots,f_{k}

of univariate functions
f_{i}(x)\coloneqq a_{i}\cdot x+b_{i}
, where
a_{i}\in\mathbb{N}_{\geq 1}
and
b_{i}\in\mathbb{Z}
:

1.

there are infinitely many
n\in\mathbb{N}
such that
f_{1}(n),\dots,f_{k}(n)
are all primes, or

2.

there is a single integer
\alpha\geq 2
dividing, for every
m\in\mathbb{N}
, the product
\prod_{i=1}^{k}f_{i}(m)
.

We simply have to exclude the second of the two cases above, showing that there are two integers
m_{1},m_{2}\in\mathbb{N}
such that
f_{1}(m_{1})\cdot f_{2}(m_{1})
and
f_{1}(m_{2})\cdot f_{2}(m_{2})
are coprime.
This holds already for
m_{1}=1
and
m_{2}=2
:

f_{1}(1)\cdot f_{2}(1)=11\cdot 23

and

f_{1}(2)\cdot f_{2}(2)=3\cdot 13\cdot 19
.

Moving to the density estimation, since the first of the two cases in Dickson’s conjecture applies,
the Bateman–Horn conjecture implies that there is a real number
C\in\mathbb{R}

dependent on
f_{1}
and
f_{2}
(and independent on
n
)
such that
\#S(n)\geq C\cdot\int_{2}^{n}\frac{dt}{(\log t)^{2}}
;
i.e.,
\#S(n)
is in
\Omega(\frac{n}{(\log n)^{2}})

(in fact, the Bateman–Horn conjecture gives
\Theta(\frac{n}{(\log n)^{2}})
, but we only need a lower bound).
∎

Appendix B
The algorithm for deciding ILEP:

Further information on Steps I and III

In this appendix, we provide a further information on the Steps I and III
of the algorithm in
[
CMS24
]
.
In particular, we import the pseudocode of these steps,
as well as their complexity analysis.
When appealing to formal statements from
[
CMS24
]
, we refer to the full version of the paper (as indicated in the corresponding bibliography entry).

Some additional notation.

Throughout this appendix, we sometimes write a divisibility constraint
d\mathrel{|}\tau
as
\tau\equiv_{d}0
.
We need a few definitions from
[
CMS24
]
.
Below, let
\theta
be the ordering of exponentiated variables
\theta(\bm{x})\coloneqq{2^{x_{n}}\geq 2^{x_{n-1}}\geq\dots\geq 2^{x_{0}}=1}
, for some
{n\geq 1}
.

Definition 4

(Quotient System)
.

A
quotient system induced by

\theta

is a system
\varphi(\bm{x},\bm{q},\bm{r})

of equalities, inequalities, and divisibility constraints
\tau\sim 0
, where
{\sim}\in\{{<},{\leq},=,\equiv_{d}:d\geq 1\}
and

\tau
is an
quotient term (induced by
\theta
)
, that is,
a term of the form

a\cdot 2^{x_{n}}+f(\bm{q})\cdot 2^{x_{n-1}}+b\cdot x_{n-1}+\tau^{\prime}(x_{0},\dots,x_{n-2},\bm{r})\,,

where
a,b\in\mathbb{Z}
,
f(\bm{q})
is a linear term on
quotient variables

\bm{q}
, and

\tau^{\prime}
is a linear-exponential term in which the
remainder
variables
\bm{r}
do not occur exponentiated.
Furthermore, for every remainder variable
r
, the
quotient system
\varphi
features the inequalities
0\leq r<2^{x_{n-1}}
.

A disclaimer:
Observe that quotient systems can be syntactically equal to linear-exponential programs. In particular, this happens when every linear term
f(\bm{q})
appearing in quotient terms is an integer. However,
[
CMS24
]
keeps the two types of objects somewhat separated, as if they are distinct “types” (in the sense of programming languages). That is, quotient systems are not linear-exponential programs. In the algorithm from
[
CMS24
]
, quotient systems only appear at the beginning of Step I of the algorithm. To be more precise, let us look at the pseudocode of Step I (
Algorithm

10
). In input, this step takes an ordering
\theta
, and a linear-exponential program with divisions
\varphi
.
The
foreach
loop of line
5
translates
\varphi
into a quotient system (adding new quotient and remainder variables). When the procedure reaches line
12
, the translation is complete.
All other systems
constructed by the algorithm (in Step I,
\gamma
and
\psi
) are linear-exponential programs.

The above distinction between linear-exponential programs and quotient systems is important for the definition of

least significant part
of a term (introduced in
Section

6
for linear-exponential programs, but restated below to avoid confusion). In this definition, quotient terms and linear-exponential terms are treated differently, and the definition becomes ill-formed if quotient systems are mistakenly regarded as linear-exponential programs.

Definition 5

(Least Significant Part)
.

The
least significant part
of a term
\tau
, with respect to the ordering
\theta
, is defined as follows:

1.

If
\tau
is a
linear-exponential term

a\cdot 2^{x_{n}}+b\cdot x_{n}+\tau^{\prime}
, with
\tau^{\prime}
linear-exponential term only featuring
x_{n}
in remainders
(x\bmod 2^{y})
, its least
significant part
is the term
b\cdot x_{n}+\tau^{\prime}
.

2.

If
\tau
is a
quotient term

a\cdot 2^{x_{n}}+f(\bm{q})\cdot 2^{x_{n-1}}+b\cdot x_{n-1}+\tau^{\prime}
, with
\tau^{\prime}
not featuring
x_{n}
nor
x_{n-1}
, its least significant part is the
term
b\cdot x_{n-1}+\tau^{\prime}
.

Moreover, let
\varphi
be either a linear-exponential program with divisions or a quotient system.
We denote by
\textit{lst}(\varphi,\theta)
the following set of least significant terms:

\textit{lst}(\varphi,\theta)=\left\{\pm\rho:\begin{array}[]{l}\rho\text{ is the least significant part of a term }\tau\text{ appearing in an (in)equality }\tau\sim 0\\
\text{of }\varphi,\text{ with respect to }\theta\end{array}\right\}.

An analogous distinction arises in the definition of
linear norm
:

Definition 6

(Linear norm)
.

The
linear norm

\lVert{\tau}\rVert_{\!\mathfrak{L}}
of a term
\tau
is defined as follows:

1.

If
\tau
is a
linear-exponential term

\sum_{i=1}^{n}\big(a_{i}\cdot x_{i}+b_{i}\cdot 2^{x_{i}}+\sum\nolimits_{j=1}^{n}c_{i,j}\cdot(x_{i}\bmod 2^{x_{j}})\big)+d
,
then
\lVert{\tau}\rVert_{\!\mathfrak{L}}\coloneqq\max\{\left|a_{i}\right|,\left|c_{i,j}\right|:i,j\in[1..n]\}
,
i.e., it reflects the maximum absolute value among the coefficients of the linear terms
x_{i}
and the remainder terms
(x_{i}\bmod 2^{x_{j}})
.

2.

If
\tau
is a quotient term induced by
\theta
, of the form
\tau=a\cdot 2^{x_{n}}+f(\bm{q})\cdot 2^{x_{n-1}}+b\cdot x_{n-1}+\tau^{\prime}
,
then
\lVert{\tau}\rVert_{\!\mathfrak{L}}\coloneqq\max(\left|b\right|,\lVert{\tau^{\prime}}\rVert_{\!\mathfrak{L}},\lVert{f}\rVert_{\!\mathfrak{L}})
.
Note that
\lVert{\tau}\rVert_{\!\mathfrak{L}}
accounts for the coefficients of the variables
\bm{q}
.

Moreover, let
\varphi
be either a linear-exponential program with divisions or a quotient system. The linear norm of
\varphi
is defined as

\lVert{\varphi}\rVert_{\!\mathfrak{L}}\coloneqq\max\{\lVert{\tau}\rVert_{\!\mathfrak{L}}:\tau\text{ is a term appearing in an (in)equality of $\varphi$}\}
.

The parameters
\#{\varphi}
,
\lVert{\varphi}\rVert_{1}
and
\textit{mod}(\varphi)
extend instead trivially to quotient systems
\varphi
:

•

\#{\varphi}
for the number of constraints (inequalities, equalities and divisibility constraints) in
\varphi
;

•

\textup{terms}(\varphi)
for the set of all terms
\tau
occurring in inequalities
\tau\leq 0
or equalities
\tau=0
of
\varphi
;

•

\lVert{\varphi}\rVert_{1}\coloneqq\max\{\lVert{\tau}\rVert_{1}:\tau\in\textup{terms}(\varphi)\}
.

For a quotient term
\tau=a\cdot 2^{x_{n}}+f(\bm{q})\cdot 2^{x_{n-1}}+b\cdot x_{n-1}+\tau^{\prime}
,
we have
\lVert{\tau}\rVert_{1}\coloneqq\left|a\right|+\left|b\right|+\lVert{f}\rVert_{1}+\lVert{\tau^{\prime}}\rVert_{1}
.

•

\textit{mod}(\varphi)
is the least common multiple
of the divisors
d
of the divisibility constraints
d\mathrel{|}\tau
of
\varphi
.

We recall that, in these constraints, all integers appearing in
\tau
belong to
[0..d-1]
.

presents the pseudocode of Step I.
This pseudocode is obtained by merging (exclusively to simplify the presentation)
lines 4–14 of Algorithm 2 with lines 1–21 of Algorithm 3 from
[

]
.
The full specification of
Algorithm

is recalled below.

Proof.

The correctness of
Algorithm

10
follows from
[
