, Proposition 4 and Lemma 23]
:

•

Lines
4
–
11
.

These lines are analyzed in the proof of
[
CMS24
, Proposition 4]
(Appendix C.3, page 54). The
foreach
loop of line
5
(deterministically) manipulates
\varphi

into a quotient system
\varphi^{\prime}
. Let
\bm{q}
and
\bm{r}^{\prime}
be the set of all fresh quotient and reminder variables introduced in line
6
(across all iterations of the loop). This part of the algorithm ensure that

\left[\begin{matrix}x\\
\bm{r}\end{matrix}\right]=\left[\begin{matrix}q_{x}\\
\bm{q}\end{matrix}\right]\cdot 2^{y}+\left[\begin{matrix}r_{x}\\
\bm{r}^{\prime}\end{matrix}\right]\text{ \ implies \ }(\varphi\land\theta)\iff(\varphi^{\prime}\land\theta)

(34)

In
[
CMS24
]
, lines 4–14 of Algorithm 2
construct
\varphi^{\prime}
to then pass it to Algorithm 3, which performs (with respect to our pseudocode) the following lines
12
–
34
.

•

Lines
12
–
34
.

These lines are analyzed in the proof of
[
CMS24
, Lemma 23]
(Appendix C.2, page 40; see in particular the subsection titled “Correctness of Step (i)”).
In a nutshell, these lines “divide” each quotient term
in
\varphi^{\prime}
by
2^{y}
, by relying on the equivalences in
Lemma

3
. For example, an equality

{a\cdot 2^{x}+f(\bm{q})\cdot 2^{y}+b\cdot y+\tau^{\prime}=0}

is (non-deterministically) rewritten as

{a\cdot 2^{x-y}+f(\bm{q})+r=0}\land{b\cdot y+\tau^{\prime}=r\cdot 2^{y}}
.
Note that
b\cdot y+\tau^{\prime}
is the least significant part of the term in the initial equality.
Constraints concerning these least significant parts (in our example,
b\cdot y+\tau^{\prime}=r\cdot 2^{y}
) are added to the formula
\psi
(lines
21
,
26
,
30
and
34
), whereas the remaining constraints
featuring the variable
x
(in our example,
{a\cdot 2^{x-y}+f(\bm{q})+r=0}
) are added to the formula
\gamma

(lines
29
and
33
; note that the algorithm uses
u
as a proxy for
2^{x-y}
).

Since these lines of the algorithm are guided by the equivalences in
Lemma

3
, one obtains

\left[\begin{matrix}x\\
\bm{r}\end{matrix}\right]=\left[\begin{matrix}q_{x}\\
\bm{q}\end{matrix}\right]\cdot 2^{y}+\left[\begin{matrix}r_{x}\\
\bm{r}^{\prime}\end{matrix}\right]\text{ \ implies \ }(\varphi^{\prime}\land\theta)\iff\bigvee_{\beta}\exists u\,\big(\gamma_{\beta}\land\psi_{\beta}\land(u=2^{x-y})\land\theta\big),

(35)

where
\bigvee_{\beta}
ranges over all non-deterministic branches
\beta
.

The lemma follows by
Equations

34
and
35
.
Consider a solution to
\varphi\land\theta
.
We can uniquely decompose the values
x
,
y
and
\bm{r}
take in this solution by following the system
\left[\begin{matrix}x\\
\bm{r}\end{matrix}\right]=\left[\begin{matrix}q_{x}\\
\bm{q}\end{matrix}\right]\cdot 2^{y}+\left[\begin{matrix}r_{x}\\
\bm{r}^{\prime}\end{matrix}\right]
and the equation
u=2^{x-y}
,
producing a solution to
\bigvee_{\beta}\big(\gamma_{\beta}\land\psi_{\beta}\land(u=2^{x-y})\land(x=q_{x}\cdot 2^{y}+r_{x})\land\theta\big)

thanks to
Equations

34
and
35
.
Conversely, from a solution to
\bigvee_{\beta}\big(\gamma_{\beta}\land\psi_{\beta}\land{(u=2^{x-y})}\land{(x=q_{x}\cdot 2^{y}+r_{x})}\land\theta\big)
,
we can compute (unique) values for the variables
\bm{r}
following the system

\bm{r}=\bm{q}\cdot 2^{y}+\bm{r}^{\prime}
, producing
a solution to
\theta\land\varphi
.
∎

These lines are analyzed in

, Lemma 37]
(Appendix D.2, page 59; see in particular the subsection titled “Step (a)”).
Let
(\gamma,\psi)
be an output of
Algorithm

.
The following bounds are established:

\hskip-28.45274pt\text{if \ }\begin{cases}\#\textit{lst}(\varphi^{\prime},\theta)&\hskip-5.0pt\leq\ell\\
\#\varphi^{\prime}&\hskip-5.0pt\leq s\\
\lVert{\varphi^{\prime}}\rVert_{\!\mathfrak{L}}&\hskip-5.0pt\leq a\\
\lVert{\varphi^{\prime}}\rVert_{1}&\hskip-5.0pt\leq c\\
\textit{mod}(\varphi^{\prime})&\hskip-5.0pt\hskip 3.0pt\mathrel{|}\hskip 2.0ptd\end{cases}\text{ \ then \ }\begin{cases}\#\textit{lst}(\psi,\theta^{\prime})&\hskip-5.0pt\leq\ell\\
\#\psi&\hskip-5.0pt\leq s+2\cdot\ell\\
\lVert{\psi}\rVert_{\!\mathfrak{L}}&\hskip-5.0pt\leq a\\
\lVert{\psi}\rVert_{1}&\hskip-5.0pt\leq 2\cdot c+1\\
\textit{mod}(\psi)&\hskip-5.0pt\hskip 3.0pt\mathrel{|}\hskip 2.0ptd\\
\end{cases}\text{ \ and \ }\begin{cases}\#\gamma&\hskip-5.0pt\leq s\\
\lVert{\gamma[2^{u}\,/\,u]}\rVert_{\!\mathfrak{L}}&\hskip-5.0pt\leq a\\
\lVert{\gamma}\rVert_{1}&\hskip-5.0pt\leq c+1\\
\textit{mod}(\gamma)&\hskip-5.0pt\hskip 3.0pt\mathrel{|}\hskip 2.0ptd\end{cases}

(37)

Note that above (and in the statement of the lemma)
\lVert{\gamma[2^{u}\,/\,u]}\rVert_{\!\mathfrak{L}}

is considered instead of
\lVert{\gamma}\rVert_{\!\mathfrak{L}}
. As reported in
[
CMS24
, Lemma 37]
,
this is because only the coefficients of the variables distinct from
u
are interesting
for the overall analysis of the complexity of the algorithm (in any case, we have a bound of
c+1
on this coefficient, given by
\lVert{\gamma}\rVert_{1}
).
Performing the substitution
[2^{u}\,/\,u]
makes it so that the coefficients of
u

are not accounted when computing
\lVert{\cdot}\rVert_{\!\mathfrak{L}}
.

The bounds in the statement of the lemma are obtained by imply conjoining the bounds
in
Equations

36
and
37
.
The fact that
Algorithm

10
runs in non-deterministic polynomial time
follows again directly from
[
CMS24
, Lemma 6 and Lemma 37]
.

Here is the complexity of
Algorithm

See

Proof.

The proof of this lemma follows from the proofs of
[
CMS24
, Lemma 36 and Lemma 37]
.
For completeness, we give below a standalone analysis of the bounds on
\gamma^{\prime\prime}
and
\psi^{\prime\prime}
.

Analysis on
\gamma^{\prime\prime}
:

The relevant lines are line
10
, line
18
, line
23
and line
28
.

bound on
\#{\gamma^{\prime\prime}}
:

The system
\gamma^{\prime\prime}
is initialized with
\#{\gamma^{\prime}}
constraints in lines
10
or
18
. The subsequent lines
23
and
28
add at most
2
constraints.

bound on
\lVert{\gamma^{\prime\prime}}\rVert_{\!\mathfrak{L}}
:

Recall the
\gamma^{\prime\prime}
is a linear program with divisions featuring a single variable
q_{x}
(see
Lemma

6
). When this system is initialized in lines
10
or
18
, the absolute values of the coefficients of
q_{x}
are bounded by
\lVert{\gamma^{\prime}[2^{u}\,/\,u]}\rVert_{\!\mathfrak{L}}\leq a
. Lines
23
and
28
only add constraints in which
q_{x}
appears with coefficient
1\leq a
. Hence,
\lVert{\gamma^{\prime\prime}}\rVert_{\!\mathfrak{L}}\leq a
.

bound on
\lVert{\gamma^{\prime\prime}}\rVert_{1}
:

When the system
\gamma^{\prime\prime}
is initialized in lines
10
or
18
,
its norm
\lVert{\cdot}\rVert_{1}
is bounded by
{\lVert{\gamma^{\prime}}\rVert_{1}\cdot\max(2^{C},\textit{mod}(\gamma^{\prime}))}
,
where
C
is the integer defined in line
6
.
The (in)equalities added in lines
23
and
28
feature term with norm
\lVert{\cdot}\rVert_{1}
bounded by
C+1\leq 2^{C}
.
Therefore,

\displaystyle\lVert{\gamma^{\prime\prime}}\rVert_{1}

\displaystyle\leq c\cdot\max(2^{C},d)

\displaystyle\Lbag\text{by $\lVert{\gamma^{\prime}}\rVert_{1}\leq c$ and $\textit{mod}(\gamma^{\prime})\leq d$}\Rbag

\displaystyle\leq c\cdot\max(2^{3+2\cdot(\log(c)+1)},d)

\displaystyle\Lbag\text{by def.~of~$C$ and $2^{n}\leq\textit{mod}(\gamma^{\prime})$}\Rbag

\displaystyle\leq c\cdot\max(2^{5}c^{2},d)\,\leq\,\max(2^{5}c^{3},c\cdot d).

bound on
\textit{mod}(\gamma^{\prime\prime})
:

When
\gamma^{\prime\prime}
is initialized in lines
10
or
18
, its parameter
\textit{mod}(\cdot)

is equal to
\textit{mod}(\gamma^{\prime})
. Line
23
adds no divisibility constraints, whereas
28
adds a single divisibility constraints with divisor
d^{\prime}
. Here,
d^{\prime}
is the multiplicative order of
2
modulo the largest odd factor of
\textit{mod}(\gamma^{\prime})
. That is,
d^{\prime}
is a divisor of
\phi(\textit{mod}(\gamma^{\prime}))
, which in turn is a
divisor of
\phi(d)
, where
\phi
is Euler’s totient function.
Therefore,
\textit{mod}(\gamma^{\prime})
divides
{\rm lcm}(d,\phi(d))
.

Analysis on
\psi^{\prime\prime}
:

The relevant lines are line
23
and line
28
, which define
\psi^{\prime\prime}
depending on
\chi
.

bound on
\#\psi^{\prime\prime}
:

The system
\psi^{\prime\prime}
has a single equality when defined in line
23
, and three constraints when defined in line
28
.

bound on
\lVert{\psi^{\prime\prime}}\rVert_{\!\mathfrak{L}}
:

The variables occurring in
\psi^{\prime\prime}
are
r_{x}
and
y
. Following lines
23
and
28
, these variables always appear with coefficients
\pm 1
.

bound on
\lVert{\psi^{\prime\prime}}\rVert_{1}
:

Following lines
23
and
28
, we see that
\lVert{\psi^{\prime\prime}}\rVert_{1}\leq 2+2\cdot C
,
where
C
is the integer defined as in line
6
. Therefore,

\displaystyle\lVert{\psi^{\prime\prime}}\rVert_{1}

\displaystyle\leq 2+2\cdot C

\displaystyle\leq 2+2\cdot\max(\left\lceil\log(d)\right\rceil,3+2\left\lceil\log(c)\right\rceil)

\displaystyle\Lbag\text{using $\lVert{\gamma^{\prime}}\rVert_{1}\leq c$ and $2^{n}\leq\textit{mod}(\gamma^{\prime})\leq d$}\Rbag

\displaystyle\leq\max(2\log(d)+4,4\log(c)+12)

\displaystyle\leq 12+4\cdot\log(\max(c,d)).

bound on
\textit{mod}(\psi^{\prime\prime})
:

From line
28
we conclude that
\textit{mod}(\psi^{\prime\prime})
is a divisor of
d^{\prime}
. As discussed in the analysis of
\textit{mod}(\gamma^{\prime\prime})
, this implies that it also divides
\phi(d)
.
∎

Appendix C
Proofs of statements from Part
I

C.1
Proofs of statements from Section
3

See
7

Proof.

For brevity, define
p\coloneqq\textit{mod}(x,\varphi)
.
Consider two solution
\nu
and
\nu+[x\mapsto m]
to
\varphi
,
with
m\geq p
. (Recall that
\nu+[x\mapsto m]
denotes the map obtained from
\nu
by increasing the value assigned to
x
by
m
.)
We prove that
\nu^{\prime}\coloneqq\nu+[x\mapsto p]
is also solution to
\varphi
.
We analyse divisibility constraints and (in)equalities separately.
Clearly,
\nu^{\prime}
satisfies all constraints in which
x
does not appear, hence below we only consider constraints featuring
x
(linearly, as per hypothesis).

divisibility constraints.

Consider a divisibility constraint
\psi\coloneqq(d\mathrel{|}c\cdot x+\tau)
. By definition of
p
, we have
d\mathrel{|}p
,
and therefore
c\cdot\nu(x)
and
c\cdot(\nu(x)+p)
have the same reminder modulo
d
. Hence,
\nu^{\prime}
satisfies
\psi
.

equalities and inequalities.

We only show the case of inequalities (the proof for equalities is analogous, as they can be seen as a conjunction of two inequalities).
Consider an inequality
\chi\coloneqq(c\cdot x+\tau\leq 0)
.
Both
\nu
and
\nu+[x\mapsto m]
satisfy this inequality, so we have
{c\cdot\nu(x)+\nu(\tau)\leq 0}
and
{c\cdot(\nu(x)+m)+\nu(\tau)\leq 0}
.
Recall that
m\geq p\geq 1
.
If
c<0
, then
c\cdot(\nu(x)+p)+\nu(\tau)<c\cdot\nu(x)+\nu(\tau)\leq 0
.
If instead
c>0
,
then
c\cdot(\nu(x)+p)+\nu(\tau)<c\cdot(\nu(x)+m)+\nu(\tau)\leq 0
.
In both cases,
we conclude that
\nu^{\prime}
satisfies
\chi
.
∎

See
8

Proof.

We prove the lemma for the case of maximization.
The case of minimization is analogous.
For simplicity, let
\bm{x}=(x_{1},\dots,x_{n})
.
Given a solution
\nu
to
\varphi
,
we write
\Delta_{x}^{p}[f](\nu)
as a shortcut for
\Delta_{x}^{p}(\nu(x_{1}),\dots,\nu(x_{n}))
.
Assume that an optimal solution to
(f,\varphi)
exists.

The lemma is trivially true when
x
occurs in an equality of
\varphi
,
as all optima must satisfy that equality. Moreover, if
x
does not occur in equalities nor inequalities of
\varphi
,
then the existence of an optimum implies that
{\Delta_{x}^{p}[f](\nu)=0}
whenever
\nu
and
\nu+[x\mapsto p]
are solutions to
\varphi
.
We only need to make sure to satisfy the divisibility constraints,
and therefore it suffices to consider equations
x=r

with
r\in[0..p-1]
. These equations are among those considered in the statement of the lemma
(since we consider
\textup{terms}(\varphi\land x\geq 0)
).
Below, we assume that
x
occurs in an inequality of
\varphi

but in no equality.
We divide the proof depending on the sign of

\Delta_{x}^{p}[f]
.

case:
{\Delta_{x}^{p}[f](\nu)>0}
whenever
\nu
and
{\nu+[x\mapsto p]}
are solutions to
\varphi
.

Consider a solution
\nu
to
\varphi
,
and assume that it does not satisfy any equation
a\cdot x+\tau+r=0

having
(a\cdot x+\tau)\in{\textup{terms}(\varphi\land x\geq 0)}
,
a\neq 0
,
and
r\in[0..\left|a\right|\cdot p-1]
.
We show that then
\nu^{\prime}\coloneqq\nu+[x\mapsto p]
is still a solution
to
\varphi
. From
{\Delta_{x}^{p}[f](\nu)>0}
,
the value of the objective function
f
for the solution
\nu^{\prime}
is greater than the one for
\nu
. In particular, this means that
\nu
cannot be optimal.

First, observe that
\nu^{\prime}
still
satisfies all inequalities in
\varphi
of the form
b\cdot x\leq\tau
with
b\leq 0
, as well as all divisibility constraints.
For the inequalities, this follows from

b\cdot\nu^{\prime}(x)\leq b\cdot\nu(x)\leq\nu(\tau)=\nu^{\prime}(\tau)
.
For the divisibility constraints, it suffices to observe that
\nu(x)
and
\nu^{\prime}(x)
have the same residue modulo
p=\textit{mod}(x,\varphi)
.
Consider then an inequality
a\cdot x\leq\tau
with
a>0
.
We have
(a\cdot x-\tau)\in\textup{terms}(\varphi)
,
and so
\nu
does not satisfy any equation
a\cdot x-\tau+r=0
with
r\in[0..a\cdot p-1]
.
Hence, there is a positive integer
k\geq a\cdot b

such that
a\cdot\nu(x)-\tau+k=0
.
We have
a\cdot\nu^{\prime}(x)-\tau+k^{\prime}=0
,
where
k^{\prime}\coloneqq k-a\cdot b\geq 0
;
that is,
\nu^{\prime}
satisfies
a\cdot x\leq\tau
.

case:
{\Delta_{x}^{p}[f](\nu)<0}
whenever
\nu
and
{\nu+[x\mapsto p]}
are solutions to
\varphi
.

Consider a solution
\nu
that does not satisfy any equation
a\cdot x+\tau+r=0
,
where
(a\cdot x+\tau)\in\textup{terms}(\varphi\land x\geq 0)
,
a\neq 0
,
and
r\in[0..\left|a\right|\cdot p-1]
.
This time we show that

\nu^{\prime}\coloneqq\nu+[x\mapsto-p]
is still a solution
to
\varphi
. From
{\Delta_{x}^{p}[f](\nu)<0}
,
the value that
f
takes for
\nu^{\prime}
is greater that the value that it takes for
\nu
; and therefore
\nu
cannot be optimal.

As in the previous case, it is trivial to see that
\nu^{\prime}
satisfies all inequalities of the form
b\cdot x\leq\tau
, with
b\geq 0
, as well as all divisibility constraints.
Consider then an inequality
a\cdot x\leq\tau
with
a<0
. Since
\nu
does not satisfy any equation
a\cdot x-\tau+r=0
with
r\in[0..\left|a\right|\cdot p-1]
,
we conclude that
\nu(\tau)\geq a\cdot\nu(x)+\left|a\right|\cdot p
.
Since
a<0
, we have
a\cdot\nu(x)+\left|a\right|\cdot p=a\cdot\dot{(}\nu(x)-p)
. Hence,
\nu^{\prime}
satisfies
a\cdot x\leq\tau
.

case:
{\Delta_{x}^{p}[f](\nu)=0}
whenever
\nu
and
{\nu+[x\mapsto p]}
are solutions to
\varphi
.

Let
\nu
be an optimal solution.
Recall that we are assuming that
x
occurs in an inequality
a\cdot x\leq\tau
of
\varphi
, and in no equality.
Suppose that
a>0
(the case for
a<0
is analogous).
Let
a_{1}\cdot x\leq\tau_{1},\,\dots\,,\,a_{j}\cdot x\leq\tau_{j}
be an enumeration of all inequalities featuring
x
and such that
a_{i}>0
.
Let
k\in[1..j]
satisfying

\frac{\nu(\tau_{k})}{a_{k}}=\min\left\{\frac{\nu(\tau_{i})}{a_{i}}:i\in[1..j]\right\}.

(38)

Let
\ell\in\mathbb{N}
be the maximum natural number
such that
a_{k}\cdot(\nu(x)+\ell\cdot p)\leq\nu(\tau_{k})
,
and define
\nu^{\prime}\coloneqq\nu+[x\mapsto\ell\cdot p]
.
We show that
\nu^{\prime}
is still an optimal solution to
\varphi
,
and that it satisfies an equation
a_{k}\cdot x-\tau_{k}+r=0
,
for some
r\in[0..a_{k}\cdot p-1]
.

To prove that
\nu^{\prime}
is a solution to
\varphi
,
observe first that, exactly as in the first case of the proof (
{\Delta_{x}^{p}[f](\nu)>0}
), the map
\nu^{\prime}
satisfies all inequalities in
\varphi
of the form
b\cdot x\leq\tau^{\prime}
with
b\leq 0
, as well as all divisibility constraints.
Given an inequality
a_{i}\cdot x\leq\tau_{i}
with
i\in[1..j]
,
from
Equation

38
we conclude that

\nu^{\prime}(x)\leq\frac{\nu(\tau_{k})}{a_{k}}\leq\frac{\nu(\tau_{i})}{a_{i}}
,
and therefore
a_{i}\cdot\nu^{\prime}(x)\leq\nu(\tau_{i})=\nu^{\prime}(\tau_{i})
.
Hence,
\nu^{\prime}
is a solution to
\varphi
.
It is also an optimal solution.
Indeed, since the set of solutions of
\varphi
is
(x,p)
-periodic (
Lemma

7
), and
{\Delta_{x}^{p}[f](\nu^{\prime\prime})=0}
whenever
\nu^{\prime\prime}
and
\nu^{\prime\prime}+[x\mapsto p]
are solutions to
\varphi
,
we conclude that
\nu
and the optimal solution
\nu^{\prime}
have the same value with respect to the objective function
f
.

Lastly,
\ell
has been defined to be such that
a_{k}\cdot\nu^{\prime}(x)\leq\nu(\tau_{k})<a_{k}\cdot(\nu^{\prime}(x)+p)
.
Observe that
{a_{k}\cdot(\nu^{\prime}(x)+p)-a_{k}\cdot\nu^{\prime}(x)}=a_{k}\cdot p
.
Therefore, there is an
r\in[0..a_{k}\cdot p-1]

such that
a_{k}\cdot\nu^{\prime}(x)+r=\nu(\tau_{k})
;
that is,
\nu^{\prime}
satisfies
a_{k}\cdot x-\tau_{k}+r=0
.
∎

See
2

Proof.

Consider the objective function
f
that simply returns the value assigned to
x
.
This is clearly
(x,\textit{mod}(x,\varphi))
-monotone locally to the set of solutions of
\varphi
.
Since we are looking at non-negative solutions to
\varphi
,
f
ranges over the natural numbers, and so it has a minimum.
The corollary follows then immediately from
Lemma

8
.
∎

See
9

Proof.

By definition of monotone decomposition,

\varphi
is equivalent to
\psi_{1}\lor\dots\lor\psi_{t}
.
Let
\nu
be a solution to
\varphi
that maximizes (or minimizes) the objective function
f
.
There is
i\in[1..t]
such that

\nu
is a solution to
\psi_{i}
.
Since
\psi_{i}
implies
\varphi
,
the solution
\nu
is optimal for
(f,\psi_{i})
.
By
Lemma

8
,
there is an optimal solution
\nu^{\prime}
to
(f,\psi_{i})

that satisfies an equation
a\cdot x+\tau+r=0
,
where
a\neq 0
,
(a\cdot x+\tau)\in\textup{terms}(\psi_{i}\land x\geq 0)
,
and
r\in[0..\left|a\right|\cdot\textit{mod}(x,\psi_{i})-1]
.
The value
f
takes with respect to the two solutions
\nu
and
\nu^{\prime}
is the same, and since
\psi_{i}
implies
\varphi
,
the map
\nu^{\prime}
is also a solution to
\varphi
.
∎

C.2
Proofs of Lemma
10
and Claim
2
from Section
4

See
10

Proof.

From
C\geq 2
we get

2^{C}-2^{C/2}-d\cdot C\geq 2^{C/2}(2^{C/2}-1)-d\cdot C\geq 2^{C/2}-d\cdot C
. So, it suffices
to prove
2^{C/2}-d\cdot C\geq 0
.
Consider the function
f(x)\coloneqq 2^{x/2}-d\cdot x
,
as well as its first derivative
f^{\prime}(x)=\frac{1}{2}\cdot\ln(2)\cdot 2^{x/2}-d
and second derivative
f^{\prime\prime}(x)=\frac{1}{4}\cdot\ln(2)^{2}\cdot 2^{x/2}
.
Note that
f^{\prime\prime}
is positive for all
x\geq 0
,
and
f^{\prime}(4\cdot\log_{2}(d)+8)=8\cdot\ln(2)\cdot d^{2}-d\geq 4\cdot d^{2}-d\geq 0
.
Therefore,

f
is increasing for every
x\geq 4\cdot\log_{2}(d)+8
,
and

f(C)\geq f(4\cdot\log_{2}(d)+8)=16\cdot d^{2}-d\cdot(4\cdot\log_{2}(d)+8)\geq 16\cdot d^{2}-12\cdot d^{2}\geq 0
.
∎

The following claim refers to the objects defined throughout
the proof of
Proposition

3
.

See
2

Proof.

Let us assume that the quantifier-free part of the formula
\chi_{\sigma}

implies
\overline{x}_{m}\geq x_{m}

(a similar argument applies if it instead implies
x_{m}\geq\overline{x}_{m}
).
We show that
for any two solutions

\nu
and
\nu^{\prime}\coloneqq\nu+[q\mapsto p]
of
\varphi\land\varphi[q+p\,/\,q]\land\chi_{\sigma}
, the objective function
{C}[{x_{m}}]
evaluated at
\nu^{\prime}
is at least as large as its value at
\nu
.

Let
\nu
and
\nu^{\prime}
be such a pair of solutions.
We start by focusing on
\nu
.
Define
\nu(\bm{w})
as the vector of integers
obtained by evaluating
C
and
C^{+p}
according to
\nu
,
to then extract the values corresponding to the variables in
\bm{w}

(which include
\overline{x}_{m}
and
x_{m}
).
Since
\chi_{\sigma}
contains the equations describing the assignments in
C
and
C^{+p}
,
the vector

\nu(\bm{w})
is the only one that satisfies
the quantifier-free part of
\chi_{\sigma}
for the solution
\nu
.
Let
a
and
\overline{a}
represent the values in
\nu(\bm{w})
corresponding to
x_{m}
and
\overline{x}_{m}
, respectively.
Specifically,
a
is the value taken by
{C}[{x_{m}}]
for
\nu
,
and
\overline{a}
is the value taken by
{C^{+p}}[{\overline{x}_{m}}]
for
\nu
.
Since
\nu^{\prime}
also satisfies
\varphi
,

1

implies that the value of
{C}[{x_{m}}]
for
\nu^{\prime}

is the same as the value of
(\bar{x}_{m},C^{+p})
for
\nu
,
which is
\overline{a}
.
Finally, because we assume that
\overline{x}_{m}\geq x_{m}

is implied by the quantifier-free part of
\chi_{\sigma}
,
we conclude that
\overline{a}\geq a
, completing the proof.
∎

C.3
Bareiss algorithm

In this appendix we recall the classical Bareiss algorithm from
[
Bar68
]
and of some of its standard properties.
These properties are then lifted to our variation in
Section

C.4
, where we provide the proofs of
Lemmas

15
,
16
,
17
and
18
.
Throughout this and the next appendix, we follow the notation introduced in
Section

5.3
,
in particular when it comes to defining the subdeterminants
b^{(\ell)}_{i,j}
and
b^{(\ell)}_{r\leftarrow j}
of a given matrix with entries denotes as
b_{i,j}
.

We describe the standard Bareiss’s algorithm from
[
Bar68
]
.
Consider an
m\times d
integer matrix
B_{0}
:

B_{0}\coloneqq\begin{pmatrix}b_{1,1}&\ldots&b_{1,d}\\
\vdots&\ddots&\vdots\\
b_{m,1}&\ldots&b_{m,d}\end{pmatrix}.

As done in
Section

5.3
,
fix
k\in[0..\min(m,d)]
(the number of iterations the algorithm will perform), and define
\lambda_{0}\coloneqq 1

and
{\lambda_{\ell}\coloneqq b_{\ell,\ell}^{(\ell-1)}}
, for every
\ell\in[1..k]
.
We assume that every
\lambda_{\ell}
is non-zero.

Starting from the matrix
B_{0}
,
Bareiss algorithm iteratively constructs a sequence of matrices
B_{1},\dots,B_{k}
as follows.
Consider
{\ell\in[0..k-1]}
, and let
B_{\ell}
be the matrix

B_{\ell}\coloneqq\begin{pmatrix}g_{1,1}&\ldots&g_{1,d}\\
\vdots&\ddots&\vdots\\
g_{m,1}&\ldots&g_{m,d}\end{pmatrix}.

The matrix
B_{\ell+1}
is constructed from
B_{\ell}

by applying the following transformation

1:

for
every row
i
except row
\ell+1

do

2:

multiply the
i
th row of
B_{\ell}
by
g_{\ell+1,\ell+1}

\triangleright

the entry
(i,\ell+1)
of
B_{\ell}
is now
g_{\ell+1,\ell+1}\cdot g_{i,\ell+1}

3:

subtract
g_{i,\ell+1}\cdot(g_{\ell+1,1},\dots,g_{\ell+1,d})
from the
i
th row of
B_{\ell}

\triangleright

the entry
(i,\ell+1)
is now
0

4:

divide each entry of the
i
th row of
B_{\ell}
by
\lambda_{\ell}

\triangleright

these divisions are without remainder

The next three results from
[
Bar68
]
give a complete description of the entries of
B_{0},\dots,B_{k}
:

Lemma

33
contains a trivial observation that we will use
many times in the other two lemmas,
Lemma

34
describes
the last
m-\ell
rows of these matrices,
and
Lemma

35
describes the first
\ell
rows.

Lemma 33
[
Bar68
]
.

For all
\ell\in[0..k-1]
,
the
(\ell+1)
th rows of the matrices
B_{\ell}
and
B_{\ell+1}

are equal.

Lemma 34
[
Bar68
]
.

Consider
\ell\in[0..k]
.
For every
i\in[\ell+1..m]
and
j\in[1..d]
,
the entry in position
(i,j)
of the matrix
B_{\ell}
is

b^{(\ell)}_{i,j}
.
In particular, this entry is zero whenever
j\leq\ell
.

Lemma 35
[
Bar68
]
.

Consider
\ell\in[1..k]
.
For all
i\in[1..\ell]
and
j\in[1..d]
,
the entry in position
(i,j)
of
B_{\ell}
is

b^{(\ell)}_{i\leftarrow j}
.
In particular,
this entry is zero if
j\leq\ell
and
i\neq j
,
and it is instead
b^{(\ell-1)}_{\ell,\ell}
when
i=j
.

C.4
Analysis of the variation of Bareiss algorithm

We are now ready to analyze our variation of Bareiss algorithm, and prove
Lemmas

15
,
16
,
17
and
18
.
Below, let
k
,
B_{0}
,
B_{0}^{\prime}
,
\mu
,
U_{g}
and
\lambda_{\ell}

be defined as in
Section

5.3
.
We recall below how the matrices
B_{1}^{\prime},\dots,B_{k}^{\prime}
are constructed from
B_{0}^{\prime}
.
Consider
{\ell\in[0..k-1]}
, and let
B_{\ell}^{\prime}
be the matrix

B_{\ell}^{\prime}\coloneqq\begin{pmatrix}h_{1,1}&\ldots&h_{1,d}\\
\vdots&\ddots&\vdots\\
h_{m,1}&\ldots&h_{m,d}\end{pmatrix}.

The matrix
B_{\ell+1}^{\prime}
is constructed from
B_{\ell}^{\prime}

by applying the following transformation

01:

let

\pm
be the sign of
h_{\ell+1,\ell+1}
, and
\alpha\coloneqq\frac{\pm h_{\ell+1,\ell+1}}{\mu}

\triangleright

this division is without remainder

02:

multiply the row
\ell+1
of
B_{\ell}
by
\pm 1

03:

for
every row
i
except row
\ell+1

do

04:

let

\beta\coloneqq\frac{h_{i,\ell+1}}{\mu}

\triangleright

this division is without remainder

05:

multiply the
i
th row of
B_{\ell}^{\prime}
by
\alpha

\triangleright

B_{\ell}^{\prime}(i,\ell+1)
is now
\alpha\cdot g_{i,\ell+1}

06:

subtract
\pm\beta\cdot(h_{\ell+1,1},\dots,h_{\ell+1,d})
from the
i
th row of
B_{\ell}^{\prime}

07:

divide each entry of the
i
th row of
B_{\ell}^{\prime}
by
\left|\lambda_{\ell}\right|

\triangleright

these divisions are without remainder

We now show that the main relationship between
the matrices computed with the above transformation, and
the sequence of matrices
B_{0},\dots,B_{k}
computed with Bareiss algorithm.

Lemma 36.

For every
\ell\in[0..k]
,
B_{\ell}^{\prime}=\pm B_{\ell}\cdot U_{g}
,
where
\pm
is the sign of
\lambda_{\ell}
.

Proof.

Below, we write
\pm_{\ell}
for the sign of
\lambda_{\ell}
.
The proof is by induction on
\ell
.

base case:
\ell=0
.

Trivial from the definition of
B_{0}^{\prime}
(recall that
\lambda_{0}=1
in this case).

induction hypothesis.

Given
\ell\geq 1
, we have
B_{\ell-1}^{\prime}=\pm_{\ell-1}B_{\ell-1}\cdot U_{g}
.

induction step:
\ell\geq 1
.

From the induction hypothesis, for every
i\in[1..m]
the
i
th rows
\bm{r}_{i}
and
\bm{r}_{i}^{\prime}
of
B_{\ell-1}
and
B_{\ell-1}^{\prime}
are, respectively,

\displaystyle\bm{r}_{i}=(r_{i,1},\dots,r_{i,d}),\qquad\qquad\bm{r}_{i}^{\prime}=\pm_{\ell-1}(\mu\cdot r_{i,1},\dots,\mu\cdot r_{\ell,g},r_{i,g+1},\dots,r_{i,d}),

for some integers
r_{i,1},\dots,r_{i,d}
.
To show that
B_{\ell}^{\prime}=\pm_{\ell}B_{\ell}\cdot U_{g}
,
we analyze the pseudocodes used to construct
B_{\ell}
and
B_{\ell}^{\prime}
,
considering each row separately.

Let us start by considering the
\ell
th row.
In the case of
B_{\ell}
, this row coincides with
\bm{r}_{\ell}
.
In the case of
B_{\ell}^{\prime}
, this row is instead
\pm\bm{r}_{\ell}^{\prime}
,
where
\pm
is the sign of
\pm_{\ell-1}\cdot\mu\cdot r_{\ell,\ell}
. By
Lemma

34
and from the definition of
\lambda_{\ell}
, we have
r_{\ell,\ell}=\lambda_{\ell}
. Since
\mu\geq 1
,
\pm 1=\pm_{\ell-1}\pm_{\ell}1
, and therefore
\pm\bm{r}_{\ell}^{\prime}
is equal to

\pm_{\ell}(\mu\cdot r_{\ell,1},\dots,\mu\cdot r_{\ell,g},r_{\ell,g+1},\dots,r_{\ell,d})
, as required.

Consider now
i\in[1..m]
with
i\neq\ell
.
Following the code of Bareiss algorithm, the
i
th row of
B_{\ell}
is given by

\frac{1}{\lambda_{\ell-1}}\cdot(r_{\ell,\ell}\cdot\bm{r}_{i}-r_{i,\ell}\cdot\bm{r}_{\ell})
.
The entry of
B_{\ell}
in position
(i,j)
, with
j\in[1..d]
, is thus

t_{i,j}\coloneqq\frac{r_{\ell,\ell}\cdot r_{i,j}-r_{i,\ell}\cdot r_{\ell,j}}{\lambda_{\ell-1}}.

The
i
th row of
B_{\ell}^{\prime}
is instead
\frac{1}{\left|\lambda_{\ell-1}\right|}\cdot(\alpha\cdot\bm{r}_{i}^{\prime}-\pm\beta\cdot\bm{r}_{\ell}^{\prime})
,
where
\pm
is again the sign of
\pm_{\ell-1}\mu\cdot r_{\ell,\ell}
,
and
\alpha\coloneqq\frac{\pm\pm_{\ell-1}\mu\cdot r_{\ell,\ell}}{\mu}=\frac{\pm_{\ell}\mu\cdot r_{\ell,\ell}}{\mu}

and
\beta\coloneqq\frac{\pm_{\ell-1}\mu\cdot r_{i,\ell}}{\mu}

are defined as in line
4
of the pseudocode.
For every
j\in[1..g]
, the entry of
B_{\ell}^{\prime}
in position
(i,j)

is therefore

\displaystyle t_{i,j}^{\prime}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\frac{\alpha\cdot(\pm_{\ell-1}\mu\cdot r_{i,j})-\pm\beta\cdot(\pm_{\ell-1}\mu\cdot r_{\ell,j})}{\left|\lambda_{\ell-1}\right|}

\displaystyle=\frac{\pm_{\ell}r_{\ell,\ell}\cdot(\pm_{\ell-1}\mu\cdot r_{i,j})-\pm_{\ell}r_{i,\ell}(\pm_{\ell-1}\mu\cdot r_{\ell,j})}{\pm_{\ell-1}\lambda_{\ell-1}}\,=\,\pm_{\ell}\mu\cdot\frac{r_{\ell,\ell}\cdot r_{i,j}-r_{i,\ell}\cdot r_{\ell,j}}{\lambda_{\ell-1}}\,=\,\pm_{\ell}\mu\cdot t_{i,j}.

An analogous manipulation shows
t_{i,j}^{\prime}=\pm_{\ell}t_{i,j}
,
for every
j\in[g+1..d]
.
We thus have
(t_{i,1}^{\prime},\dots,t_{i,d}^{\prime})=\pm_{\ell}(\mu\cdot t_{i,1},\dots,\mu\cdot t_{i,g},t_{i,g+1},\dots,t_{i,d})
,
which concludes the proof.
∎

By relying on
Lemma

36
, we can easily rephrase the properties of Bareiss algorithm from
Section

C.3
to our variation,
proving
Lemmas

15
,
16
and
17
.

See
15

Proof.

This is a simple observation on the effects of lines
1

and
2
of the procedure.
∎

See
16

Proof.

Directly from
Lemma

36
and
Lemma

34
.
∎

See
17

Proof.

Directly from
Lemma

36
and
Lemma

35
.
∎

Lastly, we move to the proof of
Lemma

18
,
which relies on Laplace expansions.
37
below
recalls this notion using determinants
of the form
a_{r\leftarrow j}^{(\ell)}
. These determinants have two
straightforward properties:

1.

If
j\leq\ell
and
r\neq j
, then the
j
th and
r
th columns of the submatrix corresponding to this determinant are identical. Therefore,
a_{r\leftarrow j}^{(\ell)}=0
.

2.

If
j=r
then
a_{j\leftarrow j}^{(\ell)}=a_{\ell,\ell}^{(\ell-1)}
.

Lemma 37

(Laplace Expansion)
.

For every
i,j,\ell\in\mathbb{N}
satisfying
\ell\geq 1
,
\ell<i\leq m
and
\ell<j\leq d
,

a^{(\ell)}_{i,j}=a^{(\ell-1)}_{\ell,\ell}\cdot a_{i,j}-\sum\nolimits_{r=1}^{\ell}a^{(\ell)}_{r\leftarrow j}\cdot a_{i,r}.

(39)

Proof.

Equation

39
is not the standard way of writing the Laplace expansion,
but it is one that will be very natural for our purposes.
A more standard way of writing this identity is:

a^{(\ell)}_{i,j}=a^{(\ell-1)}_{\ell,\ell}\cdot a_{i,j}+\sum\nolimits_{r=1}^{\ell}(-1)^{r+\ell+1}m_{r}\cdot a_{i,r}\,,

(40)

where
m_{r}\coloneqq\det\begin{pmatrix}a_{1,1}&\ldots&a_{1,r-1}&a_{1,r+1}&\dots&a_{1,\ell}&a_{1,j}\\
\vdots&\ddots&\vdots&\vdots&\vdots&\ddots&\vdots\\
a_{\ell,1}&\ldots&a_{\ell,r-1}&a_{\ell,r+1}&\dots&a_{\ell,\ell}&a_{\ell,j}\end{pmatrix}
.

The matrix
M
used to compute
m_{r}
features the same columns as the matrix
M^{\prime}
used to compute
a_{r\leftarrow j}^{(\ell)}
. In particular,
M^{\prime}
is obtained from
M
by applying a cyclic permutation to the last
\ell-r+1
columns, so that the column
(a_{1,j},\dots,a_{\ell,j})
appears first among those.
Recall that swapping two rows of a matrix changes the sign of its determinant. The permutation used to compute
M^{\prime}
form
M
can be realized with
\ell-r
swaps, hence
(-1)^{\ell-r}m_{r}=a_{r\leftarrow j}^{(\ell)}
.
Therefore,
(-1)^{r+\ell+1}m_{r}=(-1)^{r+\ell+1+r-r}m_{r}=-a_{r\leftarrow j}^{(\ell)}
, showing that
Equations

39
and
40
are equivalent.
∎

See
18

Proof.

Note that if
\ell=0
, then the transformation does not modify
B_{0}
(recall that
\lambda_{0}=1
),
and the lemma is therefore trivially true.
Let us consider then
\ell\geq 1
,
and write
\bm{v}
for the
i
th row of
B_{0}^{\prime}

after
the transformation.
Let us compute an expression for the entries in
\bm{v}
.
After executing line
1
,
the
i
th row of
B_{0}
is of the form

\pm b_{\ell,\ell}^{(\ell-1)}\cdot(\mu\cdot b_{i,1},\dots,\mu\cdot b_{i,g},\,b_{i,g+1}\dots,b_{i,d})
,
where
\pm
is the sign of
\lambda_{\ell}=b_{\ell,\ell}^{(\ell-1)}
.
From
Lemma

17
,
the vector
\bm{u}_{r}
from line
2

is
\bm{u}_{r}=\pm(\mu\cdot b_{r\leftarrow 1}^{(\ell)},\dots,\mu\cdot b_{r\leftarrow g}^{(\ell)},\,b_{r\leftarrow g+1}^{(\ell)},\dots,b_{r\leftarrow d}^{(\ell)})
.
After performing all subtractions from line
2
to the
i
th row,
we obtain the vector
\bm{v}
.
Its
j
th entry
is:

\displaystyle\pm\mu_{j}\cdot(b_{\ell,\ell}^{(\ell-1)}\cdot b_{i,j}-\sum\nolimits_{r=1}^{\ell}b_{r\leftarrow j}^{(\ell)}\cdot b_{i,r}),

where
\mu_{j}\coloneqq\mu
whenever
j\leq g
, and otherwise
\mu_{j}\coloneqq 1
.

We show that the expression
{(b_{\ell,\ell}^{(\ell-1)}\cdot b_{i,j}-\sum\nolimits_{r=1}^{\ell}b_{r\leftarrow j}^{(\ell)}\cdot b_{i,r})}

equals
b_{i,j}^{(\ell)}
,
thus establishing that
\bm{v}
is the
i
th row of
B_{\ell}^{\prime}

by
Lemma

16
.
When
j>\ell
, this results follows directly from
37
.
When
j\leq\ell
instead, observe
that
b_{i,j}^{(\ell)}=0
(
Lemma

16
),
and it suffices to show that the expression
(b_{\ell,\ell}^{(\ell-1)}\cdot b_{i,j}-\sum\nolimits_{r=1}^{\ell}b_{r\leftarrow j}^{(\ell)}\cdot b_{i,r})
is also zero.
For every
r\in[1..\ell]

with
r\neq j
, the determinant
b_{r\leftarrow j}^{(\ell)}
is zero;
as the
j
th and
r
th columns of the corresponding matrix are identical.
The expression thus simplifies
to

(b_{\ell,\ell}^{(\ell-1)}\cdot b_{i,j}-b_{j\leftarrow j}^{(\ell)}\cdot b_{i,j})\ =\ (b_{\ell,\ell}^{(\ell-1)}\cdot b_{i,j}-b_{\ell,\ell}^{(\ell-1)}\cdot b_{i,j})\ =\ 0
.
∎

C.5
Proofs of Lemma
13
from Section
5

See
13

Proof.

Let us first observe that
\gamma
contains an inequality
a\cdot q_{n-\ell}\geq 0
for some
a\geq 1
, by definition of
\mathcal{I}_{k}^{\ell}
, and therefore
\textup{terms}(\gamma)
contains
-a\cdot q_{n-\ell}
. This implies that the guess performed in line
3
is never on an empty set.

Let
\rho\coloneqq(a\cdot q_{n-\ell}-\tau)
be the guessed term.
By definition of
\mathcal{I}_{k}^{\ell}
,

\gamma
is a linear program in variables
u
and
\bm{q}_{[\ell,k]}
, and in which
every inequality and equality is such that
all the coefficients of the variables in
\bm{q}_{[\ell,k]}

are divisible by
\mu_{C}
.
The statement is thus true when
\rho

belongs to
{\textup{terms}(\gamma\land\gamma[q_{n-\ell}+p\,/\,q_{n-\ell}])}
.

Suppose
\rho
to be instead computed using
Algorithm

2
.
In this case there are
b,d\in\mathbb{Z}
, and
q^{\prime},q^{\prime\prime}
from
\bm{q}_{k-1}
,
such that

\rho
is obtained from
{b\cdot u+\mu_{C}\cdot(q^{\prime}-q^{\prime\prime})+d}

by simultaneously applying two substitutions
[\frac{\tau^{\prime}}{\lambda}\,/\,\mu_{C}\cdot q^{\prime}]
and
[\frac{\tau^{\prime\prime}}{\lambda}\,/\,\mu_{C}\cdot q^{\prime\prime}]
.
By definition of simultaneous substitution,

\rho
is thus of the form

\left(\lambda\cdot b\cdot u\pm\tau^{\prime}\mp\tau^{\prime\prime}+\lambda\cdot d\right)
,
where the signs
\pm
and
\mp
depend on the sign of
\lambda
.
Here,
\lambda\coloneqq\frac{\eta_{C}}{\mu_{C}}
,
and the terms
\tau^{\prime}
and
\tau^{\prime\prime}
are computed as described
in lines
5
–
8
.
From the definition of
\mathcal{I}_{k}^{\ell}
, recall that
\mu_{C}
divides
\eta_{C}
, as well as all coefficients of the variables
\bm{q}_{[\ell,k]}
occurring in linear terms
\tau_{n-i}(u,\bm{q}_{[\ell,k]})
featured in assignments
q_{n-i}\leftarrow\frac{\tau_{n-i}}{\eta_{C}}
of
C
, with
i\in[0..\ell-1]
.
Looking at
lines
5
–
8
,
it is then easy to see that the terms
\tau^{\prime}
and
\tau^{\prime\prime}

are linear terms in variables
u
and
\bm{q}_{[\ell,k]}
, and that in these terms the coefficients of
\bm{q}_{[\ell,k]}
are all divisible by
\mu_{C}
.
Then, the same is true for the term
\pm\tau^{\prime}\mp\tau^{\prime\prime}
, and in turn also for
\rho
.
∎

C.6
Proofs of the claims from Lemma
20
(Section
5
)

The following claims refer to objects defined throughout the proof of
Lemma

20
.

See
9

Proof.

By induction hypothesis, the
\ell
th rows of
M_{\ell-1}
and
B_{\ell-1}^{\prime}
are equal, and by
8
, they contain the variable coefficients of
a\cdot q_{n-(\ell-1)}-\tau
.
Following Bareiss algorithm (see line
2
and
Lemma

15
), the
\ell
th row of
B_{\ell}^{\prime}

contains the variable coefficients of
\pm a\cdot q_{n-(\ell-1)}-(\pm\tau)
.

In the case of
M_{\ell}
, from its definition (Item (
i.
)), the
\ell
th row
contains the variable coefficients of the term
\eta_{\ell}\cdot q-\tau^{\prime}
such
that
q\leftarrow\frac{\tau^{\prime}}{\eta_{\ell}}
is the first assignment in
C_{\ell}
.
From line
2
and the last item in line
14
of
Algorithm

5
,
we conclude that this assignment is
q_{n-(\ell-1)}\leftarrow\frac{\pm\tau}{\pm a}
. Therefore, the
\ell
th rows of
M_{\ell}
and
B_{\ell}^{\prime}
are equal.

Again from
14
of
Algorithm

5
, we see that in
C_{\ell}
all assignments to variables in
\bm{q}_{k}
have
\pm a
as a denominator, that is,
\eta_{\ell}=\pm a
.
Furthermore, from
Lemma

17
,
the entry of
B_{\ell}^{\prime}
in position
(\ell,\ell)
is
\pm^{\prime}\mu\cdot b_{\ell,\ell}^{(\ell-1)}
, where
\pm^{\prime}
is the sign of
\lambda_{\ell}=b_{\ell,\ell}^{(\ell-1)}
. By definition of
M_{\ell}
, the
\ell
th column contains the coefficients of
q_{n-(\ell-1)}
.
This means that the entry of
M_{\ell}
in position
(\ell,\ell)

is
\pm a
, and therefore
\pm^{\prime}\mu\cdot\lambda_{\ell}=\pm a
.
It follows that
\alpha=\frac{\eta_{\ell}}{\mu}=\left|\lambda_{\ell}\right|\neq 0
.
∎

See
11

Proof.

This proof is similar to the one of
10
.
By definition, the
i
th rows of
M_{\ell-1}
and
M_{\ell}

contain the variable coefficients of the terms in
the (in)equalities
{\Lambda_{\ell-1}(\rho_{i-j}\sim_{i-j}0)}
and
\Lambda_{\ell}(\rho_{i-j}\sim_{i-j}0)
, respectively.
By definition of
\Lambda_{\ell-1}
and
\Lambda_{\ell}
,

\Lambda_{\ell}(\rho_{i-j}\sim_{i-j}0)
is the (in)equality obtained from
\Lambda_{\ell}(\rho_{i-j}\sim_{i-j}0)
when
running lines
4
–
9
of
Algorithm

5
.
Below we analyze the updates performed in these lines of the algorithm, and compare them to those Bareiss algorithm performs on the
i
th row of
B_{\ell-1}^{\prime}
in order to produce
B_{\ell}^{\prime}
.

Let us write
\beta\cdot\mu\cdot q_{n-(\ell-1)}+\tau^{\prime}

for the term in the (in)equality
\Lambda_{\ell-1}(\rho_{i-j}\sim_{i-j}0)
. Line
4
applies the substitution
[\frac{\pm\tau}{\alpha}\,/\,\mu\cdot q_{n-(\ell-1)}]
on this term, obtaining the term

\pm\beta\cdot\tau+\alpha\cdot\tau^{\prime}
.
Then, following lines
7
and
9
, we see that the
i
th row
of
M_{\ell}
holds the variable coefficients
of the term
(\pm\beta\cdot\tau+\alpha\cdot\tau^{\prime})/\frac{\eta_{\ell-1}}{\mu}
obtained by dividing each integer in
\pm\beta\cdot\tau+\alpha\cdot\tau^{\prime}
by
\frac{\eta_{\ell-1}}{\mu}
. As in the proof of
10
, we will see below
that all variable coefficients of
\pm\beta\cdot\tau+\alpha\cdot\tau^{\prime}
are divisible by
\frac{\eta_{\ell-1}}{\mu}
.

Let us look at Bareiss algorithm.
By induction hypothesis, the
i
th row of
B_{\ell-1}^{\prime}

contains the variable coefficients of
\beta\cdot\mu\cdot q_{n-(\ell-1)}+\tau^{\prime}
.
The algorithm first multiplies this row by
\alpha
, and then subtracts
\pm\beta\cdot\bm{r}_{\ell}
, where
\bm{r}_{\ell}
is the
\ell
th row of
B_{\ell-1}^{\prime}
.
By
8
,

\bm{r}_{\ell}
holds the variable coefficients of
a\cdot q_{n-(\ell-1)}-\tau
. Hence, after this subtraction, the
i
th row of
B_{\ell-1}^{\prime}
is updated to contain the variable coefficients of the term
\pm\beta\cdot\tau+\alpha\cdot\tau^{\prime}
. Lastly, each entry of the
i
th row is divided by
\left|\lambda_{\ell-1}\right|=\frac{\eta_{\ell-1}}{\mu}
.
From
Lemma

16
, these divisions are exact. This means that every variable coefficient in
\pm\beta\cdot\tau+\alpha\cdot\tau^{\prime}
is divisible by
\frac{\eta_{\ell-1}}{\mu}
; so the divisions performed in
lines
7
and
9

of
Algorithm

5
are also without remainder.
The divisions performed by Bareiss algorithm
completes the construction of the
i
th row of
B_{\ell}
,
which thus contain the variable coefficients of
(\pm\beta\cdot\tau+\alpha\cdot\tau^{\prime})/\frac{\eta_{\ell-1}}{\mu}
. Hence, the
i
th rows of
M_{\ell}
and
B_{\ell}^{\prime}
coincide.

Let us discuss the second statement of the claim.
Every (in)equality in
{\gamma_{\ell-1}[\frac{\pm\tau}{\alpha}\,/\,\mu\cdot q_{n-{\ell-1}}]}

is obtained by applying the substitution
[\frac{\pm\tau}{\alpha}\,/\,\mu\cdot q_{n-{\ell-1}}]
to an (in)equality
\Lambda_{\ell-1}(\rho_{i-j}\sim_{i-j}0)
, with
i\in[j+1..j+t]
.
Following the notation above, let
\pm\beta\cdot\tau+\alpha\cdot\tau^{\prime}
be the term resulting from one such substitution.
We have already shown that all variable coefficients of this term are divisible by
\frac{\eta_{\ell-1}}{\mu}
.
Then, the coefficient of
u
is divisible by
\frac{\eta_{\ell-1}}{\mu}
. As for the remaining variables,
Lemma

16

guarantee that, once divided by
\frac{\eta_{\ell-1}}{\mu}
, their coefficients are still divisible by
\mu
;
hence before divisions these coefficients are divisible by
\eta_{\ell-1}
.
∎

See
12

Proof.

By definition, the contents of the
i
th rows of
M_{\ell-1}
and
M_{\ell}

depend on the type of the equality
e_{i-1}
. We divide the proof in two cases, depending on this type.

If
e_{i-1}
is of Type
I.
,
then by definition the
i
th rows of
M_{\ell-1}
and
M_{\ell}
contain the variable coefficients of the terms in
the (in)equalities
{\Lambda_{\ell-1}(g_{i-1})}
and
\Lambda_{\ell}(g_{i-1})
, respectively. Moreover,
the generator
g_{i-1}
of
e_{i-1}
is an (in)equality of
\gamma_{0}
.
Therefore, there is
r\in[j+1..j+t]

such that the
i
th and
r
th rows of
M_{\ell-1}
(resp.
M_{\ell}
) are equal. Since Bareiss algorithm performs the same updates on every row different from
\ell
,
the claim then follows from
11
.

Suppose now
e_{i-1}
to be of Type
II.
.
Let
g_{i-1}\coloneqq(\rho=0)
be the generator of
e_{i-1}
. Recall that
\rho
is of the form
{b\cdot u+\mu\cdot(q^{\prime}-q^{\prime\prime})+d}
, for some
b,d\in\mathbb{Z}
and
q^{\prime},q^{\prime\prime}
from
\bm{q}_{k}
.
By Item (
iii.
) in the definition of
M_{\ell}
,
the
i
th row of
M_{\ell}
contains the coefficients of the variables
\bm{q}_{k}
and
u
from the term obtained from
\rho
as follows:

1:

multiply every integer in
\rho
by the quotient of the division of
\eta_{\ell}
by
\mu

2:

for

r
in
[1..\ell]

do

\rho\leftarrow\rho[\tau_{r}\,/\,\eta_{\ell}\cdot q]
,
where
q\leftarrow\frac{\tau_{r}}{\eta_{\ell}}
is the
r
th assignment in
C_{\ell}

Moreover, again by definition of
M_{\ell}
,
the variable coefficients of the term
\eta_{\ell}\cdot q-\tau_{r}
corresponding to the
r
th assignment in
C_{\ell}
is
are stored in the
r
th row of
M_{\ell}
.
By Claims
9

and
10
,
this row is equal to the
r
th row of
B_{\ell}^{\prime}
.
Therefore, from
Lemma

17
,
we conclude that
q
is the variable
q_{n-(r-1)}
,
and that
\tau_{r}
is a linear term in variables
u
and
\bm{q}_{[\ell,k]}
.
Note that then, the terms
\tau_{1},\dots,\tau_{\ell}

do not feature any of the variables
q_{n-(\ell-1)},\dots,q_{n}
,
which means that the code above is in fact
simultaneously
applying the substitutions

{[\frac{\tau_{1}}{\alpha}\,/\,\mu\cdot q_{n}],\dots,[\frac{\tau_{\ell}}{\alpha}\,/\,\mu\cdot q_{n-(\ell-1)}]}
to
\rho

(by
9
,
\alpha=\frac{\eta_{\ell}}{\mu}
).
We conclude that the
i
th row of
M_{\ell}

holds the variable coefficients of

\alpha\cdot b\cdot u+\tau^{\prime}-\tau^{\prime\prime}+\alpha\cdot d,

(41)

where
\tau^{\prime}
stands for
\eta_{\ell}\cdot q^{\prime}
if
C_{\ell}
assigns no expression to
q^{\prime}
, and otherwise it is the term such that
q^{\prime}\leftarrow\frac{\tau^{\prime}}{\eta_{\ell}}
occurs in
C_{\ell}
;
and similarly,
\tau^{\prime\prime}
stands for
\eta_{\ell}\cdot q^{\prime\prime}
if
C_{\ell}
assigns no expression to
q^{\prime}
, and otherwise it is the term such that
q^{\prime\prime}\leftarrow\frac{\tau^{\prime\prime}}{\eta_{\ell}}
occurs in
C_{\ell}
.

Let us look at Bareiss algorithm.
By
Lemma

18
,
the
i
th row of
B_{\ell}^{\prime}
can be computed as follows:

1:

multiply the
i
th row of
B_{0}^{\prime}
by
\left|\lambda_{\ell}\right|

2:

for

r
in
[1..\ell]

do

subtract
b_{i,r}\cdot\bm{u}_{r}
to the
i
th row of
B_{0}^{\prime}
, where
\bm{u}_{r}
is the
r
th row of
B_{\ell}^{\prime}

Above
b_{i,r}
is the entry of
B_{0}
in position
(i,r)
—whereas the entry of
B_{0}^{\prime}
in that position is
{\mu\cdot b_{i,r}}
. Also, by
9
,
\left|\lambda_{\ell}\right|=\alpha
. Following Claims
9

and
10
, the
i
th row of
B_{\ell}^{\prime}

contains the variable coefficients of the term

\alpha\cdot b\cdot u+\eta_{\ell}\cdot(q^{\prime}-q^{\prime\prime})+\alpha\cdot d-\sum\nolimits_{r=1}^{\ell}b_{i,r}\cdot(\eta_{\ell}\cdot q_{n-(r-1)}-\tau_{r})

(42)

Now, if
q^{\prime}=q^{\prime\prime}
, then all entries
b_{i,s}
of
B_{0}
, where ranges in
s\in[1..k+1]
, are zero, and so
Equation

42
simplifies to
\alpha\cdot b\cdot u+\alpha\cdot d
; which
is equal to the term in
Equation

41
, since
q^{\prime}=q^{\prime\prime}
implies
\tau^{\prime}=\tau^{\prime\prime}
.
If instead
q^{\prime}\neq q^{\prime\prime}
, then all entries
b_{i,s}
of
B_{0}
(with
s\in[1..k+1]
) are equal to zero,
except for the entry in the position corresponding to
q^{\prime}
,
which is equal to
1
, and the entry in the position corresponding to
q^{\prime\prime}
, which is equal to
-1
.

Equation

42
can be rewritten as

\alpha\cdot b\cdot u+\eta_{\ell}\cdot(q^{\prime}-q^{\prime\prime})+\alpha\cdot d-\rho^{\prime}+\rho^{\prime\prime},

(43)

where
\rho^{\prime}\coloneqq 0
if the column corresponding to
q^{\prime}
is not among the first
\ell
,
and otherwise
\rho^{\prime}\coloneqq(\eta_{\ell}\cdot q^{\prime}-\tau^{\prime})
,
with
q^{\prime}\leftarrow\frac{\tau^{\prime}}{\eta_{\ell}}
occurring in
C_{\ell}
; and similarly,

\rho^{\prime\prime}\coloneqq 0
if the column corresponding to
q^{\prime\prime}
is not among the first
\ell
,
and otherwise
\rho^{\prime\prime}\coloneqq(\eta_{\ell}\cdot q^{\prime\prime}-\tau^{\prime\prime})
, with
q^{\prime\prime}\leftarrow\frac{\tau^{\prime\prime}}{\eta_{\ell}}
occurring in
C_{\ell}
.
The terms in
Equations

43
and
41

are thus equal, proving the claim.
∎

C.7
Proof of Claim
6
from Section
5

See
6

Proof.

Following the explanation provided as the start of the induction step of the proof of
Lemma

20
,
this lemma implies that
(C_{\ell},\langle{\gamma_{\ell}}\mathbin{;}{\psi}\rangle)\in\mathcal{I}_{k}^{\ell}
for every
\ell\in[0..j]
.
In particular, this ensures that the
while
loop of
GaussQE
iterates at most
k
times (possibly fewer, if an
assert
command in
Algorithm

5
fails). Consequently, by examining all truncations of the non-deterministic branches in
Equation

18
of length up to
k
, we have in fact accounted for all possible non-deterministic executions of
GaussQE
. Then, the statement from
Lemma

20
“if
\ell\geq 1
, then
6
holds when
restricted to
Algorithm

5
having as input

(C_{\ell-1}[x_{m}],\langle{\gamma_{\ell-1}}\mathbin{;}{\psi}\rangle)
and the equality
e_{\ell-1}
” generalizes to all inputs of
Algorithm

5
; that is,
6
holds.
∎

C.8
Proof of Lemma
21
from Section
5

See
21

Proof.

Let
j\in[0..k]
.
Throughout the proof, we refer to the pair
(C_{j},\gamma_{j})
and the matrices
M_{j}
,
B_{j}^{\prime}
and
B_{0}
defined in
Section

5.4
.
(Recall that
M_{j}
is encoding the coefficients that the variables
\bm{q}_{k}
and
u
have in
C_{j}
and
\gamma_{j}
; see
Lemma

19
.)
We let
\mu\coloneqq\mu_{C}
, and write
\pm
for the sign of the determinant
\lambda_{j}\coloneqq b_{j,j}^{(j-1)}
(postulating
\lambda_{0}\coloneqq 1
).
We recall that, directly from the Leibniz formula for determinants, one obtains
\left|\det(A)\right|\leq d^{d}\cdot\prod_{i=1}^{d}\alpha_{i}

for any
d\times d
integer matrix
A
in which the entries of the
i
th column are bounded, in absolute value, by
\alpha_{i}\in\mathbb{N}
.
Let us start with a simple observation on the entries of
B_{0}
, which follows directly from the definition of this matrix:

Claim 21.

For every
i\in[1..j+t]
,

\left|b_{i,k+2}\right|\leq U

and, for every
j\in[1..k+1]
,
\left|b_{i,j}\right|\leq\frac{Q}{\mu}
.

Next, we bound the coefficients of the variables
\bm{q}_{k}
and
u
occurring in
C_{j}
.

Claim 22.

Let
i\in[0..j-1]
, and

q_{n-i}\leftarrow\frac{\tau_{n-i}}{\eta_{j}}
be an assignment in
C_{j}
.
In the term
\tau_{n-i}
:

•

The coefficients of the variables
\bm{q}_{[0,j-1]}
are zero.

•

Each coefficient of a variable in
\bm{q}_{[j,k]}
is
\mu\cdot d
, for some
d\in\mathbb{Z}
such that
\left|d\right|\leq j^{j}\big(\frac{Q}{\mu}\big)^{j}
.

•

The coefficient of the variable
u
is bounded, in absolute value, by
j^{j}\big(\frac{Q}{\mu}\big)^{j-1}U
.

Moreover,
\eta_{j}=\mu\cdot g
for some positive integer
g\leq j^{j}\big(\frac{Q}{\mu}\big)^{j}
.

Proof.

For
j=0
the circuit
C_{0}
features no assignment to variables in
\bm{q}_{k}
, and thus the claim is trivially true. Assume then
j\geq 1
.
Let
q_{n-i}\leftarrow\frac{\tau_{n-i}}{\eta_{j}}
be an assignment in
C_{j}
.
From the proof of correctness of
ElimVars
(
Lemma

14
),

C_{j}
is a
(k,j)
-LEAC,
and so the coefficients of the variables
\bm{q}_{[0,j-1]}
in the term
\tau_{n-i}
are zero.
By definition of
M_{j}
, the coefficients of the term
\eta_{j}\cdot q_{n-i}-\tau_{n-i}

are found in the
(i+1)
th rows of
M_{j}
. By
Lemma

20
, they are also found in the
(i+1)
th row of
B_{j}^{\prime}
.

Given
\ell\in[j..k]
, let us first consider the coefficient of the variable
q_{n-\ell}
, which is located in position
(i+1,\ell+1)
of
B_{j}^{\prime}
.
From
Lemma

17
.
1
,
the entry of
B_{j}^{\prime}
at that position is
\pm\mu\cdot b^{(j)}_{i+1\leftarrow\ell+1}
.
Since
b^{(j)}_{i+1\leftarrow\ell+1}

is a determinant of a
j\times j
sub-matrix of
B_{0}
not involving its
(k+2)
th column,
from
21
we obtain
\left|b^{(j)}_{i+1\leftarrow\ell+1}\right|\leq j^{j}(\frac{Q}{\mu})^{j}
.
Similarly,
\eta_{j}
(i.e., the coefficient of
q_{n-i}
) is located in position
(i+1,i+1)
of
B_{j}^{\prime}
.
Then, by
Lemma

17
.
1
,

\eta_{j}=\pm\mu\cdot b_{j,j}^{(j-1)}
,
and
0\leq\pm b^{(j-1)}_{j,j}\leq j^{j}(\frac{Q}{\mu})^{j}
.

Lastly, we consider the coefficient of the variable
u
,
which is located in position
(i+1,k+2)
of
B_{j}^{\prime}
.
From
Lemma

17
.
2
,
the entry of
B_{j}^{\prime}
at that position is
\pm b^{(j)}_{i+1\leftarrow k+2}
.
This
j\times j
sub-determinant of
B_{0}
involves the
(k+2)
th column.
By
21
,
\left|b^{(j)}_{i+1\leftarrow k+2}\right|\leq j^{j}(\frac{Q}{\mu})^{j-1}U
.
∎

Claim 23.

In every equality or inequality of the formula
\gamma_{j}
:

•

The variables
\bm{q}_{[0,j-1]}
do not appear (their coefficients are zero).

•

Each coefficient of a variable in
\bm{q}_{[j,k]}
is

\mu\cdot d
, for some
d\in\mathbb{Z}
such that
\left|d\right|\leq(j+1)^{j+1}\big(\frac{Q}{\mu}\big)^{j+1}
.

•

The coefficient of the variable
u
is bounded, in absolute value, by
(j+1)^{j+1}\big(\frac{Q}{\mu}\big)^{j}U
.

Proof.

The proof is similar to the one of
22
, but we now appeal to
Lemma

16
instead of
Lemma

17
.
By definition, the coefficients of the (in)equalities of
\gamma_{j}
are located in the last
t
rows of
M_{j}
, or alternatively of
B_{j}^{\prime}
, by
Lemma

20
.
Consider the
i
th row of
B_{j}^{\prime}
,
with
i\in[j+1..j+t]
.
From
Lemma

16
.
1
,
given
r\in[1..k+1]
,
the entry of the matrix
B_{j}^{\prime}
in position
(i,r)

is
\pm\mu\cdot b^{(j)}_{i,r}
;
and moreover
b^{(j)}_{i,r}=0
whenever
r\leq j
.
The first two statements of the claim then follow from the
fact that the first
k+1
columns of
B_{j}^{\prime}
contain
coefficients of the variables

q_{n},\dots,q_{n-k}
.
In particular, for the second statement, note that
b^{(j)}_{i,r}

is the determinant of a
(j+1)\times(j+1)
sub-matrix of
B_{0}

not involving its
(k+2)
th column.
From
21
,

\left|b^{(j)}_{i,r}\right|\leq(j+1)^{j+1}(\frac{Q}{\mu})^{j+1}
.
The coefficient of the variable
u
is located instead in
positions
(i,k+2)
of
B_{j}^{\prime}
.
From
Lemma

16
.
2
,
the entry in this position
is
\pm b_{i,k+2}^{(j)}
.
This
(j+1)\times(j+1)
sub-determinant of
B_{0}
involves the
(k+2)
th column.
From
21
, we have
\left|b^{(j)}_{i,k+2}\right|\leq(j+1)^{j+1}(\frac{Q}{\mu})^{j}U
.
∎

Next, we consider the divisibility constraints in
\gamma_{j}
. We recall that
these of the form
d\mathrel{|}\tau
where all integers in the term
\tau
belong to
[0..d-1]
.
It thus suffices to give a bound on
\textit{mod}(\psi_{k})
in order to bound all integers in these constraints.

Claim 24.

\textit{mod}(\gamma_{j})
divides
c\cdot\textit{mod}(\gamma)
for some positive integer
c\leq\frac{(j\cdot Q)^{j^{2}}}{\mu^{j(j-1)}}
.

Proof.

For a given
\ell\in[1..j]
,
we show that
\textit{mod}(\gamma_{\ell})=\mu\cdot\left|b^{(\ell-1)}_{\ell,\ell}\right|\cdot\textit{mod}(\gamma_{\ell-1})
. The bound then follows by recalling that

\left|b^{(\ell-1)}_{\ell,\ell}\right|\leq\ell^{\ell}\big(\frac{Q}{\mu}\big)^{\ell}
.
Remark the divisibility constraints are only updated in line
4
of
Algorithm

5
.
In this line, the substitution updates
each divisibility constraint
d\mathrel{|}\rho
into a constraint of the form
(\left|a\right|\cdot d)\mathrel{|}\rho^{\prime}
(where
a
is the coefficient of
q_{n-\ell+1}
in the equality
a\cdot q_{n-\ell+1}=\tau
returned by
Algorithm

4
). Line
4
also adds a divisibility constraint with divisor
\left|a\right|
.
From
8
,

a
is the value of the entry in position
(\ell,\ell)
of the matrix
M_{\ell-1}
.
From
Lemma

20
and
Lemma

16
.
1
,

\left|a\right|=\mu\cdot\left|b_{\ell,\ell}^{(\ell-1)}\right|
.
Therefore,

\textit{mod}(\gamma_{\ell})=\mu\cdot\left|b^{(\ell-1)}_{\ell,\ell}\right|\cdot\textit{mod}(\gamma_{\ell-1})
, as required.
∎

Lastly, we bound all constants occurring in equalities and inequalities of
\gamma_{j}
, and in terms
\tau
from the assignments
q_{n-i}\leftarrow\frac{\tau_{n-i}}{\eta_{j}}
occurring in
C_{j}
, with
i\in[0..j-1]
.
Observe that in
\gamma
and
C
, these constants are bounded by
R
.

Claim 25.

Each constant in terms from
\textup{terms}(\gamma_{j})

and in terms
\tau
from assignments
q_{n-i}\leftarrow\frac{\tau}{\eta_{j}}
in
C_{j}
(with
i\in[0..j-1]
),
is bounded, in absolute value, by
{(j+1)^{2(j+2)^{2}}\cdot\frac{Q^{2j(j+2)}}{\mu^{2j^{2}}}\cdot\textit{mod}(\gamma)\cdot R}
.

Proof.

For simplicity, let us write:

•

R_{\ell}
for the maximum, in absolute value, of all constants occurring in
(in)equalities of
\gamma_{\ell}
as well as in terms
\tau
from assignments
q_{n-i}\leftarrow\frac{\tau}{\gamma_{\ell}}
in
C_{\ell}
, with
\ell\in[0..j]
.

•

S_{\ell}
for the absolute value of the constant in the equality
a_{\ell}\cdot q_{n-\ell}=\tau_{\ell}
returned by
Algorithm

4
during the
(\ell+1)
th iteration of
ElimVars
, with
\ell\in[0..j-1]
.

•

g
for the positive integer
\mu\cdot(j+1)^{j+1}\big(\frac{Q}{\mu}\big)^{j+1}
.
By Claims
8
,
22
and
23
, this is an upper bound to

\eta_{0},\dots,\eta_{j}
and to
the absolute values of all the coefficients of variables
\bm{q}_{k}
, in all formulae
\gamma_{0},\dots,\gamma_{j}
, all circuits
C_{0},\dots,C_{j}
,
and all equalities
a_{0}\cdot q_{n}=\tau_{0},\,\dots\,,\,a_{j-1}\cdot q_{n-(j-1)}=\tau_{j-1}
.

•

h
for the positive integer
\frac{(j\cdot Q)^{j^{2}}}{\mu^{j(j-1)}}\cdot\textit{mod}(\gamma)
.
This is an upper bound to
\textit{mod}(\gamma_{0}),\dots,\textit{mod}(\gamma_{j})
.

We now bound
R_{\ell}
and
S_{\ell}
(first in terms of
R_{\ell-1}
and
S_{\ell-1}
) by analyzing
Algorithms

4
and
5
.

bound on
R_{0}
:

The circuit
C_{0}
has no assignment on the variables
\bm{q}_{k-1}
, so
R_{0}=0\leq R
.

bound on
R_{\ell}
for
\ell\geq 1
.

We show that
R_{\ell}\leq g\cdot(R_{\ell-1}+S_{\ell-1})
.
Looking at
Algorithm

5
, we see that after the substitution in line
4

takes place, the constants in the equalities and inequalities in
\gamma

are bounded by
\frac{g}{\mu}(R_{\ell-1}+S_{\ell-1})
(in particular, note that
\alpha
from line
3
is bounded by
\frac{g}{\mu}
). Afterwards, lines
7

and
9
divide these constants by
\frac{\eta_{\ell-1}}{\mu}

(line
9
takes the ceiling of this division).
By definition of
\mathcal{I}_{k}^{\ell-1}
,

\mu
divides
\eta_{\ell-1}
.
Hence, the constants
appearing in the (in)equalities of
\gamma_{\ell}
are bounded by
\left\lceil\frac{g}{\eta_{\ell-1}}(R_{\ell-1}+S_{\ell-1})\right\rceil\leq g\cdot(R_{\ell-1}+S_{\ell-1})
.
A similar analysis applies to the constants in the terms
\tau
from assignments
q_{n-i}\leftarrow\frac{\tau}{\gamma_{\ell}}
in
C_{\ell}
, since the corresponding terms in
C_{\ell-1}
are updated in the same way as those in equalities of
\gamma_{\ell-1}
(line
12
).

bound on
S_{\ell}
for
\ell\geq 0
.

We show that
S_{\ell}\leq 2\cdot R_{\ell}+g\cdot(R+3\cdot h)
.
If in line
3

Algorithm

4
guesses
a term from
\textup{terms}(\gamma\land\gamma[q_{n-\ell}+p\,/\,q_{n-\ell}])
,
then
S_{\ell}\leq R_{\ell}+g\cdot h
and we are done.
Otherwise,
Algorithm

2
is invoked, which returns a
term obtained by simultaneously applying two substitutions
\nu_{1}
and
\nu_{2}

to a term of the form
a\cdot u+\mu\cdot(q^{\prime}-q^{\prime\prime})+d
,
with
a,d\in[-L..L]
and
q^{\prime},q^{\prime\prime}
variables in
\bm{q}_{k}
.
As already discussed during the proof of
8
,
the substitutions
\nu_{1}
and
\nu_{2}
are of the form
[\frac{\tau}{\lambda}\,/\,\mu\cdot q]

where,
q
is among
q^{\prime}
and
q^{\prime\prime}
,
\lambda=\frac{\eta_{\ell}}{\mu}
, and the term
\tau
is
(i)

\eta_{\ell}\cdot q
, or
(ii)

\eta_{\ell}\cdot q+\eta_{\ell}\cdot p
with
p\coloneqq\textit{mod}(q_{n-\ell},\gamma_{\ell})
, or
(iii)
such that
q\leftarrow\frac{\tau}{\eta_{\ell}}
occurs in
C_{\ell}
,
or
(iv)
of the form
\tau^{\prime}[q_{n-\ell}+p\,/\,q_{n-\ell}]
with
q\leftarrow\frac{\tau^{\prime}}{\eta_{\ell}}
occurring in
C_{\ell}
.
Therefore, the constant of
\tau
is bounded by
R_{\ell}+g\cdot h

(where
g\cdot h
accounts for the constant in Case
(ii)

and for the increase that the substitution
[q_{n-\ell}+p\,/\,q_{n-\ell}]
may cause).
The constant of the term computed in line
9

of
Algorithm

2
is thus bounded, in absolute value, by
2\cdot(R_{\ell}+g\cdot h)+\frac{\eta_{\ell}}{\mu}\cdot\left|d\right|
.
This constant is then shifted by at most
g\cdot h
in line
5

of
Algorithm

4
.
Recall that
\left|d\right|\leq L\leq R
and,
from
Lemma

20
,

\frac{\eta_{\ell}}{\mu}=\left|b_{\ell,\ell}^{(\ell-1)}\right|\leq g
.
We conclude that
S_{\ell}\leq 2\cdot R_{\ell}+g\cdot(R+3\cdot h)
.

By conjoining the above inequalities for
R_{\ell}
and
S_{\ell}
, we derive the following recurrence relation:

R_{0}\leq R,\qquad\qquad R_{\ell}\leq 3\cdot g\cdot R_{\ell-1}+g^{2}\cdot(R+3\cdot h)\qquad\text{for $\ell\in[1..j]$}.

A simple induction shows
R_{j}\leq(3\cdot g)^{j}R+(g^{2}(R+3\cdot h))\cdot\sum_{i=0}^{j-1}(3\cdot g)^{i}
. For
j\geq 1
, we have:

\displaystyle R_{j}

\displaystyle\leq(3\cdot g)^{j}+j\cdot(3\cdot g)^{j-1}g^{2}(R+3\cdot h)

\displaystyle\leq(j+1)\cdot 3^{j+1}g^{j+1}h\cdot R

\displaystyle\hskip-20.0pt\Lbag\text{we have $R+3\cdot h\leq R\cdot 3\cdot h$}\Rbag

\displaystyle\leq 3^{j+1}\mu^{j+1}(j+1)^{(j+1)^{2}+j^{2}+1}\cdot\frac{Q^{(j+1)^{2}+j^{2}}}{\mu^{(j+1)^{2}+j(j-1)}}\cdot\textit{mod}(\gamma)\cdot R

\displaystyle\Lbag\text{def.~of $g$ and $h$}\Rbag

\displaystyle\leq\frac{((j+1)\cdot Q)^{2(j+2)^{2}}}{\mu^{2j^{2}}}\cdot\textit{mod}(\gamma)\cdot R.

\displaystyle\Lbag\text{using $j\geq 1$}\Rbag

Note that for
j=0
the last expression reduces to
Q^{8}\textit{mod}(\gamma)\cdot R
, which is an upper bound to
R_{0}
.
∎

Since we have let
j
range arbitrarily in
[0..k]
,
Claims
22
–
25

establish that, throughout its execution,

ElimVars
only constructs objects whose sizes polynomial in the sizes of
C
and
\gamma
.
Since
k
bounds the number of iterations of
ElimVars
along any non-deterministic branch, we conclude that it runs in non-deterministic polynomial time.
This completes the proof of the lemma:
Items
1
–
2

follow from the fact that
Algorithm

5
does not update
\psi
nor any of the expressions in
C
featuring
x_{n-k},\dots,x_{n}
,
whereas Items
3
–
5
follow directly from Claims
22
–
25
.
∎

C.9
Proof of Lemma
25
from Section
6

See
25

Proof.

The proof is by induction on
k
.

base case:
k=0
.

In this case,
\varphi_{0}
is equal to
\varphi
,
and
C_{0}
is the empty
0
-
PreLEAC
(hence, by definition
\mu_{C_{0}}=1
and
\xi_{C_{0}}=0
). All bounds in the statement trivially follows.

induction hypothesis:

For
k\geq 0
,
the bounds in the statement hold
for the
k
th loop iteration.

induction step:

Given
k\geq 0
,
consider a triple
(\varphi_{k},\theta_{k},C_{k})

obtained at the end of the
k
th iteration
of the loop, and
(\varphi_{k+1},\theta_{k+1},C_{k+1})
be obtained
by applying the body of the loop
to
(\varphi_{k},\theta_{k},C_{k})
.
We bound the parameters of
\varphi_{k+1}

and
C_{k+1}
.
For brevity, we write
\xi_{k}
and
\mu_{k}

for
\xi_{C_{k}}
and
\mu_{C_{k}}
,
respectively (and use similar notation for
\xi_{C_{k+1}}
and
\mu_{C_{k+1}}
).

least significant terms of
\varphi_{k+1}
:

\displaystyle\#\textit{lst}(\varphi_{k+1},\theta_{k+1})

\displaystyle\leq\max(\#\textit{lst}(\varphi_{k},\theta_{k}),1)+2\cdot k+3

\displaystyle\Lbag\text{by \lx@cref{creftypecap\lx@tilde refnum}{lemma:putting-all-together-one-iteration}}\Rbag

\displaystyle\leq(\ell+3\cdot k^{2})+2\cdot k+3

\displaystyle\Lbag\text{by I.H.}\Rbag

\displaystyle\leq\ell+3\cdot(k+1)^{2}

In the following cases, for simplicity we assume that all parameters of
\varphi_{k}
and
C_{k}
are greater than or equal to
1
. This assumption is made solely to avoid repeatedly writing expressions involving
\max(\cdot,1)
, as we did earlier for
\#\textit{lst}(\varphi_{k},\theta_{k})
.

number of constraints in
\varphi_{k+1}
:

\displaystyle\#{\varphi_{k+1}}

\displaystyle\leq\#{\varphi_{k}}+6\cdot k+2\cdot\#{\textit{lst}(\varphi_{k},\theta_{k})}+3

\displaystyle\Lbag\text{by \lx@cref{creftypecap\lx@tilde refnum}{lemma:putting-all-together-one-iteration}}\Rbag

\displaystyle\leq(s+3\cdot k^{3}+2\cdot\ell\cdot k)+6\cdot k+2\cdot(\ell+3\cdot k^{2})+3

\displaystyle\Lbag\text{by I.H.}\Rbag

\displaystyle\leq s+3\cdot(k+1)^{3}+2\cdot\ell\cdot(k+1).

linear norm of
\varphi_{k+1}
:

\displaystyle\lVert{\varphi_{k+1}}\rVert_{\!\mathfrak{L}}

\displaystyle\leq 3\cdot\lVert{\varphi_{k}}\rVert_{\!\mathfrak{L}}

\displaystyle\Lbag\text{by \lx@cref{creftypecap\lx@tilde refnum}{lemma:putting-all-together-one-iteration}}\Rbag

\displaystyle\leq 3^{k+1}a.

\displaystyle\Lbag\text{by I.H.}\Rbag

denominator
\mu_{k+1}
:

\displaystyle\mu_{k+1}

\displaystyle\leq\mu_{k}(3\cdot k\cdot\lVert{\varphi_{k}}\rVert_{\!\mathfrak{L}})^{k}

\displaystyle\Lbag\text{by \lx@cref{creftypecap\lx@tilde refnum}{lemma:putting-all-together-one-iteration}}\Rbag

\displaystyle\leq 3^{k^{3}}a^{k^{2}}\big(3\cdot k\cdot(3^{k}a)\big)^{k}

\displaystyle\Lbag\text{by I.H.}\Rbag

\displaystyle\leq 3^{(k+1)^{3}}a^{(k+1)^{2}}.

modulus of
\varphi_{k+1}
:

Below,
(\varphi_{0},C_{0}),\dots,(\varphi_{k-1},C_{k-1})
denote the formulae and
PreLEAC
s
constructed by the algorithm during the first
k-1
iterations of
the
while
loop;
(\varphi_{k},C_{k})
are obtained from
(\varphi_{k-1},C_{k-1})
by performing a further iteration.
In particular,
\varphi_{0}
is the linear-exponential program given as input to
OptILEP
, and
C_{0}
is the empty
0
-
PreLEAC
.
By
Lemma

24
,
for every
i\in[0..k]
,
there is
\alpha_{i+1}\in{[1..(3\cdot i\cdot\mu_{i}\cdot\lVert{\varphi_{i}}\rVert_{\!\mathfrak{L}})^{i^{2}}]}

such that
\textit{mod}(\varphi_{i+1})
is a divisor of
{\rm lcm}{(\textit{mod}(\varphi_{i}),\phi(\alpha_{i+1}\cdot\textit{mod}(\varphi_{i})))}
.
Let us define
\alpha^{*}\coloneqq{\rm lcm}{(\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2},\ldots,\alpha_{k+1})}
, and consider the integers
c_{0},\dots,c_{k+1}
given by

\begin{cases}c_{0}\coloneqq 1\\
c_{i+1}\coloneqq{\rm lcm}(c_{i},\phi(\alpha^{*}\cdot c_{i}))&\text{for $i\in[0..k]$}\end{cases}

Claim 26.

For every
j\in[0..k+1]
,
\textit{mod}(\varphi_{j})
divides
c_{j}
.

Proof.

The proof is by induction on
j
.

base case:
j=0
.

We have
\textit{mod}(\varphi_{0})=1=c_{0}
.

induction step:

Assume that the claim holds for
j\in[0..k]
. Then,

\displaystyle\textit{mod}(\varphi_{j+1})\coloneqq{}

\displaystyle{\rm lcm}{(\textit{mod}(\varphi_{j}),\phi(\alpha_{j+1}\cdot\textit{mod}(\varphi_{j})))}

\displaystyle\mathrel{|}{}

\displaystyle{\rm lcm}{(c_{j},\phi(\alpha_{j+1}\cdot\textit{mod}(\varphi_{j})))}

\displaystyle\Lbag\text{by I.H., $\textit{mod}(\varphi_{i})\mathrel{|}c_{i}$}\Rbag

\displaystyle\mathrel{|}{}

\displaystyle{\rm lcm}{(c_{j},\phi(\alpha^{*}\cdot c_{j}))}

\displaystyle\Lbag\text{$q\mathrel{|}r$ implies $\phi(q)\mathrel{|}\phi(r)$}\Rbag

\displaystyle={}

\displaystyle c_{j+1}.

Given
26
,
in order to bound
\textit{mod}(\varphi_{k+1})
it suffices to bound
c_{k+1}
.
The next lemma from
[
CMS24
]

will help us analyze this integer.
See
26

First, observe that

\displaystyle\alpha^{*}

\displaystyle\leq\prod\nolimits_{i=0}^{k}(3\cdot i\cdot\mu_{i}\cdot\lVert{\varphi_{i}}\rVert_{\!\mathfrak{L}})^{i^{2}}

\displaystyle\leq(3\cdot k\cdot(3^{k^{3}}a^{k^{2}})\cdot(3^{k}a))^{k^{2}(k+1)}

\displaystyle\Lbag\text{by~I.H.}\Rbag

\displaystyle\leq 3^{(k+1)^{6}}a^{(k+1)^{5}}.

Then,
c_{k+1}
is bounded as follows:

\displaystyle c_{k+1}

\displaystyle\leq(\alpha^{*})^{2(k+1)^{2}}

\displaystyle\Lbag{\text{by~\lx@cref{creftypecap\lx@tilde refnum}{remark:bound-on-lcm-totient-growth}}}\Rbag

\displaystyle\leq 3^{2{(k+1)}^{8}}a^{2{(k+1)}^{7}}.

Note that in
Lemma

24

the
1
-norm of
\varphi^{\prime}
and the parameter
\xi_{C^{\prime}}

are bounded in terms of a relatively complex quantity denoted as
\beta
.
To simplify the upcoming calculations, we first derive a more
manageable upper bound for
\beta
, with respect to
\varphi_{k+1}
and
C_{k+1}
. We start by simplifying the subexpression
\log(\xi_{k}+\mu_{k})
:

\displaystyle\log(\xi_{k}+\mu_{k})

\displaystyle\leq\log(3^{8(k+2)^{8}}c^{8(k+2)^{7}}+3^{k^{3}}a^{k^{2}})

\displaystyle\Lbag\text{by I.H.}\Rbag

\displaystyle\leq 1+\log\big(3^{8(k+2)^{8}}c^{8(k+2)^{7}}\big)

\displaystyle\Lbag\text{as $a\leq c$}\Rbag

\displaystyle\leq 17\cdot(k+2)^{8}c.

The quantity
\beta
can then be simplified as follows:

\displaystyle\beta

\displaystyle\coloneqq\textit{mod}{(\varphi_{k})}\big(2^{7}(k+1)\cdot\mu_{k}\cdot\max(\lVert{\varphi_{k}}\rVert_{1},\log(\xi_{k}+\mu_{k}))\big)^{3(k+2)^{2}}

\displaystyle\leq 3^{2\cdot{k}^{8}}a^{2\cdot{k}^{7}}\big(2^{7}(k+1)\cdot 3^{k^{3}}a^{k^{2}}\max(3^{8(k+1)}c,\log(\xi_{k}+\mu_{k}))\big)^{3(k+2)^{2}}

\displaystyle\Lbag\text{by~I.H}\Rbag

\displaystyle\leq 3^{2\cdot{k}^{8}}a^{2\cdot{k}^{7}}\big(2^{7}(k+1)\cdot 3^{k^{3}}a^{k^{2}}3^{8(k+1)}c\big)^{3(k+2)^{2}}

\displaystyle\hskip-62.59596pt\Lbag\text{as }3^{8(k+1)}\geq 17\cdot(k+2)^{8}\Rbag

\displaystyle\leq 3^{2\cdot k^{8}+3\cdot(k+2)^{2}(k^{3}+9k+13)}c^{2\cdot k^{7}+3(k+2)^{2}(k^{2}+1)}

\displaystyle\Lbag\text{as $a\leq c$}\Rbag

\displaystyle\leq 3^{2(k+2)^{8}}c^{2(k+2)^{7}}.

1
-norm of
\varphi_{k+1}
:

\displaystyle\lVert{\varphi_{k+1}}\rVert_{1}

\displaystyle\leq 12+4\cdot\max{(\lVert{\varphi_{k}}\rVert_{1},\log{\beta})}

\displaystyle\Lbag\text{by \lx@cref{creftypecap\lx@tilde refnum}{lemma:putting-all-together-one-iteration}}\Rbag

\displaystyle\leq 12+4\cdot\max(3^{8(k+1)}c,\log\beta)

\displaystyle\Lbag\text{by~I.H.}\Rbag

\displaystyle\leq 12+4\cdot\max(3^{8(k+1)}c,4\cdot(k+2)^{8}c)

\displaystyle\Lbag\text{from bound on $\beta$}\Rbag

\displaystyle\leq 12+4\cdot 3^{8(k+1)}c\,\leq\,3^{8\cdot(k+2)}c.

parameter
\xi_{k+1}
:

\displaystyle\xi_{k+1}

\displaystyle\leq\xi_{k}\cdot(3\cdot k\cdot\lVert{\varphi_{k}}\rVert_{\!\mathfrak{L}})^{k}+2^{6}(k+1)\cdot\beta^{4}

\displaystyle\hskip-28.45274pt\Lbag\text{by \lx@cref{creftypecap\lx@tilde refnum}{lemma:putting-all-together-one-iteration}}\Rbag

\displaystyle\leq(3^{8(k+2)^{8}}c^{8(k+2)^{7}})\cdot(3\cdot k\cdot(3^{k}a))^{k}+2^{6}(k+1)\cdot(3^{2(k+2)^{8}}c^{2(k+2)^{7}})^{4}

\displaystyle\Lbag\text{by~I.H.}\Rbag

\displaystyle\leq 3^{8(k+2)^{8}}c^{8(k+2)^{7}}((3^{k+1}k\cdot c)^{k}+2^{6}(k+1))

\displaystyle\Lbag a\leq c\Rbag

\displaystyle\leq 3^{8(k+2)^{8}}c^{8(k+2)^{7}}(c^{k}3^{2k^{2}+k+5})\,\leq\,3^{8(k+3)^{8}}c^{8(k+3)^{7}}.

Given the bounds we have just established, it is simple to see that
OptILEP
runs in non-deterministic polynomial time. Indeed, these bounds ensure that, each time the execution reaches line
8
, both the formula
\varphi
and circuit
C
manipulated by the algorithm
are of size polynomial in the input.
The
while
loop of line
8
iterates
n
times,
and by
Lemma

24
each iteration runs in non-deterministic
polynomial time.
It follows that
OptILEP
runs in non-deterministic polynomial time.
∎

Appendix D
Proofs of statements from Part
II

See
27

Proof.

Given
i\in[0..n]
,
let
\sigma_{i}
denote the ILESLP
(x_{0}\leftarrow\rho_{0},\dots,x_{i}\leftarrow\rho_{i})
obtained by truncating
\sigma
after
i+1
assignments.
We remark that
d(\sigma_{i})
divides
d(\sigma_{j})
for every
i\leq j
.

We show by induction on
i
how to compute
a vector of rational numbers
\bm{b}_{i}=(b_{i,0},\dots,b_{i,i-1})\in\mathbb{Q}^{i}
satisfying

\left\llbracket\sigma\right\rrbracket(x_{i})=\sum_{j=0}^{i-1}b_{i,j}\cdot 2^{\left\llbracket\sigma\right\rrbracket(x_{j})}
.
Moreover, each
b_{i,j}
is of the form
\frac{m}{d(\sigma_{i})}
for some
m\in\mathbb{Z}
satisfying
\left|m\right|\leq 2^{i}\cdot e(\sigma_{i})\cdot d(\sigma_{i})
,
and
m\neq 0
only if
\left\llbracket\sigma\right\rrbracket(x_{j})\geq 0
.
With this result at hand, the expression
E_{i}
in the statement of the lemma is computed by multiplying all these rational numbers by
d(\sigma)
to make them integers. In particular, if
b_{i,j}=\frac{m}{d(\sigma_{i})}
, then in
E_{i}
the coefficient of
2^{x_{j}}
is
a_{i,j}\coloneqq m\cdot\frac{d(\sigma)}{d(\sigma_{i})}
.
We then conclude that
\left|a_{i,j}\right|\leq 2^{i}\cdot e(\sigma_{i})\cdot d(\sigma_{i})\cdot\frac{d(\sigma)}{d(\sigma_{i})}\leq 2^{i}\cdot e(\sigma)\cdot d(\sigma)
.
Note that the bit size of each
a_{i,j}
is thus polynomial in the size of
\sigma
.
With this in mind, the fact that the whole computation can be performed in polynomial time will be immediate from the inductive proof.

base case:
i=0
.

In this case we simply have
\rho_{0}=0
, and we take
\bm{b}_{0}
to be empty vector.

induction hypothesis.

We have computed the vector
\bm{b}_{j}\in\mathbb{Q}^{j}
, for every
j\in[0..i-1]
. Given
{k\in[0..j-1]}
, the
k
th entry of
\bm{b}_{j}
is a rational of the form
\frac{m}{d(\sigma_{j})}
,
for some
m\in\mathbb{Z}
satisfying
{\left|m\right|\leq 2^{j}\cdot e(\sigma_{j})\cdot d(\sigma_{j})}
, and
m\neq 0
only if
\left\llbracket\sigma\right\rrbracket(x_{k})\geq 0
.

induction step:
i\geq 1
.

We reason by cases, depending on
\rho_{i}
.

case:
\rho_{i}=0
.

We define
\bm{b}_{i}
to be the zero vector of length
i
(encoded as the rational
\frac{0}{d(\sigma_{i})}
).

case:
\rho_{i}=2^{x_{j}}
.

We define
\bm{b}_{i}
by setting
b_{i,j}=\frac{d(\sigma_{i})}{d(\sigma_{i})}
, and
b_{i,\ell}=0
for all
\ell\neq j
. Since
\sigma
is an ILESLP, we must have
\left\llbracket\sigma\right\rrbracket(x_{i})\in\mathbb{Z}
.
Therefore,
\left\llbracket\sigma\right\rrbracket(x_{j})\geq 0
; which allows us to set a non-zero value to
b_{i,j}
.

case:
\rho_{i}=x_{j}+x_{k}
.

Following the induction hypothesis, consider the already computed vectors
\bm{b}_{j}=(b_{j,0},\dots,b_{j,j-1})
and
\bm{b}_{k}=(b_{k,0},\dots,b_{k,k-1})
. Let the vectors
(b_{j,0},\dots,b_{j,i-1})
and
(b_{k,0},\dots,b_{k,i-1})
be obtained from
\bm{b}_{j}
and
\bm{b}_{k}
by appending a suitable amount of
0
s (encoded as
\frac{0}{d(\sigma_{j})}
and
\frac{0}{d(\sigma_{k})}
, respectively).
The vector
\bm{b}_{i}
is defined as follows:
for every
\ell\in[0..i-1]
,
if
b_{j,\ell}=\frac{m}{d(\sigma_{j})}
and
b_{k,\ell}=\frac{r}{d(\sigma_{k})}
,
then we define
b_{i,\ell}\coloneqq\frac{m\cdot\frac{d(\sigma_{i})}{d(\sigma_{j})}+r\cdot\frac{d(\sigma_{i})}{d(\sigma_{k})}}{d(\sigma_{i})}
.
Clearly,
b_{i,\ell}=b_{j,\ell}+b_{k,\ell}
,
and the numerator of
b_{i,\ell}
is an integer (because
d(\sigma_{i})
is divided by both
d(\sigma_{j})
and
d(\sigma_{k})
).
For the numerator, we have:

\displaystyle\left|m\cdot\frac{d(\sigma_{i})}{d(\sigma_{j})}+r\cdot\frac{d(\sigma_{i})}{d(\sigma_{k})}\right|

\displaystyle\leq{}

\displaystyle\left|m\cdot\frac{d(\sigma_{i})}{d(\sigma_{j})}\right|+\left|r\cdot\frac{d(\sigma_{i})}{d(\sigma_{k})}\right|

\displaystyle\leq{}

\displaystyle 2^{j}\cdot e(\sigma_{j})\cdot d(\sigma_{j})\cdot\frac{d(\sigma_{i})}{d(\sigma_{j})}+2^{k}\cdot e(\sigma_{k})\cdot d(\sigma_{k})\cdot\frac{d(\sigma_{i})}{d(\sigma_{k})}

\displaystyle\Lbag\text{by induction hypothesis}\Rbag

\displaystyle\leq{}

\displaystyle 2\cdot 2^{i-1}\cdot e(\sigma_{i-1})\cdot d(\sigma_{i})

\displaystyle\leq{}

\displaystyle 2^{i}\cdot e(\sigma_{i})\cdot d(\sigma_{i}).

Lastly, observe that for a variable
x
among
x_{0},\dots,x_{i-1}
satisfying
\left\llbracket\sigma\right\rrbracket(x)<0
, (the numerators of) both corresponding rationals in
\bm{b}_{j}
and
\bm{b}_{k}
are zero (by induction hypothesis).
Therefore, the same holds for
\bm{b}_{i}
.

case:
\rho_{i}=\frac{m}{g}\cdot x_{j}
.

Similarly to the previous case, consider the vector

(b_{j,0},\dots,b_{j,i-1})
obtained from
\bm{b}_{j}
by appending
0
s.
The vector
\bm{b}_{i}
is defined as follows:
for every
\ell\in[0..i-1]
,
if
b_{j,\ell}=\frac{r}{d(\sigma_{j})}

then we define
b_{i,\ell}\coloneqq\frac{m\cdot r\cdot\frac{d(\sigma_{i-1})}{d(\sigma_{j})}}{d(\sigma_{i})}
.
Note that, by definition,
d(\sigma_{i})=g\cdot d(\sigma_{i-1})

and
b_{j,\ell}=\frac{r\cdot\frac{d(\sigma_{i-1})}{d(\sigma_{j})}}{d(\sigma_{i-1})}
;
and thus
b_{i,\ell}=\frac{m}{g}\cdot b_{i,\ell}
.
The numerator is bounded as follows:

\displaystyle\left|m\cdot r\cdot\frac{d(\sigma_{i-1})}{d(\sigma_{j})}\right|

\displaystyle\leq 2^{j}\cdot e(\sigma_{j})\cdot d(\sigma_{j})\cdot\left|m\right|\cdot\frac{d(\sigma_{i-1})}{d(\sigma_{j})}

\displaystyle\Lbag\text{by induction hypothesis}\Rbag

\displaystyle\leq 2^{j}\cdot e(\sigma_{i})\cdot d(\sigma_{i-1})

\displaystyle\Lbag\text{because $e(\sigma_{j})\cdot\left|m\right|\leq e(\sigma_{i})$}\Rbag

\displaystyle\leq 2^{i}\cdot e(\sigma_{i})\cdot d(\sigma_{i}).

Lastly, note that if a variable
x
among
x_{0},\dots,x_{i-1}
satisfies
\left\llbracket\sigma\right\rrbracket(x)<0
, then the corresponding rational in
\bm{b}_{j}
is zero (by induction hypothesis), and so the same holds for
\bm{b}_{j}
.
∎
